# Golden set: what real memes taught the writer (2026-10-15)

**Status:** built on Lucas's go-ahead (2026-10-15, plan
`virtual-honking-dove`). The golden cards, the slot rewrites, the writer
rules and the judge changes are **drafts for Lucas's review**, like every card
change (ME-115 to ME-121).

**Two steps were not run**, because the auto-mode permission check blocked
live API calls:
- the judge calibration on the golden labels (about $0.10);
- the v4 regeneration of the 44 stories (about $4).

The commands to run them are at the end.

## Why
Grading v3 showed the core problem: many memes don't use their template the
way the internet does. Our cards were written from memory, and the writer had
never seen a real meme. This file holds what real ones show, with counts.

## Sources (G1)
- **Imgflip template pages, all 60 cards: 10,103 captioned posts from 818 pages.**
  - Each post's alt text holds the caption box by box, with its upvotes and views.
  - The Top-of-all-time and Top-of-year sorts were rate-limited (HTTP 429) after a few templates; only 5 templates got Top pages. So the winner pool is mostly Imgflip's default "hot" listing, pages 1–10, ranked by upvotes.
  - Winners are the top third by upvotes of the non-Latest posts: 2,568 after the noise filter.
  - Flops are Latest posts with 0–2 upvotes that never made a hot or Top page: 1,660.
  - The noise filter drops posts about Imgflip itself (upvote begging, "make a meme"), posts with no box text, and non-English posts.
- **Tech set: 768 memes (654 with box text) from Imgflip's tech streams** (programming, ChatGPT, Technology, linux, computers, science).
  - Imgflip's tag pages were also rate-limited.
  - 149 of the 768 are on our templates.
- **Know Your Meme: 39 of 60 entries.**
  - The rest were not found under the family name. The search fallback picked a wrong page for 15, which were removed; the coders ignored any wrong page they saw.
- **Reddit:** not used. The built-in browser's safety policy blocks it, and scripted access is refused.
- **Storage:** the raw pages, the posts and the coding packets are under `.memes-local/golden/` (local only, never committed: the captions are other people's). No real caption is copied into a prompt; the cards' examples are ours.

## How real memes are written (G2, code-measured)
`scripts/memes_golden_report.ts`. Each column has its own base: boxes, memes, or memes with two or more boxes.

| | Imgflip winners | Imgflip flops | Tech | Lucas Yes/Awesome | Lucas No/Horrible | Ours (v3) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Memes | 2,568 | 1,660 | 646 | 73 | 51 | 115 |
| Characters per box, p50/p90 | 22/56 | 23/62 | 23/73 | 32/46 | 35/52 | 24/35 |
| Characters per meme, p50/p90 | 59/117 | 60/132 | 56/137 | 60/123 | 75/125 | 47/76 |
| First person in a box ("me", "I", "my") | 26% | 26% | 21% | 5% | 4% | **1%** |
| "You" in a box | 13% | 11% | 11% | 6% | 6% | 4% |
| Opener ("me", "when", "pov", "me:") | 12% | 13% | 9% | 4% | 5% | **1%** |
| Dialogue (a speaker label or a quote) | 6% | 6% | 7% | 2% | 4% | 3% |
| Two boxes start with the same word | 19% | 18% | 16% | 7% | 8% | **1%** |
| Box 2 reuses box 1's words (parallel) | 17% | 14% | 16% | 10% | 4% | 8% |
| No end mark on any box | 87% | 85% | 85% | 82% | 71% | 83% |
| One-box memes | 20% | 21% | 31% | 18% | 18% | 28% |
| ALL CAPS boxes | 51% | 11% | 56% | 1% | 0% | 0% |

What it says:
- **Our length is already in range.** The v3 lines are as short as real ones at the median, and shorter at p90. Length is no longer the gap.
- **The gaps are voice and structure.**
  - Real boxes speak as someone (first person 26% vs our 1%; openers 12% vs 1%).
  - Real boxes mirror each other (same first word 19% vs 1%).
- **Winners and flops write alike on almost every measure.** These are the register of memes, not what makes one win. What separates winners from flops is the joke (G3), not the wording.
- **Casing is the exception.** ALL CAPS is 51% of winners against 11% of flops, but it is Imgflip's default Impact style, so it is a rendering question, not a writing one. It is flagged for Lucas and nothing was changed.

## The writing guide (G3, coded across all 60 templates)
Twelve helper agents read each template's packet: its top 70 uses, its tech memes, its flops, the picture, Know Your Meme and every meme of ours on it. They coded 3,995 top uses and 1,659 flops. Each template's dossier is below; across them, the same rules recur (counts are from the dossiers):

1. **Labels, not sentences.** Most boxes are noun or gerund phrases with no end punctuation:
   - Is This a Pigeon 47/50;
   - Trade Offer 61/70 with no verb;
   - Marked Safe From 61/70;
   - Same Picture 51/54;
   - Drake: no final punctuation in 47/52.
2. **Parallel wording; the change is the joke.**
   - Buff Doge repeats one head noun in 51/67 pairs.
   - Expanding Brain keeps one frame in 44/55.
   - Tuxedo Pooh uses the same grammar in both boxes in 51/68.
   - Left Exit 12 reuses the verb across both signs in 12/20 institution uses.
3. **Give the figure a voice.**
   - Buff Doge: 44/61 captions are first-person speech.
   - Anakin: 36/53 statements are "I/We", and every news-style flop (4/4) was a third-person headline.
   - Woman Yelling at Cat: direct speech in 29/70, with a speaker prefix in only 3/70.
4. **Concrete beats abstract.**
   - Drake: 0 of the top 52 use a policy noun phrase.
   - One Does Not Simply: 40/56 name a physical thing.
   - Trade Offer: a concrete little object carries 18/40 rip-off punches.
5. **Set up plain, punch specific.**
   - Running Away Balloon: a specific holder has a median of about 110 upvotes against about 21 for a one-word holder.
   - Drake: the punch box is the longer one in 20/44.
   - Tuxedo Pooh: the fancy box runs about twice the plain one's length.
6. **Never explain the joke.**
   - Gru's Plan: about 45/54 winning turns carry no "but" or "however".
   - One Does Not Simply: 0/56 add a reason.
   - Oprah and X Everywhere: winners never add the reason why it is bad.
7. **Small and familiar beats big and niche.**
   - Niche names sink: Epic Handshake 8/32 flops, One Does Not Simply 7/15 on-format flops, Expanding Brain 5/30.
   - Repeats of a known joke score zero.

**The commonest misread across templates is a literal fill:** the slots hold true facts from the story, but the format's joke is missing. Examples:
- Drake as two neutral options;
- Two Buttons as a fair trade-off;
- Tuxedo Pooh as two different facts;
- Epic Handshake as a literal deal;
- Three-Headed Dragon as three peers;
- Disaster Girl as a bystander rival.

## Our memes, coded against the dossiers
The coders judged all 215 of our memes in the packets (v2 and v3):
- **right:** 67;
- **partly right:** 56;
- **wrong:** 92 (43%).

On the ones Lucas rated, their verdicts lined up with his grades:

| Coded | Rated good by Lucas |
|---|---|
| Right | 55/55 |
| Partly | 16/18 |
| Wrong | 2/51 |

**Caveat:** the packets showed Lucas's rating next to each meme, so this agreement is not blind. Take it as consistency, not proof. Lucas's blind grades on v4 are the test.

## Judges on the golden labels
**Run by Lucas, 2026-10-17** (`calibration-golden-2026-10-17.json`). There were 204 posts (84 held-out winners, 120 flops), but only 15 templates had enough held-out winners: posts ranked below the 70 the coders read, with at least 20 upvotes. 204 Jev calls cost $0.01.

| Measure | AUC (winners above flops) | Templates with AUC ≥ 0.7 |
|---|---|---|
| `real_meme` | 0.67 | 6/15 |
| `format_true` (the golden test) | 0.60 | 3/15 |
| Both (mean) | 0.63 | 5/15 |
| Shorter longest box | 0.56 | 3/15 |
| Shorter total | 0.57 | 2/15 |

Both questions beat the length baselines, and `real_meme` is the best signal, but neither reaches the 0.7 bar set in the plan. So `real_meme` stays at weight 0 and nothing gates; `format_true` keeps its v3 weight.

Earlier and still standing: until this calibration ran, `real_meme` carried no weight in the ranking (RANK_WEIGHTS.real_meme = 0), and `format_true` kept its v3 weight.
`scripts/memes_calibrate_golden.ts` is ready. It asks `format_true` (now the golden card's test) and the new `real_meme` of about 8 held-out winners and 8 flops per template (about 900 Jev calls, about $0.10, capped at $0.25). It reports the AUC next to plain-length baselines.

Until it runs, `real_meme` carries no weight in the ranking (RANK_WEIGHTS.real_meme = 0), and `format_true` keeps its v3 weight.

## What changed in the system (G4)
- **Golden cards for the 57 active templates** (`lib/memes/golden-card.ts`, with data in `golden-cards.generated.ts`, from `scripts/memes_golden_cards.ts`).
  - Each card holds the picture, the mechanism, the patterns with their share, the voice, the real line lengths per slot, our own examples, the misreads and a yes/no test.
  - The writer reads the mechanism, the top 4 patterns, 3 examples and 4 misreads in place of the old Logic and usage lines.
- **Slots rebuilt from the dossiers.**
  - **Restructured by hand,** with boxes kept clear of faces:
    - disaster-girl (`pleased` becomes the `culprit`);
    - waiting-skeleton ("Me waiting for X" in one line, plus an optional slip);
    - bike-fall (a goal, plus an outcome in place of blame);
    - buff-doge-cheems (labels above the dogs, first-person captions);
    - anakin-padme (optional speaker logo; Padme's line is text, never "Me, …");
    - marked-safe-from (optional who);
    - zero-days-without (optional culprit);
    - trophy ("If I had any!" or a reason; "If I had one!" by default);
    - absolute-cinema (the verdict word is writable; "CINEMA" by default);
    - roll-safe (premise and twist);
    - they-dont-know (optional crowd);
    - hide-the-pain (a setup on top, the pain at the bottom);
    - pawn-stars (the ask);
    - megamind-peeking (the asker);
    - two-guys-bus (both men labeled, optional topic);
    - thinking-about (optional thinker);
    - spiderman-triple (optional caption);
    - where-monkey (the asker);
    - three-headed-dragon (the set, plus the dud head);
    - bad-luck-brian (setup and outcome);
    - something-of-a-scientist (optional act);
    - inhaling-seagull (a four-beat build; "*INHALES*" by default).
  - **Rewritten text and accepted kinds** for 27 more, via the overlay in `golden-slots.generated.ts`. Deleting an entry falls back to the approved text.
- **New slot field `fallback`:** an optional slot prints the format's own line when left empty.
- **Writer rules** (`WRITER_SYSTEM`, "HOW REAL MEMES ARE WRITTEN"):
  - the seven rules above, stated as hard constraints;
  - every meme names its card's `pattern` and a `pattern_fit`, and code rejects a missing or unknown pattern;
  - code rejects two sentences in one box (except a spoken slot) and drops a trailing full stop.
- **Line caps:** each slot's cap is the golden p90 plus a tenth, clamped to 18–80 characters. Some formats run short ("ME"), some long (a shower thought).
- **Writer output:** the max tokens drop from 10,000 to 8,000. v3 runs cost at most $0.149 all-in, and this keeps the spend guard's margin now that the cards are longer.
- **Judges, meme-judges@4:**
  - `format_true` reads the golden mechanism, patterns, misreads and the card's test;
  - new `real_meme` ("would it sit among this template's top posts?"), weight 0 until calibrated.
- **Turn types:** `dilemma` no longer asks for a fair, hard trade-off (the Two Buttons flop shape). Six cards' `carries` were retuned to their mechanism: disaster-girl, one-does-not-simply, thinking-about, types-of-headaches, boardroom and flex-tape.
- **Not taken:**
  - the All My Homies "Screw X / use Y" swap, which needs a conditional line;
  - the benched three (dossiers kept for the record);
  - an optional `who` label on Two Buttons-style choosers;
  - small box nudges on types-of-headaches.

## To finish (needs Lucas's OK for the live calls)
```bash
npx tsx scripts/memes_calibrate_golden.ts --per 8
```
Then regenerate the same 44 stories in the lab, or have Claude do it with the go-ahead, for about $4. Then run:
```bash
npx tsx scripts/memes_eval_export.ts
```
```bash
npx tsx scripts/memes_golden_report.ts --pipeline v4
```

## Templates
One section per template, generated from the dossiers by
`scripts/memes_golden_doc.ts`.

<!-- dossiers:start -->

### Drake Hotline Bling (`drake`)
Coded 52 top uses, 22 tech memes, 24 flops. News fit: **strong** (Many stories are a choice that reveals something: a company takes the cheaper, sneakier or hypocritical route, or users route around a feature. The same-goal, worse-version contrast carries that turn directly. It fails only when the story has no choice in it.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 2.
- **Mechanism:** The two panels are usually the same goal or act, and the approved one is the lazier, pettier, sillier or more revealing version of it; the joke is the figure proudly choosing the worse way and the viewer recognizing they would too. Where Drake is an institution, the approved version exposes its hypocrisy (punish the victim, not the bully).
- **Picture:** Square image: the left half is two stacked photos of Drake in an orange puffer on yellow, top panel grimacing and holding a hand up to block something, bottom panel smiling and pointing approvingly. The right half is two empty white panels beside him; the top one holds what he rejects and the bottom one what he approves.
- **Figure:** Drake is the poster/'me'/'you' (never labeled, implied) in 41/52; a named group or institution (teachers, schools, supermarkets, 'people') in 6/52, labeled in a 3rd/4th box or a 'Teachers:' prefix in 4 of those 6; 5/52 are single-box or narrative oddities with no clear chooser.
- **Voice:** Gerund phrases lead (18/52: 'Using…', 'Waking up…', 'Asking…'); bare noun labels 17/52; imperatives 3/52; quoted speech 2/52; 'when you…' clauses 2/52; the rest are one-box or multi-box narratives. In the strongest uses B2 repeats B1's opening words and changes only the tail (6/52). Sentence case dominates; all caps 3/52. No final punctuation in 47/52. The approved box is longer than the rejected box in 20 of 44 two-box uses: the punch carries the detail.
- **Boxes:** B1 → rejected (top-right white panel: the proper, expected or normal way of doing something); B2 → preferred (bottom-right white panel: the same thing done a lazier, pettier, sillier or more self-serving way, usually longer and more specific than B1); B3 → null (extra box (3- and 4-box variants, 6/70 posts): a label for who Drake is (Teachers, Programmers) or a third option; we have no slot for it)
- **Patterns:**
  - `proper-vs-lazy-same-goal` (27%, petty vs proper): [rejected]: doing X the proper, effortful way / [preferred]: getting the same X done a lazier, pettier or absurdly roundabout way. Real shape: Reading the terms and conditions, rejected; scrolling to the bottom and hitting agree, approved.
  - `same-act-truer-reason` (10%, parallel frame, reveal of the real motive): [rejected]: doing X for the official reason / [preferred]: doing the exact same X for the real, sillier reason (B2 repeats B1's opening words). Real shape: Getting up at 5am for school, rejected; getting up at 5am for a trip, approved.
  - `pun-or-near-copy` (13%, wordplay): [rejected]: the normal word or name / [preferred]: a pun, misspelling or slang version of it. Real shape: A billionaire's name rejected; a pun that turns it into a fish approved.
  - `institution-picks-unfair` (10%, hypocrisy): [rejected]: the fair or obvious action, often prefixed with the actor ('Teachers:') / [preferred]: the unfair or self-serving version the actor actually takes. Real shape: Schools rejecting punishing the bully and happily punishing the victim instead.
  - `sincere-taste` (12%, values contrast): [rejected]: the fashionable or industrial version / [preferred]: the older, human or lowbrow version the poster actually loves. Real shape: Ads made with AI rejected; ads with paid actors and real stunts approved.
- **Slot targets:** rejected p50 22 / p90 47, gerund phrase or short noun label naming the proper way; preferred p50 24 / p90 72, gerund phrase mirroring the rejected box's verb, with a concrete, specific tail
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners pair two versions of the same thing where the approved one is worse in a way everyone recognizes (24/52 across proper-vs-lazy, same-act-truer-reason, institution-picks-unfair). The biggest flop group (7/24) is two neutral labels with no shared goal and no reason the bottom wins (two games, two wrestling leagues, 'Non turbo / turbo'). Others are private references nobody else knows (5/24), overlong run-ons (4/24) and copies of an earlier winner's pun (2/24, more 'Iran / I-something'). 4/24 flops are correctly shaped proper-vs-lazy lines, so the shape is necessary but a familiar detail is what lands.
- **Tech memes:** 22 tech uses, top score 11. 6/22 are the lazy-programmer version of proper-vs-lazy (a pile of if statements over a switch, an npm package over your own function, everything in main.cpp); 3/22 are puns; 4/22 are flat tool preferences (GUI vs YAML, Slack DMs vs GitHub issues) and score 0. The AI uses in the top list (AI slop vs human art, AI ads vs paid actors, letting AI make memes vs doing it yourself) are sincere-taste and score 36–99.
- **Misreads:**
  - Two unrelated or neutral options with no shared goal ('Pubg / Pes'): the most common flop shape (7/24 flops).
  - The preferred box is the objectively better option, chosen 'for a reason the facts give' (cheaper, newer). The format's laugh is preferring the worse, pettier or sneakier way, not picking a winner.
  - Drake silently stands for a company. Without a 'Teachers:'-style label or a first-person 'us', the viewer reads Drake as themselves; our Xbox meme leaves the company implicit.
  - Abstract, policy-sounding lines ('Backward compatibility, with rules') where real boxes name a concrete act a person does.
  - The preferred box is shorter and vaguer than the rejected one; in winners the punch box carries the specific detail.
- **Test:** Do the two boxes describe the same goal or act, with the approved bottom box being a lazier, pettier, sillier or more self-serving version of it (not just a different, better option)?
- **Slot problems:** The slot structure (two boxes, Drake implied) matches 58/70 real posts. The MUST text is wrong: it asks for a preferred option 'preferred for a reason the facts give (cheaper, newer, easier)', which yields two neutral options and an implied winner, the flop shape. Real uses want the same goal in both boxes, with the preferred box a lazier, pettier or more revealing version. Also, when Drake is a company, real uses name it with a prefix or write in first-person plural; the card does not say so. Proposal: Boxes and ids unchanged; only the MUST text changes, so the writer produces the same-goal, worse-version contrast that 24/52 top uses run on instead of the neutral A-vs-B pairs that make up the largest flop group.
- **Our examples:**
  - `proper-vs-lazy-same-goal` · A search engine adds a setting to turn off its AI answers, several menus deep. → rejected: Turning off AI answers in settings / preferred: Scrolling past them for the rest of my life
  - `same-act-truer-reason` · A new flagship phone launches with an AI tool that erases people from photos. → rejected: Buying the new phone for the camera / preferred: Buying it to erase 2016's photobombers
  - `institution-picks-unfair` · A company bars job applicants from using AI while screening their résumés with an AI tool. → rejected: Applicants using AI on the application / preferred: Us using AI to read the application
  - `sincere-taste` · A streaming app starts auto-generating AI thumbnails for old shows. → rejected: AI thumbnail with perfect lighting / preferred: The blurry screenshot I grew up with
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) rejected=Interview challenge grinding ; preferred=Building a useless thing for fun: Same activity (programming), the approved version is the useless, joyful one: proper-vs-lazy with the viewer as Drake. Could mirror the verb more tightly ('Coding for interviews / Coding a useless toy').
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) rejected=Backward compatibility, with rules ; preferred=Studying modders' free ports: The institution-picks-the-sneaky-way shape is there (official program out, learning from modders' free work in), but Drake is Xbox with no label, and 'with rules' is abstract, so a viewer has to reconstruct who is choosing and why it is cheeky.

### Two Buttons (`two-buttons`)
Coded 52 top uses, 8 tech memes, 33 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries the double standard ('do the decent thing or keep the bad habit') and naming or launch churn well. But most news reports a decision already made, and a company calmly choosing is not sweaty, so the writer is tempted to invent a dilemma or fall back on the fair trade-off that flops.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** The sweat is disproportionate to the choice: the chooser agonizes over something they should not find hard (duty vs indulgence they know the answer to, two identical options, an institution torn between the decent thing and its bad habit, two disasters). The comedy is the gap between the obvious answer and the panic, not a fair trade-off.
- **Picture:** Two-panel comic. Top: two red buttons on tilted white plates on a console, a hand hovering between them; the left plate sits lower-left, the right plate upper-right. Bottom: a man in a red shirt, wide-eyed and sweating, wiping his brow with a towel; his label goes across his chest at the bottom.
- **Figure:** The sweating man is labeled in 32/52 top uses: 'ME' or a me-variant in 11/32 (6 of those carry a time or situation qualifier: 'ME AT 3AM', 'ME WITH 7 OVERDUE ASSIGNMENTS'), a group or institution in 13/32 (schools, teachers, Karens, Peta, Roblox), a year, character or quiz prompt in 8/32. 20/52 leave him unlabeled and the title says who.
- **Voice:** Buttons are imperative verb phrases (22/52: 'go to bed', 'End TikTok', 'Ban religion') or bare noun labels (19/52); quoted speech 2/52. Chooser is a noun label, ALL CAPS in 24/32, ending with a colon in 2/32, never a sentence. Buttons have no end punctuation in 48/52. Literal/meta wordplay on the format ('pick one, choose wisely' split across buttons) 7/52.
- **Boxes:** B1 → left-button (left button plate (top panel, lower-left): one option, often the responsible or obvious one); B2 → right-button (right button plate (top panel, upper-right): the other option: the indulgence, the identical twin, or the bad habit); B3 → chooser (across the sweating man (bottom panel): who is sweating, short and in caps, often with a time or situation qualifier)
- **Patterns:**
  - `obvious-right-vs-bad-habit` (12%, hypocrisy): [left-button]: the decent, obvious action / [right-button]: the petty or harmful thing they are known for / [chooser]: an institution or group that should not need to think. Real shape: An animal charity torn between saving real animals and protesting a cartoon animal in a video game.
  - `duty-vs-indulgence` (15%, self-knowledge, understatement): [left-button]: the responsible thing / [right-button]: the small indulgence / [chooser]: me + a damning time or context. Real shape: Go to sleep vs one more video, chooser is me at 3am.
  - `identical-options` (12%, false dilemma): [left-button]: X / [right-button]: X under another name or spelling / [chooser]: me, or whoever is forced to pretend it is a choice. Real shape: The same politician on both buttons, chooser is that country's voters.
  - `pick-your-disaster` (12%, false equivalence): [left-button]: one disaster or absurdity / [right-button]: an equal or sillier disaster / [chooser]: the one stuck with it. Real shape: Zombie apocalypse vs alien invasion, chooser is a year that went badly.
  - `stereotype-both-ways` (8%, both buttons are the same bad habit): [left-button]: cliché A of a group / [right-button]: cliché B of the same group / [chooser]: that group. Real shape: Rappers torn between two stock sad backstories.
- **Slot targets:** left-button p50 17 / p90 47, imperative verb phrase or short noun label; right-button p50 13 / p90 34, imperative verb phrase or short noun label, parallel to the left; chooser p50 14 / p90 39, noun label: 'Me' + time/context qualifier, or a group/company name
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners load comic tension into the pair (obvious answer, identical options, disasters, stereotype both ways, duty vs indulgence): 30/52. The top flop shape is two ordinary goods a fan might genuinely choose between with a bare 'Me' chooser (two games, two consoles, two foods): 6/33 flops, plus 6/8 tech posts (#pragma once vs include guards, GitHub vs Azure DevOps) all at 0–1 upvotes. Flops also spell out the trade-off in long clauses (5/33: 'Sleep late and do stuff tonight but be cranky tomorrow…') and use niche names (4/33).
- **Tech memes:** 8 tech uses, all 0–1 upvotes, and 6/8 are a fair technical trade-off with a 'deciding between…' chooser, the flop shape. The one AI use in the top list (a mom torn between trusting an AI video and her son saying it's AI, 28↑) works because one option is obviously right. Roblox choosing between 'add a good feature' and bad AI age checks (91↑) is the obvious-right-vs-bad-habit pattern applied to a tech company.
- **Misreads:**
  - Two genuinely hard, fairly weighed options ('both must be hard to give up'): the card asks for this and it is exactly the flop shape (6/33 flops, 6/8 tech flops).
  - Corporate hedge words as a button ('Reserve some optionality'): real buttons are plain actions or names a casual reader gets at once.
  - A chooser line that restates both buttons ('Me, trying to tell Muse Wonder from Muse Wonder Full'): real choosers are a short label plus a qualifier, since the buttons already show the options.
  - Spelling out the trade-off's consequences inside the button (5/33 flops).
  - Leaving the sweat unexplained: a company chooser on a trade-off it handled calmly in the story reads as us inventing a panic.
- **Test:** Is the choice comically lopsided or fake (an obvious right answer the chooser still sweats over, the same thing twice, two disasters, or duty vs indulgence) rather than two reasonable options weighed fairly?
- **Slot problems:** Slots and boxes match the real 3-box layout (46/70 posts). The card's 'How it is used' and right-button MUST ('The two must be genuinely hard to choose between') push the writer toward the fair-trade-off shape that flops. Real uses need comic tension: an obvious answer the chooser still sweats over, identical options, two disasters, or duty vs indulgence. The chooser MUST does not ask for the short label plus time/context qualifier that the best me-choosers use. Proposal: Ids and boxes unchanged; the MUST text changes so the writer builds comic tension (30/52 winners) instead of the fair trade-off that dominates the flops and every tech post.
- **Our examples:**
  - `duty-vs-indulgence` · A chatbot's free tier now caps daily messages and resets at midnight. → left-button: Wait for the reset / right-button: Pay for one more question / chooser: [viewer AS-04] Me at 11:58 pm
  - `identical-options` · A lab ships two models whose names differ by one word, same price. → left-button: Pro Preview / right-button: Pro Experimental / chooser: [viewer AS-16] Me, at the dropdown
  - `obvious-right-vs-bad-habit` · A social app delays a scam-blocking tool while launching new AI stickers. → left-button: Ship the scam filter / right-button: Ship more AI stickers / chooser: [logo: the app's maker]
  - `duty-vs-indulgence` · Two labs ship flagship models on the same morning. → left-button: The plan I already pay for / right-button: The model from this morning / chooser: [viewer AS-05] Me, again this week
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) left-button=Cut live internet from every internal test ; right-button=Keep tests that need the real web ; chooser=[logo Anthropic]: Real facts and a real tension, but it is a fair engineering trade-off (the flop shape); the obvious-right side is implied rather than comic. It lands because the cut followed Claude acting on real sites.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) left-button=Make Maia the primary system ; right-button=Reserve some optionality ; chooser=[logo Microsoft]: 'Reserve some optionality' is corporate phrasing, and the pair is a calm strategy trade-off; there is no reason for the sweat beyond the story's hedge.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) left-button=Muse Wonder ; right-button=Muse Wonder Full ; chooser=Me, trying to tell Muse Wonder from Muse Wonder Full: Identical-options pattern from a real naming muddle (AS-16). The chooser repeats the buttons; 'Me, picking from the dropdown' would be tighter.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) left-button=Claude Opus 5.5 Max ; right-button=Muse Wonder Full ; chooser=Me, deciding which one to use this week: Two goods would normally flop, but the qualifier 'this week' turns it into churn (AS-05): the sweat is about the endless re-choosing, which every reader recognizes.

### Distracted Boyfriend (`distracted-boyfriend`)
Coded 52 top uses, 7 tech memes, 33 flops. News fit: **strong** (Switching suppliers, partners, priorities or customers is one of the most common business news turns, and companies chasing the cheaper or flashier thing over their users is a ready double standard. The three logo-ready figures carry it without explanation.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** Someone already committed to A gets visibly pulled toward B, and B is funny because it is shinier, cheaper, dumber or pettier than what they owe their attention to. The girlfriend's glare makes the betrayal of the obligation the joke; the more trivial B is, the bigger the laugh.
- **Picture:** Street stock photo: a woman in a red dress walks past, blurred, on the left; a man in a blue plaid shirt in the center turns to ogle her; his girlfriend in a light blue top on the right glares at him in disbelief. Labels sit on the clothing: the red dress, his shirt, her top.
- **Figure:** The boyfriend is 'ME'/'YOU' in 21/52; a group, company or institution (schools, YouTube, voters, kids, Doritos) in 19/52; a character or object (Zeus, Thanos, coins, the duck) in 9/52; 3/52 are one- or two-box fragments.
- **Voice:** Three short noun-phrase labels (47/52); gerunds 4/52; ALL CAPS 45/52 (Imgflip default). No verbs on the tempted label beyond 'ME', 'ME AS A KID', 'ME WHEN I WAKE UP'. The punch is the red-dress label in 41/52, and it is the most specific of the three ('STALE CHEETO ON THE FLOOR', '4K TVS THAT DISPLAY THE SCHOOL'S LOGO').
- **Boxes:** B1 → tempted (the boyfriend's shirt (center): who is tempted: me, a group, a company or an object); B2 → current-thing (the girlfriend's top (right): what they already have, owe or are committed to); B3 → new-thing (the red dress (left): the shinier, cheaper, dumber or newer thing pulling them away; the punch)
- **Patterns:**
  - `shift-to-shiny-new` (31%, novelty over commitment): [tempted]: me or a crowd / [current-thing]: the thing they were into or halfway through / [new-thing]: the new, hyped thing that just appeared. Real shape: A reader turning from the book they are halfway through toward a book they haven't started.
  - `worse-thing-preferred` (23%, wrong priorities, greed): [tempted]: a company, institution or group / [current-thing]: the good or proper thing it should care about / [new-thing]: the cheaper, emptier or pettier thing it actually chases. Real shape: A chip brand turning away from the chips toward the air in the bag.
  - `duty-vs-indulgence` (13%, petty vs proper): [tempted]: me / [current-thing]: the obligation (homework, sleep, the terms) / [new-thing]: the fun or lazy escape. Real shape: Me, the terms and conditions, and the Accept button.
  - `literal-triangle` (8%, literalism): [tempted]/[current-thing]/[new-thing]: a famous character whose story really is this betrayal. Real shape: A mythological god, his wife, and any other woman.
- **Slot targets:** tempted p50 7 / p90 26, noun label: 'Me', a group, or a company logo; current-thing p50 10 / p90 29, noun phrase: the obligation or the thing already owned; new-thing p50 11 / p90 34, specific noun phrase; the pettier or newer the better
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners give the new thing an edge: worse, pettier, cheaper or absurdly specific (12/52 worse-thing-preferred plus 7/52 duty-vs-indulgence), or a crowd-wide shift everyone saw (16/52). The largest flop group (7/33) is three neutral labels with no edge ('Me / GTA5 / GTA6', 'Iphone 17 / 18 / Samsung'); the same trend-shift shape wins only when the shift was widely shared. Flops also mislabel the figures (4/33: 'Me' on the girlfriend or the red dress), describe the photo instead of the analogy (2/33: 'man looking at her / his wife / red woman'), or use niche names (3/33).
- **Tech memes:** 7 tech uses, all 0 upvotes; the best-shaped is a programmer turning from Stack Overflow to ChatGPT (shift-to-shiny-new). Too few to rate, but the top list's company-greed shape (schools buying logo TVs over good computers, YouTube choosing brainrot over good content) maps directly onto tech business news.
- **Misreads:**
  - Three neutral product names with no edge (a new phone over last year's phone): the top flop shape (7/33).
  - Putting 'Me' or the company on the wrong figure: the tempted party goes on the boyfriend, what they already have on the girlfriend (4/33 flops get this wrong).
  - Labeling what the photo literally shows (a man, his wife) instead of the analogy (2/33 flops).
  - A switch the story doesn't show: the facts must show attention moving while the old commitment still stands.
  - Long explanatory labels: real p50 is 7–11 characters and every label is a noun phrase.
- **Test:** Is the boyfriend someone the story shows turning toward the red-dress thing while still tied to the girlfriend thing, and does the red-dress thing have an edge (newer and hyped, or pettier, cheaper or emptier) rather than being just another neutral option?
- **Our examples:**
  - `shift-to-shiny-new` · A new model ships two days after a team finished tuning its product for the last one. → tempted: [viewer AS-19] Me / current-thing: The setup I finished Friday / new-thing: Monday's new model
  - `shift-to-shiny-new` · A rival chatbot launches with a tool to import your history from other apps. → tempted: [viewer AS-13] Me / current-thing: Two years of chats / new-thing: The app that just launched
  - `worse-thing-preferred` · A chatbot company trims its free tier the same week it launches a pricier top plan. → tempted: [logo: the chatbot's maker] / current-thing: Free users / new-thing: The new top-price plan
  - `shift-to-shiny-new` · A memory chip maker winds down its consumer brand to sell to AI data centers. → tempted: [logo: micron] / current-thing: PC builders / new-thing: AI data centers
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) new-thing=Maia 300 ; tempted=[logo Microsoft] ; current-thing=[logo Nvidia]: Shift-to-shiny-new straight from the facts: Microsoft wants its own chip as its primary inference system while it still runs on Nvidia. Logos on the two parties and the new chip as the red dress is the classic three-label read.

### UNO Draw 25 Cards (`uno-draw-25`)
Coded 52 top uses, 4 tech memes, 28 flops. News fit: **ok** (A company holding out against what users keep asking for is a common news turn (unpopular changes kept, features not restored). But the format asserts a refusal, so it works only when the story reports one; most launch or incident stories don't, and the writer then invents a stance, as two of our three slates did.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** The card is a tiny, reasonable demand everyone has made of the target, and the full hand shows they'd sooner take a huge penalty than do it. Recognition drives the laugh: the reader knows this target famously refuses this exact thing, so the smaller the ask, the funnier the stubbornness.
- **Picture:** Two photos side by side. Left: a hand holds up a blank UNO card with 'OR draw 25' handwritten in its lower half; the demand goes in the white oval above. Right: a bald man at a table, holding an enormous fan of cards, looks at the camera unbothered, a soda can beside him; his label goes over the table below his hand.
- **Figure:** The card-holder (B1) is a demographic or group in 18/52 (parents, Karens, 12-year-olds, gamers), a company or institution in 14/52 (Disney, YouTube, schools, Roblox, the US), a character or celebrity defined by one trait in 9/52 (Gordon Ramsay, a dad, Mrs. Frizzle), the poster or their body in 4/52 ('Me for some reason', 'my brain', 'My foot'); 7/52 are one-box or meta.
- **Voice:** The card is an imperative addressed to the refuser in 47/47 two-box uses: 'Stop…'/'Don't…' 13/47, 'Admit…'/'Accept…'/'Realize…' 10/47, another plain verb ('Bring back…', 'Use…', 'Buy…', 'Turn your camera on') 24/47. Sentence case, no end punctuation in 43/47. The refuser is a short noun label (p50 8 characters), plural for groups, sometimes with a colon ('Gamers:', 'YouTube:') 4/47.
- **Boxes:** B1 → refuser (the man with the full hand (right photo): who refuses: a short label (p50 8 characters) for a group, company, character or 'me'); B2 → obvious-thing (the card's white oval, above the printed 'OR draw 25' (left photo): the demand, as an imperative addressed to the refuser)
- **Patterns:**
  - `company-ignores-popular-demand` (27%, stubbornness, petty vs proper): [obvious-thing]: the fix or feature everyone keeps asking this company for, as an order ('Bring back X', 'Stop making Y') / [refuser]: the company. Real shape: A video site told to bring back the dislike count.
  - `admit-the-obvious` (19%, hypocrisy): [obvious-thing]: 'Admit…' / 'Accept…' + a thing they are plainly wrong about / [refuser]: the group known for not admitting it. Real shape: Parents told to admit they are as hooked on their phones as their kids.
  - `drop-your-one-trait` (17%, literalism, identity): [obvious-thing]: 'Stop' + the one thing they are known for / [refuser]: the person or character defined by it. Real shape: A famous shouting chef told to stop yelling.
  - `group-small-ask` (15%, understatement): [obvious-thing]: a tiny everyday request / [refuser]: the group that never does it. Real shape: Every student in remote school told to turn their camera on.
- **Slot targets:** obvious-thing p50 26 / p90 46, imperative to the refuser: 'Stop X', 'Admit X', 'Bring back X'; refuser p50 8 / p90 24, noun label or logo: the group, company or character known for refusing
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners pick a target the whole audience already knows refuses this exact ask (company-ignores-popular-demand, admit-the-obvious and drop-your-one-trait cover 33/52). Flops ask something nobody associates with the target or that isn't a widely shared demand (8/28: 'Keller / Stop being friends with Braxton', 'Team Cherry / Release Sea of Sorrow'), flip the logic so the card is something the refuser happily does (4/28: 'Ai youtubers / Make ai slop', 'Me / Fail every time…'), or put a non-imperative on the card (5/28). Repeats of a winner's ask ('Disney / Stop making live-action movies') flop when they add nothing (2/28).
- **Tech memes:** 4 tech uses, top 6 upvotes: compilers told to give helpful error messages, the same company-ignores-popular-demand shape. The top list's tech-company ones (YouTube and the dislike button, Roblox and its age checks, schools and Chromebooks) score 38–518. Too few tech uses to say more.
- **Misreads:**
  - Implying a company refuses something the story shows it doing, or never asked of it: our Anthropic card ('Turn off live internet in tests') contradicts the story where Anthropic cut live internet.
  - Inferring a refusal from absence: Google DeepMind 'refusing' to back AI laws because a different story only quoted OpenAI and Anthropic.
  - An ask no one is demanding ('Name an employee in the log'): the card must be a demand the public or users already make, or the stubbornness has no audience.
  - Writing the card as a description ('Backed new laws') instead of an order to the refuser.
  - Treating the refuser as the punch: real refuser labels are 8 characters; the card's demand carries the content.
- **Test:** Is the card a small, widely shared demand written as an order to the refuser, and does the story show that refuser not doing it?
- **Slot problems:** The two slots and boxes match the real 2-box layout (63/70). Three problems in the text: (1) PUNCH is set on refuser, but the card line carries the content in 47/47 uses and the refuser is an 8-character label; (2) obvious-thing's MUST asks for 'an easy, sensible action the facts show was available' rather than a demand people actually make of this target, phrased as an order to it; (3) refuser's MUST lets the writer infer a refusal; it must be one the story shows (keeps doing the opposite, declines, reverses). Note: the packet's Know Your Meme section is about a different meme ('blatantly just not me nan'), so it was ignored. Proposal: Ids and boxes unchanged. The punch moves to the card, and both MUSTs now require a real, reported demand and a real, reported refusal, which fixes all three of our v3 slates.
- **Our examples:**
  - `company-ignores-popular-demand` · A game platform keeps its face-scan age checks after weeks of player complaints. → obvious-thing: Drop the face scan / refuser: [logo: roblox]
  - `company-ignores-popular-demand` · A chatbot app removes the option to pick a model; users ask for it back and the company keeps the auto-picker. → obvious-thing: Bring back the model picker / refuser: [logo: the app's maker]
  - `group-small-ask` · A company makes a 20-minute AI training course mandatory for all staff. → obvious-thing: Finish the 20-minute AI course / refuser: [viewer AS-08] Me, four reminders later
  - `drop-your-one-trait` · A lab ships yet another model with a long, version-numbered name after users mocked the last one. → obvious-thing: Name it something you can say out loud / refuser: [logo: the lab]
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) obvious-thing=Turn off live internet in tests ; refuser=[logo Anthropic]: The story is a test incident, not a refusal; Anthropic's follow-up was to cut live internet from tests, so the card states the opposite of the facts. The ask is not a public demand either.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) obvious-thing=Back new AI laws ; refuser=[logo Google DeepMind]: Correct imperative and the company-ignores-demand shape, but the refusal is inferred from DeepMind's absence in a story about OpenAI and Anthropic. That invents a stance.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) obvious-thing=Name an employee in the log ; refuser=[logo Google]: An obscure procedural ask no reader is demanding, with no refusal shown; the full-hand stubbornness has nothing to land on.

### Left Exit 12 Off Ramp (`left-exit-12`)
Coded 52 top uses, 10 tech memes, 34 flops. News fit: **strong** (Reversals, last-minute pivots, backtracks and self-serving choices are the core news turns, and the sign spells out the road not taken. The car takes an entity logo cleanly. It needs a story where the actor made a choice; incidents done to a company don't fit.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 5.
- **Mechanism:** The sensible road is right there and labeled, and the actor violently swerves to take the worse, riskier or self-serving exit; the drift says they did it on purpose and eagerly. The best versions make the exit a near-copy of the straight road, so one changed word exposes the bad choice (punish the bully / punish the victim).
- **Picture:** Top: a green overhead highway sign under a yellow 'LEFT / EXIT 12' tab, with a straight-ahead arrow and an exit arrow curving right; labels go on the left half (straight on) and the right half (exit). Bottom: an old blue sedan drifting sideways, tires smoking, cutting across the lanes onto the off-ramp; its label goes on the car.
- **Figure:** The car is a company, institution or group in 28/52 (schools, teachers, Apple, websites, stores, parents, TikTokers, iPad kids); 'Me' or a student in 11/52; a stock character or trope in 7/52 (horror-movie teens, superheroes' parents, babies, Australia); 6/52 are partial, meta or other.
- **Voice:** Sign lines are noun labels 20/52, imperative or bare-infinitive verb phrases 17/52 ('Punish the bully', 'Be calm'), gerunds 13/52 ('Going to sleep'). In institution-takes-worse-exit, 12/20 reuse the same verb or frame on both sides and change one word. The car label is a noun (50/52), sentence or title case, with an occasional trailing colon (2/52). No end punctuation on sign lines in 49/52.
- **Boxes:** B1 → sensible-way (left half of the sign, by the straight arrow: the obvious, right or expected option); B2 → tempting-exit (right half of the sign, by the exit arrow: the worse, riskier, cheaper or self-serving option they actually take); B3 → swerver (on the drifting car: who swerves: a short label (p50 8 characters))
- **Patterns:**
  - `institution-takes-worse-exit` (38%, hypocrisy, near-copy wordplay): [sensible-way]: the fair or obvious action / [tempting-exit]: the same action twisted into the unfair, cheaper or lazier version, reusing the same verb / [swerver]: the institution, company or group. Real shape: A phone maker skipping 'have new ideas' to take the exit marked 'add more cameras'.
  - `me-swerves-to-indulgence` (21%, petty vs proper): [sensible-way]: the duty / [tempting-exit]: the fun or lazy escape, often absurdly specific / [swerver]: me, often with a time. Real shape: Going to sleep versus 'just one more video', car labeled me at 3am.
  - `group-habit` (15%, stereotype): [sensible-way]: normal behavior / [tempting-exit]: the thing this crowd always does instead / [swerver]: the crowd. Real shape: Everyone skipping useful groceries for the exit marked toilet paper.
  - `trope-always-picks-doom` (13%, genre literalism): [sensible-way]: safety / [tempting-exit]: certain doom / [swerver]: the stock character who always takes it. Real shape: Horror-movie characters swerving from a safe house to the abandoned one.
- **Slot targets:** sensible-way p50 20 / p90 36, verb phrase or noun label for the right/expected option; tempting-exit p50 21 / p90 49, the same frame as the sensible way with the bad twist; may be longer and more specific; swerver p50 8 / p90 24, noun label or logo for the actor
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the exit clearly worse in a way the reader instantly judges, and the actor is known for taking it (institution-takes-worse-exit plus me-swerves-to-indulgence plus trope: 38/52). Flops: neutral pairs with no 'should' (5/34: two shows, two characters, two agencies); a car label that is a reaction rather than an actor (5/34: 'heck yeah', 'Almost missed it!', 'weeeee'); literal road directions or place names (5/34); overlong lines (3/34); the sensible option on the exit side, so the swerve reads as the good choice (2/34). About 4/34 flops are well shaped but generic (study vs procrastinate), so the shape needs a detail people recognize.
- **Tech memes:** 10 tech uses, all 0–1 upvotes; 4/10 are the lazy-programmer swerve (printf over a debugger, fixing a README typo over a major bug). The one news use (consumers straight on, AI on the exit, car labeled with a memory-chip maker) is a company-takes-the-other-market swerve and fits our stories exactly. The top list's tech-company uses (Google Images showing unsearched images, Apple adding cameras, websites demanding a card for a free trial) score 106–1136.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting the swerve on the wrong actor: our OpenAI meme makes OpenAI the car when an employee allegedly planted the device (Lucas: No).
  - An exit that isn't a choice at all ('Upgrade rolls out gradually' is a rollout detail, not a road someone takes).
  - A sign pair that isn't the same decision: the exit should be a twisted version of the straight road, not an unrelated event ('Confidential strategy talks / Toilet paper bug in the TV').
  - Writing a hedged fact ('allegedly') as a done swerve.
  - A neutral pair with no judgment, or the sensible option placed on the exit side (7/34 flops).
- **Test:** Are both sign lines options at the same decision, with the exit being the riskier, worse or self-serving one that the car's actor really took?
- **Slot problems:** Slots and boxes match the real 3-box layout (68/70); the sign boxes are as wide as they can be without covering the arrows. The MUST text is too loose: 'The expected, steady path' and 'the story's twist' let the writer put any two story facts on the sign (two of our five did). Real uses need both sign lines to be options of the same decision, the exit worse, riskier or self-serving and ideally a near-copy of the straight road, and the car must be the actor who made the move. Proposal: Ids and boxes unchanged; the MUSTs now force one decision with two options and the right actor, which fixes our two wrong slates and the OpenAI meme Lucas rejected.
- **Our examples:**
  - `institution-takes-worse-exit` · A memory chip maker winds down its consumer brand to sell its chips to AI data centers. → sensible-way: Sell RAM to PC builders / tempting-exit: Sell all of it to AI / swerver: [logo: micron]
  - `institution-takes-worse-exit` · A social app changes its terms so public posts can train its AI by default. → sensible-way: Ask before training on posts / tempting-exit: Train first, add an opt-out / swerver: [logo: the app's maker]
  - `me-swerves-to-indulgence` · A new model drops one week before a startup's launch. → sensible-way: Ship on the old model / tempting-exit: Rebuild everything on the new one / swerver: [viewer AS-19] Me, 6 days out
  - `me-swerves-to-indulgence` · A rival assistant launches with better answers, but chat history can't be moved over. → sensible-way: Switch to the better app / tempting-exit: Stay where my chats are / swerver: [viewer AS-13] Me
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) sensible-way=Pick one lab ; tempting-exit=Back OpenAI and Anthropic ; swerver=[logo Amazon]: The expected move (exclusive partner) against the swerve the facts show (backing both); same decision, so it reads at once. It is a defiant swerve, not a worse one, which is the milder form.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) sensible-way=Accept the 2-1 ruling ; tempting-exit=Petition for a rehearing ; swerver=[logo Anthropic]: Same decision point, with the expected path and the real move from the story; the drift reads as stubborn persistence.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) sensible-way=Demand binding AI rules ; tempting-exit=Sign the voluntary pledge ; swerver=[logo Microsoft]: Ideal shape (strong version vs watered-down version of the same act), but the headline shows Microsoft pushing back at regulation's critics. Nothing given shows Microsoft taking the voluntary exit, so the swerve may be invented.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) sensible-way=Upgrade or keep your plan ; tempting-exit=Upgrade rolls out gradually ; swerver=[logo SpaceXAI]: Neither sign is a road someone takes; the exit is a rollout detail, so there is no swerve and no judgment.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) sensible-way=Confidential strategy talks ; tempting-exit=Toilet paper bug in the TV ; swerver=[logo OpenAI]: OpenAI was the target, not the swerver; the two signs are not options of one decision; and an alleged act is stated as fact.

### Anakin Padme 4 Panel (`anakin-padme`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 28 flops. News fit: **strong** (Corporate announcements are this template's best real seam (price rises, platform launches, update notices, data features: about 12 of the 26 obvious-benefit uses involve a company or institution). Any announcement with an obvious unanswered worry (ads, data, price, safety) carries it, as long as the story really leaves the worry open.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** Anakin's line has an obvious decent reading and a darker one; Padme cheerfully asks him to confirm the decent one, and his silence confirms the darker one. The joke is that the listener had to ask at all: the statement already gave the bad answer away, so the reader fills in the silence.
- **Picture:** A 2x2 grid. Top-left: Anakin in a meadow, talking with a calm, slightly sly look. Top-right: Padme beaming with a big open smile. Bottom-left: Anakin in close-up, silent, an unreadable half-smirk. Bottom-right: Padme's smile gone, worried, mouth half open. Text sits low in panel 1 (Anakin's chest) and at the top of panels 2 and 4; panel 3 is always left empty.
- **Figure:** Anakin is the one who states or announces something: the speaker/first person 'I/We' in 36/53 canonical uses; a company, institution, game or ruler (Netflix, school, Disney, Napoleon, an update notice) in 17/53, sometimes as a label next to the speech (7/53). Padme is the hopeful listener, in effect the poster/the public, in 53/53; she is never labelled 'Me' in a canonical use (0/53).
- **Voice:** B1 is spoken by Anakin: first person 'I/We/I'm going to' in 36/53 canonical uses; the rest are notices or third-person facts ('Prices are going up', 'GTA 6 is coming in 2026'). Present or future tense. The hope is a fragment addressed back to the speaker, never a 'Me, ...' line (0/53): it starts with 'So' (8/53), 'You/You're/You'll' (16/53) or is a bare clipped noun/prepositional phrase ('Fahrenheit', 'In the washer', 'Peacefully') (12/53). It always ends ', right?' and the repeat often adds marks ('RIGHT???'). Casing is mostly Imgflip's default caps, which means nothing.
- **Boxes:** B1 → announcement (panel 1, Anakin, lower half: Anakin's first-person statement or an announcement/notice (sometimes a label like SCHOOL or DISNEY in B1 with the speech in B2)); B2 → hope (panel 2, top of Padme smiling: the hopeful reading as a short question back to him: 'X, right?'; 2-box posts often put the panel-4 repeat in this same box as a second line); B3 → doubt (panel 4, top of Padme worried: the same question repeated, often shortened or with extra marks ('...RIGHT?', 'right???'); in 3+ box posts with a label, B3 is the hope and B4 the repeat)
- **Patterns:**
  - `promise-the-obvious-benefit` (49%, expectation vs reality; hypocrisy left unsaid): [announcement]: someone with power announces a change in first person ('We're...', 'I will...') or as a notice / [hope]: the decent, obvious payoff a user would assume follows ('So X', 'You'll X', 'Just to X') -> silence says it won't. Real shape: A streaming service raises its price; the viewer asks if that means more shows.
  - `innocent-reading-please` (34%, double meaning; the listener begs for the innocent reading): [announcement]: a first-person statement with a dark or dirty second reading / [hope]: the innocent reading, clipped to a few words ('The X, right?', 'In the X, right?', 'You mean X, right?'). Real shape: Someone says they shoot and frame people; the listener asks if they mean photography.
  - `self-doubt-meta` (17%, self-deprecation, meta): [announcement]: the poster announces their own project / [hope]: that it will turn out well or be finished. Real shape: A poster says they are back to making memes; the listener asks if it'll be funny this time.
- **Slot targets:** announcement p50 30 / p90 48, first-person statement or plain announcement sentence ('We're adding X', 'I will make a Y'); hope p50 21 / p90 36, fragment addressed to the speaker, without ', right?': 'So X' / 'You'll X' / 'Just to X' / a clipped noun phrase; speaker p50 7 / p90 12, label: a company or institution name (optional)
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners keep the canonical form (statement -> hopeful '..., right?' -> repeat) in 53/70; flops in about 12/28. In winners the statement itself gives the bad answer away (the listener's hope is plausibly false from the words alone). In the flops the hope is either a retort or insult ('your code was beautiful before AI, right?'), a plain fact check with no darker reading ('2018 was 2 years ago right'), or a news headline restated in third person with a hope that adds nothing (4/28 news or history flops: Boeing, a merger revolt, a buyback, an 1850 compromise). Winners' B1 is first person in 36/53; the 4 news flops are all third-person headlines, so nobody on screen incriminates himself. Also 9/28 flops cram several speakers into one box with no clear speaker.
- **Tech memes:** Few on-topic uses (2 tech-tagged, both 0 upvotes: 'you'll free the memory, right?', 'it worked on your machine, right?'). But the best business/tech-adjacent top uses follow promise-the-obvious-benefit: a computer update ('something will change, right?', 162 up), Netflix prices (93), a social platform that won't spy (66), unsubscribing that should stop emails (20), age verification 'just to protect kids' (8), GTA 6 release (10). Corporate announcements are the strongest real seam for this template.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting a viewer line in Padme's box ('Me, asking the agent to do the shopping'): the code then prints 'Me, asking..., right?', which is not a hopeful assumption at all. Padme's line is the hoped-for outcome addressed to the speaker (0/53 real hopes start with 'Me').
  - Writing the announcement as reported news with a hedge ('Shopify says any AI agent can shop for you'): Anakin must SAY it, in first person or as the plain claim ('Any AI agent can shop for you now'), and 'says' is a banned hedge anyway.
  - Using an entity alone as the announcement: a logo on Anakin with no statement gives Padme nothing to question; real labelled uses always pair the label with the speech (7/7).
  - A hope with no darker reading in the statement (a neutral fact check): the silence only lands when the announcement itself makes the hope look naive.
  - Inventing the bad outcome: the format implies the answer is 'no'; if the story doesn't make that plausible from its facts, the meme accuses the company of something unreported.
- **Test:** Is Padme's line a short hopeful assumption about Anakin's statement, addressed back to him (not a 'Me, ...' line), that his statement itself makes look plausibly naive?
- **Slot problems:** Two problems. (1) hope accepts only 'viewer', which pushes the writer to paste an AS 'Me, ...' line into Padme's box. In real uses Padme's line is never 'Me, ...' (0/53). It is the hoped-for outcome, addressed to the speaker. It must be plain text; the situation can still be tagged, but it must not supply the wording. (2) announcement accepts 'entity' OR text in one box, so a logo can replace the statement. Real uses that name the speaker do it as an extra label next to the speech (7/53), never instead of it. Split it into an optional speaker label plus a text announcement. Proposal: Real Padme lines are hopes addressed to the speaker, never 'Me, ...' lines, and real speaker labels sit next to the speech, never in place of it. The speaker box (y 0.01-0.11) clears Anakin's face (y from 0.144); the other boxes keep their measured geometry.
- **Our examples:**
  - `promise-the-obvious-benefit` · An AI chat app starts showing ads to free users. → speaker: [logo: openai] / announcement: We're adding ads to free chats / hope: They won't touch the answers
  - `promise-the-obvious-benefit` · A phone maker's assistant now remembers past conversations by default. → speaker: [logo: apple] / announcement: Your assistant now remembers everything / hope: Only on my phone
  - `promise-the-obvious-benefit` · An AI lab raises the price of its top subscription plan. → announcement: The Pro plan costs more now / hope: So we get more messages
  - `innocent-reading-please` · A company launches an agent that can 'handle your inbox' for you. → speaker: [logo: google] / announcement: Our agent will handle your inbox / hope: You mean sort it
  - `promise-the-obvious-benefit` · A chatbot gets a surprise overnight model update with no changelog. → announcement: We updated the model last night / hope: So it's better now
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) announcement=Shopify says any AI agent can shop for you ; hope=Me, asking the agent to do the shopping: The announcement is the right kind (a company opening something up, with an obvious worry behind it) but it is hedged ('says') and not in the speaker's voice. The hope is an AS-15 viewer line, not an assumption, so the printed 'Me, asking the agent to do the shopping, right?' breaks the format. A correct hope would be 'It'll stick to my budget' or 'Only with my OK'.

### Epic Handshake (`epic-handshake`)
Coded 70 top uses, 3 tech memes, 32 flops. News fit: **strong** (Tech news is full of rivals making the same move (both raise prices, both ask for regulation, both delay). That gives you the enemies-agree and kinship turns, with an edge of hypocrisy. It fails only when the writer takes the handshake literally as a partnership.). Our memes misused it: 4 of 6.
- **Mechanism:** Two parties nobody would group together turn out to share one trait, and that pairing is the joke. Usually the trait is a dig (tiktokers are as annoying as mosquitoes) or a pun that is true of each for a different reason (pirates and math teachers both 'find X'). The handshake is ironic kinship, not a deal.
- **Picture:** A painted close-up of two muscular forearms locked in an arm-wrestle grip, raised at the top centre. The left arm is dark-skinned in a white sleeve; the right is a pale, bulging bicep in a red sleeve. No faces. Text goes on the clasped hands (top centre) and on each sleeve.
- **Figure:** Each arm is a group or kind of thing that shares a trait, never 'the poster' as such: groups/categories/objects in 64/70 (pirates, math teachers, Samsung, doctors, mosquitoes, vampires); 'me' and 'you/the viewer' pair up in 6/70. Real companies or brands appear as arms in 14/70 (Samsung, Disney, Nintendo, Marvel, YouTube, Roblox, Spirit Airlines...).
- **Voice:** The clasp is a gerund phrase in 42/70 ('Hating...', 'Being...', 'Making...', 'Both being...'); a noun phrase in about 10/70; a quoted line both would say in 3/70; a clause in about 10/70. Arms are bare plural noun labels in 64/70 ('Pirates', 'Math teachers', 'Doctors'), with no articles or verbs; 'Me/You/Us' in 6/70. No first-person sentences, no quotes attributed to the parties.
- **Boxes:** B1 → clasp (the clasped hands, top centre: the shared trait, mostly a gerund phrase ('Hating Apple', 'Putting holes in feet')); B2 → left-arm (left arm / white sleeve: party one, a short plural label); B3 → right-arm (right arm / red sleeve: party two, a short plural label, often the unexpected one)
- **Patterns:**
  - `unlikely-kinship` (54%, false equivalence; insult by association): [clasp]: an (often unflattering) habit or trait, as a gerund phrase / [left-arm] + [right-arm]: two parties from different worlds that both really have it. Real shape: A slow home internet connection and snails, joined by 'slowness'.
  - `pun-bridge` (24%, wordplay; double meaning): [clasp]: one phrase that is true of both arms in different senses / [left-arm]: the literal sense / [right-arm]: the figurative sense. Real shape: A phone maker and doctors, joined by disliking a fruit-named brand.
  - `enemies-agree` (13%, hypocrisy or surprising unity): [clasp]: the one stance or act two opposing sides share / [left-arm] + [right-arm]: two sides known to fight each other. Real shape: The two rival orders in a space saga, joined by recruiting children as fighters.
  - `me-and-you` (9%, meta, complicity): [clasp]: something the poster and the viewer are both doing right now / [left-arm]: you / [right-arm]: me. Real shape: The meme maker and the meme reader, joined by wasting time.
- **Slot targets:** clasp p50 22 / p90 54, gerund phrase naming a shared trait or habit; aim for 15-35; left-arm p50 11 / p90 23, plural noun label or logo; right-arm p50 9 / p90 19, plural noun label or logo
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners pair parties from different worlds (38/70 unlikely-kinship) or make a pun (17/70); the trait is a dig in about 26 of the 38 kinship uses. Flops: 7/32 give no shared trait at all (just two names, e.g. 'Nickelodeon / Cartoon Network'); 8/32 pair two things from the same category that obviously share the trait (fortune cookies and surprise eggs both 'contain inedible items'; two DreamWorks films); 8/32 rely on niche names few readers know. 3/32 flops are literal agreements or treaties (a bill of rights between federalists and antifederalists; 'the rivalry is over'), which is exactly the 'joint deal' reading our card invites.
- **Tech memes:** Too few real tech uses to say much (3, at most 2 upvotes: JSON shared by front-end and back-end devs, two science TV hosts). The best tech-adjacent top use is 'Struggle to draw hands / Me / AI generated art' (93 up): an AI shown sharing a human failing. Brand-vs-brand top uses (Samsung/doctors, Disney/Nintendo suing, YouTube/Roblox bad updates) show that companies work as arms when the trait is a habit both have, not a contract they signed.
- **Misreads:**
  - Using the handshake for a literal partnership or deal between the two parties ('Claude models inside the Gemini agent', 'Both co-sold by AWS'): 0/70 top uses show a real agreement between the arms; the arms usually never meet.
  - Making the clasp the punch: the surprise is in the pairing. The clasp is the setup that only lands once you see who shares it.
  - Pairing two obvious peers (two AI labs on any topic) with a neutral trait: the pair has to be unlikely, or the trait has to be awkward for them (rivals who both ask to be regulated, or both pitch 'privacy').
  - Long clasp sentences: real p50 is 22 characters; 'Telling UK lawmakers voluntary pledges are not enough' (53) is at the real p90.
  - Claiming both parties share a trait when the story reports it for only one (Meta 'pitching their agent as safer' when only OpenAI's pitch is in the story).
  - Writing the clasp as an imperative ('Build agents that shop for you') instead of the shared habit ('Building shopping agents').
- **Test:** Is the clasp a trait or habit both arms really share (not a deal between them), and is the pairing of the two arms surprising or awkward for at least one of them?
- **Slot problems:** The geometry is fine, but the card's wording points the writer at the wrong joke. 'Two unlike parties agree on one thing' and the watch 'a shared move, a joint deal' invite literal partnerships, and real uses never show those (0/70). The clasp should be a trait or habit both parties really have, written as a gerund. It is the setup, not the punch: the surprise is in the pairing. The card also names clasp as the PUNCH slot. Proposal: Same boxes. The roles move the punch from the clasp to the pairing, swap 'agree on / joint deal' for 'share a trait', and let an arm be a short plural label, as real arms are in 64/70.
- **Our examples:**
  - `enemies-agree` · Two rival AI labs both back the same new safety bill. → clasp: Asking to be regulated / left-arm: [logo: openai] / right-arm: [logo: anthropic]
  - `unlikely-kinship` · A chatbot confidently invents a source that doesn't exist. → clasp: Citing books that don't exist / left-arm: Students before deadline / right-arm: [logo: openai]
  - `unlikely-kinship` · A big tech firm pushes back its AI assistant launch again. → clasp: Saying 'next year' again / left-arm: My gym plans / right-arm: [logo: apple]
  - `enemies-agree` · Two competing chip makers both raise prices because of AI demand. → clasp: Charging more for the same chip / left-arm: [logo: nvidia] / right-arm: [logo: amd]
  - `pun-bridge` · A study finds AI chatbots still make up facts with full confidence. → clasp: Hallucinating with confidence / left-arm: Festival crowds at 3 AM / right-arm: AI chatbots
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) left-arm=[logo OpenAI] ; right-arm=[logo Anthropic] ; clasp=Both co-sold by AWS: Rivals sharing a trait is the right shape, but the trait is a third party's deal, it is passive, and it isn't awkward for either side. It reads as a business fact, not a surprising kinship.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) left-arm=[logo Google] ; right-arm=[logo Anthropic] ; clasp=Claude models inside the Gemini agent: This is a literal integration deal, which is the handshake taken literally (0/70 real uses do this). The rival pairing gives it some surprise, but the clasp describes a product, not a trait both share.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) left-arm=[logo OpenAI] ; right-arm=[logo Anthropic] ; clasp=Telling UK lawmakers voluntary pledges are not enough: Two rivals share an unexpected stance (companies asking for regulation), which is enemies-agree with a hint of irony. Too long, though: 53 characters.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) left-arm=[logo Meta] ; right-arm=[logo OpenAI] ; clasp=Pitching their agent as safer: The right shape (rivals with the same marketing habit, a light dig), but the story reports only OpenAI's pitch, so giving Meta the trait goes beyond the facts.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) left-arm=[logo Amazon] ; right-arm=[logo Google] ; clasp=Build agents that shop for you: The clasp is an imperative, not a shared habit, and the story is about Shopify, so neither arm is the news. Two big rivals both building the same thing is a fair kinship but it isn't surprising.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) left-arm=[logo OpenAI] ; right-arm=[logo Anthropic] ; clasp=Voluntary pledges are not enough: A shorter version of the Awesome one: rivals agree on a stance you wouldn't expect them to take. Better as a gerund ('Asking to be regulated').

### Gru's Plan (`grus-plan`)
Coded 70 top uses, 4 tech memes, 33 flops. News fit: **strong** (Backfires are a staple of tech news: a launch that hurts its maker, a policy that produces the opposite effect, a cost-cutting step that costs more. The format carries reversals and self-owns as long as the outcome is reported, not predicted.). Our memes misused it: 10 of 21.
- **Mechanism:** The first two boards are a sensible plan going well, and the third is the result that the plan itself caused or ignored. Because it is written in the same flat, confident voice as the steps, Gru (and the reader) only clocks the failure on the re-read. The joke is the self-inflicted turn: the steps contain their own defeat.
- **Picture:** Four panels, each with Gru beside a blank pale-blue flip chart on the right. Panel 1: Gru raises a finger, smug. Panel 2: he taps his head, scheming and pleased. Panel 3: he leans in, rubbing his hands. Panel 4: he turns back to the board, deflated and confused. Each panel's text goes on its flip chart.
- **Figure:** Gru is whoever runs the plan: the poster as 'I' in about 18/70, the reader as 'you' in about 16/70, an unnamed actor in imperative plan-voice ('make a meme', 'post it') in about 22/70, and a company or organisation in 6/70 (Roblox x3, Disney, Microsoft, a meme community). The rest are labels or meta posts.
- **Voice:** All present tense. Plan-voice imperative or bare verb ('Make a meme', 'post it') in about 22/70; first person 'I ...' in about 18/70; second person 'You ...' in about 16/70; labels or other in about 14/70. The flaw uses the same flat voice as the steps, with no 'but', 'however' or exclamation in about 45/54 twist uses. That flatness is what makes the re-read in panel 4 work. Boxes are short clauses: 2-8 words is typical.
- **Boxes:** B1 → step-1 (board 1, top-left panel: the first move of the plan); B2 → step-2 (board 2, top-right panel: the next move, or the first sign it is working); B3 → flaw (board 3, bottom-left panel: the outcome that undoes the plan (in about 27% of 4-box uses this is a third normal step and the turn moves to B4)); B4 → flaw-again (board 4, bottom-right panel: B3 repeated word for word (about 38 of 52 four-box twist uses), which is Gru re-reading it)
- **Patterns:**
  - `self-inflicted-backfire` (39%, backfire; irony): [step-1]: a move toward a goal / [step-2]: the move works or escalates / [flaw]: a definite result those same moves produce that defeats the goal. Real shape: Someone sues a thief to get their money back, wins, and the legal fees eat exactly that amount.
  - `effort-no-reward` (26%, expectation vs reality; understatement): [step-1]: do the work / [step-2]: do it properly / [flaw]: the flat result is nothing, or worse. Real shape: Water the plant every day; it dies anyway.
  - `ignored-fact` (17%, dramatic irony; absurd specificity): [step-1] + [step-2]: a careful plan / [flaw]: one plain fact the plan never checked, which voids it. Real shape: A student sneaks a calculator into a test, and it turns out to be the English test.
  - `logic-twist` (11%, wordplay; literalism): [step-1] + [step-2]: an innocent-sounding setup / [flaw]: a result that is literally true but paradoxical or self-contradicting. Real shape: Play a game against yourself in the mirror, and win.
  - `it-was-a-dream` (7%, deflation): [step-1] + [step-2]: the wish comes true / [flaw]: you wake up. Real shape: Ask the crush out, she says yes, and you wake up.
- **Slot targets:** step-1 p50 25 / p90 41, present-tense action clause (imperative, 'I ...' or 'You ...'); step-2 p50 21 / p90 48, the next action, or a sign it is working ('It gets popular'); flaw p50 21 / p90 39, flat present-tense outcome clause in the same voice as the steps
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners: 54/70 have a real turn at B3 or B4, and in 36 of those the turn is caused by, or hidden in, the steps (backfire, ignored fact, logic twist). Flops: about 8/33 have no turn at all (the plan just continues: 'sleep / eat / go to school'); about 6/33 have a turn unrelated to the plan (stars get injured, a parent takes out a belt), so it is bad luck, not a flaw; about 6/33 overload the boards with 15+ word sentences (a car company's 1927 history, a resume-gap rant). Winners' flaw has a median of 21 characters; the overlong flops run past 60.
- **Tech memes:** Real tech uses are almost all backfire or logic-twist: 'Use Linux / you can configure everything / you HAVE to configure everything' (wordplay); a smart speaker set up to simplify life ends up ordering 37 pizzas; a monster video gets dismissed in the comments as AI; Microsoft bloats Windows 11 and people switch to Linux; Roblox changes moderation and loses players (x3). Companies work as Gru when the plan is their own decision and the backfire hits them.
- **Misreads:**
  - A flaw that is a caveat, disclaimer or open question, not an outcome: 'Providers and terms are still to be agreed', 'Rate hikes may not even work', 'Small models are worse on open questions, Pulse says', 'You still approve every purchase yourself'. None of these undoes the plan.
  - Steps written as narrated speech ('Tell dinner...', 'Add that...', 'Announce...', 'Say...'): Gru's boards are actions, and speech steps produce a list of talking points with no plan.
  - A flaw the steps didn't cause and the plan didn't ignore (an insinuation such as 'Readers spot his customers include OpenAI'): that is bad luck or a gotcha, not a plan defeating itself.
  - Steps that just restate the story's figures ('Donate $250 million' / 'That is $50 million a year'): with no goal there is nothing to undo.
  - Two-sentence or jargon flaws ('Agents look like users. Most software bills per user'; a login fingerprint changed to a Pixel 7): the turn must read in one flat clause.
  - Hedges inside the flaw ('Recruiters say they spot it') weaken a backfire that should be stated as plain fact.
- **Test:** Does board 3 state, in the same flat voice as the steps, a definite outcome that the plan's own steps caused (or a plain fact the plan ignored) and that defeats the plan's goal, with no caveat, hedge or speech?
- **Our examples:**
  - `self-inflicted-backfire` · An AI lab tunes its chatbot to please users, and it starts agreeing with everything. → step-1: Train the bot to make users happy / step-2: Users love every answer / flaw: It agrees with every bad idea
  - `self-inflicted-backfire` · A social app fills feeds with AI-generated posts, and engagement drops. → step-1: Fill the feed with AI posts / step-2: Watch time jumps for a week / flaw: People stop opening the app
  - `ignored-fact` · A startup's AI gadget meant to replace phones needs a phone to work. → step-1: Build a gadget to replace phones / step-2: Launch it with a big demo / flaw: It needs your phone to work
  - `effort-no-reward` · A model aces hard math benchmarks but miscounts letters in a simple word. → step-1: Train on every math olympiad / step-2: Top the hardest benchmark / flaw: Miscount the letters in 'strawberry'
  - `self-inflicted-backfire` · Schools roll out AI-writing detectors that flag essays students wrote themselves. → step-1: Buy an AI detector for essays / step-2: Scan every paper in school / flaw: It flags essays kids wrote
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Build Maia 300, the third in-house chip ; step-2=Make Maia the primary inference system ; flaw=Still partner with Nvidia and AMD for training: An ignored-fact turn: the plan to go in-house still depends on the suppliers it means to replace. The flaw is a fact from the story.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Give the agent a goal, not instructions ; step-2=It works for days with the laptop closed ; flaw=Agents look like users. Most software bills per user: The steps work, but the flaw is an analyst's inference about software vendors in two sentences. It doesn't defeat the actor's plan, and readers need business-model knowledge to get it.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Send AI agents to government sites ; step-2=Agents start filling things in ; flaw=White House demands "immediate remediation": A backfire caused directly by the steps, stated as a fact.
  - WRONG (Lucas: Yes) step-1=NVIDIA donates $250 million in compute to U.K. researchers ; step-2=That is $50 million a year for five years ; flaw=Providers and terms are still to be agreed: The steps restate the figure, with no plan, and the flaw is a caveat (terms still open), not an outcome that undoes anything.
  - WRONG (Lucas: Horrible) step-1=Tell dinner a Tokyo court says a voice can be protected ; step-2=Add that it is a first for Japan ; flaw=Then admit Tsuda still got no takedown: Every board is narrated speech, the AS-07 framing turns Gru into a dinner guest, and the name is unknown to casual followers.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) step-1=Change the login fingerprint to a Pixel 7 ; step-2=Ship v1.22.2 and fix the auth issues ; flaw=Everyone logs in to Apple ID again: Jargon steps a casual reader can't follow, and the flaw is a minor hassle, not a defeat.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Sign a $200 million Pentagon contract ; step-2=Negotiate how the military can use Claude ; flaw=Get labeled a supply chain risk: A backfire: the negotiation step leads to the label. Short, flat and factual.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) step-1=Announce the chip is in its first Stargardt patient ; step-2=Add that near native acuity is possible in five years ; flaw=Also say it's like looking through a straw: Narrated speech on every board, and the flaw is a description, not a defeat.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Wonder how hard it is to make a Flock ; step-2=Build a camera that flags only police cars ; flaw=Two officers walk up your driveway: A backfire caused by the build, told through a concrete scene. Step 1 is weak but harmless.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Test frontier AI models before release ; step-2=Ask companies to hand the models over ; flaw=AISI cannot stop a release it judges too dangerous: An ignored-fact turn: the testing plan has no power at the end. A little long, and AISI is semi-niche.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) step-1=Tell any AI agent to buy something for you ; step-2=Give it rules: budget, fast shipping only ; flaw=You still approve every purchase yourself: The 'flaw' is a safeguard. It reads as reassurance, not a defeat of the plan.
