  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) step-1=Hire people away from Apple ; step-2=Build a device that could rival the iPhone ; flaw=A judge hands Apple your interview files: A textbook backfire: step 1 is exactly what causes the flaw, told as a concrete, flat event.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Apply to an AI biotech research job ; step-2=No drug discovery experience needed, great ; flaw=Needs a PhD and a research track record: An ignored-fact turn in a relatable first-person register.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) step-1=Say AI doom talk is unhelpful ; step-2=Say the industry overlooked AI's promise ; flaw=Readers spot his customers include OpenAI: Narrated speech steps, and the flaw is an insinuation the plan didn't cause.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Open registrations and line up sponsors ; step-2=Plan the whole celebration in Anaheim, CA ; flaw=Money lands below what covers costs: Effort with no reward, stated as fact; the flaw's wording is clunky ('Funding falls short' would be cleaner).
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Reward the robot for reaching the part ; step-2=Reward it for lining the part up too ; flaw=Success goes down: A counter-intuitive backfire in three words, the closest of ours to the real register.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) step-1=Raise rates to cool prices ; step-2=Raise them again, markets expect it this month ; flaw=Rate hikes may not even work: A hedged caveat ('may') instead of an outcome.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) step-1=Use an AI app to answer interview questions live ; step-2=Get smooth answers fed to you in real time ; flaw=Recruiters say they spot it and end the interview: A backfire caused by the plan. 'Say' is a hedge the code should drop: 'Recruiters spot it and end the call'.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) step-1=Keep every money question on my own Mac ; step-2=Download about 2.9 GB of AI models once ; flaw=Small models are worse on open questions, Pulse says: The flaw is the company's own caveat with a hedge, and it doesn't defeat keeping data local.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) step-1=Build AI data centers everywhere ; step-2=Push prices up everywhere ; flaw=AI firms' borrowing costs more: The right backfire shape (rising prices come back as costlier loans), but step 2 is a side effect, not a step, and the chain has to match the Fed's reported reasoning.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) step-1=Reward only a finished assembly ; step-2=Also reward reaching and aligning ; flaw=Success rate drops: Same strong backfire as the graded AlphaNIST meme, flat and short.

### Running Away Balloon (`running-away-balloon`)
Coded 70 top uses, 7 tech memes, 34 flops. News fit: **strong** (Tech news constantly puts a gate between users and a new thing: paid tiers, staged rollouts, region locks, usage caps, hardware requirements. Each is a ready-made pink figure, and the facts supply the specific detail that makes winners win.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** Something universally wanted floats away while one small, specific, mundane thing calmly holds you back. The pink figure's placid face sells it: the obstacle doesn't even try hard. The laugh is recognition, and the more specific and petty the obstacle, the bigger the laugh.
- **Picture:** A two-panel cartoon. Top: a grinning grey stick man reaches with both arms for a big yellow balloon drifting away at top right. Bottom: the same man, still grinning but now sweating, reaches for the balloon (smaller, further right) while a calm pink blob grabs him around the waist from behind. Labels go on the balloon, on the man's body (both panels) and on the pink figure.
- **Figure:** The man is the poster: a bare 'ME/me/Me:/YOU' in 43/70, 'Me' plus a clause in 5/70, a group the poster belongs to ('STUDENTS', 'PLAYERS', 'WEB DEVS', 'THE MEME COMMUNITY') in 12/70, other in 10/70. The pink figure is an obligation or institution (school, homework, Monday) in about 27/70; a tiny, specific mundane obstacle in about 22/70; a person or authority (mom, girlfriend, a dictator, a game studio) in about 15/70, overlapping the first two; the poster's own brain or anxiety in 5/70.
- **Voice:** Noun-phrase labels throughout, not sentences: the want is a noun or gerund phrase in about 60/70 ('FREEDOM', 'WEEKEND PLANS', 'TAKING A SCREENSHOT'); the wanter is a bare 'ME' in 43/70; the holder is a noun phrase in about 58/70 and a quoted line in about 4/70. Numbers make holders specific ('43 HOMEWORK ASSIGNMENTS', '13 MISSING ASSIGNMENTS'). Casing is Imgflip's default caps and means nothing.
- **Boxes:** B1 → want (top balloon: the desired thing, generic and short); B2 → wanter (top man's body: 'ME' or a short group label); B3 → want-again (bottom balloon: the want repeated verbatim (in about 50 of the 57 five-box uses)); B4 → holder (pink figure's body, bottom left: the obstacle: the punch); B5 → wanter-again (bottom man's body: B2 repeated)
- **Patterns:**
  - `joy-vs-chore` (39%, relatable frustration): [want]: a generic good thing (weekend, sleep, free time) / [wanter]: Me / [holder]: the routine obligation or institution that always wins. Real shape: Video games on the weekend, held back by a pile of homework with an exact count.
  - `petty-specific-saboteur` (31%, absurd specificity; petty vs proper): [want]: a generic good thing / [wanter]: Me / [holder]: an oddly specific, tiny, mundane obstacle (a form field, a sound, crumbs, a setting). Real shape: A free trial, held back by the box asking for your card number.
  - `authority-blocks-group` (9%, power imbalance): [want]: something a group wants / [wanter]: the group / [holder]: the company, ruler or rule that controls it. Real shape: Game players reaching for a promised update, held back by the studio that makes the game.
  - `inner-saboteur` (7%, self-deprecation): [want]: a simple achievable thing / [wanter]: Me / [holder]: my own anxiety, brain or habit. Real shape: A great meme idea, held back by my brain forgetting it.
- **Slot targets:** want p50 13 / p90 29, short noun or gerund phrase naming the desired thing; wanter p50 2 / p90 21, 'Me' or a 1-3 word group label; holder p50 14 / p90 48, concrete noun phrase naming the obstacle, ideally with a detail or number
- **Winners vs flops:** Specific holders win. The posts whose holder is a specific detail ('43 homework assignments', 'enter your credit card number', 'the crumbs on my bed') have a median of about 110 upvotes; posts whose holder is one generic word ('SCHOOL', 'MOM', 'HOMEWORK') have a median of about 21. Among flops, about 9/34 use a generic pair ('Video games / Chores', 'Candy / Vegetables', 'Playing video games / My responsibilities'); about 10/34 break the structure (want in the man's box, missing repeats, 1-3 boxes); about 6/34 put a sentence where a label goes ('Brain: Darwin's theory says every species came from a single ancestor'). In 3/34 the holder doesn't hold anything back (it explains or comments instead).
- **Tech memes:** The tech uses (7, all at most 5 upvotes) are dev in-jokes: learning to code / Ctrl+C Ctrl+V; heap allocation / memory fragmentation; TS conversion / tsconfig.json. The strongest tech-shaped top uses are consumer ones: 'FREE TRIAL / ENTER YOUR CREDIT CARD NUMBER' (213), 'SWITCH 2 & THE GAMES / THE PRICES' (30), 'ROBLOX CHAT / AGE CHECK' (9). Paywalls, prices and verification gates are the natural tech holders.
- **Misreads:**
  - Filling the man with a full viewer sentence ('Me, refreshing the app for X'): the real wanter is a bare 'Me' in 43/70 (p50 2 characters), and our code prints it twice.
  - A generic one-word holder ('Limits', 'Price'): one-word holders do about five times worse than specific ones. Name the actual gate ('The $200 tier', 'Waitlist', 'Not in your country').
  - A holder that comments on the want instead of physically blocking it (a joke reply or a fact about it).
  - Making the balloon the punch: the balloon is a generic, universally wanted thing; the punch is the holder.
  - Using it when nothing blocks the user: the story must report a real gate (a paywall, region lock, waitlist, usage limit or device requirement).
- **Test:** Does the pink figure name a specific, real gate (a price, tier, region, waitlist, limit or device) that keeps a short 'Me'/group label from a clearly desirable balloon?
- **Slot problems:** wanter accepts only 'viewer', so the writer produces an AS-style 'Me, ...' sentence (25-45 characters). Real wanters are a bare 'Me' (43/70) or a 1-3 word group (12/70), with p50 2 and p90 21 characters, and our code prints the wanter twice. The holder MUST also needs a push to be specific, since specificity is the strongest winner signal. Proposal: The structure and geometry already match Imgflip's five boxes. Only the wanter needs to become a short label, and the holder's MUST needs to demand a concrete gate. The wanter box (y 0.38-0.47) stays clear of the man's face (y 0.17-0.33).
- **Our examples:**
  - `petty-specific-saboteur` · A new AI video model launches only on a $200-a-month plan. → want: The new video model / wanter: Me / holder: The $200-a-month plan
  - `authority-blocks-group` · A voice assistant upgrade rolls out everywhere except the EU. → want: The new voice mode / wanter: EU users / holder: Not available in your region
  - `petty-specific-saboteur` · Phone AI features require the latest model of the phone. → want: The new AI features / wanter: Me / holder: My two-year-old phone
  - `joy-vs-chore` · A chatbot app adds tighter daily message caps on the free tier. → want: One more question / wanter: Free users / holder: The daily message cap
  - `petty-specific-saboteur` · A buzzy AI assistant launches with a waitlist instead of open access. → want: Trying it today / wanter: Me / holder: The waitlist

### Disaster Girl (`disaster-girl`)
Coded 70 top uses, 7 tech memes, 33 flops. News fit: **ok** (Self-owns fit well: a company killing its own popular feature or product, a ban or lawsuit that wrecks something, a hasty fix that removes the whole thing. But the culprit reading implies intent, so it fails for accidents, outages and bugs, and the fire photo rules out anything with victims. The usable news is a narrower slice than the backfire or gate templates.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** The smirk confesses: she did it, and she's fine with it. The joke is a calm, smug culprit next to a wildly disproportionate result, often a 'solution' that wildly overshoots the problem (no room, so no room to clean). The fire is never stated; the caption makes you infer the arson.
- **Picture:** A photo of a house engulfed in flames at a fire-department drill, with firefighters, a hose and a fire truck behind. In the right foreground a little girl looks at the camera with a small, knowing smirk. The top caption sits over the fire (left), the bottom caption over or near the girl (right).
- **Figure:** The girl is the culprit who caused the fire in 54/70: the poster ('me') in about 22/70; a named third party who wrecked something (the writers, Monday, the internet, Disney, AI, YouTube) in about 15/70; kids or a group in about 10/70; the rest unlabelled, with the act implied by the caption. She is a bystander who merely benefits from someone else's mess in only 2/70 (a poster who sold a stock the day before it crashed; AI-slop creators after a platform change).
- **Voice:** Two short lines that split one thought: setup on top, a deadpan payoff below (about 30/52 two-box uses), or two labels (about 15/52). The bottom line is often first person and understated ('it's gone now', 'I used it', 'bad idea'). The fire is stated outright in only about 7/70 winners. 17/70 are one-box captions, mostly 'Me after...' or 'Me when...'. Casing is Imgflip's default caps.
- **Boxes:** B1 → mess (top, over the burning house: the setup or provocation ('mom said no phone', 'there was a spider') or a label for what got wrecked); B2 → pleased (bottom, over or near the girl: her line or label: the drastic response, or who did it ('I said no house', 'it's gone now', 'THE WRITERS'))
- **Patterns:**
  - `provocation-and-payback` (24%, escalation; literalism): [mess]: what someone did to her, or a rule she was given / [culprit]: her outsized, literal-minded response. Real shape: Told not to play with fire, so she plays with the stove instead.
  - `overkill-fix` (21%, disproportion; understatement): [mess]: a small problem / [culprit]: 'solved' (it's gone now), with the fire as the solution. Real shape: There was a bug in the room; the room is gone now.
  - `wreck-and-wrecker` (21%, blame by label): [mess]: label for the thing that got wrecked / [culprit]: label for whoever wrecked it, smug. Real shape: A beloved film franchise's sequel, labelled with the screenwriters as the smirking girl.
  - `me-after-chaos` (10%, confession; mischief): [mess] or one caption: 'Me after [starting the chaos myself]'. Real shape: Me after starting an argument in a comment section and walking away.
- **Slot targets:** mess p50 26 / p90 54, setup clause ('There was a X') or a noun-phrase label for what got wrecked; pleased p50 19 / p90 42, deadpan first-person payoff, or a label/logo for the culprit
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners imply the arson: about 7/70 mention fire or burning, against about 7/33 flops ('i burned my school down', 'the house is on fire / i wonder why', 'me with the oven when I was five'), so stating it kills the joke. About 10/33 flops have no culprit relation at all (a random label over the picture: 'pnut bata', 'Final score: 20-19', 'Rarest thing on youtube'). Winners make the girl the cause in 54/70; flops in about 14/33. One tech-adjacent flop ('go ahead / build that data center in my neighborhood') reads as a threat, not a confession.
- **Tech memes:** The tech uses are weak (7, at most 4 upvotes): label pairs like 'THE MISSING ; / MY CODE' and 'GIT PUSH --FORCE / PROBLEM SOLVED' (an overkill fix). The better tech-shaped top uses are corporate self-owns where the company is the girl: 'YouTube watching their platform die / after removing the skip-ad button' (16), 'Wonders of Life pavilion / Disney' (15), '5000 years of human knowledge / AI' (9). A company smirking at the wreck of a product or feature it killed itself is the strongest news use.
- **Misreads:**
  - Casting the girl as a lucky bystander or rival who just benefits from someone else's stumble: that is 2/70 real uses. In 54/70 she caused the fire, so a rival who didn't cause the mess makes the meme accuse them of sabotage.
  - Putting someone who did not cause the mess on the girl: with this picture that reads as 'they did it on purpose', which is an accusation the facts must support.
  - Stating the fire ('X burns down'): winners imply it; flops say it (about 21% of flops vs 10% of winners).
  - Using an accident or outage with victims as the mess: the culprit reading makes it look deliberate and malicious. The mess must be a deliberate decision by the culprit (a feature removed, a product shut down, a price raised, a lawsuit filed).
  - A mess label with no link to the girl's label, so the reader can't tell who did what to what.
- **Test:** Does the girl stand for whoever deliberately caused the wrecked thing, per the facts, so her smirk reads as 'I did this' rather than a bystander enjoying someone else's trouble, without the caption stating a fire?
- **Slot problems:** The card casts the girl as 'who quietly benefits from that stumble: a rival or the viewer'. In real uses she is the one who caused the fire (54/70); a mere beneficiary appears in 2/70. Asking for a rival who benefits produces memes that falsely imply the rival did it. The slot should be the culprit: whoever made the deliberate decision that wrecked the thing. It should also accept a short text payoff line, since about 30/52 real bottom boxes are the girl's deadpan line, not a label. The mess should accept a setup line (a problem or provocation) as well as a label. Situations limited to AS-10 also miss the natural fits AS-04 (a free feature removed) and AS-13 (an old app shut down). Proposal: The geometry stays (the culprit box ends at y 0.45, clear of her face from y 0.52). The roles change from 'who benefits' to 'who did it', which is what the smirk means in 54/70 real uses, and the culprit slot now accepts the deadpan text line.
- **Our examples:**
  - `wreck-and-wrecker` · A big tech company shuts down a popular free app it launched years ago. → mess: The free app you used daily / culprit: [logo: google]
  - `overkill-fix` · A platform deals with AI spam by switching comments off entirely. → mess: AI spam in the comments / culprit: Comments are off now
  - `wreck-and-wrecker` · A social platform removes a much-loved free feature to push its paid plan. → mess: The feature everyone used / culprit: [logo: meta]
  - `me-after-chaos` · A survey finds most office workers use AI tools their employer never approved. → mess: The company AI policy / culprit: [viewer AS-08] Me, using my own chatbot anyway
  - `provocation-and-payback` · Major news sites block an AI company's web crawler after it scraped their articles. → mess: Scraping every news site / culprit: News sites: blocked
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) mess=Three ex-Apple staff get deposed ; pleased=[logo Apple]: It works because Apple, as the plaintiff, caused the depositions, so the smirk reads as the culprit pleased with the chaos it started (wreck-and-wrecker). It works for the real format's reason, not the card's ('who benefits'). The mess wording names people; 'Your interview files' or 'OpenAI's hiring records' would keep it on the business.

### Waiting Skeleton (`waiting-skeleton`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 34 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries exactly one news turn, the delay: staged rollouts, waitlists, region locks and slipped release dates are common in AI news and map onto the real 'another delay' and 'they said 5 minutes' uses. It cannot carry a reversal, a double standard or a backfire, and it's empty when nothing is late.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** Hyperbole of time: a wait that should be short or was promised ('5 minutes', 'any minute now', 'coming soon') is shown as having lasted so long the waiter died of old age on the bench. The joke lands when the awaited thing is universally recognisable as the kind of wait that never ends, so the gap between the promise and the corpse is felt instantly.
- **Picture:** A photo (not a cartoon) of a plastic anatomical skeleton slumped on a weathered wooden bench in front of grass, skull tilted up, arms resting on the bench. The only open space for text is the grass strip above the skull (top ~10%) and the bench/feet strip at the bottom (~12%); the skull sits at about y 0.11-0.30, centre.
- **Figure:** The poster/me (or 'you'/'POV') in 58/70; a named third party or group (my friend, boomers, TF2 fans) in 4/70; an object (a toilet) in 1/70; no figure at all, the skeleton is just a 'dead' illustration for a pun or unrelated joke, in 7/70.
- **Voice:** ALL CAPS in 58/70. First-person label clause opening with 'ME WAITING FOR' / 'ME' in 29/70, 'WHEN [YOU / MOM]...' in 10/70, bare 'WAITING FOR' / 'STILL WAITING' in 7/70, 'POV' in 5/70. Present-progressive gerund ('waiting for X to finish'), no final punctuation in ~60/70. The promised duration is quoted verbatim ('5 MINS', '"ONE MINUTE CALL"', '"ANY MINUTE NOW"') in 8/70. The awaited thing is concrete and everyday (mom talking: 15/70; teacher, friend's text, a bus, a game release).
- **Boxes:** B1 → awaited (top strip above the skull: In 37/70 the whole meme in one line, 'ME WAITING FOR [X]', with the waiter ('me') and the awaited thing together; in two-box uses the first half of that sentence or the setup ('WHEN MUM SAYS SHE'LL BE 2 MINUTES').); B2 → waiter (bottom strip over the feet and bench: Used in 32/70 (33 posts carry a B2). Either the second half of the split sentence (11/32: 'ME WAITING FOR | CHRISTMAS BREAK') or a time-stamp / still-waiting / another-delay twist (10/32: 'PS: IT'S BEEN AN HOUR', 'THEY ADD ANOTHER DELAY'); the rest are joke punchlines. It names the waiter alone in only 2/70. Our 'waiter' viewer slot has no real counterpart.)
- **Patterns:**
  - `me-waiting-for` (53%, hyperbole of time): [wait]: Me waiting for [someone or something that should be quick] to [finish / arrive / reply]. Real shape: the poster waiting for their mom to stop chatting with a friend in the shop
  - `broken-time-promise` (11%, expectation vs reality): [wait]: When [they] said [the short time they promised, in quotes] / [still]: Still waiting  (or a time stamp that blows past it). Real shape: a friend who said 'be there in 10 minutes', the skeleton is what's left of you
  - `another-delay` (7%, escalation): [wait]: Me waiting for [a famous, long-promised release] / [still]: [they push the date again]. Real shape: waiting for the next big video game, and its studio adds another delay
  - `endured-to-death` (9%, hyperbole): [wait]: Me after [enduring a long, repetitive thing someone else keeps doing]. Real shape: the poster after a know-it-all answers a tiny mistake with a ten-page correction
- **Slot targets:** wait p50 43 / p90 72, first-person clause 'Me waiting for [X]' (the whole meme when one box); still p50 27 / p90 45, short time-stamp or still-waiting/another-delay clause; optional
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners: 50/70 are literally about a wait, and the wait is one everybody has had (mom chatting: 15/70; a promised '5 minutes': 8/70; a famous delayed release: 5/70). Flops (34): 12/34 are not about waiting at all (a fart, an ex, a cockroach), 7/34 wait for something only a niche fandom knows (a game patch number, a channel anniversary, a sports roster move), 7/34 are generic with nothing specific to wait for ('waiting for life to get better', 'that one app to download'), and 4/34 run over 90 characters with the joke buried. Winners name the exact awaited thing and, when they have a number, it's the promised short one.
- **Tech memes:** No Imgflip tech-tagged uses. The closest real cluster is game-release delays (GTA 6, a TF2 update; 5/70), which map directly onto delayed AI features, staged rollouts and waitlists. Data note: the packet's Know Your Meme section is the wrong page (a 2025 TikTok audio, 'not me nan'), so ignore it for this template.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting the waiter in its own bottom box ('Me, refreshing the app') and the awaited thing alone on top: real uses say 'Me waiting for X' as one line (37/70 one box; only 2/70 name the waiter alone in B2), so the split reads as two disconnected captions.
  - Waiting for something that was never promised or is not late: the skeleton only works when the wait outlived a promise; a feature that just launched on time is not a skeleton.
  - A niche awaited thing (a model codename, an API version, a beta flag) the casual follower doesn't recognise: 7/34 flops die this way.
  - Using the skeleton as generic 'dead/bored/tired' with no wait (12/34 flops): for news we need the wait.
  - Inventing the duration ('it's been 400 days'): any number or promised date has to come from the story.
  - Calling a rollout fake or late when the story only says 'gradually' (AS-11's keep-clear rule).
- **Test:** Does one line say who is waiting and name a specific thing that was promised or dated and still hasn't arrived?
- **Slot problems:** Our card splits the meme into 'awaited' (top, text) and 'waiter' (bottom, viewer). Real uses don't: the waiter and the awaited thing are one line, 'Me waiting for X' (29/70 open with ME; 37/70 use one box). The bottom box, when it's used for the joke (10/32 two-box uses), carries the time stamp or the further delay ('still waiting', 'they add another delay'), never the waiter. So the viewer line should be the top line itself and the bottom should be an optional 'still' twist from the facts. Minor: the watch note calls it a 'cartoon skeleton'; it's a photo of a prop skeleton. Proposal: Real uses put the waiter and the awaited thing in one top line and use the bottom only for a time-stamp/another-delay twist; our viewer-at-the-bottom slot has no real counterpart and splits the sentence apart.
- **Our examples:**
  - `another-delay` · Apple pushes its upgraded, more personal Siri to a later release again. → wait: [viewer AS-11] Me waiting for the Siri from the keynote / still: New date: next year
  - `broken-time-promise` · OpenAI ships a feature to Pro users first and says other plans get it 'in the coming weeks'. → wait: [viewer AS-11] Me on the free plan, "in the coming weeks" / still: 
  - `me-waiting-for` · Google launches a new AI feature in the US only, with other countries 'to follow'. → wait: [viewer AS-11] Me outside the US waiting for "to follow" / still: 
  - `another-delay` · A hardware startup delays shipping its AI gadget a second time after a big launch video. → wait: Me waiting for the gadget I preordered / still: Shipping moved again
  - `me-waiting-for` · A popular AI app opens a waitlist for its new agent mode. → wait: [viewer AS-11] Me, still on the agent waitlist / still: 

### X, X Everywhere (`x-everywhere`)
Coded 70 top uses, 8 tech memes, 30 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries one news shape well, convergence or saturation: several companies shipping the same thing, a wave of hires, ads or AI features landing everywhere at once. It can't carry a reversal, a backfire or a double standard, and on a single-company story there's nothing to be 'everywhere' unless the scope is one place.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** Buzz's sweeping, delighted gesture presents a flood as if it were a wonder, while Woody's face shows what it actually feels like: something you're tired of, or worried about, has spread into every corner. The joke is saturation, the sense that you can't get away from X, and it lands when X is a thing the viewer has personally been swamped by.
- **Picture:** Toy Story 2 still: Buzz Lightyear (right) has an arm round Woody and sweeps his other hand at the horizon with a delighted grin; Woody (left) stares the same way with a grimace of dread. Text sits in a band across the top (above Woody's hat) and a band across the bottom (over their legs and the floor); Woody's face is at about x 0.23-0.43, y 0.17-0.46 and Buzz's at about x 0.45-0.57, y 0.28-0.42.
- **Figure:** In the 32/70 literal 'X, X everywhere' uses Buzz is the unlabeled voice announcing X and Woody is the unlabeled poster seeing it (Woody labeled in only 1/32). In 18/70 the picture is a two-person roles format (labels 'me' / 'my friend' / 'my mom' on the figures); Woody is the poster or the one on the receiving end in about 10/18. In 20/70 Buzz just says a line to Woody (a joke, a quote, a bad take).
- **Voice:** Literal X uses (32/70): X is a bare noun or noun phrase, never a sentence: 1 word in 15/32, 2 words in 14/32, 3 words in 3/32; ALL CAPS in 22/32; plural or mass noun in 30/32 ('IDIOTS', 'AI SLOP', 'UNREAD EMAILS'). Second box repeats the same words + 'EVERYWHERE' with no verb in 30/32. No hedging, no explanation, no punctuation beyond an optional comma or '!'. Roles and Buzz-speech uses (38/70) are full clauses, usually 'ME / MY FRIEND [doing X]'.
- **Boxes:** B1 → thing (top band, above the heads: In two-box X uses the bare X ('AI ART', 'INFLATION'); in one-box uses the whole 'X, X EVERYWHERE'; in 5/32 a setup or scope line ('DISNEY NOWADAYS BE LIKE:') with X moved to B2.); B2 → everywhere (bottom band, over the legs and floor: 'X EVERYWHERE', repeating B1's word exactly (our code derives it). In roles uses B2 labels the second figure instead.); B3 → noticer (a label on Woody (roles and 4-box uses only): Labels a figure ('ME, A NEW USER') in roles uses; Woody is labeled in only 1/32 literal X uses, so our optional noticer has a weak counterpart.)
- **Patterns:**
  - `saturation-complaint` (30%, hyperbole (saturation)): [thing]: something the viewer is sick of or overwhelmed by, in 1-3 words / [everywhere]: [thing] everywhere. Real shape: low-effort AI-generated content filling every feed, said with weary disgust
  - `scoped-saturation` (7%, hyperbole with a scope): [thing]: a place, group or period plus the thing flooding it (e.g. '[the company]'s new hires') / [everywhere]: [thing] everywhere. Real shape: a film studio's recent output, and flops as far as the eye can see
  - `fad-everywhere` (9%, observational, fond irony): [thing]: a fad everyone suddenly adopted at once / [everywhere]: [thing] everywhere. Real shape: everyone switching their profile picture to the same retro paperclip mascot
  - `two-person-roles` (26%, role contrast): Labels on each figure ('me', 'my friend') with Buzz explaining or bragging and Woody enduring it. Real shape: the poster who got socks for Christmas while a friend describes his new console
  - `buzz-says` (29%, absurd claim / quote): One line of speech from Buzz to Woody: an absurd claim, a bad take, a quote. Real shape: Buzz reassuring Woody that a mess is fine, trust me
- **Slot targets:** thing p50 9 / p90 18, noun phrase, 1-3 words, plural or mass noun; everywhere p50 20 / p90 30, derived: '[thing] everywhere'; noticer p50 14 / p90 30, short first-person label ('Me, ...'); usually left empty
- **Winners vs flops:** Winning X's are things the general audience is personally swamped by (AI slop, lawyer ads, unread emails, inflation, cringe): 21/32 literal winners are complaints. Of the 10 literal X flops, 5 name something niche (a fandom team, a game mechanic, a snail family) and 5 are too generic to sting ('Ideas', 'Memes', 'Trashy sequels'). 7/30 flops are rants over 100 characters, and 2/30 are plain news statements with no joke (a car reveal date stated flat). Winners never explain why X is bad; the picture does it.
- **Tech memes:** 8/70 top uses are tech: 5 are complaints about AI saturation (AI art, 'Artificial Intelligence', AI slop twice, a product that turned out to be AI), 2 are the retro-mascot fad, 1 is a Buzz-speech line about a platform's AI checks. AI-everywhere is already a native use of this template, so it's a natural fit for 'everyone shipped the same thing this week' stories.
- **Misreads:**
  - Treating it as awe or excitement: the card says 'awed', but 21/32 literal winners are weary complaints; Buzz's delight is the irony and Woody's dread is the true feeling.
  - An abstract or argued X ('Fluent mistakes') instead of a concrete, visible thing people keep running into.
  - An X that the story doesn't show spreading: one study or one company doing something is not 'everywhere'; it needs many instances, or many instances in one place (ours: 'Ex-Apple staff' at OpenAI).
  - A clunky compressed label that needs the headline to decode ('AI firm money') instead of a phrase that reads on its own.
  - Writing X as a sentence or a news statement: the 2 news-statement flops (a car reveal date) got 0 upvotes.
  - A niche X the casual follower can't picture (model codenames, benchmark names): 5/10 literal X flops are niche.
  - Filling the Woody noticer by default: real literal uses label Woody in 1/32, so a viewer caption on his chest is usually clutter.
- **Test:** Is the X a concrete thing in 1-3 words that the story shows popping up in many places (or all over one place), framed as too much of it rather than as good news?
- **Slot problems:** The slots match the format (bare X on top, derived 'X everywhere' at the bottom), but the card's text misreads it: 'Attitude: awed' is wrong, because real literal uses are weary or alarmed complaints in 21/32 and fond-ironic in 6/32, and none are genuinely awed. The 'thing' MUST should say the X is something the viewer is swamped by, and that 'everywhere' may be scoped to one place the facts show it flooding. The optional noticer is fine but should default to empty (Woody labeled in 1/32 real literal uses). No geometry change.
- **Our examples:**
  - `saturation-complaint` · Within one month, OpenAI, Google and Anthropic each launch an 'agent' mode. → thing: Agents / everywhere: Agents everywhere / noticer: 
  - `saturation-complaint` · Several AI chat apps begin showing ads in their free tiers. → thing: Chatbot ads / everywhere: Chatbot ads everywhere / noticer: [viewer AS-04] Me, on the free plan
  - `scoped-saturation` · Meta's new AI lab is staffed largely with researchers hired away from OpenAI. → thing: Ex-OpenAI researchers / everywhere: Ex-OpenAI researchers everywhere / noticer: 
  - `saturation-complaint` · Phone makers add a dedicated AI button to their new flagship phones. → thing: AI buttons / everywhere: AI buttons everywhere / noticer: 
  - `fad-everywhere` · A new image model goes viral and everyone posts themselves as the same cartoon style. → thing: Cartoon selfies / everywhere: Cartoon selfies everywhere / noticer: 
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) thing=AI leader videos: Concrete and visible: AI leaders really are on every podcast and stage, which is why someone built a tracker. Reads as a weary saturation line.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) thing=AI firm money: The story's turn is circularity (AI firms funding AI firms), which this template carries only weakly; and 'AI firm money' is an awkward label that needs the headline to make sense. A scoped version ('AI money, inside AI firms') or another template would land better.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) thing=Fluent mistakes: Abstract, not something you can see piling up, and the story is a question about translation's future, not a spreading thing. Nothing in the facts makes it 'everywhere'.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) thing=Ex-Apple staff: A scoped saturation: the facts show many ex-Apple hires at OpenAI, so 'everywhere' means all over one company. Concrete, short (14 chars), and the dread reads as Apple's.

### Trade Offer (`trade-offer`)
Coded 70 top uses, 9 tech memes, 28 flops. News fit: **strong** (Lopsided terms are a constant in AI and tech news: price rises for the same product, ads in free tiers, data for features, acqui-hires, licensing deals. The format carries a one-sided deal or double standard natively, and the company-as-offerer is the most common real reading (33/70).). Our memes misused it: 2 of 2.
- **Mechanism:** A deal framed as fair and businesslike that is obviously rigged: the offerer takes something big and precious (your money, years of your life, your attention) and hands back something tiny, junky or insulting (a sticker, dirt, the same phone again). The calm salesman's pose sells it as reasonable, and the joke is the gap in scale between the two boxes, with the punch in 'you receive'.
- **Picture:** A TikTok still of a long-haired man in a suit and tie, fingers steepled, with a knowing half-smile under purple light. Burned in: a red '⚠TRADE OFFER⚠' banner at the top, 'i receive:' at upper left and 'you receive:' at upper right; the text goes under those two labels (y about 0.21-0.35), and the offerer's name usually sits low on the man's suit. His face is at about x 0.37-0.65, y 0.48-0.69.
- **Figure:** The man is the party making the offer, named in B3 in 56/70. He stands for a company, brand or institution in 33/70 (schools/colleges/teachers 15, brands like EA, Apple, Rockstar, Lego, Disney 11, governments or historical powers 4, other organisations 3); an animal or game character (dogs, villagers, piglins, mosquitoes) in 10/70; the poster/me in 12/70; another person or thing (mom, a scammer, early meetings) in 15/70.
- **Voice:** Noun phrases, never sentences, in 61/70 (B1 p50 14 chars, B2 p50 16). Lowercase or sentence case in 46/70. Second-person possessive in B1 ('your', 'ur') in 19/70; money or a number in one of the boxes in 22/70; 'nothing' as a whole box in 6/70. B3 is a bare plural or brand name ('schools', 'EA games') in 50/56; it's never a full sentence in the winners. No verbs explaining the deal; the burned-in labels do the grammar.
- **Boxes:** B1 → i-receive (under 'i receive:' (upper left): What the offerer takes: big and valuable, often second-person ('your savings', '12 years of your life') in 19/70, or money in 22/70.); B2 → you-receive (under 'you receive:' (upper right): What you get back: the punch. In the 40 rip-off uses it's a small concrete object (18/40), an abstract bad outcome (12/40), 'nothing' (2/40) or a pitiful fraction of what was taken.); B3 → offerer (on the man's suit (lower area): The offerer's name as a plain label ('schools', 'EA', 'apple'); present in 56/70, short (p50 8 chars). Our logo-plus-name fits it.)
- **Patterns:**
  - `rigged-deal` (57%, scale contrast (exploitation framed as fairness)): [i-receive]: something big the offerer takes from you (your money / time / data) / [you-receive]: something tiny, junky or insulting in return / [offerer]: the company or institution. Real shape: a phone maker taking a lot of money and handing over a phone identical to the last few
  - `generous-deal` (7%, inversion (an honest deal in a scam's clothes)): [i-receive]: a small price / [you-receive]: something large and lasting / [offerer]: a company that's a genuinely good deal. Real shape: a game maker taking twenty dollars and giving years of fun
  - `wholesome-swap` (11%, petty vs proper (wholesome)): [i-receive]: something small or annoying / [you-receive]: affection or beauty / [offerer]: a pet, nature, a friend. Real shape: a dog that takes your leftover fries and gives unconditional love
  - `beg-for-engagement` (10%, self-aware begging): [i-receive]: an upvote or follow / [you-receive]: a tiny favour back / [offerer]: me. Real shape: an upvote in exchange for the satisfaction of helping a small creator
- **Slot targets:** i-receive p50 14 / p90 45, noun phrase, often 'your [big valuable thing]' or an amount; you-receive p50 16 / p90 51, noun phrase: a small concrete object or pitiful amount; offerer p50 8 / p90 29, name label (our logo + name)
- **Winners vs flops:** In the 40 winning rip-offs the gap in scale is instantly readable (big vs small) and B2 is a concrete little thing (18/40) or 'nothing' (2/40). Flops (28): 10/28 rest on references a casual viewer doesn't know (an anime power-up, a WWII peace offer, a colonial-era war, a game character's sprite); 5/28 trade two things of equal size, so there's no lopsidedness (a massage for a cookie, 70% for 30% robux); 5/28 cram bulleted lists or explanation sentences into a box; 2/28 are heavy political grievances (data centers taking land and water, the government taking your liberty), where B2 explains the harm instead of naming a petty object; 1/28 retypes 'I RECEIVE:' inside the box.
- **Tech memes:** 9 tech-adjacent uses in the top 70 (EA twice, Apple, Rockstar, Microsoft Rewards, RobTop, Roblox, a scammer taking your password, plus the 1 tagged tech post, a 1-upvote malvertising joke). The tech winners are all consumer rip-offs: price vs what you actually get (a pay-to-win skin, a near-identical phone, a decade of waiting for a pre-order). For AI news that maps onto pricing tiers, ads, data-for-features and acqui-hires; the one AI-data-center flop shows the trap of turning it into a political grievance.
- **Misreads:**
  - A 'you receive' that sounds big to a casual viewer ('A $20 million bid'): the punch box must read as small or silly next to the left box, or there's no joke.
  - Spinning what the company takes beyond the facts ('Your chat read on your phone' for an on-device scam warning): the take must be the story's real terms, not our framing of them.
  - Writing the boxes as clauses or explanations instead of noun phrases ('Being forced out of your home and job'): 5/28 flops explain.
  - Trading two equal things: with no gap in scale (5/28 flops) it's just a description of a deal.
  - Making the right box an abstract harm list when a concrete object is available: concrete objects carry 18/40 winning punches.
  - Retyping 'I receive' / 'You receive' inside the box: the labels are burned in.
  - Using it for a grievance about real harm (land, jobs, liberty): those flopped and turn the meme into a protest sign.
- **Test:** Does the left box name something big the offerer really gets and the right box something clearly smaller or sillier the other side gets, both as short noun phrases taken from the story?
- **Slot problems:** The slots match the real format (B1 left under 'i receive', B2 right under 'you receive', B3 the offerer's name on the man) and the punch is correctly 'you-receive'. Only the MUST text is loose: 'you-receive' should require that it read as clearly smaller, cheaper or sillier than 'i-receive' (a concrete object or pitiful amount from the facts), and both should be noun phrases, not clauses. No slot or geometry change.
- **Our examples:**
  - `rigged-deal` · An AI company raises the price of its paid plan while keeping the same message limits. → i-receive: $10 more a month / you-receive: The same message cap / offerer: [logo: openai]
  - `rigged-deal` · A chatbot starts showing ads to users on its free tier. → i-receive: Your attention between answers / you-receive: Staying free / offerer: [logo: openai]
  - `rigged-deal` · A big tech company licenses a startup's model and hires its founders, leaving the startup behind. → i-receive: The founders and the model / you-receive: The company name / offerer: [logo: google]
  - `rigged-deal` · A chatbot turns on a memory feature that keeps past conversations by default. → i-receive: Your chat history / you-receive: It remembers your dog's name / offerer: [logo: google]
  - `generous-deal` · Google offers university students a free year of its paid AI plan. → i-receive: Your student email / you-receive: A year of AI Pro / offerer: [logo: google]
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) i-receive=Your chat read on your phone ; you-receive=A pop-up telling you to pause ; offerer=[logo Google DeepMind]: The shape is right (something personal taken, a small pop-up given back), but 'Your chat read on your phone' is a clumsy clause, not a noun phrase, and framing an on-device scam detector as taking your chats spins the facts. The pop-up is a good concrete punch.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) i-receive=Founders and dozens of staff ; you-receive=A $20 million bid for the rest ; offerer=[logo Google]: Right instinct (an acqui-hire is a lopsided trade), but the punch doesn't land: '$20 million' sounds big to a casual viewer, and who 'you' is (the startup? its creditors?) is unclear. The left box needs to sound precious and the right box pitiful ('whatever's left').

### Woman Yelling At Cat (`woman-yelling-at-cat`)
Coded 70 top uses, 3 tech memes, 27 flops. News fit: **strong** (News is full of backlash followed by a flat corporate response, and of hypocrisy (the accuser doing the same thing), which are exactly the unbothered-reply and comeback-flip engines. The one trap is sympathy: the cat usually wins, so the company's line has to be visibly, absurdly flat for the joke to land on it.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 7.
- **Mechanism:** Hysterical outrage meets total calm: the woman's accusation is overblown, hypocritical or misreads things, and the cat's flat reply (an understatement, a literal reading, a flip back on her, or plain indifference) deflates it. The gap in emotional temperature is the joke, and the viewer usually sides with the cat, so a company placed on the cat must come off as callously, absurdly flat for the joke to land on it.
- **Picture:** Two panels under a white caption strip. Left: a blonde woman screams, crying and pointing, while a dark-haired friend holds her back. Right: a white cat sits at a dinner table behind a plate of salad, mouth half-open in a sneering, unimpressed look. Text goes in the white strip above each panel (y about 0.01-0.19); the women's faces are at about x 0.12-0.47 and the cat's at about x 0.69-0.86, y 0.45-0.70.
- **Figure:** Woman (left): an overreacting accuser, usually an authority or a crowd (parents/mom 12/70, 'people'/Karens/fans 13/70, the poster in 4/70) or the speaker of an angry quoted line (29/70). Cat (right): the poster/me in 23/70, a calm third party (an employee, Hermione, a laptop) in 6/70, the speaker of the comeback in 29/70. The cat is the sympathetic or winning side in about 51/70.
- **Voice:** Two registers. Quoted-speech uses (29/70): the woman's line is direct speech in second person, ending in '!' in 13/29, sometimes mock-casing ('vIdEo gAmES cAuSE vIOLencE', 2/70); the cat's line is shorter, calm, no '!'. Label uses (25/70): noun phrase for the accuser ('Karens', 'My mom yelling at me') and 'Me who…' / 'Me, just…' relative clause for the cat in 8/25. Speaker prefixes ('EX:', 'Karen:', 'Me:') appear in only 3/70. B1 p50 41 chars, B2 p50 29: the reply is shorter than the complaint in 38/49 two-box uses.
- **Boxes:** B1 → complaint (white strip above the woman (left): The accusation, as a quoted angry line (often with '!') or a label for the accuser ('Karens', 'My parents telling me video games cause violence'). In 21/70 one box spans the whole top and holds both lines ('You said X! I said Y.').); B2 → reply (white strip above the cat (right): The comeback line, or a 'me who…' label for the calm or innocent target; the punch in 49/49 two-box uses.)
- **Patterns:**
  - `comeback-flip` (26%, hypocrisy / inversion): [complaint]: a heated accusation, with '!' / [reply]: a short answer that turns it back on the accuser (hypocrisy, 'that's the point', a fact that undercuts her). Real shape: someone scolding a generation for living on social media, answered by: your generation invented it
  - `literal-wordplay` (16%, wordplay / literalism): [complaint]: an accusation or command / [reply]: the same words read literally or regrouped. Real shape: 'you said no more candy' answered by repunctuating it as 'no, more candy'
  - `unbothered-reply` (13%, understatement): [complaint]: an outraged accuser / [reply]: the accused's flat indifference or understatement, carrying on as if nothing happened. Real shape: the family in a screaming match while the poster only wants to eat dinner
  - `overblown-vs-innocent` (23%, false equivalence / petty vs proper): [complaint]: [authority] accusing [the target] of something terrible / [reply]: me, doing the most harmless version of the thing. Real shape: parents warning games cause violence while the kid builds a house for friends in a block game
  - `sign-vs-typo` (6%, literalism (object labeling)): [complaint]: the correct name / [reply]: the misspelled or broken version. Real shape: a store sign whose missing letter turns 'coat' into 'cat'
- **Slot targets:** complaint p50 41 / p90 85, quoted angry line in second person with '!', or a label for the accuser; reply p50 29 / p90 55, short calm quoted line (flip, understatement, literal reading) or 'Me, [doing the harmless thing]'
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the reply defeat or deflate the accusation: 29/70 are comebacks that win the exchange and 25/70 make the cat innocent or unbothered. Flops (27): 6/27 are two labels with no clash between them (a cheating girl and a snitch, two sports fan bases, a correct word and a typo that makes no joke); 5/27 have a reply that doesn't beat the accusation (a weak pun, 'well why do you care'); 5/27 rest on niche references (a game squad member, a mobile football mode, a Linux distro rivalry, a foreign TV channel); 3/27 are over 120 characters; 2/27 are political jabs. Only 1 of 3 tech-tagged uses got more than 0 upvotes.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say from Imgflip (3 developer jokes, 0-3 upvotes). The closest strong real cluster is 'video games cause violence' (5/70: an authority's moral panic vs a kid doing something harmless), which maps onto moral-panic AI headlines. For company news, the version that works is outrage vs a flat corporate line (our 3 Yes-rated memes all do this).
- **Misreads:**
  - The reply is an honest excuse or explanation ('Spam filter caught it. Found out later.'): it must deflate the anger by flatness, a flip or a literal reading, not explain.
  - Forgetting the viewer sides with the cat (about 51/70): a company on the cat with a reasonable answer makes the meme defend the company. Its line must read as absurdly flat or callous ('standard male voice').
  - Speaker prefixes with hedges ('Tsuda says:', 'TikTok told the court:'): 'says' is banned, the prefix eats the box, and only 3/70 winners use a prefix.
  - A complaint with no heat ('Some AI leaders shouldn't talk in public'): the woman is screaming; the line needs outrage aimed at someone.
  - Muddled roles: the complaint and the reply must come from two opposing sides of the same dispute, the reply answering the complaint directly.
  - A vague comeback ('Civilians have the same concern') instead of a sharp flip that names the hypocrisy.
  - Two labels with no clash (6/27 flops): if the boxes don't argue, there's no joke.
  - Inventing a quoted accusation for a real critic or official: the complaint is the viewer's or a paraphrase of the facts, never a made-up quote.
- **Test:** Is the left box a heated accusation and the right box a reply to it that deflates it (flat understatement, literal reading, flip back on the accuser, or harmless innocence), not an excuse or an unrelated statement?
- **Slot problems:** The two slots match the real boxes (complaint left, reply right, punch in reply). The card text misses two things: (1) the reply must deflate the anger by being flat, literal or a flip, never an explanation or excuse; (2) viewers side with the cat in about 51/70, so when the company is the cat its line must read as absurdly flat or callous, otherwise the meme defends it. It should also discourage speaker prefixes (3/70 winners use them) since the logo names the cat. No slot or geometry change.
- **Our examples:**
  - `unbothered-reply` · OpenAI shipped a voice that many said sounded like a famous actress; the company said it had hired a different voice actress. → complaint: That voice is obviously her! / reply: [logo: openai] Hired a different actress
  - `unbothered-reply` · A chatbot's confidently wrong answers go viral, and the company points to its 'can make mistakes' disclaimer. → complaint: [viewer AS-14] It was so sure! It was wrong! / reply: [logo: google] It says it can make mistakes
  - `comeback-flip` · An AI lab that trained on public web data accuses a rival of training on its model's answers. → complaint: You trained on our model's answers! / reply: Learned it from watching you
  - `unbothered-reply` · An AI app cuts its free message limit, and the company says only a small share of users are affected. → complaint: [viewer AS-04] You halved my free messages! / reply: A small share of users affected
  - `overblown-vs-innocent` · A school district bans an AI study app while students say they mostly use it for flashcards. → complaint: AI is ruining homework! / reply: Me, making flashcards with it
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) complaint=Two months to report the fake tip is unacceptable ; reply=Minimal impact. Live internet is off for internal tests: Heated demand vs a flat corporate understatement ('Minimal impact'), the unbothered-reply pattern with the company on the cat; the flatness keeps the joke on the company.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) complaint=Two-month delay is unacceptable ; reply=Reported as soon as our technical review was complete: Same unbothered corporate line; the bureaucratic phrasing does the deflating. Slightly long for the cat (53 chars, near the p90 of 55).
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) complaint=Tsuda says: that AI voice imitates me ; reply=TikTok told the court: "standard male voice": The engine is perfect: a personal accusation answered by an absurdly flat legal description. The execution is weaker: 'says' is a banned hedge and both speaker prefixes waste space.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) complaint=Some AI leaders shouldn't talk in public ; reply=We weren't super good with communities: Roles are muddled: the complaint has no heat and isn't aimed at the cat, and the reply doesn't answer it. It reads as two unrelated statements.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) complaint=At least 188 videos in my voice ; reply=TikTok: "standard male voice": Tighter version of the Yes-rated voice meme: a concrete number from the facts as the outrage, a deadpan legal phrase as the cat. The 'TikTok:' prefix could go (the logo can carry it).
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) complaint=What are you doing with that camera? ; reply=Civilians have the same concern: The right pattern (comeback-flip: the watchers hate being watched) but the reply is vague commentary, not a sharp flip, and the complaint is a made-up line put in the police's mouth.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) complaint=Police: two months is unacceptable ; reply=Spam filter caught it. Found out later.: The reply is an honest excuse, so it explains instead of deflating; the 'Police:' prefix adds clutter. The two Yes-rated memes on the same story show the flat corporate line working.

### Marked Safe From (`marked-safe-from`)
Coded 70 top uses, 9 tech memes, 34 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries two news turns: an exemption (a region, plan or company spared a price rise, an ad rollout or a deprecation) and an inverted dig (a company 'safe from' shipping on time). Most real uses are about evergreen annoyances, not events, and it can't carry a reversal or escalation; and it must stay away from any story with real victims.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** Mock-solemnity: it borrows the gravity of a disaster check-in for something trivial, so a hated trend, an annoying group or an overhyped news cycle gets treated as a catastrophe you survived. The more ubiquitous and petty the X, the funnier the solemn flag; flipping it ('safe from good moderation') turns it into a dig at whoever lacks a good thing.
- **Picture:** A mock Facebook Safety Check on white: a blue flag icon at the top centre (x about 0.42-0.62, y 0.08-0.38), the words 'Marked Safe From' below it (y about 0.42-0.50), a blank line for the thing (y about 0.50-0.70), and 'Today' near the bottom. No people or faces; white space on either side of the flag.
- **Figure:** No figure. The one marked safe is the implied poster or viewer in 62/70; the viewer addressed directly ('everyone who reads this', 'you have been') in 5/70; a named place or company in 2/70 (a US state exempt from clock changes, a game platform 'safe from good moderation'); a period of time in 1/70.
- **Voice:** Bare noun phrase or gerund phrase, never a full sentence: noun phrase in 46/70 ('AI slop', 'Skibidi toilet kids'), gerund phrase in 15/70 ('caring about the Grammys', 'accidentally liking a 2014 photo'), other 9/70. Title or sentence case in 50/70, no final punctuation in 64/70. Very short: B1 p50 14 chars, 1-3 words in 38/70. 'Caring about…' opens 4/70. An emoji by the flag stands in for an icon in 6/70. No winner retypes 'Marked safe from'.
- **Boxes:** B1 → dodged (the blank line under 'Marked Safe From': In 45/70 one-box uses, the X itself (p50 14 chars). In 2-box uses B1 is something else: who is safe (10/23: a place, a group, 'everyone who reads this'), an emoji placed by the flag (6/23), or an aside (7/23).); B2 → dodged (the blank line under 'Marked Safe From' (2-box uses): The X when B1 names who is safe or holds an emoji; p50 14 chars.); B1 → who (proposed) (beside the flag, upper left: Who is marked safe, in 10/23 two-box uses ('Hawaii', 'anyone with a brain', a company). We have no slot for it.)
- **Patterns:**
  - `spared-from-the-annoying` (43%, mock-solemnity (hyperbole)): [dodged]: a trend, group or kind of content everyone is sick of, as a bare noun phrase. Real shape: the reader declared safe from the low-effort AI content clogging every feed
  - `petty-mishap` (16%, absurd specificity): [dodged]: one small, specific everyday misfortune (often a gerund phrase). Real shape: safe from finding a hair in your soup, with a soup-bowl emoji by the flag
  - `opted-out-of-the-hype` (13%, understatement / apathy): [dodged]: 'Caring about [this week's overhyped event]' or the event itself. Real shape: safe from caring about a pop star's engagement news
  - `inverted-safety` (9%, inversion (dig)): [who]: someone or some company / [dodged]: a good thing they conspicuously lack. Real shape: a game platform marked safe from good moderation
  - `who-was-spared` (4%, factual exemption): [who]: the place, group or plan the facts exempt / [dodged]: the bad thing hitting everyone else. Real shape: a state that skips clock changes, safe from pointless time changes
- **Slot targets:** dodged p50 14 / p90 49, noun phrase or gerund phrase, 1-5 words; who p50 12 / p90 25, a short name or group label (or a logo); usually empty
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners name something the whole audience recognizes and is tired of (30/70 trends, groups or content) or a specific everyday mishap (11/70), in 1-5 words. Flops (34): 8/34 name something niche (a sports player, a fan-made character, a regional cheese brand); 8/34 name a trend that had already saturated (a number meme, 'iPad kids', 'AI slop forever' at 0 upvotes vs 'AI slop' at 36); 6/34 are incoherent (the X isn't bad, or the two boxes don't connect); 3/34 drift into real harm (stepping on needles, violence); 3/34 retype 'Marked safe from' or run as a sentence over 70 characters.
- **Tech memes:** No Imgflip tech tags, but 9/70 top uses are tech: AI slop, AI deepfakes and AI slop ads (3), YouTube ads and recommendations (2), suspicious email links, the 'are you still watching' loop, Reddit, a platform's moderation. AI saturation and annoying product behaviour are native subjects. For news, the strongest real hook is the factual exemption ('who was spared') and the inverted dig at a company.
- **Misreads:**
  - Writing it as relief at a real escape: the card says 'relieved', but the real tone is mock-solemn; the X is trivial or annoying, never a genuine danger.
  - Cramming who and what into one line ('EU users from the ad rollout'): real uses that name who is safe put it in its own box (10/23 two-box uses).
  - A niche X the casual follower can't picture (a model codename, an API version): 8/34 flops are niche.
  - Retyping 'Marked safe from' or writing a sentence: the label is burned in, and the X is a bare phrase (p50 14 chars).
  - Anything near real harm (job losses, injuries, data breaches with victims): the watch rule, and 3/34 flops that drifted there.
  - Claiming the viewer dodged something the facts don't exempt them from: the exemption must be real (a region, a plan, a platform the story names).
- **Test:** Is the dodged thing a short phrase naming a non-tragic annoyance (or, inverted, a good thing someone lacks) that the facts show missing the named party or the viewer, said with mock solemnity rather than real relief?
- **Slot problems:** Our MUST asks the facts to 'show who it hit and who it missed', but the card gives one slot, so the 'who' has nowhere to go and the writer is pushed to cram it into the X line. Real 2-box uses name who is safe in a separate box in 10/23 cases. The card's 'Attitude: relieved' is also off: real uses are mock-solemn about trivia (30/70 hated trends or groups, 11/70 petty mishaps). The packet's Know Your Meme section is the wrong page (a TikTok audio), so ignore it. Proposal: The format's news engine is an exemption (who was spared) or an inverted dig (who lacks a good thing); both need a 'who' that real uses put in its own box (10/23 two-box uses) and our single-slot card can't hold.
- **Our examples:**
  - `who-was-spared` · An AI chatbot starts showing ads to free users, in the US only for now. → who: [viewer AS-04] Everyone outside the US / dodged: Chatbot ads
  - `who-was-spared` · Big tech rivals announce record AI data-center budgets while Apple keeps its spending far lower. → who: [logo: apple] / dodged: The data center bill
  - `inverted-safety` · Apple's long-promised Siri upgrade misses another release window. → who: [logo: apple] / dodged: Shipping on time
  - `opted-out-of-the-hype` · Three labs each claim the top benchmark score in the same week. → who:  / dodged: Caring about benchmark charts
  - `spared-from-the-annoying` · A browser adds one setting that hides all of its AI features. → who:  / dodged: Surprise AI sidebars

### Ancient Aliens (`ancient-aliens`)
Coded 50 top uses, 1 tech memes, 20 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries one news turn well: 'the usual suspect again', where a story shows the same cause (AI, data centers, a vested interest) behind yet another effect. It cannot carry a reversal or backfire, and most real uses (44/50) are crackpot leaps or shower thoughts that need no news, so for news it is a one-trick format.). Our memes misused it: 8 of 11.
- **Mechanism:** Certainty wildly out of proportion to the evidence: a flimsy leap (one catch-all cause, a bogus chain of logic, an obvious fact framed as a discovery) is delivered with the face of a man who has cracked the case. The audience laughs at the gap between how sure he looks and how little the reasoning holds, and, in the classic form, at hearing the same pet cause yet again.
- **Picture:** Giorgio Tsoukalos in a suit, mid-sentence, eyes half shut and hands spread in a knowing 'I have figured it out' gesture, warm studio background, History HD logo burned in at bottom right. Text goes in the strip of background above his hair (top) and across his jacket at the bottom, left of the logo.
- **Figure:** The figure is the person making the confident leap. In 22/50 he voices a crackpot claim or a shower-thought 'revelation' (unattributed speaker); in 8/50 he is the believer whose answer to everything is 'aliens'; in 6/50 he is 'me' explaining something absurd to someone ('me explaining to the cops/judge…'); 3/50 are space puns; 11/50 use him as a blank backdrop for an unrelated joke.
- **Voice:** All caps in 43/50. Statements dominate: 35/50 are declarative claims; 7/50 are questions ('what if…', 'why do we never…'); 6/50 are 'me explaining…'/'when…' scene frames. 22/50 use one box only. When the pet-cause pattern is used, the bottom box is 1-2 words in 6/8 ('ALIENS', 'ME: ALIENS', 'PLOT TWIST: ALIENS'), with no hedging: never 'maybe' or 'probably'. 'I'm not saying it's aliens… but it's aliens' appears as the catchphrase variant in 2/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → setup (top strip above the head: the observation, question or 'evidence' (in one-box uses, the whole claim)); B2 → explanation (bottom strip over the jacket, left of the logo: the leap: the catch-all cause ('ALIENS'), the bogus conclusion, or the punchline of the reasoning); B3 → null (extra boxes (4/70 posts): labels split across the image for a multi-beat joke; no slot of ours)
- **Patterns:**
  - `crackpot-leap` (22%, bogus logic / absurd specificity): [setup]: a real fact or pseudo-fact presented as evidence / [explanation]: the absurd conclusion drawn from it, stated as proven. Real shape: Spelling an astronaut's name backwards turns it into 'alien', so that settles it.
  - `shower-thought-revelation` (22%, obvious stated as profound / wordplay): [setup]: an obvious or wordplay observation framed as a deep discovery (often one box). Real shape: Noticing that the day always ends in night, said as if it were a breakthrough.
  - `pet-cause` (16%, one answer for everything / absurd overreach): [setup]: why/how did [real event] happen? / [explanation]: the one catch-all cause (1-2 words) that gets blamed for everything. Real shape: Asked where the homework went, the answer is simply 'aliens'; a cat staring at a wall is downloading orders from the mothership.
  - `me-explaining` (12%, confident justification of the indefensible): [setup]: me explaining [something indefensible] to [an authority]. Real shape: Me explaining to the judge why a food pun proves I'm innocent.
  - `space-pun` (6%, wordplay): [setup]: a setup about places, people or planets / [explanation]: a pun that lands on aliens or a planet. Real shape: A whole continent to themselves, so they must be 'Austr-aliens'.
- **Slot targets:** setup p50 40 / p90 70, a why/how question or a stated fact used as evidence; plain, factual, no hedges; explanation p50 8 / p90 20, bare noun phrase, 1-3 words, the catch-all cause; no verb, no 'because'
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners commit to one leap that a reader gets in a second: 22/50 crackpot leaps, 11/50 shower thoughts, 8/50 'aliens'. Flops (20) are mostly not a leap at all: 9/20 are plain statements or opinions with no false certainty ('a woman should always…', 'I like rabbits'), 4/20 are news recaps with a long literal bottom ('so let's merge into a $111b megastudio', 'marvel + star wars + kushner: nba laundering plot'), 3/20 are one-word labels with no setup ('persian science', 'Michael Bay', 'HUMANS!'), and 2/20 are long rambling paragraphs (one over 200 chars). The news flops all spend the bottom box on a specific factual detail instead of a catch-all cause, which is exactly what our weaker memes did.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: one tech post (0 upvotes), a three-way label of who invented a language (company / aliens / me). It uses 'aliens' as the joke answer, so the pet-cause form transfers to tech, but there is no tech evidence of what works.
- **Misreads:**
  - Answering the setup literally: 'Why is OpenAI adding 200 jobs in Dublin?' / 'European headquarters' is a quiz answer, not a pet cause. The bottom must be the cause people reach for every time, not the specific fact.
  - Using a 'how' question whose answer is a mechanism ('How did OpenAI trace the device?' / 'Its own models'). Mechanisms are trivia; the format needs a 'why' with a catch-all culprit.
  - Answering with a niche name, version or site ('xtool v1.22.2', 'blumify.io', 'Spray and pray'). The catch-all only lands if everyone already blames it.
  - Economist or analyst jargon as the cause ('Overheating demand'). The cause must be a blunt everyday culprit ('AI', 'data centers', 'money'), said like 'aliens'.
  - Hedged or narrated setups ('How did OpenAI say it found…'): the format has no room for 'said'; the setup states the event as fact.
  - Treating the image as a generic 'confident guy' and writing a plain opinion with no leap (the most common flop, 9/20).
  - Writing a news recap in the bottom box: the bottom is 1-3 words, never a clause.
- **Test:** Is the bottom line a short catch-all cause (the kind of answer someone gives to everything, like 'AI' or 'money') rather than the specific factual answer to the top question?
- **Slot problems:** The two slots match the pet-cause form, but the explanation's MUST still lets literal answers through: 3 of Lucas's 5 'No' memes and 1 of his 'Yes' memes put the specific factual answer in the bottom box. The setup's MUST allows 'who' and 'how' questions, which invite literal answers. Boxes are fine and clear the face. Proposal: Boxes unchanged; MUST text only. The bottom has to be a reusable catch-all cause, not the answer to the question, and the top has to be a 'why' so the cause can overreach.
- **Our examples:**
  - `pet-cause` · Laptop and phone makers raise prices as memory chips get pricier on AI data center demand. → setup: Why does every gadget cost more this year? / explanation: AI
  - `pet-cause` · Households near new data center clusters see electricity bills climb. → setup: Why did my power bill jump again? / explanation: Data centers
  - `pet-cause` · A cloud provider's CEO says AI will create far more jobs than it removes. → setup: Why is a cloud boss so sure AI makes jobs? / explanation: Cloud bills
  - `pet-cause` · Universities report a surge of near-identical admission essays. → setup: Why do 900 essays sound exactly alike? / explanation: AI
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) setup=Who gave government sites false information? ; explanation=AI agents: It works only because 'AI' is the catch-all culprit; as written it is the literal answer to a 'who' question, so the overreach is missing. 'Why are government sites suddenly wrong?' / 'AI' would be the real form.
  - WRONG (Lucas: Yes) setup=Why is OpenAI adding 200 jobs in Dublin? ; explanation=European headquarters: Literal trivia answer, exactly the quiz the card bans; no pet cause, no overreach.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) setup=How did OpenAI trace a device to an employee? ; explanation=Its own models: A 'how' question answered with a fact from the story; reads as a quiz.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) setup=Why does Apple see a Pixel 7 at login? ; explanation=xtool v1.22.2: Niche tool and version number; nobody blames it for everything, so there is no leap.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) setup=Who gets a page on blumify.io next? ; explanation=Voters: Unknown site, not a 'why', and the answer is not a cause at all.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) setup=How did OpenAI say it found who planted the device? ; explanation=Its own models: Hedge ('say'), a 'how' question, and a literal answer.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) setup=Why does a chip CEO want less AI doom talk? ; explanation=Vested interest: Real why-question, and the bottom is the cynic's catch-all cause stated with total certainty: the pet-cause pattern.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) setup=Prices are rising beyond data center parts. Why? ; explanation=Overheating demand: The bottom is the Fed's own technical explanation, not a blunt catch-all; 'Data centers' or 'AI' would carry the 'aliens' certainty.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) setup=Why is hiring misconduct at a record 92%? ; explanation=AI: Specific factual why plus a one-word catch-all cause: the canonical form.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) setup=Why did EDF close early at 12,000 applications? ; explanation=Spray and pray: Recruiting jargon as the answer, and a niche setup; no catch-all cause.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) setup=Why is a chip CEO sure of home AI? ; explanation=Chip sales: Vested-interest pet cause, blunt and short.

### Bike Fall (`bike-fall`)
Coded 50 top uses, 4 tech memes, 32 flops. News fit: **strong** (The backfire is one of the most common tech news turns: a company's own decision (a removal, a lock-in, a price move, a rushed launch) comes back on it as a reversal, a lawsuit or a user revolt. The format shows cause and effect in three beats without needing any quote.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** The disaster is self-inflicted and visible: panel 2 shows the rider doing the very thing that wrecks panel 3, so the joke is the straight line from 'my own choice' to 'my own crash', sharpened when the rider then acts baffled or blames someone else.
- **Picture:** A three-panel comic, stacked: (1) a man rides a bike calmly holding a stick; (2) he leans down and jams the stick into his own front wheel; (3) he lies on the grass clutching himself, bike flipped. Text sits in each panel beside the figure: panel 1 to the right of the bike, panel 2 on the road at left, panel 3 on the grass above the fallen man.
- **Figure:** The rider is the one who ruins things for himself. 'Me/you' in 29/50; a third party or group shown sabotaging itself in 10/50 (TikTokers, teachers, Nintendo, meme subreddits, a friend, a sibling, a sister); in 9/50 the panels are just time stages (Saturday/Sunday/Monday, school years) and the rider is nobody; 2/50 other.
- **Voice:** Short labels and present-tense clauses: panel 1 is a subject plus plan or activity in 33/50 ('me going to…', 'Tiktokers'), a bare time label in 9/50. Panel 2 is a verb phrase in present tense or gerund in 31/50 ('Adds new ingredient', 'Doing 1 push-up'). Panel 3 is an outcome clause in 22/50, a first-person quoted complaint or 'why…?' question in 8/50, a time label in 9/50. Sentence case or lowercase in 37/50; all caps in 6/50. Asterisk actions ('*loses*') in 2/50.
- **Boxes:** B1 → rider (panel 1, beside the riding man: who is riding plus what they set out to do ('I'm going to get work done', 'Tiktokers', 'I want to make my app more secure')); B2 → self-move (panel 2, beside the stick in the wheel: the rider's own self-defeating action); B3 → blamed (panel 3, above the man on the ground: the result that hits the rider (15/50 self-move uses), the rider's baffled 'why is this happening?' (7/50), or blame on someone else (1/50))
- **Patterns:**
  - `self-move-backfire` (32%, self-inflicted consequence / expectation vs reality): [rider]: actor + what they set out to do / [self-move]: the action they themselves take / [outcome]: the result that lands on them. Real shape: Starting to cook, adding one experimental ingredient, ruining the whole meal.
  - `oblivious-why` (16%, hypocrisy / obliviousness): [rider]: actor (often a group or company) / [self-move]: the thing they do that causes the problem / [outcome]: their baffled 'why is X happening?' or blame of an outside party. Real shape: A games company that sues everyone, then wonders aloud why people think it is mean.
  - `someone-else-jams-it` (14%, innocent plan ruined): [rider]: me doing something harmless / [self-move]: another person's interference / [outcome]: my plan wrecked. Real shape: Telling a parent a joke and getting a half-hour lecture about grades.
  - `time-stages` (18%, decline over time): [panel 1]: a good period / [panel 2]: the turning point / [panel 3]: the bad period. Real shape: Weekend day one, weekend day two, Monday.
- **Slot targets:** rider p50 19 / p90 38, logo plus a short plan or activity: actor + 'going to'/gerund; self-move p50 21 / p90 39, present-tense verb phrase or gerund: the rider's own action; blamed p50 22 / p90 44, outcome clause in present tense; or, only when documented, the rider's own complaint
- **Winners vs flops:** In winners, panel 2 is the rider's own action and panel 3 follows directly from it in 24/50 (self-move-backfire plus oblivious-why); readers see cause and effect at once. Flops (32): 9/32 make panel 3 something that doesn't follow from panel 2 or isn't a crash at all ('Weekend', 'Good night', 'Me:'); 7/32 put another party's action in panel 2 so the 'self' sabotage is lost ('Teacher: adds square roots', 'Ronaldo retired by…'); 5/32 run long, over-written clauses (60+ characters, '47 tabs of panic and productivity'); 5/32 are bare labels with no action ('OpenAI | AI Agent | Hugging Face Hacking Incident', 'EXISTING | DEPRESSION | DEPRESSION'). The other 6/32 flops use the format correctly (self-move then crash) and simply drew no audience, so a correct shape is necessary but not sufficient. The two tech news flops ('OpenAI / AI Agent / Hacking Incident', 'Jaguar' rebrand) both list nouns instead of a plan, a move and a result.
- **Tech memes:** 4 tech posts, all developer self-own jokes: plan in panel 1 ('make my app more secure', 'optimize my workflow'), the developer's own choice in panel 2 ('learn Vim', 'block password pasting'), the backfire in panel 3 ('can't use normal IDEs', 'why are my users getting hacked?'). The best (3 upvotes) is the oblivious-why form. A news flop on OpenAI used three noun labels and got 0.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting an excuse or a defense in panel 3 ('Claude was only producing example content'): panel 3 is the crash, the result that hits the rider, not what the rider says about it.
  - Writing panel 2 as an omission or a rule ('Rules did not rule out submitting forms'): the stick is an action the rider takes, a verb the reader can picture.
  - Requiring documented blame of someone else: real uses blame an outsider in 1/50; demanding blame makes the card reject most good backfire stories.
  - Letting someone else jam the stick: when another party causes the fall, it is a different joke (bad luck), and it loses the self-own (7/32 flops).
  - Listing nouns across the panels (company / product / incident) instead of plan, move and result.
  - Panel 3 that does not follow from panel 2: the reader must see that the move caused the fall.
- **Test:** Does the middle panel show the rider's own action, and does the last panel show that action coming back to hit the same rider (not an excuse, a defense, or someone else's doing)?
- **Slot problems:** Panel 3 is defined as 'who they blame', but real uses make panel 3 the outcome that lands on the rider (22/50) or the rider's baffled complaint (8/50); explicit blame of an outsider appears in 1/50. The card therefore rejects the strongest news fit (a company's own decision backfires on it) and pushes the writer toward invented blame or excuses, as in our one meme. Panel 1 also lacks the rider's plan or goal, which real uses state in 24/50 and which makes the backfire ironic ('set out to do X, did Y, got the opposite of X'). Proposal: Replace 'blamed' with 'outcome' (the crash is the self-inflicted result, not the blame) and add 'goal' in panel 1 so the backfire reads as defeating the rider's own aim. The new goal box sits right of the stick tip (x 0.49) and below the panel border, clear of the face at (0.34, 0.06).
- **Our examples:**
  - `self-move-backfire` · A chatbot maker removes its model picker to simplify the app, then restores it after users push back. → rider: [logo: openai] / goal: Simplify the app / self-move: Removes the model picker / outcome: Brings it back in days
  - `self-move-backfire` · A search giant puts AI answers on top of results, and odd answers go viral. → rider: [logo: google] / goal: Keep searchers on the page / self-move: AI answers on every search / outcome: Screenshots go viral
  - `self-move-backfire` · A phone maker pairs repair parts to serial numbers; a state passes a law banning the practice. → rider: [logo: apple] / goal: Control every repair / self-move: Locks parts to serial numbers / outcome: A state bans it
  - `self-move-backfire` · An AI lab trains on newspaper archives without a license and is sued by a major paper. → rider: [logo: openai] / goal: Train on the whole web / self-move: Scrapes paywalled news / outcome: Sued by the paper
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) rider=[logo Anthropic] ; self-move=Rules did not rule out submitting forms ; blamed=Claude was only producing example content: Panel 2 is an omission, not an action the rider took; panel 3 is a defense of the company's conduct, not a crash or a consequence, so there is no self-inflicted fall. It also sits close to an incident (a false police tip), which the card warns against.

### Mocking Spongebob (`mocking-spongebob`)
Coded 50 top uses, 0 tech memes, 28 flops. News fit: **weak** (It can carry a double standard or a stock corporate dismissal, but it needs the exact hollow line, which news rarely supplies without quoting a real person; the risk of mocking a person or a launch is high, and only 6/50 real uses even use the echo. It is benched for good reason.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** Repeating someone's line back in a dumb voice (alternating caps plus the chicken face) shows it is hollow without arguing: the echo itself is the rebuttal. The joke works only when the reader already recognizes the line as the kind of thing people say at others to dismiss them.
- **Picture:** SpongeBob bent forward in a chicken pose, head tilted, eyes bulging unevenly, mouth slack, arms tucked behind like wings: a deliberately dumb, mocking face. One figure fills the centre; text goes in a strip at the top and a strip at the bottom.
- **Figure:** On Imgflip the figure is mostly 'me' or a mocked group acting dumb, not a mocker: 'me' doing the pose in a situation in 12/50; a group mocked as acting this way ('six year olds making memes be like', 'that one kid') in 10/50; the voice repeating someone's line in alternating caps in 6/50 (the KYM canonical use); a pun on the chicken or sponge in 5/50; 1/50 hypocrisy; 16/50 unrelated jokes using the image as a backdrop.
- **Voice:** One box in 24/50, two or more boxes in 26/50. Alternating caps appear in only 6/50 top uses (11/70 overall) but in every mocking-echo use. The plain line is usually tagged with its speaker ('teacher:', 'when people say…') in 5/6 echoes. Scene frames dominate the rest: 'X be like' / 'when…' / 'me when…' / 'POV' in 25/50. Mixed or lowercase casing in 34/50.
