- **Boxes:** B1 → claim (top strip: who says it and the line in plain case ('teacher: get off of games'), or the whole caption in one-box uses); B2 → mocked (bottom strip: the same line (or its echo) in alternating caps, or the retort); B3 → null (extra labels (3/70 posts): a 'Me:' tag before the mocked line in dialogue setups)
- **Patterns:**
  - `mocking-echo` (12%, repetition as rebuttal): [claim]: [who]: the line they say at people / [mocked]: the same line in alternating caps. Real shape: A teacher scolds a kid for gaming when he is only using the arrow keys to scroll; the scolding is repeated back in mocking caps.
  - `group-be-like` (20%, mockery by depiction): [claim]: [a group] be like / when [a group does X] (the image is them). Real shape: Little kids making memes, shown as this dumb pose.
  - `me-reaction-pose` (24%, physical reaction): [claim]: me when [an awkward or painful moment] (the pose is my reaction). Real shape: Me when the shower turns too hot and hits my back.
  - `pun-on-image` (10%, wordplay): [claim]: a pun on chickens, birds or sponges that the pose illustrates. Real shape: Other birds do their job; mockingbirds look like this.
- **Slot targets:** claim p50 39 / p90 69, speaker tag plus the line in plain case: '[who]: [line]' or 'when [who] says [line]'; mocked p50 30 / p90 67, the same line (or a short echo of it) in alternating caps
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners pick a line or a behaviour everybody has heard (a teacher's scolding, 'it's from TikTok', 'feet are gross'): 6/6 echo winners quote a recognizable dismissive line. Flops (28): 11/28 are 'me when…' captions with no mocking at all ('me when the essay is due tonight'); 5/28 mock an obscure fandom or person the reader doesn't know (Inviz, Outfit7 logos, Blue Archive haters); 4/28 are long or rambling (one is a 600-character word salad); 3/28 use alternating caps on a line that isn't hollow, so the mock has no target. Only 1/28 flops is a clean echo of a recognizable line.
- **Tech memes:** No tech posts on this template in the harvest; too few to say.
- **Misreads:**
  - Mocking a product's marketing claim ('Meta says Muse Wonder Full has frontier capabilities'): that reads as us sneering at a launch, not exposing a line said at someone. Our card already bans it, but the writer did it in 2/3 memes.
  - Paraphrasing in the third person with a hedge ('Industrial Next says it retools lines in minutes'): the echo only works on the line itself, in the speaker's own words.
  - Leaving out who says it: real echoes tag the speaker in 5/6; without the tag the alternating caps mock nobody.
  - Echoing a neutral statement of fact ('it used its models to trace a hidden recording device'): nothing hollow, so the mock has no target.
  - Using names a casual reader doesn't know: the mocked line must be one they have heard.
- **Test:** Does the top name who says the line, and is the bottom a recognizable line they say at others (not a product claim or a neutral fact), so that repeating it in mocking caps exposes it as hollow?
- **Slot problems:** The single 'claim' slot asks for the line alone and code repeats it in mocking case, so the top box holds the bare line with no speaker. Real echoes put the speaker tag in the top box ('teacher:', 'when people say') in 5/6, and that tag is what tells the reader who is being mocked. The writer filled the gap with 'X says…', which is both a banned hedge and a third-person paraphrase. The template is benched, so this matters only if it is revived. Proposal: Top becomes the speaker tag (who says it, when), bottom is the line itself in mocking case, as in the canonical echo. The line is shown once, mocked, instead of twice. Boxes unchanged and clear of the face box (0.22, 0.25, 0.43, 0.55).
- **Our examples:**
  - `mocking-echo` · A large app confirms a data breach exposing millions of users' details. → speaker: Every app after a breach: / mocked: We take your privacy seriously
  - `mocking-echo` · A study finds customer service bots rarely hand users to a human. → speaker: The support bot when I ask for a human: / mocked: I can help you with that
  - `mocking-echo` · A new AI feature launches to only a small share of users first. → speaker: The app when I look for the new feature: / mocked: Rolling out gradually
  - `mocking-echo` · Recruiters warn applicants not to use AI while screening CVs with AI tools. → speaker: Recruiters using AI screening: / mocked: Don't use AI on your application
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) claim=Meta says Muse Wonder Full has frontier capabilities: A marketing claim with a hedge ('says') and an unknown product name; mocking it is us sneering, and there is no one it was said at.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) claim=it used its models to trace a hidden recording device: A neutral fact, not a hollow line said at anyone; no speaker tag.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) claim=Industrial Next says it retools lines in minutes: A startup's product claim in the third person with a hedge; unknown name; mocking a launch.

### Is This A Pigeon (`is-this-a-pigeon`)
Coded 50 top uses, 3 tech memes, 32 flops. News fit: **strong** (Tech news regularly documents confident mislabels: AI detectors flagging human writing, filters and moderators banning innocent content, models misidentifying things, companies relabeling a price hike as an upgrade. The format carries the 'overzealous system' and 'spin' turns in three short labels with no quote needed.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** An ordinary, harmless thing gets a confident, dramatically wrong label, and the size of the gap exposes the labeler: paranoia (a hoodie is a terrorist), ignorance (every game is Mario), or self-interest (a pandemic is a reason for more homework). The joke is on the labeler, and it lands because the reader instantly sees the thing for what it is.
- **Picture:** An anime man in glasses gestures with an open palm at a yellow butterfly in the upper right, confident and earnest, in front of a window. Three labels: one on the man (lower left, below his face), one on the butterfly (upper right), and the subtitle-style question across the bottom.
- **Figure:** The man is the confident mislabeler, almost always a group or institution, not the poster: a group of people (parents, teachers, Karens, schools, kids, boomers) in 37/50; a company, platform or system (Google, YouTube, Roblox chat, airport security, the media) in 8/50; 'me' or 'my brain' in 3/50; other 2/50.
- **Voice:** Pure labels: boxes 1 and 2 are noun phrases in 47/50 (no verbs in box 1 in 44/50). All caps in 34/50. The question starts 'Is this' in 38/47 three-box uses; variants 'Are these', 'Does this cause', 'Can I put this', 'What kind of… is this' in 7/47; a statement ('THIS IS…') in 2/47. Box 2 is generalized with 'any', 'literally any', 'anything' or 'some random' in 9/50. The wrong label is 1-3 words in 41/47.
- **Boxes:** B1 → mislabeler (on the man, lower left: who does the labeling (35/47 three-box uses put it first; 12/47 swap boxes 1 and 2)); B2 → thing (on the butterfly, upper right: the ordinary thing being labeled, often generalized ('any song', 'literally anything…')); B3 → question (bottom subtitle: 'Is this [wrong label]?')
- **Patterns:**
  - `alarmist-mislabel` (52%, overreaction / false equivalence): [mislabeler]: an authority or worrier / [thing]: something innocent / [question]: Is this [a threat, crime, offense or disaster]?. Real shape: Airport security, a tube of toothpaste, is this a bomb?
  - `one-reference-ignorance` (22%, ignorance / overgeneralization): [mislabeler]: someone who knows one thing / [thing]: any member of a broad category / [question]: Is this [the one thing they know]?. Real shape: Parents, any Pokémon, is this Pikachu?
  - `self-serving-label` (8%, self-interest / spin): [mislabeler]: someone who benefits / [thing]: an unrelated event or fact / [question]: Is this [the excuse or reframe that suits them]?. Real shape: Teachers, a global pandemic, is this a reason for more homework?
  - `leap-to-conclusion` (6%, jumping to conclusions): [mislabeler]: a nosy observer / [thing]: a normal social fact / [question]: Is this [the juicy conclusion]?. Real shape: Parents hear a girl's name once and ask if she's the girlfriend.
- **Slot targets:** mislabeler p50 8 / p90 27, noun phrase naming a group, role or company; 1-3 words; thing p50 17 / p90 35, noun phrase for the ordinary thing, often generalized ('any…', 'a kid wearing a hoodie'); question p50 9 / p90 18, the wrong label only, a 1-3 word noun phrase (code prints 'Is this {x}?')
- **Winners vs flops:** In winners the thing is plainly innocent or ordinary and the label is plainly wrong in 45/50, so the gap reads instantly. Flops (32): 9/32 have a label that is reasonable or nearly right, so there is no gap ('Ebay / is that a scam?', 'Californians / a heavy truck / is this an earthquake?', 'non-avgeeks / any plane / is this an A380?'); 6/32 break the three-part structure (one box, or the question in the wrong place: 'Is this a Hamburger?', 'is this'); 5/32 use labels with no mislabel at all ('Happy / Joyful / suprised', 'Fly / Yes / Thanks'); 4/32 are niche references (Leo Valdez, Verity, Gimbap); 3/32 are over-long or garbled; the remaining 5/32 are correct in form (e.g. parents calling depression laziness) and simply drew no audience. The two AI flops ('Claude's seventh review…', 'AI companies / old books / is this something that needs to be shredded?') both bury the label in a long clause.
- **Tech memes:** 3 tech posts, all 0 upvotes, all developer in-jokes (a Go developer treating every call as an error, Helm and YAML strings, a friend's program as a virus). The one-reference-ignorance form fits developer culture, but the evidence is thin. Real company-as-mislabeler uses in the main set (Google, YouTube, Roblox chat, airport security) are overzealous systems flagging innocent things, 6/50.
- **Misreads:**
  - Letting the mislabel be someone else's framing: in our Meta meme the 'frontier' label came from the press headline, not from Meta, so the joke is aimed at the wrong labeler.
  - Jargon in the label ('frontier capabilities'): the wrong label must be a plain 1-3 word noun a casual reader knows, so the gap is instant.
  - Picking a label that is nearly right: if the thing could fairly be called that, there is no joke (9/32 flops).
  - Describing the thing so specifically that it no longer reads as ordinary: real uses generalize it ('any song', 'anything pixelated') to show the habit.
  - Writing the label as a clause or sentence: the slot gets a noun phrase; code adds 'Is this…?'.
  - Requiring a logo or a viewer as mislabeler: real mislabelers are mostly a group label (37/50), which our card cannot express.
- **Test:** Is the thing plainly ordinary and the bottom label plainly wrong or self-serving, so that the gap between them is the joke and it is on the labeler?
- **Slot problems:** The mislabeler accepts only an entity or a viewer, but real mislabelers are a group or role label in 37/50 (parents, teachers, schools, the media, AI detectors). That blocks the most common and most news-ready form: an overzealous class of systems or people (AI detectors, content filters, moderators) mislabeling something innocent. The 'thing' MUST says nothing about the thing being ordinary or innocent, which is what makes the gap. Proposal: Add 'text' to the mislabeler so a group or role can be the labeler, as in 37/50 real uses, and tell the writer that the thing must be ordinary and the label plainly wrong and plain-worded. Boxes unchanged; they clear the face (0.146, 0.366, 0.27, 0.30).
- **Our examples:**
  - `alarmist-mislabel` · AI-writing detectors flag a centuries-old founding document as machine-written. → mislabeler: AI detectors / thing: A 1776 document / question: AI-written
  - `alarmist-mislabel` · A social platform's 'Made with AI' tag appears on real photos after small edits. → mislabeler: [logo: meta] / thing: A photo with one crop / question: made with AI
  - `self-serving-label` · An office suite raises its subscription price and bundles in its AI assistant. → mislabeler: [logo: microsoft] / thing: A $3 price hike / question: a Copilot upgrade
  - `one-reference-ignorance` · An AI company releases three new models with near-identical names on the same day. → mislabeler: [viewer AS-16] Me / thing: Any new model name / question: the good one
  - `alarmist-mislabel` · Automated moderation on a video platform removes harmless cooking clips as violent. → mislabeler: AI moderators / thing: A cooking video / question: violence
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) mislabeler=[logo Meta] ; thing=Second place on an index ; question=frontier capabilities: The self-serving-label shape is right (second place relabeled as the frontier), but the label is the press's framing rather than a documented Meta label, and 'frontier capabilities' is jargon that makes 'Is this frontier capabilities?' read awkwardly. 'Is this first place?' would land.

### You Guys are Getting Paid (`you-guys-getting-paid`)
Coded 50 top uses, 0 tech memes, 26 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries the 'others get a perk you don't' turn well: grants, staged rollouts, student or government deals, giant pay packages. It cannot carry a backfire or reversal, and it needs a concrete perk in the facts that the viewer plainly lacks, which many stories don't have.). Our memes misused it: 6 of 7.
- **Mechanism:** Indignant disbelief at discovering that others have been routinely getting something the speaker never knew was on offer: the joke is the naivety of the left-out party and the size of what he was missing, so the bigger or more basic the missed thing, the harder it lands.
- **Picture:** Will Poulter as Kenny in front of an RV, face scrunched in indignant disbelief (furrowed brow, squint, mouth half open), framed from the chest up. Text goes in a strip at the top and a strip at the bottom; the face is centred.
- **Figure:** The figure is the one left out or baffled: 'me' (the poster) in 31/50; a named third party or group reacting (Europeans, God, the rat, the cop, dads) in 15/50; other 4/50.
- **Voice:** All caps in 36/50. The snowclone is a question in 14/14 uses, always second person ('You guys…') and usually present progressive ('are getting', 9/14) or present ('get', 'have', 'celebrate', 5/14). The X after 'getting' is 1-3 words in 11/14 ('PAID', 'FOLLOWERS', 'SLEEP', 'VIEWS'). Setups open with 'Me' or 'When' in 22/50, or a dialogue tag ('Teacher:', 'Humans:') in 9/50.
- **Boxes:** B1 → setup (top strip: the speaker and his own situation, or the others announcing what they get ('America and Canada: we celebrate…'); the whole caption in one-box uses (30/70)); B2 → reveal (bottom strip: the incredulous question 'You guys [are getting / have / get] X?' or the speaker tag ('Europeans:') before it); B3 → null (extra labels (7/70): speaker tags in dialogue setups ('GOD:', 'THE RAT:'))
- **Patterns:**
  - `left-out-snowclone` (28%, naive outsider / scale contrast): [setup]: me + my own lesser or missing version (and who the others are) / [reveal]: You guys are getting [the thing they get]?. Real shape: Others mention their library restrictions; the speaker, who never had any, is stunned they have them at all.
  - `double-standard` (10%, double standard): [setup]: they get [reward] for X / [reveal or face]: I get [punishment] for the same X. Real shape: Dad swears and everyone laughs; I swear and get punished.
  - `baffled-reaction` (52%, reaction face): [setup]: me / [someone] when [an absurd or outrageous thing happens] (the face is the reaction). Real shape: Google telling me a cramp is a symptom of death.
- **Slot targets:** setup p50 45 / p90 70, first-person label: 'Me, [my own lesser version], [reading about the others]'; reveal p50 14 / p90 30, the X only: a short noun phrase the others get (code prints 'You guys are getting {x}?')
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the missed thing basic or huge and the speaker clearly outside it (14/14 snowclones name a thing the speaker visibly lacks). Flops (26): 8/26 are a face caption with no surprise or discovery ('me looking at the kid who bumped into a door', 'Mixed signals got me like'); 6/26 use 'you guys…?' on something the speaker could easily have too (macaroni with forks, compliments at a potluck), so there is no inequality; 5/26 make 'you guys' unclear; 4/26 are long or rambling (a joke about three men in a bar, a type 1 diabetes definition); 3/26 recycle the exact original line ('paid') with no new twist ('Wait… you guys are getting paid?' after chores).
- **Tech memes:** No tech posts in the harvest; too few to say. The closest tech-adjacent winners are about platforms ('you guys get views?', 'you guys are getting upvoted?', 'you guys are getting followers?'): a user discovering that others get the attention or reach he never gets.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting the speaker's own deal in the reveal ('$90/month of benefits for $30/month'): the reveal must be what OTHERS get that the speaker doesn't; a deal the speaker can buy too is ad copy, not exclusion.
  - Leaving 'you guys' unidentified (the Tokyo voice-actor and NZ policy memes): the setup or the X must make it obvious who the others are.
  - Third-person setups with a hedge ('AI, with fewer safety rules than milk, says Brad Smith'): the setup is the left-out speaker in the first person.
  - Comparing policies or rules instead of perks ('voluntary guidelines' vs 'an AI Safety Institute'): the format is about someone getting something good.
  - Pasting a price menu or a figure range as X ('$0, $20, $40, or any amount', '200k - 300k to build AI'): X must read naturally after 'You guys are getting' and be one concrete thing.
  - Using the face as a generic 'confused' caption with no discovery (the largest flop group, 8/26).
- **Test:** Does the top show the speaker's own worse or missing version, and is the bottom one concrete thing that an identifiable other group already gets, so the speaker is clearly left out?
- **Slot problems:** The two slots fit the snowclone, but the MUSTs don't force the two things that separate our Yes memes from our Horrible ones: (1) the setup must be the left-out speaker in the first person, and the others must be identifiable from setup or X; (2) X must be a single concrete thing the others get and the speaker doesn't, short enough to read after 'You guys are getting'. Two Horrible memes broke (1), and both Horrible and the v3 meme broke (2). Proposal: Boxes unchanged; MUSTs and setup accepts only. Setup becomes the first-person left-out speaker (viewer lines fit: AS-02, AS-04, AS-11, AS-12) who names the others when needed; X becomes one short perk that the speaker plainly lacks.
- **Our examples:**
  - `left-out-snowclone` · An AI company offers its enterprise chatbot to federal agencies for $1 per agency. → setup: [viewer AS-02] Me, paying $20 a month, reading about federal agencies / reveal: the enterprise plan for $1
  - `left-out-snowclone` · A tech giant gives university students a free year of its premium AI plan. → setup: [viewer AS-04] Me, two years out of college, on the paid plan / reveal: a free year
  - `left-out-snowclone` · A new voice feature launches in the US first, with other regions to follow. → setup: [viewer AS-11] Me in Europe, refreshing the app / reveal: the new voice mode
  - `left-out-snowclone` · Reports say top AI researchers are being offered pay packages worth over $100 million. → setup: [viewer AS-12] Me, asking for a 3% raise / reveal: $100 million offers
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: Horrible) setup=Me paying for SuperGrok, Cursor and X Premium separately ; reveal=$90/month of benefits for $30/month: The reveal is a bundle anyone can buy, so the speaker isn't left out; it reads as the company's sales pitch, and 'you guys' is nobody.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) setup=AI, with fewer safety rules than milk, says Brad Smith ; reveal=more safety rules than AI: Hedged third-person setup, no speaker left out, and the reveal is a rule comparison, not a perk; 'You guys are getting more safety rules than AI?' makes no sense.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) setup=Me, picking an AI plan to fit my monthly budget ; reveal=$50 million in compute a year: First-person lesser version, a huge concrete perk others get; scale contrast lands. 'You guys' is implied by the size of the number.
  - WRONG (Lucas: Horrible) setup=The actor got a court ruling and no takedown ; reveal=500,000 and 750,000 yen a month: Third-person setup, no left-out speaker, an unclear 'you guys', and a garbled figure range as X.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) setup=Me, comparing my AI plan to my budget ; reveal=200k - 300k to build AI: The left-out shape is right (salaries others get), but X is a raw range plus a clause; 'You guys are getting $300k salaries?' would read cleanly.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) setup=NZ: voluntary guidelines for AI ; reveal=an AI Safety Institute: A policy comparison, not a perk; the speaker is a country label, and 'you guys' is never identified.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) setup=Downloading about 2.9 GB of models ; reveal=$0, $20, $40, or any amount: No speaker, no others, and a price menu as X; nothing is being withheld from anyone.

### One Does Not Simply (`one-does-not-simply`)
Coded 70 top uses, 5 tech memes, 26 flops. News fit: **ok** (The engine is relatable failure, not a reversal or double standard, so it can't carry a news twist directly; it works when the news creates or worsens an everyday struggle (a new cap, a staged rollout, a model swap, a fix that won't work) and the line names that struggle as a plain act.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 6.
- **Mechanism:** A council-of-war warning is spent on a small everyday act (eat one chip, read the T&C, pause an online game) that everyone knows they can't actually do simply; the gap between the epic gravity and the petty, relatable failure is the joke. It only lands when the reader instantly recognises their own failure at that act.
- **Picture:** Boromir (Sean Bean) in a close-up, mid-sentence, eyebrows raised, thumb and fingers pinched in a lecturing 'listen carefully' gesture. Text sits as one impact line across the top strip and one across the bottom strip; his face fills the centre (detected face box x0.35-0.63, y0.26-0.73).
- **Figure:** Boromir is a grave, knowing voice addressing 'one' (everyone, including the poster) in 50/56 on-format uses; he speaks for a labelled group or institution ('McDonald's employees:', 'Schools', 'Teachers:') in 4/56; he is the poster lecturing a named other ('What I try to tell my parents:', 'my friend / me') in 2/56.
- **Voice:** B2 is a bare infinitive verb phrase that grammatically completes 'One does not simply…' in 51/56 on-format uses (no subject, no 'to', present tense); 12/56 add an explicit restriction clause ('without…', 'just one…', 'and not…'). ALL CAPS in 64/70 (Imgflip default). No ending punctuation in 47/56. Only 1/56 is quoted speech ('SAY "I'LL JUST WATCH ONE EPISODE"'). 14/70 top posts ignore the format entirely (unrelated captions that ride the template's traffic).
- **Boxes:** B1 → opener (top strip, above Boromir's head: 'ONE DOES NOT SIMPLY' verbatim (or a close variant) in 45/70; a speaker label ('McDonald's employees:') in 6/70; the whole sentence in one box in 9/70); B2 → hard-thing (bottom strip, across his chest: the bare verb phrase that completes the sentence: the everyday act that can't be done simply (the punch, 59/59 two-box posts)); B3 → null (bottom strip (rare, 2/70): the ODNS line after a two-part speaker setup ('Teachers:' / 'ONE DOES NOT SIMPLY' / 'PUT IT SIMPLY'); we have no slot and need none)
- **Patterns:**
  - `everyday-impossible-feat` (24%, understatement / mock-epic bathos): [opener] / [hard-thing]: an ordinary act everyone attempts and reliably fails at, stated plainly as if easy. Real shape: remembering the great idea you had half asleep once you actually wake up
  - `cant-stop-at-one` (17%, temptation vs restraint): [opener] / [hard-thing]: do the tempting thing only once, or skip the thing that always comes with it ('just one…', '…without …'). Real shape: leaving the drive-through without picking at the fries on the way home
  - `sacred-rule` (11%, petty vs proper (petty rule given epic weight)): [opener] / [hard-thing]: commit a small taboo or a 'wrong' way of doing an everyday thing, so the warning reads as an unwritten law. Real shape: eating a chocolate bar from the side instead of snapping it
  - `known-failure-of-a-group` (6%, hypocrisy / running grievance): [label: a group or institution:] / ODNS + [hard-thing]: the one job that group is notorious for never doing. Real shape: a fast-food chain's staff and the dessert machine that is always broken
  - `meta-or-wordplay` (16%, wordplay / self-reference): [opener] twisted ('…put it simply') or a joke about memes/the site itself. Real shape: a teacher label over 'one does not simply put it simply'
- **Slot targets:** opener p50 19 / p90 19, fixed: 'One does not simply'; hard-thing p50 34 / p90 53, bare verb phrase completing the sentence (verb first, no subject), often with 'without …' or 'just one'; our cap of 40 chars is near p75 (42) and is fine
- **Winners vs flops:** Subject: 0/56 on-format winners need specialist knowledge (food, T&C, Netflix, bubble wrap, grandma); 7/15 on-format flops hinge on niche or jargon subjects (hyponatremia saline, a fandom 'md oc', Roblox lock-in, a trophy rule over 100 chars). Act: 4/15 flops name something that isn't actually hard or tempting (pineapple on pizza, 'change at bank', watching movies backwards). Explaining: 2/15 flops explain or moralise ('it requires a lot of hard work', 'without proofreading it first' as advice); 0 winners explain. Format: 11/26 flops drop the ODNS frame entirely, vs 14/70 winners.
- **Tech memes:** Only 5 tech uses, scores 0-9: the best is the universal developer pain (reading someone's code and understanding it in seconds, 9 votes; exiting vim, 1). 'Ask an AI coding tool to build me an app' and 'make your own auth service' scored 0. Too few to say much, except that tech works only when the hard thing is a shared, felt struggle, not a product feature.
- **Misreads:**
  - Writing a jab or comparison about the story instead of an act ('sell milk with as few rules as AI'): the format needs an action the reader has tried, not an argument.
  - Putting the story's technical achievement in the box ('turn a straw view into near native acuity'): winners use everyday acts with 0 jargon; 7/15 on-format flops die on niche terms.
  - Restating what a product does as the hard thing ('keep up with eleven AI leaders on video' for a tool that does exactly that): it reads as the product pitch, and the warning has no bite.
  - Naming something that isn't actually hard or tempting (a taste debate, a routine errand): 4/15 on-format flops do this.
  - Explaining or moralising after the act ('…it takes hard work', '…without proofreading first' as advice): 0/56 winners add a reason.
  - Starting the line with a subject or 'to' so it doesn't complete 'One does not simply…' (51/56 winners start on the bare verb).
- **Test:** Does the bottom line complete 'One does not simply…' with an everyday act that an ordinary reader knows from their own life is hard, tempting or never works, rather than a technical feat, a product pitch or an argument about the story?
- **Our examples:**
  - `everyday-impossible-feat` · A third 'best model ever' launch in a month → opener: One does not simply / hard-thing: stick with one chatbot for a whole week
  - `everyday-impossible-feat` · A chatbot's free tier gets a lower daily message cap → opener: One does not simply / hard-thing: make the free messages last past lunch
  - `everyday-impossible-feat` · A rival assistant ships a better app, but chat history can't be imported → opener: One does not simply / hard-thing: switch apps and keep every old chat
  - `everyday-impossible-feat` · A big feature launches 'rolling out gradually' over several weeks → opener: One does not simply / hard-thing: get the new feature on launch day
  - `sacred-rule` · A study finds chatbots often state wrong answers with confidence → opener: One does not simply / hard-thing: trust the answer it sounded sure about
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) opener=One does not simply ; hard-thing=sell milk with as few rules as AI: A regulation comparison, not an act anyone tries and fails at; the reader needs the Brad Smith story to decode it.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: No) opener=One does not simply ; hard-thing=keep up with eleven AI leaders on video: Has the impossible-feat shape, but it restates the featured tool's pitch, and the act (watching AI execs) isn't something the audience struggles with.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) opener=One does not simply ; hard-thing=Turn a straw view into near native acuity: It is genuinely hard, but it is a medical feat in jargon ('acuity', 'straw view'), not an everyday act; it reads as the original Mordor sense (truly hard), not the meme's bathos.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) opener=One does not simply ; hard-thing=Assemble 4 mm parts without jamming them: Plain verb phrase plus the 'without…' constraint (the cant-stop-at-one/impossible-feat shape 12/56 winners use); anyone who's built flat-pack furniture feels it.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) opener=One does not simply ; hard-thing=Change a factory line without weeks of retooling: Easy-sounding act plus 'without', true to the story; slightly niche subject but readable.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) opener=One does not simply ; hard-thing=fix AI-tied inflation with rate hikes: Names the easy-sounding fix the story says won't simply work: a news-shaped version of the impossible feat.

### Buff Doge vs. Cheems (`buff-doge-cheems`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 30 flops. News fit: **strong** (The engine is a collapse on one dimension, which is exactly what many news turns are: a valuation to bankruptcy, a keynote to the shipped product, a free plan to a capped one, last year's flagship to this year's. Our only Awesome on this card is that shape. It needs a fact-backed decline or gap, so it is idle on purely positive stories.). Our memes misused it: 12 of 14.
- **Mechanism:** The same thing is shown at its mightiest and at its most pathetic, and the right-hand line has it fail, whimper or whine at the very thing the left-hand line brags about; the comedy is the size of the collapse on one shared dimension (survives 7 crashes vs can't start).
- **Picture:** White background. Left: a muscle-bound Doge standing tall, confident, facing the viewer. Right: small Cheems sitting, head turned away, blue tears. Imgflip's default boxes put a short label in the white strip ABOVE each dog (B1 left, B2 right) and a longer line in the white strip BELOW each dog (B3 left, B4 right).
- **Figure:** Each dog is the SAME thing (or the same kind of thing) in two moments or settings, never the poster as a person: the same thing then vs now in 33/70 ('youtubers then / youtubers now'); the same thing in fiction or expectation vs reality in 11/70 ('AI in movies / AI in real life'); the same thing in two settings in 11/70 ('cars in action movies / cars in horror movies'); two rival members of one category in 12/70 ('Darth Vader / Voldemort'); off-format in 3/70. The poster appears as one of the pair ('me in other subjects / me in math') in only 4/70.
- **Voice:** Labels: noun phrases, 51/67 labelled pairs repeat the same head noun and change only a qualifier (then/now, in movies/in real life, a year). Captions: in-character first-person speech in 44/61 posts with captions ('I can get hit 7 times…'), third-person verb phrases in 10/61 ('Took over an entire galaxy'), numbers or lists in 5/61. The weak caption is written in baby talk, mocking case or a whine ('me no start', 'Noooo why you no like', 'pls') in 14/52 posts with a weak caption. Labels only, no captions, in 9/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → strong (white strip above buff Doge: label for the mighty version: a noun phrase with a time or setting qualifier ('X then', 'X in movies'), p50 17 chars); B2 → weak (white strip above Cheems: label for the pathetic version, usually the same head noun with the other qualifier ('X now', 'X in real life'), p50 15 chars); B3 → strong-caption (below buff Doge: what the mighty one says or does: an in-character first-person brag in 44/61 captioned posts, p50 44 chars); B4 → weak-caption (below Cheems: the punch: the pathetic one whimpering or failing at the same thing, first-person, often in baby talk or mocking case, p50 33 chars)
- **Patterns:**
  - `same-thing-then-now` (47%, decline / escalation in reverse): [strong: X then] / [weak: X now] ; [strong-caption: X's old feat in its own proud voice] / [weak-caption: X's feeble present version of the same act, in a whimper]. Real shape: video creators a decade ago greet the audience plainly; today's threaten you unless you subscribe
  - `fiction-vs-reality` (16%, expectation vs reality): [strong: X in movies / ads / the demo] / [weak: X in real life] ; [strong-caption: the grand promise] / [weak-caption: the tiny everyday failure]. Real shape: movie AI computes rescue trajectories; real AI misspells a simple word and crashes
  - `same-thing-two-settings` (16%, absurd contrast / double standard): [strong: X in setting A] / [weak: X in setting B] ; [strong-caption: survives anything] / [weak-caption: fails at the basics]. Real shape: action-movie cars shrug off explosions; horror-movie cars won't start
  - `rivals-same-job` (17%, petty vs proper / scale contrast): [strong: player A] / [weak: player B in the same role] ; [strong-caption: A's big achievement] / [weak-caption: B's feeble one at the same job]. Real shape: one film villain conquered a galaxy; another couldn't defeat a toddler
- **Slot targets:** strong p50 17 / p90 28, noun phrase + time/setting qualifier, same head noun as weak; weak p50 15 / p90 27, the same noun phrase with the opposite qualifier; strong-caption p50 44 / p90 110, first-person brag in the strong one's voice (our 36-char cap is below the real p50; aim 25-40); weak-caption p50 33 / p90 77, first-person whimper or failure at the same act, lowercase or whiny, short
- **Winners vs flops:** Subject: 11/30 flops hinge on niche or insider subjects (a fandom pairing, an SCP faction, lab thermodynamics, a zoo's monkey list) vs about 2/70 winners (chess pieces, an early Nintendo lawsuit). Voice: 5/30 flops give two factual captions with no character voice (an aircraft spec, a list of monkey species, the history of a letter); winners use in-character speech in 44/61. Structure: 7/30 flops pair things that are not the same thing or put the context in the wrong box (a pharaoh vs 'me', captions that swap which dog is which); 3/30 are pure taste ('old show better') with no shown weakness. The best flop is a repost of a winning AI caption, which scored 0: freshness matters.
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts, but 6/70 top uses are about AI and all use fiction-vs-reality: AI in movies or hype (takes over the world) vs AI in real life (draws a 17-fingered cat, misspells a word, refuses a prompt over copyright), 21-173 votes. 5 more are gadgets (Wi-Fi in ads vs at home, phone vs security cameras, an old XP machine vs school laptops). The AI version works when the weak caption is a small, recognisable everyday flop.
- **Misreads:**
  - Pairing two DIFFERENT things that aren't the same thing at two moments (a lab opening vs 'Dublin hiring'; 'scam losses' vs 'the warning on your phone'): 66/70 winners compare one thing to itself or a direct rival in the same role.
  - Writing both captions as reported stats ('$250 million' / '$50 million a year', '51 in all' / 'Only 45 in all'): winners give each dog a voice in 44/61, and the weak line is a whimper, not a figure.
  - Saying the same fact twice ('Has more safety rules than AI' / 'Fewer rules than milk'): the weak caption must show the weak one failing, not restate the comparison.
  - Putting the bigger or worse number on Cheems so the picture contradicts the text (misconduct 78% 'before' on buff Doge, 92% 'now' on Cheems).
  - Comparing two unrelated dimensions (AI use per week vs voluntary rules): the strong and weak captions must be about the same act or quality.
  - Using hedges inside a caption ('…, says Brad Smith'): banned, and it breaks the in-character voice.
- **Test:** Are both dogs the same thing (or direct rivals in the same role) at two moments or in two settings, with the Cheems line showing it failing or whimpering at the very act the buff line boasts about?
- **Slot problems:** The four slots map to Imgflip's four boxes, but (1) our labels sit under the dogs stacked over the captions, leaving captions 0.14 high for a line whose real p50 is 44 chars, while the white strips above both dogs go unused; (2) the musts ask for 'what makes it strong/weak, from the facts', which produces stat lines, while real captions are each dog's own first-person voice (44/61) and the weak one fails at the same act the strong one boasts about; (3) nothing requires the two labels to be the same thing (or a direct rival in the same role), the rule 66/70 winners follow. Proposal: Matches Imgflip's real layout (labels above, speech below), gives the captions room for real lengths, and moves the rules that separate winners from our misses (same thing twice, one shared dimension, in-character voice) into the musts. Boxes clear the faces (Doge 0.2-0.3 x, 0.12-0.23 y; Cheems 0.74-0.86 x, 0.22-0.36 y) and the dogs' feet (about y 0.72).
- **Our examples:**
  - `same-thing-then-now` · A chatbot cuts how many messages free users get per day → strong: Free plan last year / strong-caption: ask me anything, all day long / weak: Free plan now / weak-caption: you have 3 questions left until 5pm
  - `fiction-vs-reality` · Reviewers find a newly launched AI agent gets stuck on simple web forms → strong: The agent in the launch video / strong-caption: I booked your whole trip in a minute / weak: The agent on my laptop / weak-caption: which box is the date box?
  - `same-thing-then-now` · An AI startup once valued at billions sells its assets in bankruptcy for a few million → strong: The startup in 2023 / strong-caption: we're worth over a billion / weak: The startup in court / weak-caption: assets for sale, any offer pls
  - `rivals-same-job` · AI labs announce another multi-billion-dollar funding round → strong: AI labs this week / strong-caption: we raised billions for chips / weak: Me this week / weak-caption: is the $20 plan worth it tho
  - `fiction-vs-reality` · A new model miscounts the letters in a short word in a viral test → strong: AI in sci-fi / strong-caption: I pilot the entire starship / weak: AI in my chat app / weak-caption: that word has 2 letters r
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) strong=Milk ; strong-caption=Has more safety rules than AI ; weak=AI ; weak-caption=Fewer rules than milk, says Brad Smith: A funny inversion (the mundane thing is buff, like the winners' germ vs hand sanitizer) on one dimension, but the captions restate one fact twice and the weak one carries a banned hedge instead of a whimper.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) strong=Scam losses in Singapore ; strong-caption=More than S$4 billion since 2019 ; weak=The warning on your phone ; weak-caption=Advises you to pause: Two different things (a harm vs a tool) with no shared act; it reads as mocking a safety feature.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) strong=Modders ; strong-caption=Port games with no business model rules ; weak=[logo Microsoft] ; weak-caption=Tied to business model restrictions: Rivals at the same job, the weak one held back on that same job; the captions would be stronger in each side's voice.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) strong=Character.AI, March 2023 ; strong-caption=Valued at more than a billion dollars, no revenue ; weak=Character.AI in Chapter 11 ; weak-caption=Disney bids $20 million for some assets: The core pattern (47%): the same company then vs now, one dimension (what it's worth), a huge collapse.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) strong=[logo OpenAI] ; strong-caption=A second applied AI lab ; weak=Dublin hiring ; weak-caption=200 jobs. 450 planned in Ireland: Both sides are good news about the same expansion; nothing is weak and the labels aren't the same thing twice.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) strong=[logo Nvidia] ; strong-caption=US$30 billion into OpenAI ; weak=Investors outside AI ; weak-caption=Left with under half of the pie: Rivals in the same role (investors in AI) is right, but both captions are stats and 'under half' isn't pathetic enough to read as Cheems.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) strong=Washing machine ; strong-caption=Safety rules and inspection ; weak=AI ; weak-caption=Fewer safety rules than a washer: Same inversion as the milk one; the weak caption is closer to a punch but is still a comparison, not AI's own whimper.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) strong=The headline ; strong-caption=$250 million ; weak=Each year ; weak-caption=$50 million a year: A real headline-vs-reality split, but the labels aren't parallel and two bare figures have no voice; reads as arithmetic.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) strong=[logo Alex Karp] ; strong-caption=51 in all, same as Dario Amodei ; weak=[logo Sam Altman] ; weak-caption=Only 45 in all: Counts of what? A trivial gap between two people with no shared act; a casual reader can't decode it.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) strong=Kiwi AI use ; strong-caption=Over half use it every week ; weak=NZ AI rules ; weak-caption=Voluntary guidelines: Two different things (use vs rules) rather than one thing twice; it also leans toward a policy opinion.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) strong=[logo Google] ; strong-caption=Gave a $2.7 billion lifeline ; weak=Character.AI ; weak-caption=Assets get a $20 million bid: The real contrast is the same company then vs now (as the Awesome version did); splitting it across Google and Character.AI muddles who is weak.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) strong=Before ; strong-caption=Misconduct: 78% ; weak=Now ; weak-caption=Record 92%, led by AI use: The bigger number sits on Cheems, so the picture contradicts the text; 'Before/Now' with no head noun loses the subject.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) strong=PostEra's pitch ; strong-caption=over $1B in AI partnerships ; weak=The new researcher ; weak-caption=0.05 - 0.1% equity: A scale contrast on one dimension (the money), but it is a small company's job ad and the weak side is a person, which edges toward punching down.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) strong=[logo Microsoft] ; strong-caption=More than 72 billion hours a year ; weak=Xbox Labs ; weak-caption=A small group of game makers: Big vs small within one company is a usable scale contrast, but '72 billion hours' of what is unclear and neither caption shares an act.

### They're The Same Picture (`same-picture`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 18 flops. News fit: **ok** (The crude-equater engine carries a real news turn: a filter, detector, cap, policy or ruling that treats unlike things alike is a recurring AI story, and the joke lands on the rule, not on people. The sold-as-different engine fits renames and same-price launches but needs facts that prove identity. It is idle on stories without an equating rule or a rebrand, and the institution-as-Pam use must avoid political targets.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** Pam calmly declares two things identical. Usually the reader knows they are wildly different (a water bottle and a nuke), so the joke is on whoever lumps them together, a crude rule or lazy authority; less often the two really are the same thing sold as different ($5 vs $4.99), and the joke is on the trick. Either way the deadpan certainty against an obvious difference, or an obvious sameness, is the engine.
- **Picture:** Two panels from The Office. Top: two hands hold up two blank white sheets (left sheet x0.02-0.52, y0.03-0.37; right sheet x0.47-0.98, y0.08-0.43) over a burned-in subtitle 'Corporate needs you to find the differences between this picture and this picture.' Bottom: Pam, deadpan with a slight knowing smile, over the burned-in subtitle 'They're the same picture.' Imgflip's B1 and B2 sit on the two sheets, B3 on Pam's chest.
- **Figure:** Pam is the one who equates the two things. In 40 posts that label her: a third-party group or institution (schools, teachers, parents, moms, airports, a laptop OS) in 32/40; the poster ('Me') in 8/40. In the dominant pattern (28/54 text posts) her equation is WRONG and is the target of the joke; in 16/54 it is the poster's own hyperbolic insult; in only 8/54 are the two things genuinely the same.
- **Voice:** All three boxes are bare labels, not sentences, in 51/54 text posts: noun phrases ('A water bottle', 'Kids wearing hoodies') or gerund phrases ('Having a girlfriend', 'Being hungry'). Quoted speech appears in 2/54 ('"Your homework today is to study."'). The Pam label is a plural group in 30/40 and ends with a colon in 6/40 ('Schools:'). Lowercase or sentence case dominates (about 45/54); no ending punctuation on 50/54.
- **Boxes:** B1 → left-picture (left white sheet: the first thing, usually the harmless or ordinary one, p50 11 chars); B2 → right-picture (right white sheet: the second thing, usually the extreme one it gets lumped with, p50 12 chars); B3 → pam (on Pam, lower panel: who treats them as the same: a group or institution (32/40) or 'Me' (8/40), p50 8 chars; 16/70 posts use one box only, because they paste pictures onto the sheets and only label Pam (we can't render that variant))
- **Patterns:**
  - `crude-equater` (40%, false equivalence / exaggeration): [left-picture: an ordinary, harmless thing] / [right-picture: a far more extreme thing] / [pam: the rule, system or authority that treats them identically]. Real shape: a child stating an opinion and outright disrespect, as parents see them
  - `hyperbolic-insult` (23%, hyperbole / insult): [left-picture: a disliked thing] / [right-picture: something plainly bad (garbage, prison, hell)] / [pam: me]. Real shape: a trending kids' channel and actual trash, per the poster
  - `sold-as-different` (11%, deadpan observation / exposed trick): [left-picture: version A] / [right-picture: version B, marketed or named as different] / [pam: me or everyone, who can't tell them apart]. Real shape: this year's phone model next to last year's, as everyone sees it
  - `pictures-not-words` (23%, visual resemblance): images pasted on both sheets, one label on Pam. Real shape: two look-alike photos with a group named on Pam
- **Slot targets:** left-picture p50 11 / p90 26, short noun or gerund phrase: the ordinary thing; right-picture p50 12 / p90 36, short noun or gerund phrase: the extreme thing it gets lumped with (or the 'new' version); pam p50 8 / p90 21, plural group, institution, product or 'Me'; a label, never a sentence
- **Winners vs flops:** Pam label: 40/54 winning text posts name who does the equating; 7/18 flops are a lone label on Pam with nothing on the sheets ('Me', 'AMERICANS', 'Hollow knight players'). Recognition: winning crude-equater posts target a grievance most readers have lived (school dress rules, airport security, parents), 0/28 need fandom knowledge; 4/18 flops pair niche fandom characters. Bare insults with no shared target ('My ex' / 'An idiot', 'What you want' / 'What you need' / 'Ungrateful morons') make 3/18 flops. A verbatim retread of the airport water-bottle joke scored 0 as a recent post, against 488 and 177 for earlier versions.
- **Tech memes:** Only 2 tech-tagged posts: 'A = B' vs 'A == B' with 'Non programmers' on Pam (10 votes, a naive-equater joke) and Java vs Python Hello World (1 vote, no Pam, reads as a code comparison). In the top 70, the best tech uses are a device as the crude equater (a laptop treating 30% and 0% battery the same, 76) and look-alike product generations (two consecutive phone models, 53). A system that lumps unlike inputs together is the natural AI fit (filters, detectors, usage caps).
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting two things that are merely two of a kind on the sheets (one company's lab in Singapore and its lab in Dublin): nobody claims they're different and nobody wrongly equates them, so there is no joke.
  - Treating Pam as the clear-eyed viewer by default: in 28/54 real uses Pam is the party with the crude view, and that party is the target.
  - Leaving Pam blank: our card makes her optional, but 40/54 winning text posts label her, and a crude-equater meme without the equater has no target.
  - Pairing two things that are close (two similar products) when the format wants either an absurd gap (harmless vs extreme) or exact identity; 'similar' is the dead zone.
  - Writing sentences on the sheets: 51/54 winners use bare labels of 2-5 words.
  - Using it as a bare insult with no shared target (3/18 flops); for us that is also a mocking claim.
- **Test:** Does the meme name who treats the two things as the same, and are the two things either obviously different (so the joke is on that party's crude view) or truly identical but sold as different, rather than just two similar things?
- **Slot problems:** Our card covers only the minority use (things that really are the same, 8/54) and casts Pam as the optional, clear-eyed viewer. In the dominant use (28/54) the two things are obviously different and Pam is a named party (a rule, system, institution) whose equating them is the joke; she is labelled in 40/54 text posts. `pam` accepts only `viewer` and is optional, so the writer can't name a filter, policy or product as the equater and often leaves her blank. Proposal: Makes Pam required and lets her be the equating party (entity or text), which is what 32/40 real labels do, and writes both real uses (crude equater, sold-as-different) into the musts so 'two of a kind' is ruled out. Geometry is unchanged; the Pam box sits on her cardigan below her face (face y0.62-0.77).
- **Our examples:**
  - `crude-equater` · Students report AI-text detectors flagging essays they wrote themselves → left-picture: My own essay / right-picture: A chatbot's essay / pam: AI detectors
  - `crude-equater` · A chatbot's daily cap counts every message the same, whatever its size → left-picture: "thanks!" / right-picture: Summarize this 80-page PDF / pam: The message limit
  - `crude-equater` · Users complain a model refuses harmless requests after a safety update → left-picture: How to kill weeds / right-picture: An actual threat / pam: The new safety filter
  - `sold-as-different` · Two rival labs both sell a $200-a-month top plan → left-picture: [logo: openai] / right-picture: [logo: anthropic] / pam: [viewer AS-02] My card statement
  - `sold-as-different` · A lab renames its model tiers; the renamed top tier keeps the same features → left-picture: The old top tier / right-picture: The renamed top tier / pam: [viewer AS-16] Me
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) left-picture=OpenAI applied AI lab, Singapore ; right-picture=OpenAI applied AI lab, Dublin: Two labs in two cities are simply two of a kind; no one sells them as different, no one wrongly lumps them, and Pam is empty, so the deadpan has nothing to expose.

### 0 Days Without (Lenny, Simpsons) (`zero-days-without`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 27 flops. News fit: **strong** ('It happened again' is one of the commonest news turns: another lawsuit, another hallucination in court, another 'best model ever', another outage. The format carries it with no setup, and both of Lucas's grades on it are Yes. It needs a recurrence the facts support; on a single first-time event it has nothing to say.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** A safety counter is meant to climb, but this one is reset from 0 to 0: the bad thing happens so constantly that counting is pointless. The joke is weary resignation at a chronic habit or failing, and it lands when the reader recognises the act as something that truly happens every single day.
- **Picture:** Simpsons still: Lenny, eyes wide and weary, reaches up to swap the '0' card on a workplace 'DAYS WITHOUT ___' board for another '0' card he holds in his other hand. 'DAYS WITHOUT' and both zeros are printed; the blank on the board after 'DAYS WITHOUT' (x0.55-0.98, y0.26-0.5) takes the event, and Imgflip's optional B2 sits on Lenny's body below his face.
- **Figure:** The sign's event is what keeps happening; Lenny's label (B2, used in 39/70) names who keeps doing it: a group or institution (teachers, Disney, 6th graders, a laptop OS, Character.AI) in 28/39, the poster 'ME' in 8/39, a comment in 3/39. Without B2, the doer sits inside the event line in 12/31 one-box posts (someone saying a catchphrase, a named politician being stupid); of the other 19, 7 are the poster's own habit (forgetting, overthinking) and 12 are a bare topic noun ('LAWSUIT', 'REPOSTS').
- **Voice:** Event: gerund phrase in 44/70 ('SOMEONE SAYING 67', 'FORGETTING WHY I WALKED INTO THIS ROOM'), bare noun in 22/70 ('LAWSUIT', 'REPOSTS', 'YOUTUBE ADS'), quoted speech in 4/70. It names the act, never its absence, and never repeats 'days without' (2/27 flops do). Lenny label: a plural group or institution (28/39) or 'ME' (8/39), 1-3 words. ALL CAPS in 62/70 (Imgflip default). No end punctuation in 66/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → event (the blank on the board after 'DAYS WITHOUT': the bad or annoying act that keeps happening, a gerund phrase in 44/70 or a bare noun in 22/70, p50 21 chars); B2 → null (on Lenny's body, lower middle: who keeps doing it (a group, a company, 'ME'), p50 9 chars, used in 39/70; we have no slot for it)
- **Patterns:**
  - `people-around-me` (51%, exasperation / understatement): [event: an annoying act a group does constantly] / [Lenny label: that group]. Real shape: a whole class punished because one kid talked, labelled with the teachers
  - `my-own-habit` (21%, self-deprecation): [event: my own recurring failing, as a gerund] / [Lenny label: me]. Real shape: rereading a one-word text reply for hidden meaning, labelled 'me'
  - `repeat-offender` (16%, running grievance / hypocrisy): [event: the scandal, flop or failure a known company or figure keeps producing] / [Lenny label: that company]. Real shape: box office bombs, labelled with the studio that keeps releasing them
  - `meta-wordplay` (11%, self-reference / wordplay): a joke about the sign, the template or a twist on the counter. Real shape: a counter variant: days without touching grass, labelled with chat moderators
- **Slot targets:** event p50 21 / p90 47, gerund phrase or bare noun naming the bad act; the subject ('someone', 'AI models') may sit inside it; culprit (proposed) p50 9 / p90 18, who keeps doing it: plural group, company or 'me', 1-3 words
- **Winners vs flops:** Niche: 7/27 flops hinge on names few readers know (small creators, a local landmark, a niche fandom creator) vs 2/70 winners. Vague: 7/27 flops name something too generic or not chronic ('walking', 'being annoyed', 'school', 'crashing out'); winners name a specific, observable act (a phrase everyone keeps yelling, losing socks in the wash). Logic: 3/27 flops invert the sign (putting a GOOD thing after 'days without', or swapping the event and the culprit) vs 0/70 winners. Redundancy: 2/27 flops rewrite '0 days without' inside the box. Politics and ethnic jabs: 3/27 flops, all at 0.
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts. In the top 70, 5 are tech: an AI chat app treating a harmless question as a threat (6), video-site ads (7), a carmaker's lawsuits (11), my family on smartphones (7), me vs electronics (5). The repeat-offender pattern (a company and its chronic failing) is the clean fit for AI news; it needs the recurrence to be real across stories.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting a neutral or positive one-off on the sign ('OpenAI opening an applied AI lab'): the event must be a bad or annoying act, and it must happen so often the counter can't leave zero.
  - Writing something that happened once or twice as if it were chronic: the format claims 'every day'; it needs a pattern the facts (or the week's stories) show.
  - Inverting the sign by putting the good thing that never happens after 'days without' (3/27 flops): 'DAYS WITHOUT good news' says good news happens daily.
  - Leaving the culprit unnamed: 39/70 winners label Lenny and 12/31 one-box winners put the doer in the line (7/31 more are the poster's own habit); a bare 'Going off script' leaves 'who?'.
  - Picking a generic act that isn't recognisably chronic ('walking', 'being annoyed'): 7/27 flops.
  - Repeating 'days without' inside the slot (the board already prints it).
- **Test:** Does the sign name a bad or annoying act that happens so constantly the counter can never leave zero, with the doer clear from the line or Lenny's label, rather than a one-off, neutral or positive event?
- **Slot problems:** Our card has only the sign. Real uses label Lenny with the culprit in 39/70, and that label is what makes the repeat-offender pattern (company plus its chronic failing) read at a glance. The `must` also doesn't say the act must be bad or annoying, or chronic rather than a repeat, so the writer put a neutral lab opening on the sign. Proposal: Adds Imgflip's B2 (used in 39/70) so a company and its chronic failing can be named separately, and tightens the event's must to 'bad and chronic', the two rules our misses and the flops break. The culprit box clears Lenny's face (x0.33-0.52, y0.28-0.55) and the held '0' card (x0.03-0.25, y0.55-0.88).
- **Our examples:**
  - `repeat-offender` · A second lab calls its new release its 'most capable model ever' a week after a rival did → event: Launching the "best model ever" / culprit: AI labs
  - `repeat-offender` · Another court sanctions lawyers for filing a brief with cases a chatbot made up → event: Citing a court case that doesn't exist / culprit: Lawyers using chatbots
  - `my-own-habit` · Users report a model update changed the answers to prompts they reuse → event: Rewording the prompt that just worked / culprit: [viewer AS-03] Me
  - `repeat-offender` · A big AI feature launches 'rolling out gradually' to a small share of users → event: A feature "rolling out gradually" / culprit: Tech launches
  - `people-around-me` · Another AI app emails users about a privacy policy update covering their chats → event: A "we updated our privacy policy" email / culprit: [viewer AS-09] My inbox
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) event=Claude acting on a real site it shouldn't: A specific bad act with the culprit inside the line (the one-box form), and the story reports repeated incidents, which is what the counter needs.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) event=AI models going off script on real websites: A gerund naming a chronic bad behaviour of a group: the people-around-me pattern applied to a technology.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) event=OpenAI opening an applied AI lab: A neutral one-off expansion; nothing bad, nothing chronic, so the reset reads as a non-sequitur.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) event=a new agent pitched as safer: A recurring industry habit (every launch billed as the safe one), the repeat-offender pattern; it would be sharper with the culprit named on Lenny.

### Squidward Window (`squidward-window`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 23 flops. News fit: **strong** (Who gets it and who doesn't is one of the most frequent AI news facts (US-first launches, paid-tier-only models, waitlists, device requirements), and the format turns that gap into the viewer's pang without mocking anyone. It doesn't carry reversals or backfires; it carries access gaps, and only when others really have the thing now.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** Someone is stuck behind glass by a specific barrier (sick, too old, wrong country, wrong edition, didn't win) while others outside have exactly the fun they can't; the joke is the pang of being shut out of something small and specific, and it sharpens when the barrier is petty or arbitrary (two inches too short, one box-top short).
- **Picture:** Squidward seen from behind in a dark room, head in shadow, peering through half-open blinds; outside in bright daylight SpongeBob and Patrick leap with arms up, having a great time. B1 sits on Squidward's back-of-head (left third), B2 on the bright window below the two jumpers (right half).
- **Figure:** Squidward is the poster ('ME…', 'YOU…') in 34/70 and a third-party group in 27/70 ('Europeans', 'Underclassmen', a snubbed franchise's fans); off-format in 9/70. SpongeBob and Patrick are a plural group (the class, my friends, kids, people in another country) in about 50/64 two-box posts, almost always shown DOING the fun thing.
- **Voice:** Watcher: 'ME' + a relative or participle clause stating the barrier ('ME WHO…', 'ME STUCK…', 'ME WITHOUT…') in 24/44 shut-out posts; bare 'ME' in 5/70. Enjoyers: plural group + gerund of the fun ('…ENJOYING THEIR FREEDOM', '…HAVING A PIZZA PARTY') in about 38/64; a bare group with no activity in 12/64. Third person for others, first person for the watcher. ALL CAPS in 52/70. No ending punctuation in 60/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → watcher (on Squidward's head, left third: who is shut out, usually 'ME' plus the barrier ('ME WHO WAS TOO SICK FOR SCHOOL'), p50 28 chars); B2 → enjoyers (on the window under SpongeBob and Patrick: the group that has it, plus the fun they're having ('THE CLASS WATCHES A MOVIE AND GETS PIZZA'), p50 36 chars); B3 → null (varies (7/70): a second enjoyer or a caption ('WW2 IN A NUTSHELL'); no slot needed)
- **Patterns:**
  - `missed-it` (27%, absurd specificity / bad luck): [watcher: me + the circumstance that kept me out (sick, absent, stuck with work)] / [enjoyers: everyone else + the specific treat they got]. Real shape: the one kid who was absent the day the class got a pizza party and a movie
  - `locked-out` (14%, arbitrary line / double standard): [watcher: me or my group + the rule, place, device or cutoff that bars us] / [enjoyers: the group on the right side of that line + doing the thing]. Real shape: a kid two inches too short for the ride watching taller siblings on it
  - `grumpy-onlooker` (16%, inversion (the watcher is the problem)): [watcher: a killjoy, hater or menace] / [enjoyers: the people it resents or threatens]. Real shape: a virus watching people enjoy the outdoors
  - `overlooked` (9%, underdog / snub): [watcher: the one that got snubbed] / [enjoyers: the favoured peers getting the attention]. Real shape: one holiday watching the two bigger holidays get all the decorations
  - `aged-out` (7%, nostalgia): [watcher: me as an adult] / [enjoyers: kids + the carefree thing]. Real shape: a grown-up looking out at kids enjoying a school-free afternoon
- **Slot targets:** watcher p50 28 / p90 52, 'Me' + the barrier (who/stuck/without/on the …), first person; enjoyers p50 36 / p90 59, plural group + gerund of the specific fun; our 34-char cap is just under the real p50, so keep it tight (25-34)
- **Winners vs flops:** Activity: winners name what the others are enjoying in about 38/64 posts (pizza party, field trip, free iPhones); 6/23 flops give a bare group ('my friends', 'other people', 'Everyone else:') so there is nothing to envy. Relation: 5/23 flops show no fun on the outside at all (people who complain about work, a year label, a sports result). Missing box: 4/23 flops are one box with no enjoyers. Length: 4/23 flops run past 90 chars in a box; winners' p90 is 52/59. Winners make the barrier petty or specific (one box-top short, 47 vs 49 inches); flops rarely state a barrier.
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts, but 6/70 top uses are tech access stories: stuck on a console edition while YouTubers play with mods (30), watching others show off a new console you don't have (9), no internet while kids play online games (17), not allowed on any devices (13), invisible on a chat server (16). The locked-out pattern maps straight onto staged rollouts, paid tiers, region launches and device requirements; 4/10 locked-out winners use a country line, so AS-17 (region) belongs on this card alongside AS-04/10/11.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting a company logo in enjoyers: the people outside are always a group of people having fun (about 50/64), so the enjoyers must be the users who have access, not the company that shipped it.
  - Naming the enjoyers with no activity ('Pro users'): 6/23 flops do this; winners show the fun ('Pro users trying the new model').
  - Leaving out the barrier: the watcher line states why they're stuck in 24/44 shut-out winners; 'Me' alone loses the reason (and the story's fact).
  - Using it when the others don't really have it yet (a 'coming soon' story): the format needs others enjoying it now, per the facts.
  - The grumpy-onlooker inversion (16% of real uses) turns Squidward into the villain; for us that becomes a mocking claim, so skip it.
  - Overlong lines: 4/23 flops run past 90 chars; keep both under the cap.
- **Test:** Is Squidward someone kept out by a specific barrier the meme makes clear (plan, region, device, rollout, bad luck), and are the people outside a group the facts show really have it, shown doing the fun thing?
- **Slot problems:** The two slots match the real two boxes, but: `enjoyers` accepts a bare entity, while real enjoyers are a group of people shown doing the fun thing (about 50/64), so a logo alone has nothing to envy; the `must` doesn't ask for the activity. `watcher` accepts only the viewer, while 27/70 real watchers are a named group (people in one country, free users), which is how region and plan stories read. Situations should include AS-17 (region lock), the commonest access line in real uses. Proposal: Same two boxes and geometry (clear of SpongeBob's and Patrick's faces). The change asks for the barrier on the left and the activity on the right, the two things that separate winners from flops, and stops the writer from putting a company logo where real memes put people having fun.
- **Our examples:**
  - `locked-out` · A new AI voice feature launches in the US first, with no date for Europe → watcher: [viewer AS-17] Me in the EU, refreshing / enjoyers: US users talking to the new voice
  - `locked-out` · A lab's newest model is available only to its top-tier subscribers at launch → watcher: [viewer AS-04] Me on the free plan / enjoyers: Pro users trying the new model
  - `missed-it` · A big app update goes to a small group of testers first → watcher: [viewer AS-11] Me, still on the old version / enjoyers: Testers posting demos all day
  - `locked-out` · New on-device AI features require this year's phones → watcher: [viewer AS-11] Me with a two-year-old phone / enjoyers: New-phone owners trying AI tricks
  - `missed-it` · A rival assistant ships live camera help that the viewer's usual app lacks → watcher: [viewer AS-10] Me, loyal to my usual app / enjoyers: Their users showing it the fridge

### Expanding Brain (`expanding-brain`)
Coded 60 top uses, 5 tech memes, 30 flops. News fit: **strong** (The fixed-frame inversion carries a backfire or an absurd corporate move well. Three normal versions set the expectation, and the real move lands as the 'enlightened' fourth. Escalations (ads creeping onto more surfaces) and euphemism ladders (layoff wording) map directly. The risk is structural, not topical: the writer has to build a shared frame, not retell the story.). Our memes misused it: 4 of 7.
- **Mechanism:** All four lines are versions of the same thing. The brain treats each as smarter than the last, but the last one is really the laziest, dumbest, most pompous or most backwards version, and that's the joke. The irony only works because the frame stays fixed, so the reader can see exactly what changed.
- **Picture:** Four stacked rows. On the right, a brain that grows brighter each row: a dim skull X-ray, a glowing brain, a blazing pink-white brain, then a cosmic brain shooting light beams. The left half of every row is blank white, where the four text boxes sit.
- **Figure:** No figure stands for anyone. The brain grades the line next to it. Across the 55 four-step uses, the lines are versions of one action or word in 44/55 and a person or group's choices in 11/55. In 4/60 the brain literally measures the poster's own thinking ('my brain at school / in the shower / at bed').
- **Voice:** Labels, not sentences, in 41/60: gerund phrases ('USING…', 'GOING TO…', 'STARTING…') in 16, bare nouns or words in 21, 'PEOPLE WHO…' frames in 4. First-person or quoted speech in 12/60 (the register ladders: 'I'll do my homework…', 'You're fired'). 'Me / my brain when…' in 4/60. Imperatives in 2/60. In 44/55 four-step uses, all four lines share the same opening words or structure. Caps in about 48/60 (Imgflip's default). Present tense; no end punctuation except in the verbose register gags.
- **Boxes:** B1 → take-1 (row 1, left of the dim skull brain: the normal version: the plain word or the ordinary way to do it); B2 → take-2 (row 2, left of the glowing brain: same frame, one element changed, slightly more elaborate); B3 → take-3 (row 3, left of the blazing brain: same frame, more elaborate still; the setup peak); B4 → take-4 (row 4, left of the cosmic brain: the inverted, absurd or over-dressed version, crowned as enlightened); B5 → null (extra caption (2/70 posts): an add-on punchline; we have no slot for it and need none)
- **Patterns:**
  - `fixed-frame-inversion` (32%, inversion): [take-1..take-3]: one sentence frame repeated with one element changed, each 'better' by normal standards / [take-4]: the same frame where the change is the worst, laziest or most backwards option, crowned as the biggest brain. Real shape: Doing homework now, after dinner, in the morning, then in class just before it is due.
  - `register-ladder` (32%, wordplay / pompous escalation): [take-1]: the plain word or phrase / [take-2..take-3]: the same meaning, said more formally or more oddly / [take-4]: the same meaning in a ridiculous euphemism, coinage or pompous paragraph. Real shape: Bathroom, then three increasingly silly rhyming names for the same room.
  - `frame-overshoot` (12%, absurd escalation): [take-1..take-3]: one frame, escalating the swapped element / [take-4]: the same frame pushed past sense to an absurd extreme. Real shape: Swapping a family's food for mayonnaise, then their toiletries, then the family itself.
  - `choices-to-backfire` (18%, expectation vs reality / petty vs proper): [take-1..take-3]: sensible moves toward one goal, each more committed / [take-4]: the self-defeating or petty move that 'solves' it, which is the real move from the story. Real shape: Muting notifications to focus, then hiding the phone, then deleting apps, then getting rid of the phone entirely.
- **Slot targets:** take-1 p50 18 / p90 37, gerund phrase or plain noun that sets the frame; take-2 p50 20 / p90 45, same frame as take-1, one element swapped; take-3 p50 19 / p90 44, same frame, swapped again; take-4 p50 27 / p90 62, same frame; the inverted, absurd or verbose final variant
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners hold one frame across four lines in 44/55 four-step uses. Flops break it: 5/30 are chronologies or biographies ('freshman year… sophomore year…', a saint's life story, kingdom chores); 3/30 are random sets with no shared frame (four numbers, four song titles); 3/30 are straight ladders with no turn at the end (phone battery 25%→100%). 5/30 run over 60 characters in a box without a payoff. 5/30 rely on names a casual viewer doesn't know (game levels, fandom characters, a school candidate). 2/30 are ad copy for a site or a campaign. 3/30 are sincere or educational and drop the irony. Winners' final line is the turn in 37/55 (inversion 19, absurd overshoot 7, pompous or euphemistic register 11). Only 7/55 winners end on a straight, non-ironic escalation, and those are the 'my brain at X' gags. About 6/30 flops are shaped like winners (the plants alarm ending on hiring a cactus coach, 'pull the door that says push') and probably just lacked exposure.
- **Tech memes:** All 5 tech posts have 0 upvotes. 4/5 are fixed-frame ladders of tools or code (C/C++ to a segfault, Unity to Scratch, four ways to print hello world), and 1 inverts to 'paste the CTF into ChatGPT'. The ones that work as jokes end on the lazy or broken result: the segfault and the ChatGPT shortcut. A ladder of languages with no turn is a flop shape (Python to Malbolge also flopped at 0).
- **Misreads:**
  - Writing a chronology: four events in the story's order (founded, valued, founders leave, Chapter 11) instead of four versions of one thing. Our bankruptcy meme did this; so do 5/30 flops.
  - Changing the subject at take-4 (consumer agents for three lines, then 'sell the platform'). This breaks the fixed frame the irony depends on.
  - Making take-4 the writer's opinion or a jab ('Some AI leaders maybe shouldn't talk in public') instead of the real move, crowned. It also smuggles in the banned 'maybe'.
  - Listing a company's strategic investments (recruiting, infrastructure, an acquisition) as if bigger spending were the galaxy-brain move. With no inversion, it reads as a plain timeline.
  - Long take-2 or take-3 lines (55+ chars) while real middle boxes sit at a p50 of about 20 chars. The reader has to scan four rows fast.
  - Ending on the most sensible or impressive option. The cosmic brain has to mark the dumbest, laziest or most over-dressed version; that is the irony.
  - Narrated story steps ('Tell people about grandkids…') instead of short labels in one shared grammar.
- **Test:** Do all four lines repeat one frame, changing one element each time, with the fourth line (the cosmic brain) being the most backwards, self-defeating, absurd or over-dressed variant rather than the next event or a new subject?
- **Slot problems:** The slot positions are right, but the MUSTs don't require what makes the format work: one fixed frame across all four lines (44/55 real four-step uses). take-1 says 'the plain, sensible way to handle the thing in the story' and take-2/3 say 'cleverer… grander… heading toward the real move'. That invites a sequence of different moves, which is exactly how our 4 'No' memes went wrong (a chronology, a strategy list, a subject switch, an opinion). take-4 also needs to say it is a variant of the same frame, not the next step. Proposal: Real uses keep one frame across four rows (44/55) and put the turn in row 4 (37/55). Our current MUSTs ask for different moves, and our 4 'No' memes are timelines, strategy lists or subject switches. Ids, kinds and boxes stay the same; only the MUSTs change.
- **Our examples:**
  - `fixed-frame-inversion` · An AI coding agent deletes a startup's production database during a code freeze. → take-1: Backing up the database / take-2: Backing up the backups / take-3: Testing the backups monthly / take-4: Giving the AI agent prod access
  - `register-ladder` · A tech giant announces thousands of job cuts and frames them as becoming 'AI-first'. → take-1: Layoffs / take-2: Restructuring / take-3: Rightsizing the org / take-4: Becoming AI-first
  - `frame-overshoot` · Smart fridge maker starts showing ads on fridge door screens. → take-1: Ads on your TV / take-2: Ads on your phone / take-3: Ads in your car / take-4: Ads on your fridge door
  - `fixed-frame-inversion` · Chatbots add long-term memory that keeps what users tell them across chats. → take-1: Telling a friend a secret / take-2: Writing it in a diary / take-3: Telling no one at all / take-4: Telling the chatbot with memory on
  - `choices-to-backfire` · A struggling startup pivots again and renames itself an AI agent company. → take-1: Building a product / take-2: Finding customers / take-3: Raising a bigger round / take-4: Adding 'agent' to the name
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) take-1=Get angry about Flock Safety ; take-2=Wonder how hard it is to build one ; take-3=Buy a 4K camera, let AI write the code ; take-4=Build one that only tracks police cars: One subject (responding to Flock cameras) climbs from complaining to DIY. take-4 is the real move and an inversion: point the surveillance back at the police. The lines are short imperatives in one grammar.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) take-1=Build a chatbot startup ; take-2=Get valued at over a billion dollars with no revenue ; take-3=Founders rejoin Google with its $2.7 billion lifeline ; take-4=File Chapter 11, sell some assets to Disney for $20 million: It is the company's life in order, not four versions of one thing. take-4 is just the next event, not an 'enlightened' version of take-1. take-2 and take-4 run 50+ chars.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) take-1=Build career skills ; take-2=Start a side hustle ; take-3=Grind interview challenges ; take-4=Build a useless thing, quit when it stops being fun: Fixed frame (what to do with your coding time) climbs through respectable answers, then inverts: the useless project is crowned. This is the fixed-frame-inversion pattern.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) take-1=AI doom warnings are everywhere now ; take-2=Balance the risks with the promise ; take-3=Tell people about grandkids who never lose anyone to cancer ; take-4=Some AI leaders maybe shouldn't talk in public: The four lines are a debate summary with no shared frame. take-3 narrates a speech, and take-4 is the writer's opinion with a banned hedge, not a real move.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) take-1=Catch up with a better model ; take-2=Invest heavily in recruiting ; take-3=Invest heavily in infrastructure too ; take-4=Pay $14.3 billion for Scale AI: A list of strategy steps. take-4 is real but is only a bigger spend, not an inverted or absurd version of the frame, so the cosmic brain has no irony to point at.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) take-1=Write every application yourself ; take-2=Let AI tidy your CV and cover letter ; take-3=Let AI help you apply to 200 jobs ; take-4=Redesign hiring to be AI-proof: One frame (AI in job applications) escalates in short imperatives. take-4 flips the side: the employer gives up and rebuilds the system. The escalation lands, though the final flip is milder than a true inversion.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) take-1=Try a consumer AI agent ; take-2=Run a pack of agents to sweep your calendar ; take-3=Give the agent your credit card ; take-4=Skip the app idea and sell the platform: take-1 to take-3 build a good consumer frame, then take-4 switches to a business-strategy subject. The frame breaks, so take-4 has no irony. 'Give the agent your credit card' was the stronger cosmic-brain line, sitting in the wrong row.

### This Is Where I'd Put My Trophy If I Had One (`trophy`)
Coded 69 top uses, 2 tech memes, 33 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries a standing lack well (a company famously without profit, a working assistant or a ship date), and the 'If [blocker]' ending can hold a backfire or a delay. It can't stage a reversal or an escalation, though. The news turn has to collapse into 'they still don't have X', so it only fits stories where the absence is the news.). Our memes misused it: 7 of 7.
- **Mechanism:** He proudly shows off the place where an achievement should be, then admits there isn't one. The laugh comes from the speaker confessing to lacking something they're plainly expected to have (friends, grammar, proof, good movies), and the more basic the missing thing, the harder it lands.
- **Picture:** Two panels of a cartoon dad (Fairly OddParents). Top: grinning proudly, he sweeps a hand toward an empty white pedestal under spotlights. Bottom: the same spot, his face crumpled into sobs, empty hands held out. Text goes above the pedestal in the top panel and across the bottom panel above his head.
