- **Figure:** The poster or 'me' in about 50/69. A labeled third party mocked for a famous lack in 12/69 (Disney ×3, flat-earthers, Germany, the Russia/Ukraine joke, Discord mods, 'everyone in 2021', a teacher). 7/69 are off-format with no clear speaker.
- **Voice:** First person throughout: 'This is where I'd put my X' (or 'I would', 'I put') in 43/69 standard posts, with X a short noun phrase. The ending is 'If I had one' in about 22/43 and 'If I had any/some' for plurals and mass nouns in about 19/43. 16/69 swap the ending for a reason clause ('if I could afford it', 'if X didn't exist'). Third-party versions keep the first person and put the party's name in a label above (9 cases). All caps in about 55/69. An exclamation mark or trailing '...' on the ending in about 15/69.
- **Boxes:** B1 → missing (top panel, above the empty pedestal: 'This is where I'd put my X' (code prints the frame; we write X)); B2 → punch (bottom panel, above his sobbing head: 'If I had one / any', or a reason it is missing); B1 → speaker (in 3–4 box posts B1 is a speaker label at the top ('FLAT-EARTHERS', 'MODERN DISNEY:'); we draw it as a logo bottom-left beside him: who is confessing the lack)
- **Patterns:**
  - `basic-lack-confession` (49%, self-deprecation / understatement): [missing]: a basic thing the speaker should obviously have / [punch]: If I had one (or any). Real shape: Pointing at the shelf for one's friends, then admitting there are none.
  - `third-party-roast` (13%, roast / hypocrisy): [speaker]: a known party / [missing]: the one thing that party is famous for lacking / [punch]: If I had one (or any). Real shape: A studio pointing at where its good recent movies would go, if it had made any.
  - `blocked-want` (23%, expectation vs reality): [missing]: a thing the speaker wants / [punch]: If [the real blocker named]. Real shape: The spot for a new game console, if it weren't so expensive.
  - `self-proving-lack` (4%, literalism): [missing]: the lacking skill, written in a way that proves the lack / [punch]: If I had one. Real shape: A misspelled line about where the speaker's grammar would go.
- **Slot targets:** missing p50 12 / p90 29, noun phrase only, possessed by the speaker (the X in 'This is where I'd put my X'); real B1 with the frame is p50 37, p90 54; punch p50 15 / p90 36, 'If I had one!' / 'If I had any!' / 'If' + a short reason clause; speaker p50 10 / p90 16, a name label; for us a logo
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners name something the speaker is expected to have, whose absence is the speaker's known failing: friends, life, IQ, sanity, a dad, proof, good movies (38/43 standard posts). Flops: 11/33 drop the missing-thing frame entirely and use the picture for an unrelated reaction (fridge, museum, summer break, a fire alarm). 6/33 use the frame for something merely not yet bought (a PS4, a piano, a card pull, action figures), where nobody expects you to have it. 4/33 need in-group names (ship names, fandom characters). 3/33 leave X empty or literally 'trophy'. 2/33 are walls of text. The third-party roast that flopped (a game publisher's good games, 1 upvote) has the winning shape, so exposure explains some flops.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 2 tech posts (both 2 upvotes), both self-deprecating 'finished projects… if I had any'. The obvious tech use is the third-party roast: a company pointing at the thing it is famous for not having (profit, a working assistant, a ship date).
- **Misreads:**
  - Writing X in the third person or as a clause ('proof it copied his voice'). The printed frame is 'This is where I'd put my…', so X must belong to the speaker in their own voice.
  - Leaving the speaker empty when the lack belongs to a company or person. 'My' then points at the viewer, and the joke loses its target ('billion dollar sale', 'refund plan').
  - Choosing a mild lack: ranking second on an index is a good result, so 'first place' gives nothing to sob about. Winners pick a basic, expected thing.
  - Abstract X that can't sit on a pedestal ('clean hiring year', 'refund plan'). Real X are concrete nouns: friends, proof, good movies, a play button.
  - Mocking a victim's lack: the patient's restored vision 'wider than a straw'. The format is a confession or a roast of the powerful, never a jab at the injured party.
  - A fixed 'If I had one!' after a mass or plural noun ('profit', 'proof', 'friends') reads wrong. Real posts switch to 'any' in about 19/43.
  - Using it for something the speaker simply hasn't bought yet. The 6 flops that did this had nothing embarrassing about the lack.
- **Test:** Does the top line name a concrete thing the speaker (the seated company, or the viewer) is plainly expected to have but, per the story, lacks, so that the bottom line reads as their own embarrassed confession?
- **Slot problems:** Three problems. (1) punch is fixed to 'If I had one!', but real posts use 'If I had any!' for plurals and mass nouns (about 19/43), and 16/69 end with a reason clause ('if I could afford it'), which is the variant that can carry a news blocker. A fixed 'one' breaks grammar on the card's own example 'profit'. (2) missing's MUST doesn't say X must be a concrete noun owned by the speaker in the first person, so our writer produced clauses with 'his' and abstract X. (3) speaker is optional with no rule for when it must be filled. When the lack belongs to a company it must be seated, otherwise 'my' falls to the viewer. Proposal: Real posts pick 'one' or 'any' by grammar and sometimes end on a reason, and in about 50/69 posts the speaker confesses their own lack. Our fixed punch and loose MUSTs produced third-person clauses, ownerless X and grammar breaks. The boxes are unchanged.
- **Our examples:**
  - `third-party-roast` · Apple delays its smarter, more personal Siri again. → missing: smart Siri / punch: If I had one! / speaker: [logo: apple]
  - `third-party-roast` · An AI lab reports billions in losses despite record revenue. → missing: profits / punch: If I had any! / speaker: [logo: openai]
  - `third-party-roast` · A robotaxi service launches with a safety employee riding in every car. → missing: driverless robotaxi / punch: If I had one! / speaker: [logo: tesla]
  - `blocked-want` · A new graphics card sells out in minutes to resale bots. → missing: new graphics card / punch: If bots didn't buy them all! / speaker: 
  - `blocked-want` · A phone maker's new AI features only run on its newest phones. → missing: AI features / punch: If my phone were newer! / speaker: 
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) missing=first place on the index ; speaker=[logo Meta]: The third-party roast shape is right and the lack is real, but ranking second isn't an embarrassing absence. 'on the index' adds length (p50 X is 12 chars) without bite.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) missing=refund plan: No speaker, so 'my refund plan' belongs to the viewer, who wouldn't have one. The organizer's lack isn't shown, and 'refund plan' is abstract.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) missing=billion dollar sale: The lack fits the story (valued at a billion, never sold for one), but with no speaker the joke has no owner. The startup isn't a blue-chip logo, so the card can't seat it.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) missing=proof it copied his voice: 'his' clashes with the printed 'my', the X is a clause rather than a thing, and the speaker (an actor a casual follower wouldn't know) can't be seated.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) missing=view wider than a straw: It mocks a patient's partial restored sight. That punches at a medical patient instead of a confessing speaker or a powerful party.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) missing=clean hiring year: Abstract X with no clear owner (recruiters? the viewer?), and 'clean' needs explaining. Nothing concrete sits on the pedestal.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) missing=first place ; speaker=[logo Meta]: Tighter than the first version and correctly owned by Meta, but second place is still too mild a lack to carry the sob.

### Absolute Cinema (`absolute-cinema`)
Coded 68 top uses, 0 tech memes, 21 flops. News fit: **ok** (A news turn that plays like a movie beat fits: the comeuppance (a court checks the AI's citations), the fiasco (a live demo crashing), the perfectly timed rival launch. The word swap deflates hype well (MARKETING). But it is a reaction image: the caption does all the work, the picture adds only 'masterpiece', and most news isn't scene-shaped.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** A great director's solemn, two-handed 'masterpiece' salute gets pinned on a moment that is petty, chaotic or plainly bad. The gap between auteur reverence and a school fight, a teacher's comeuppance or a botched move is the joke. Or the second word gets swapped, so the same reverence stamps the honest verdict (ABSOLUTE GARBAGE, ABSOLUTE MARKETING).
- **Picture:** A black-and-white portrait of Martin Scorsese seated in an armchair, both open hands raised high beside his head, with a solemn face. The two words sit in the dark space beside each raised hand (left and right, about a third of the way down). A caption goes across his lap and chest at the bottom.
- **Figure:** Scorsese is a reaction stand-in for whoever is reacting: the poster or a crowd in 25/68 (caption and verdict, plain reaction). He is a stamp of verdict on the thing in 33/68 (the word-swap uses, with no figure identity). In 6/68 the raised hands are read literally (hands up for police, a teacher's question, a hoodie sleeve).
- **Voice:** Swaps are one plain word in caps beside the hand (33/68; p50 about 8 chars): an everyday verdict noun or adjective (garbage, legend, banger, misclick, marketing, peak). Captions (17/68) are situation clauses: 'when…' or 'me after/when…' in 9/17, a bare noun label in 5/17, a sentence in 3/17. Captions are present tense, with no quotes and no hedges. In 21/68 'ABSOLUTE' and the second word sit in one box together.
- **Boxes:** B1 → absolute (dark space beside the left raised hand: 'ABSOLUTE' (in caption-first posts B1 holds the caption instead)); B2 → cinema-word (dark space beside the right raised hand: 'CINEMA', or the swapped verdict word (33/68)); B1 → cinema (caption across the bottom, over his lap: the moment being hailed: a 'when…' or 'me after…' clause naming one scene)
- **Patterns:**
  - `word-swap-verdict` (49%, inversion / deflation): ABSOLUTE / [cinema-word]: one plain word that replaces CINEMA with the honest verdict or the real category of the thing. Real shape: The pose stamped 'ABSOLUTE' and 'GARBAGE' instead of cinema.
  - `petty-drama-crowned` (10%, petty vs proper (mock-epic)): [cinema]: when one small, dramatic or comeuppance moment happens / ABSOLUTE CINEMA. Real shape: When a classmate finally talks back to a rude teacher, crowned as cinema.
  - `ironic-crown-of-failure` (4%, irony / understatement): [cinema]: a fiasco or self-own stated plainly / ABSOLUTE CINEMA (or a swapped verdict word). Real shape: A console asks for space, deleting a whole game frees almost nothing, and it is hailed as cinema.
  - `sincere-hype` (12%, straight praise): [cinema]: a match, film, song or personal win / ABSOLUTE CINEMA. Real shape: A football fixture name with the verdict under it.
  - `literal-hands-up` (9%, literalism): [cinema]: a situation where someone would raise both hands (surrender, innocence, no hands). Real shape: Me when police tell me to put my hands up.
- **Slot targets:** absolute p50 8 / p90 8, fixed 'ABSOLUTE'; cinema-word p50 8 / p90 15, 'CINEMA' or one plain verdict word; cinema p50 40 / p90 88, 'when…' clause or short noun label naming one moment
- **Winners vs flops:** The posts with the most upvotes are plain reactions (64 and 21 upvotes) with the subject in the title, which we can't use. Next come petty-drama captions (18, 11 upvotes), literal hands-up (17, 14) and word swaps with a common verdict word (13 for garbage, 12 for legend). Flops: 8/21 swap in an in-group word nobody outside gets (PINGAS, 8obes, FISHYBOY, fcy, EMA, DJI…), against about 3/33 swaps in the top. 4/21 are bare ABSOLUTE CINEMA with no subject. 4/21 are sincere fan praise with no drama or irony (a film anniversary, a game rated 10/10, 'this was pretty good'). 1/21 invents an absurd scenario (a smart fridge roasting you). Winners name one specific moment or one everyday verdict word; flops are generic praise or private words.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 0 tech posts. 'ABSOLUTE MARKETING' (6 upvotes) and 'CINEMER AI' (0) are the closest. The swap to a deflating word like MARKETING is the natural tech use.
- **Misreads:**
  - Hailing a launch or demo as plainly great (sincere fan praise). 4/21 flops did this, and with no gap between reverence and subject the meme is just an ad.
  - Summarising the story in the caption instead of naming one scene-like moment (a comeuppance, a twist, a fiasco).
  - Leaving the caption generic ('the new model') so the reverence has nothing specific to crown, like the 4/21 bare flops.
  - Swapping in a word only insiders know. 8/21 flops did, against about 3/33 swaps in the top.
  - Inventing a scene for the absurdity (the smart-fridge flop). The moment has to be real.
  - Placing the words on his face or hands. They sit in the dark space beside each raised hand.
- **Test:** Does the meme crown one specific, scene-like moment (a comeuppance, twist or fiasco), or stamp an honest one-word verdict in place of CINEMA, so that solemn reverence clashes with or deflates the subject instead of sincerely praising a launch?
- **Slot problems:** cinema-word is fixed to 'CINEMA', but the most common real use (33/68) swaps that word for the honest verdict (ABSOLUTE GARBAGE / MARKETING / MISCLICK). We forbid the most common use, and the one that carries a tech deflation best. The cinema MUST ('a real launch, demo, move or line… stated plainly') invites sincere launch praise, the flop shape (4/21). It should ask for one scene-like moment: a comeuppance, a twist, a fiasco or a perfectly timed rival move. Proposal: Real posts swap the second word in 33/68. Captioned posts crown a petty-dramatic moment or a fiasco, not a product. The boxes are unchanged; cinema-word becomes optionally writable, and the caption MUST names the scene-like moment.
- **Our examples:**
  - `petty-drama-crowned` · A judge fines lawyers after their AI-written brief cites cases that don't exist. → cinema: When the judge actually checks the citations / cinema-word: CINEMA
  - `ironic-crown-of-failure` · A company's smart glasses demo freezes repeatedly on stage at its own keynote. → cinema: The live demo freezing on its own stage / cinema-word: CINEMA
  - `word-swap-verdict` · A chatbot gets renamed an 'agent' with no new features. → cinema: Same chatbot, new name: agent / cinema-word: MARKETING
  - `petty-drama-crowned` · Two rival AI labs launch competing models within an hour of each other. → cinema: When both labs launch an hour apart / cinema-word: CINEMA
  - `word-swap-verdict` · A model tops a leaderboard after being tuned on that leaderboard's own tests. → cinema: Top score on the test it studied for / cinema-word: HOMEWORK

### Oprah You Get A (`oprah-you-get-a`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 28 flops. News fit: **strong** (Tech news constantly ships one change to all users at once: default-on AI features, ads, price hikes, breaches, layoffs. The format's engine (celebrating a mass handout of something unwanted) maps directly onto a forced rollout or a backfire, and the giver logo names who did it.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** Oprah's car-giveaway ecstasy gets aimed at a 'gift' nobody wants (quarantine, homework, inflation, a downvote), or at one so universal it's worthless (every book a bestseller, rainbow logos in June). The celebration jars against the gift, and 'everybody' turns one bad thing into a mass handout.
- **Picture:** Oprah in a red suit on her talk-show stage, mouth wide open in an ecstatic shout, arms flung out to the audience, a microphone in one hand. Text goes across the top (the repeated 'You get X!'), on her suit (a label for the giver) and across the bottom ('Everybody gets X!').
- **Figure:** Oprah is the giver handing something to everyone. In 43/70 posts that use the 'you get' frame, the giver is labeled in 17/43, usually an institution or force: schools ×3, teachers ×3, websites, the sun, companies, a newspaper, parents, a router, a CEO. The giver is unlabeled in 26/43 (the world, a pandemic, inflation). In 27/70 Oprah is a pure excitement reaction standing for 'me'.
- **Voice:** Within the frame (43/70): 'You get X!' repeated 2–3 times, then 'Everybody/Everyone gets X!' (about 11 everybody, 9 everyone). X is a bare noun or participle for states and mass nouns (quarantined, offended, inflation, homework, covid; 15/43) and 'a/an' plus a noun for countable things (a hurricane, a cookie, a meme, an F; about 20/43). X stays identical in every box in 41/43. Exclamation marks in about 28/43. All caps in about 58/70. Giver labels are bare nouns ending in a colon (Schools:, Math teachers:).
- **Boxes:** B1 → you-get (top left (real posts often fill the whole top with 'You get X! And you get X!'): 'You get X!'); B1 → you-get-again (top right: 'You get X!' again); B2 → giver (on her red suit (3-box posts: B2 is the giver label): who hands it out: 'SCHOOLS', 'WEBSITES', 'THE SUN'); B2 → gift (bottom (B3 in 3-box posts): 'Everybody gets X!')
- **Patterns:**
  - `unwanted-giveaway` (37%, inversion (celebrating harm)): [giver]: an institution or force / [you-get]×2: You get [bad thing]! / [gift]: Everybody gets [bad thing]!. Real shape: A school handing out stress to every student like prizes.
  - `devalued-gift` (6%, devaluation / hypocrisy): [giver]: the issuer / [gift]: an honor or perk handed to everyone, so it means nothing. Real shape: A newspaper's bestseller list where every book is a bestseller.
  - `literal-generosity` (19%, literal / wholesome): [giver]: me or a giver / [gift]: a small real gift handed out to everyone (memes, stickers, good mornings). Real shape: Forwarding every meme to every friend during lockdown.
  - `pure-excitement` (39%, reaction): [caption]: me when something good happens (no 'you get' frame). Real shape: Me realising upvoting earns points and I'm nearly at a milestone.
- **Slot targets:** you-get p50 17 / p90 25, 'You get X!' derived from gift; you-get-again p50 17 / p90 25, same as you-get; giver p50 9 / p90 16, a bare institution or company name; for us a logo; gift p50 9 / p90 17, X only: 'a/an' plus a noun, or a bare mass noun or participle, 1–3 words
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners use the 'you get' frame in 43/70 (61%), against 10/28 flops (36%). Within the frame, winners pick an unwanted or devalued X in 30/43, and the flops that use the frame mostly pick a neutral X with an unknown giver (free shoes from a named nurse, FMLA, 'a job'). 13/28 flops are pure excitement reactions about personal moments (killing a mosquito, a password working), against 27/70 in the top, and those top reactions sit lower (median about 13 upvotes, against about 33 for frame posts). 3/28 flops break the repetition by changing X mid-list (gym membership, then kale, then regret) or by repeating it far too often. Repetition is the meter: winners keep X identical in 41/43.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 1 tech post (compile errors for everyone learning a new language, 2 upvotes), which is the unwanted-giveaway shape with a tool as the giver. Mass rollouts (forced AI features, ads, price rises, breaches) are the obvious tech fit.
- **Misreads:**
  - Picking an event that hits one group once ('a cancellation') rather than a thing handed out to everyone. The repetition implies a mass rollout.
  - Explaining the joke inside X with an adjective ('unaffordable gadgets'). Real X is the bare unwanted thing (inflation, homework), and the celebration supplies the irony.
  - Drifting X away from the facts (the story says prices broadly, the meme says gadgets).
  - Leaving the giver empty when one company pushed it. Seated givers make the inversion read as a choice someone made.
  - Hanging it on an obscure story or project a casual follower doesn't know (a niche dev tool's login).
  - Using Oprah as a plain excitement reaction. It is the most common flop use (13/28).
  - Changing X between boxes or piling on repeats. Winners keep one X and two or three repeats.
- **Test:** Is the repeated X one thing that a single giver handed to everyone at once, and is it unwanted, or worthless because everyone got it, so that Oprah's celebration is ironic?
- **Slot problems:** The structure matches (repeated top line, giver on the suit, 'Everybody gets' bottom). The gift MUST ('a real launch, giveaway or default the facts show reaching everyone') leans toward literal giveaways. Real winners use an unwanted or devalued X in 30/43 frame posts, and that inversion is the joke. The MUST also lets in a bare event that hits one group once ('a cancellation'). The giver has no rule for when it must be seated. Proposal: The boxes and kinds are unchanged. The gift MUST now names the inversion that real winners use (30/43 frame posts) and rules out one-off events and explanatory adjectives. The giver becomes required when one company did the pushing.
- **Our examples:**
  - `unwanted-giveaway` · Microsoft switches Copilot on by default for every Windows user. → giver: [logo: microsoft] / gift: Copilot
  - `unwanted-giveaway` · A streaming service raises prices on every plan at once. → giver: [logo: netflix] / gift: a price hike
  - `unwanted-giveaway` · A chatbot maker starts showing ads to all free users. → giver: [logo: openai] / gift: ads
  - `devalued-gift` · Every new gadget at a trade show is labeled 'AI-powered'. → giver:  / gift: an AI sticker
  - `unwanted-giveaway` · A carrier's breach exposes call records of nearly all its customers. → giver: [logo: at&t] / gift: a data leak
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) gift=unaffordable gadgets: The unwanted-giveaway shape is right (higher prices for everyone), but 'unaffordable' spells out the irony, 'gadgets' narrows a story about everything getting pricier, and no giver is seated.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) gift=a cancellation: One event cancelled for its own attendees isn't a mass handout from a giver, and the organizer can't be seated. The repetition has nothing to multiply.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) gift=a fresh login: The story is an obscure open-source Xcode replacement; 'a fresh login' isn't a fact a casual follower would recognise and isn't a giveaway to everyone.

### Tuxedo Winnie The Pooh (`tuxedo-pooh`)
Coded 68 top uses, 6 tech memes, 33 flops. News fit: **strong** (Corporate tech language is a constant supply of dressed-up names: layoffs as reallocation, ads as sponsored suggestions, acquisitions as licensing deals, price hikes as 'now with AI'. The euphemism-launder engine exposes the double standard in one pair, and the fancy name comes straight from the story, so it stays factual.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 11.
- **Mechanism:** One thing, two names: the bottom dresses up exactly what the top says plainly. The laugh comes from seeing that the fancy name is a cover, whether a euphemism laundering something bad ('borrow permanently' for steal), a pompous paraphrase or a punny grand title. The plain top line exposes it.
- **Picture:** Two stacked panels on the left: plain Winnie the Pooh in his red shirt, half-lidded and bored in an armchair; below, the same Pooh in a tuxedo with a smug, refined smile. The right half of each panel is blank white for text: the plain name beside the top Pooh, the fancy one beside the bottom.
- **Figure:** Pooh stands for the same thing under two names in 54/68 posts. He isn't a person, he is the register: plain vs refined. In 12/68 the two panels are two different options and the tuxedo marks the 'classier' choice. In 2/68 a single caption uses both panels.
- **Voice:** Both boxes are labels, not sentences, in 51/68: nouns or gerund phrases in the same grammar top and bottom ('Steal / Borrow permanently'). First-person speech pairs in 12/68 ('I like X / I fancy…'). The top is plainer and shorter in 54/54 same-thing posts (B1 p50 13 chars, B2 p50 26): slang or blunt words up top, Latinate or formal words below (permanently, residence, subscription, distributor). Sentence case and caps are about evenly mixed. No end punctuation in about 60/68.
- **Boxes:** B1 → casual (top-right, beside plain Pooh: the plain, blunt name); B2 → fancy (bottom-right, beside tuxedo Pooh: the dressed-up name for the same thing); B3 → null (extra label (5/70 posts, e.g. 'KIDS:' header): a context label; not needed)
- **Patterns:**
  - `euphemism-launder` (25%, euphemism / hypocrisy): [casual]: the blunt, unflattering truth / [fancy]: the polite or official term that makes it sound fine. Real shape: Stealing, and in the tuxedo a phrase about borrowing forever.
  - `pompous-paraphrase` (28%, register clash): [casual]: an everyday phrase / [fancy]: the same thing in absurdly formal words, with one sly exact detail. Real shape: A snake in a boot, restated as a serpent taking up unwelcome residence in footwear.
  - `grand-rename` (26%, wordplay / rebrand): [casual]: the ordinary name / [fancy]: a grand, punny or branded title for the same thing. Real shape: October, renamed with a seasonal nickname.
  - `classier-option` (18%, preference): [casual]: the ordinary option / [fancy]: a different option the poster finds more refined. Real shape: Kids drinking grape juice from a normal glass vs from a wine glass.
- **Slot targets:** casual p50 13 / p90 48, short blunt noun or gerund phrase (1–4 words typical); fancy p50 26 / p90 74, the formal, euphemistic or branded name; same grammar as casual
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners rename one thing in 54/68 (79%). Flops compare two different things in 16/33 (48%): homework vs the teacher collecting it, working from home vs running the company, digital vs physical games. 6/33 flops are long verbose paraphrases with no sly detail (three Minecraft descriptions, a 'relatable meme' definition), against winners whose verbose lines slip in one cutting exact detail. 5/33 rely on in-group names (a city pun, a streamer, a game mode). 2/33 are flat synonyms with no register gap (excuse me / pardon me). 4 of the top 5 are same-thing pairs (the exception is an ads comparison), and 3 of the top 8 are euphemisms for something bad (stealing, insulting, drowning).
- **Tech memes:** 6 tech posts, all at 10 upvotes or fewer. 3/6 are same-thing renames, two of them inverted so the plain name sits in the tuxedo (Machine Learning vs 'brute force data science'; astrology vs 'science' as a jab). 1 is a pun (skill issue vs SKILL.md issue). 2/6 compare two different things (two square-root methods; one error vs a different error), the flop shape. The euphemism-launder pattern is the natural fit for corporate tech language.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting two different facts from the story in the panels ('200 new jobs' / 'an applied AI lab'). The format needs one thing under two names. 16/33 flops make this mistake.
  - Writing the fancy line as a narrated or attributed label ('Meta: frontier capabilities') instead of the name itself.
  - A top line with a hedge or an attribution in it ('Second place, by Meta's own count'). The plain line is the blunt truth, said flatly.
  - Making the bottom a long description instead of a name (6/33 flops). A verbose bottom only works with one sly exact detail.
  - A top that is as long or as fancy as the bottom, so the register gap disappears. Real tops are about half the length (p50 13 vs 26).
  - Using a fancy name the story doesn't use. Our fancy line must be the real product name, term or rebrand, never an invented quote.
- **Test:** Do both panels name exactly the same thing, with the top saying it bluntly and the bottom giving the dressed-up (official, euphemistic or grand) name the story uses?
- **Our examples:**
  - `euphemism-launder` · A chatbot begins placing paid product links inside its answers. → casual: Ads in your answers / fancy: Sponsored suggestions
  - `euphemism-launder` · A big tech firm hires a startup's founders and licenses its tech instead of buying it. → casual: Buying the startup / fancy: A licensing deal
  - `euphemism-launder` · A software company raises its subscription price and bundles in AI features. → casual: A price hike / fancy: Now with AI
  - `euphemism-launder` · A tech giant cuts thousands of jobs, citing a shift of resources toward AI. → casual: Layoffs / fancy: Reallocating toward AI
  - `grand-rename` · Google renames its Bard chatbot to Gemini. → casual: Bard / fancy: Gemini
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) casual=Rules you can choose to follow ; fancy=A light-touch approach: Euphemism-launder: the policy term and its blunt meaning, one thing under two names.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) casual=Watermelon ; fancy=Muse Wonder: Grand-rename: if Watermelon was the model's codename, it's the same thing plain vs branded. It depends on the reader knowing the codename, which the caption doesn't supply.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) casual=Second place, by Meta's own count ; fancy=Meta: frontier capabilities: The euphemism idea is right (second place dressed as frontier), but the top carries an attribution and the bottom is an attributed label, not a name.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) casual=200 new jobs at its Dublin office ; fancy=An applied AI lab: Two different facts from the story, not one thing under two names. No euphemism or register gap.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) casual=Chip seller funds its own buyers ; fancy=Circular financing: Textbook euphemism-launder: the blunt mechanism above the finance term the story uses for it.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) casual=Contract talks fell apart ; fancy=A supply chain risk: The official designation dresses up a contract dispute. Euphemism-launder, provided the story ties the label to the failed talks.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) casual=Rules you can choose to follow ; fancy=A light-touch approach: Same as the graded v2 meme.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) casual=Building useless things for fun ; fancy=Recreational programming: Grand-rename: the plain activity and its dignified term. The register gap is there but mild; there's no euphemism bite.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) casual=Blue check, chatbot, coding tool ; fancy=The X Subscription: Same thing (the bundle) plain vs branded. The plain list is a little long, but the rename reads.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) casual=Train, watch it fail, collect more ; fancy=Iterating on their own tasks: Euphemism-launder: failure-driven training dressed up as iteration.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) casual=Fans copy old games with AI ; fancy=Xbox Labs: Corporate branding of an existing fan practice: grand-rename with a launder edge.

### Megamind peeking (`megamind-peeking`)
Coded 70 top uses, 6 tech memes, 34 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries one turn well: a party gloating over a rival's missing feature, or a company nudging users toward its paid tier. It can't carry a reversal or escalation; it's a single beat, and it dies if X needs explaining.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 2.
- **Mechanism:** 'No X?' is a pitying, nosy question that already knows the answer: it turns someone's lack into a public humiliation, and the funniest version is when the asker is the party that profits from the lack (Spotify asking 'No Premium?'). The joke lands only when X is something everyone instantly knows the target lacks or the asker is pushing.
- **Picture:** Square close-up of Megamind's huge blue head leaning down into the frame from above, eyebrows raised, green eyes wide and sideways, lips pursed: a smug, prying, faux-pitying peek. His face fills everything below about a third of the height; the only free space is the band over his forehead at the top.
- **Figure:** Megamind is the one asking or leaning in. In the 46/70 'No X?' uses he is: a smug taunter at a third party who lacks X (UK, Germany, 'you') in 13/46; an unlabeled bare taunt where the title supplies the target in 16/46; a pushy party that wants or sells X (Spotify, mobile games, 'Companies on July 1st', a Karen, fruit beggars) in 9/46; the poster discovering an absence (fridge, homework, Zoom) in 8/46. The other 24/70 use him as someone peering in (kid under the stall, ring cam, mom checking search history, 8/70), a 'Got X?' variant (3/70), or off-format (13/70).
- **Voice:** The punch line is a bare noun question 'No X?' in 46/70 (X a noun phrase, never a sentence), 3/70 swap it for 'Got X?'. Two-box uses open with a short label or 'When…' clause naming who asks or where: labels with a colon or 'be like' in 9/30 labeled uses. All caps in 63/70. Question mark in 41/46 'No X?' lines, doubled or tripled for emphasis in 6. No hedges, no full sentences in the punch box.
- **Boxes:** B1 → null (top band over his forehead (first line): in 2-box uses: who is asking or the situation ('Spotify every 0.5 seconds', 'UK', 'When you open the fridge at midnight'); in 1-box uses: the whole 'No X?' line); B2 → missing (below B1, usually printed at the bottom over his chin in Imgflip; for us the lower part of the top band: 'No X?' with X a single famous thing (Queen, Premium, GTA 6, snacks))
- **Patterns:**
  - `taunt-the-lacker` (19%, smug pity, public humiliation): [asker label: the party that has it, or 'X to Y'] / No [the famous thing the target visibly lacks]?. Real shape: One country labeled, then 'No queen?' aimed at a former colony.
  - `bare-no-x` (23%, understatement): No [one well-known thing]? (single box; the title or common knowledge names the target). Real shape: A single 'No donuts?' over the face, title explaining who.
  - `pusher-asks` (13%, hypocrisy / pushy upsell): [company or party that profits from X, often with a frequency: 'every 5 seconds'] / No [its own product or upsell]?. Real shape: A music app labeled as nagging constantly, asking if you have no paid plan.
  - `discover-the-absence` (11%, expectation vs reality): [When you open/check the thing] / No [the thing you expected to find]?. Real shape: Opening the fridge at midnight and finding no snacks.
  - `peeking-in` (11%, intrusion): [who is prying] + [what they are peeking at], no 'No X?' line. Real shape: A kid peering under the bathroom stall door.
- **Slot targets:** missing p50 7 / p90 16, noun phrase only, the X in 'No X?' (Queen, Premium, GTA 6, leftover pizza); asker p50 17 / p90 30, label naming who leans in, often with a frequency or 'X to Y' ('Spotify every 0.5 seconds', 'America to Germany')
- **Winners vs flops:** Bare 'No X?' is both the biggest winner form and the biggest flop form: 16/46 winning 'No X?' uses are bare vs 17/18 flop 'No X?' uses (94%). Winners with a bare line pick an X everyone knows the target lacks (towers, Queen, valentine); flops pick niche or insult X ('No optical drive?', 'No premonition?', 'No Stark Raving Dad?', 'No maturity?', 'No chromosome?') that nobody can attach to a target (13/17). Labeled winners (30/46) name who is asking, which is what makes the pity readable. The other big flop group (16/34) drops the 'No X?' line entirely for long first-person narratives ('Me trying to borrow money…').
- **Tech memes:** Few: 6 tech-ish uses. The ones that score are pusher-asks with a platform as the asker ('Spotify every 0.5 seconds / No Premium?' 6, 'Mobile games be like / No ads?' 5) or a removed feature ('no like button and dislike button!?' 10). Tagged tech: 'No open source?' 6; a Java taunt that isn't 'No X?' scores 0; 'This product could have been / A prompt.' scores 0.
- **Misreads:**
  - No asker. With only 'No X?' over the face, the viewer can't tell who is taunting whom; our NZ meme ('No binding AI rules?') never says who is asking NZ, so it reads as a neutral question.
  - X is a cap, a number or a qualified phrase ('No more than 0.1% equity?') instead of one famous thing; that's a lowball (Pawn Stars) joke, not a lack.
  - X is niche or technical (the flops' 'optical drive', 'premonition'): the question only works if everyone knows the target lacks it.
  - Using it as a neutral 'they don't have X' report. The tone is smug pity or a pushy upsell; the asker should gain from or gloat over the lack.
  - Long narrative captions with no 'No X?' line (16/34 flops).
- **Test:** Can you tell who is asking 'No X?', and is X a single well-known thing the target clearly lacks (or that the asker is selling), so the line reads as a smug taunt or pushy nudge rather than a neutral question?
- **Slot problems:** One slot, 'missing', covers only the punch box. 30/46 winning 'No X?' uses also name who is asking (a country, a company, 'Companies on July 1st'), and the strongest news shape (pusher-asks: the company that profits asks 'No Premium?') needs that label. Without it our memes leave the viewer guessing who is gloating. The MUST also allows any 'product, feature or perk', which lets in qualified or numeric phrases; real X is one famous noun. Proposal: Adds the asker label that 30/46 winning uses carry and splits the existing top band (face box starts at y 0.27, so both stay off the face). It lets the writer build taunt-the-lacker and pusher-asks, the two shapes that carry a news turn.
- **Our examples:**
  - `pusher-asks` · A chatbot shows free users an upgrade prompt each time they hit the message limit. → asker: [logo: openai] / missing: Plus
  - `taunt-the-lacker` · A rival phone ships live call translation months before the iPhone gets it. → asker: [logo: samsung] / missing: live translate
  - `discover-the-absence` · A social app update quietly removes the chronological feed. → asker: Opening the app after the update / missing: chronological feed
  - `pusher-asks` · A cloud storage service keeps nudging users to upgrade as free photo storage fills up. → asker: [logo: google] / missing: Google One
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) missing=binding AI rules: X is a real lack in the story, but no one is asking: without an asker label ('EU to NZ:'), it's a flat observation, not a taunt, and 'binding' is a policy word.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) missing=more than 0.1% equity: 'No more than 0.1% equity?' is a cap, not a missing thing, and it reads as the candidate asking politely. The joke here is a lowball offer, which is Pawn Stars' job.

### Pawn Stars Best I Can Do (`pawn-stars`)
Coded 70 top uses, 7 tech memes, 23 flops. News fit: **strong** (Stingy responses are a constant news turn: tiny compensation for an outage, a token fix after a backlash, a small free tier, a settlement that is a rounding error. The ask comes from users or regulators, the offer is a real figure or measure from the story, and the gap does the work.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** Someone asks for something big, deserved or obvious, and the party with the power answers with a tiny token or a cheap, wrong substitute delivered as if it were a generous deal. The laugh is the gap between the ask and the offer, plus the haggler's straight face.
- **Picture:** Landscape still from Pawn Stars: Rick Harrison (left, bald, black polo) leans on the counter with a skeptical, unimpressed squint; his son Corey (right) looks down at the item. Real uses put the request or setup across the top and 'Best I can do is…' across the bottom; Rick is the one making the offer.
- **Figure:** Rick is the party that holds the power to give and gives a pathetic amount: schools, teachers, nurses or parents in 15/70; companies, platforms, governments or politicians in 16/70; the poster's own body, brain, device or bank account in 14/70; God, fate, reality or the weather in 4/70; 'me' or the poster in 8/70; unlabeled in 13/70.
- **Voice:** The ask is a quoted first-person line or a stage direction: 'Me: …' / 'Student: …' in 31/70, an asterisk action ('*breaks every bone*') in 6/70, a plain setup clause in the rest. The responder is a bare label with a colon in 40/70. The offer opens 'Best I can do is' (or 'Best we can do is' for a group, 6/70) followed by a short noun phrase, often with a number (96 yrs, 5 FPS, 480p, 80%, 50 gold, 37 cents: 12/70). All caps in 50/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → null (top band over both men: the request, need or effort ('Me: I want Christmas shows on Christmas Day', 'Kid breaks every bone'); 52/70 have one. We have no slot for it.); B2 → lowballer (a label before the offer, often 'Schools:' or 'My laptop' (in 3-box uses it is its own box): who answers: the institution, platform, body part or person with the power); B3 → offer (bottom band: 'Best I can do is [tiny token or cheap substitute]')
- **Patterns:**
  - `huge-ask-tiny-token` (39%, scale mismatch / understatement): [ask: a huge need, injury or effort, stated concretely] / [lowballer] / Best I can do is [a tiny token of the right kind: ice, 50 gold, 5 FPS, 480p]. Real shape: Someone hands in thousands of arcade tickets after hours of play; the cashier offers a plastic toy.
  - `ask-gets-cheap-substitute` (46%, bait and switch, petty vs proper): [ask: 'I want X'] / [lowballer] / Best I can do is [a cheaper, worse or self-serving thing that is not X: sequels, Percy Jackson, a bank]. Real shape: Fans ask a city for a cool new store; the city opens another bank.
  - `offer-only` (16%, deadpan refusal): Best I can do is [token] (no ask; title or reply context supplies it). Real shape: A lone 'best I can do is put you on mute'.
- **Slot targets:** ask p50 34 / p90 60, first-person request or effort with a speaker label ('Me: can I…?', 'Users: we want…'), concrete; lowballer p50 10 / p90 25, logo, or a bare label with colon; offer p50 12 / p90 30, noun phrase after 'Best I can do is', often with a small number or a cheap named substitute
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners have an ask the offer can be measured against (52/70 have one; 59/70 if the title supplies it). Flops: no ask, so the offer floats (7/23: 'Best I can do is a hug', 'absolutely none of that'); niche or private references (5/23: Colligative Properties, Magikarp); an offer that is bigger, equal or not a downgrade (4/23: a bucket of espresso for a small coffee, 'become an editor'); and correctly shaped but generic or repeated jokes (7/23, e.g. a rerun of the Epstein-files winner). The winners' offers are specific and tiny in a recognizable way (ice pack, 480p, one chip and a lot of air).
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts, but 7/70 top uses are tech: a platform or device is the stingy responder. WhatsApp web asking you to open the phone app (82), autocorrect (27), YouTube's temporary demonetization (21), bank cameras at 480p (14), a laptop at 5 FPS (10), Roblox chat filters to '####' (9), Twitter cancelling you (8). Both main patterns work: huge-ask-tiny-token for hardware and money, cheap-substitute for platform policy.
- **Misreads:**
  - No ask. Our card has only a logo and the offer, so 'Best I can do is $5' has nothing to be small next to; 52/70 real uses state the ask.
  - Requiring the offer to be a literal price or payout. Real offers are any token or cheap substitute (ice pack, sequels, a bank); price-only fills miss 32/70 of uses.
  - An offer that isn't a downgrade (generous terms, or a neutral fact): the flops that overdeliver score 0 (4/23).
  - A vague offer ('limited support', 'some changes'). Winners name the exact tiny thing, often with a number (12/70).
  - Rick as a sympathetic party. He is the one with power who hands out crumbs; casting the victim as Rick flips the joke.
- **Test:** Does the meme show what was asked or needed, and is the 'Best I can do is…' offer clearly a tiny token or cheaper substitute of it, so the gap between ask and offer is obvious?
- **Slot problems:** The card has no slot for the ask, which 52/70 real uses put in the top box and which carries half the joke. The offer MUST asks for 'a price, a payout, a share', which narrows the format to money and skips the cheap-substitute pattern (32/70). The lowballer logo on Rick's shirt is fine and matches the 'Schools:' label role. Proposal: Adds the top-box ask that 52/70 real uses carry, so the offer has something to be measured against, and widens the offer from prices to the substitutes 32/70 uses run on. The ask box sits above Rick's face (face box starts at y 0.117).
- **Our examples:**
  - `huge-ask-tiny-token` · A phone carrier compensates customers for a day-long network outage with a small bill credit. → ask: [viewer AS-04] Me: no signal for a whole workday / lowballer: [logo: att] / offer: a $5 credit
  - `ask-gets-cheap-substitute` · Users asked to turn off AI answers in search; the engine added a separate 'Web' results tab instead. → ask: Users: let us turn off AI answers / lowballer: [logo: google] / offer: a 'Web' tab
  - `huge-ask-tiny-token` · Free cloud storage on a phone stays at 5GB while photo libraries keep growing. → ask: Me: 40,000 photos of my dog / lowballer: [logo: apple] / offer: 5GB
  - `ask-gets-cheap-substitute` · A streaming service answers price complaints with a cheaper plan that adds ads. → ask: Me: can it cost less? / lowballer: [logo: netflix] / offer: the same shows, now with ads

### This Is Fine (`this-is-fine`)
Coded 70 top uses, 6 tech memes, 32 flops. News fit: **strong** (Denial in the face of a growing problem is a frequent news turn: an outage that keeps going, a backlash a company ignores, a regulator that sits on its hands, users living with a broken update. The fire is a fact, the sitter is the player, and the printed 'This is fine' supplies the irony.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** The dog calmly carries on inside a disaster that is either his own doing or his job to fix, and the gap between the visible fire and his 'this is fine' is the joke. It works when the fire is specific and piling up (76 missing assignments, storage 99% full) and the sitter is the one who should be panicking.
- **Picture:** Two-panel cartoon: on the left a dog in a bowler hat sits at a table with a coffee mug while the room burns and smoke fills the ceiling; on the right a close-up of the dog, smiling, saying 'THIS IS FINE.' (printed). Real captions sit over the smoke at the top (the fire) and on or under the dog (who is sitting there).
- **Figure:** The dog is the poster/'me' in 45/70 (explicit 'Me' in 17, implied in 28); a third party or group in 19/70 (teachers, schools, Karens, America, Switzerland, a bus driver, a dad, gamers); an object in 2/70 ('My argument', 'my brain'); nobody, the fire is literal, in 4/70.
- **Voice:** One-box captions (35/70) are 'Me when…', 'When you…' or 'Me + gerund' clauses in 26/35. Two-box uses (35/70) are a noun-phrase fire label plus a one- or two-word sitter label ('Me', 'Teachers', 'Karens') in 29/35. Digits appear in 15/70 captions (76 missing assignments, 99% full, 17 views). Sentence or lower case in 69/70; end punctuation in 1/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → fire (top of the left panel, over the smoke (in 1-box uses the whole caption runs across the top): the mess: a specific, often numbered, pile-up or chaos ('76 missing assignments', 'Storage is 99% full'), or a 'Me when…' clause that names both fire and sitter); B2 → sitter (on or below the dog in the left panel: who sits calmly in it: 'Me' (17/35 two-box uses), or a responsible party ('Teachers', 'The bus driver', 'America'))
- **Patterns:**
  - `ignoring-my-own-pileup` (37%, denial, understatement): [fire: a specific, counted mess the sitter made or let grow] / [sitter: me, or the party that made it]. Real shape: A count of missing school assignments over the burning room, 'me' on the dog.
  - `enduring-others-chaos` (31%, resignation): [fire: chaos other people cause around the sitter] / [sitter: the one stuck in it, resigned]. Real shape: Kids fighting in the aisles of a bus; the driver on the dog.
  - `responsible-party-shrugs` (9%, hypocrisy / negligence): [fire: a problem that is their job] / [sitter: the institution or group that should act and doesn't]. Real shape: People being bullied over the flames; teachers on the dog.
  - `literal-fire` (21%, literalism / wordplay): [a real fire, heat or fire drill, often with a pun on the instruction]. Real shape: A fire drill where the teacher says 'stay out' and a kid named Out sits there.
- **Slot targets:** fire p50 22 / p90 50, noun phrase naming a specific, piling-up mess, ideally with a number ('Storage 99% full'); sitter p50 3 / p90 20, bare label: 'Me', or a logo or group name
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners name a concrete mess that keeps growing and that the sitter owns or should fix: procrastination pile-ups alone are 14/70. Flops: miscast scenes where nothing is burning or the dog isn't in denial (13/32, e.g. 'NASA found an existential threat / I will sit here and wait'); vague one-liners with no specific mess (7/32: 'It's Monday', 'fine'); niche references (5/32); science puns (2/32); ad copy (2/32: a product 'TradeOff' and a data-integration pitch). 3/32 flops put a company on the dog with a vague fire ('Losing money / Disney', 'The pain Wumpus is going through / Discord rn'): a logo alone doesn't carry it; the fire must be specific.
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts in the top list, but 4/70 top uses are tech and all are me-in-my-own-mess: a code bug from a missing '>' (98), 'Storage is 99% full / Me' (74), Roblox players after another bad update (208), 24 days on a video for 17 views (26). Tech flops: 'Quantum AI saw the truth' (1, no fire), a data-integration pitch (0), a product ad (0).
- **Misreads:**
  - A fire that is a single past incident ('Claude filed a false tip in testing') rather than an ongoing, growing mess the sitter is still sitting in.
  - Softening the fire with qualifiers ('in testing', 'some users'): the room has to be visibly burning.
  - A sitter who actually responded. If the facts show the player acted (paused the product, cut access), putting them on the dog misstates the story.
  - Company on the dog with a vague fire ('Losing money / Disney' scored 0): the fire must be a concrete, counted problem.
  - Using it for real harm to people: banned by our card and absent from winners (0/70 are about victims, except the schools-and-bullies hypocrisy jokes, which aim at the institution).
- **Test:** Is the fire a specific, ongoing mess, and is the sitter the one who caused it or should fix it, shown carrying on calmly per the facts?
- **Slot problems:** Boxes and ids match the two-box form (35/70). The card calls 'sitter' the punch, but in real uses the punch is the fire (a specific, counted mess) and the sitter is usually just 'Me' or a name. The fire MUST allows any 'problem in the story'; real fires are ongoing and piling up, and the sitter must be the one who made it or should fix it and is visibly carrying on per the facts. Proposal: Same boxes; the MUST text moves the punch to the fire and requires an ongoing, specific mess and a sitter who is actually carrying on, which is what separates winners from the 13/32 miscast flops.
- **Our examples:**
  - `ignoring-my-own-pileup` · A cloud provider's region goes down for hours, taking many popular apps with it. → fire: Half the internet down since 3am / sitter: [logo: amazon]
  - `responsible-party-shrugs` · A social network floods feeds with AI-generated posts while rolling out more AI posting tools. → fire: AI slop in every feed / sitter: [logo: meta]
  - `ignoring-my-own-pileup` · A phone update installs a large on-device AI model and many users run out of storage. → fire: Storage 98% full after the update / sitter: [viewer AS-18] Me
  - `enduring-others-chaos` · Workplaces add a new AI tool every month and staff juggle half a dozen assistants. → fire: 6 AI tools, 0 training / sitter: [viewer AS-08] Me
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) fire=Claude filed a false tip in testing ; sitter=[logo Anthropic]: Company sits in its own mess (the right shape), but 'in testing' defuses the fire and a single past event isn't a burning room. It also implies Anthropic shrugged, which the story (a reported test result) does not clearly show.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) fire=Police: two-month delay unacceptable ; sitter=[logo Anthropic]: A quoted complaint as the fire reads as pressure building around a calm company, which is the responsible-party-shrugs shape. But the fire is someone's speech rather than the mess itself, and the story's headline is that Anthropic did act (cut internet access), so the calm sitter is a stretch.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) fire=Agent sent police a false tip ; sitter=[logo Anthropic]: A concrete, embarrassing mess made by the sitter's own product with the company on the dog: ignoring-my-own-pileup with a company as 'me'. Stronger with a sense of scale or ongoing-ness.

### I Bet He's Thinking About Other Women (`thinking-about`)
Coded 70 top uses, 3 tech memes, 31 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries one news turn well, fear versus mundane reality: people dread what an AI or company is up to while the facts show it fumbling something trivial. It can't carry reversals, backfires or double standards, and its real engine (unrelated shower thoughts) has no news hook, so most stories won't fit.). Our memes misused it: 5 of 5.
- **Mechanism:** She assumes the dramatic, threatening thing; he is actually lost in a harmless, half-profound shower thought that has nothing to do with her. The joke is the gap in stakes, and his thought has to be funny on its own: 57/70 are standalone shower thoughts.
- **Picture:** Stock photo of a couple in bed, backs to each other: the woman on the left, arms crossed, glances back at him suspiciously; the man on the right hugs his pillow and stares into space, lost in thought. Text sits over the blanket under each of them: her suspicion on the left, his actual thought on the right.
- **Figure:** She is the suspicious partner and he is an oblivious man in 66/70; the man is never a company or group in the top 70 (0/70), and only 2/70 label them 'Her:' / 'Him:'. The roles are fixed; the text inside them changes.
- **Voice:** Her box is the canonical line in 66/70, word for word or nearly ('I bet he's thinking about other women', 'He's probably thinking about other girls'), sentence case, no end punctuation in most. His box is a question in 42/70 ('If…, is…?', 'Why…?', 'Do…?', 'Are…?') and a flat declarative in 15/70; first person only in 4/70. End punctuation in 40/70, almost all question marks. No all-caps posts (0/70).
- **Boxes:** B1 → worry (over the blanket under the woman (left): the fixed line 'I bet he's thinking about other women' or a close variant (66/70)); B2 → thought (over the blanket under the man (right): his unrelated shower thought: a paradox, a pedantic question, a 'wait, what?' observation)
- **Patterns:**
  - `fixed-worry-shower-thought` (60%, deflation / absurd specificity): [worry: the fixed suspicion line] / [thought: an unrelated paradox or pseudo-profound question, 'If X, then is Y?' or 'Why are X called Y?']. Real shape: Her fixed suspicion; him wondering whether a fire truck should be called a water truck.
  - `fixed-worry-odd-fact` (21%, deflation, literalism): [worry: the fixed suspicion line] / [thought: a weird true-ish fact stated flatly, as if it just hit him]. Real shape: Her suspicion; him musing that a pasta dish is really a cake.
  - `meta-scene` (9%, meta / recursion): [worry: the fixed line] / [thought: something about the meme itself: why they're in bed at midday, why she thinks he's thinking about other women]. Real shape: Him wondering why they are in bed in broad daylight.
  - `worry-variant` (6%, inversion): [worry: the suspicion line bent to a new subject] / [thought: a twist or confirmation]. Real shape: She bets he's thinking about his psychic powers; he thinks 'I am.'
- **Slot targets:** worry p50 37 / p90 45, 'I bet [it's / they're] [the dramatic suspicion]', the fixed line bent to the story; thought p50 54 / p90 85, a question ('If X, is Y?', 'Why is X called Y?') or a flat odd observation, unrelated to her suspicion
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners keep her line fixed and give him a thought that is funny alone and unrelated to her (57/70). Flops: broken or misplaced format, the fixed line in his box or nowhere (9/31); weak or dark shower thoughts that aren't paradoxes (7/31); thoughts that are personal and related or confirm her worry ('Samantha looked so pretty today', 'I want another woman': 5/31); niche references (Inter Miami, Arcane, deltarune: 5/31); edgy or offensive (3/31); reruns of famous shower thoughts (ketchup smoothie, 2/31).
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 3 tech-tagged posts, best 4 upvotes ('Will coconut.png fix my code?', a developer in-joke). None in the top 70.
- **Misreads:**
  - His thought confirms her worry ('Over half of us plan AI screening' under 'I bet they're all using AI on us'). Confirming kills the joke; the 2 flops that do it score 0 and 1.
  - His thought is a business line, a quote or a stat from the story ('Leave the consumer ideas to other founders', 'Reading this answer twice. 12 to 14 seconds.'): not a harmless, absurd preoccupation.
  - Her line isn't a suspicion at all ('Some AI leaders shouldn't talk in public'). The left box is always a bet about what he is secretly up to.
  - Treating both boxes as 'two sides of the same situation'. Our card says so, but in 57/70 the thought is about something unrelated; the stakes drop, not the topic.
  - Capping his thought at 40 characters. Real thoughts run p50 54, p90 85; squeezing them removes the 'If X, then Y?' setup.
- **Test:** Is the left box a dramatic suspicion and the right box a harmless, trivial or absurd preoccupation that lowers the stakes without confirming the suspicion?
- **Slot problems:** The card says both slots are 'one shared situation', with his thought 'smaller, stranger or pettier from the facts'. In 57/70 real uses his thought is unrelated to her worry and is an absurd shower thought, and it never confirms or answers her. The card also gives no way to say who the man is in a news meme, so our memes leave 'they' unnamed, and the 40-character cap is below the real p50 of 54. Proposal: Adds a logo for the man (on his shirt, clear of his face box at x 0.68) so the viewer knows whose mind we are reading, and rewrites the thought MUST from 'the same situation' to a stakes drop that never confirms the worry, which is how 57/70 real uses work.
- **Our examples:**
  - `fixed-worry-shower-thought` · A viral test shows a frontier chatbot still miscounting the letters in a simple word. → worry: I bet it's planning to replace us all / thinker: [logo: openai] / thought: How many r's are in 'strawberry'? Two. Final answer.
  - `fixed-worry-odd-fact` · A smart speaker maker says most requests to its assistant are timers, weather and music. → worry: [viewer AS-09] I bet it's listening to everything I say / thinker: [logo: amazon] / thought: Why does everyone only want a 10-minute timer?
  - `fixed-worry-shower-thought` · Researchers warn models trained on AI-written web text get worse over generations. → worry: I bet it's reading all my DMs / thinker: [logo: google] / thought: If an AI learns from AI posts, is it just copying its own homework?
  - `fixed-worry-odd-fact` · A humanoid robot demo shows it taking minutes to fold a single towel. → worry: I bet it's plotting to take over the house / thinker: [logo: tesla] / thought: Is this a towel or a very flat shirt?
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) worry=I bet they're grinding interview challenges ; thought=A useless project. 2-4 hours. Just for fun.: Her line is a suspicion and his thought is smaller and sillier, so the stakes drop. The thought is a status update, not a musing, and the worry isn't dramatic. It works as the gentle version.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) worry=I bet it gets to know me and uses my credit card ; thought=Leave the consumer ideas to other founders: A good dramatic worry, but his thought is a CEO's strategy line, not a harmless absurd preoccupation, and it is unclear who 'he' is.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) worry=I bet they send my spending questions to a server ; thought=Reading this answer twice. 12 to 14 seconds.: The worry is right, but the thought is a cryptic latency stat that no viewer can read as what the app is 'really thinking'.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) worry=Some AI leaders shouldn't talk in public ; thought=We weren't super good with communities: Her box is an opinion, not a suspicion about him, and his box is a corporate admission on the same topic: neither box plays its role.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) worry=I bet they're all using AI on us ; thought=Over half of us plan AI screening: His thought confirms her worry with a stat. The format needs him preoccupied with something trivial, not admitting she's right.

### Bell Curve (`bell-curve`)
Coded 70 top uses, 11 tech memes, 31 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries one news turn: after a lot of complication, the plain answer won (a simple rule replaces a complex one, the default option beats the fancy one, scale beats cleverness). It can't carry backfires or double standards, and it turns into an opinion piece when the 'right' blunt line is contested.). Our memes misused it: 4 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** The dumbest and the wisest reach the same blunt conclusion, and the middle is mocked for the effort it spends resisting it. The joke needs the blunt line to sound naive at first and turn out right, and the middle to sound sophisticated but miss it.
- **Picture:** A blue IQ bell curve with a score axis and percentages. A dim, smiling brainlet sits at the low end (left), a crying, sweating midwit with glasses peeks over the peak (top middle), and a calm hooded monk sits at the high end (right). Text goes over the midwit at the top and above each end figure.
- **Figure:** The three figures are viewpoints, not people we know: in 47/70 horseshoe uses the low and high ends say the same blunt line and the midwit objects. The midwit stands for the confident, conventional or 'well-actually' view (critics, pedants, enthusiasts); the ends stand for the poster's own blunt view in about 40/47. 10/70 instead label the three positions with groups or eras ('iPad kids', 'Memes in 2012'), and 11/70 use three different takes.
- **Voice:** Both ends are short blunt declaratives in the present tense (p50 about 22 characters) and repeat each other word for word in 47/70. The middle is a protest: it opens with 'No', 'Nooo' or 'Actually' in 13/47 horseshoes and ends in '!' in about 24/47; it contradicts the ends in 37/47 and gives an overcomplicated route to the same end in 10/47. All caps in 37/70. End punctuation is rare in the end lines.
- **Boxes:** B1 → overthought (top middle, over the crying midwit: the objecting or overcomplicated take, often opening 'Nooo' or 'Actually' (in 1- and 2-box posts it is concatenated with one or both end lines)); B2 → simple (above the brainlet (left): the blunt line); B3 → expert (above the hooded monk (right): the same blunt line, word for word (in 47/70 posts))
- **Patterns:**
  - `simple-practice-wins` (19%, horseshoe: effort vs plain truth): [simple]: the plain, almost lazy practice / [overthought]: 'Nooo, you need X, Y and Z' piling on method / [expert]: the same plain practice. Real shape: Fitness: the middle lists diets, trackers and protocols; both ends say eat well, move, sleep.
  - `taste-vindicated` (27%, horseshoe: snobbery deflated): [simple]: a plain liking or verdict ('X is good') / [overthought]: the snob's objection ('Nooo, X is cringe / not real Y') / [expert]: the same plain liking. Real shape: A kids' cartoon called cringe by the middle; both ends just say they like it.
  - `pedant-defeated` (21%, horseshoe: pedantry backfires, wordplay): [simple]: the naive or literal answer / [overthought]: the pedantic correction / [expert]: the naive answer again, now right for a deeper reason. Real shape: '1+1=10' at both ends while the middle insists on 2.
  - `labeled-positions` (14%, ranking): [three group or era labels on the three positions]. Real shape: Games from three different years placed on the curve.
- **Slot targets:** simple p50 22 / p90 40, short blunt declarative or imperative ('Just write simple code', 'Drink water'); overthought p50 33 / p90 100, protest opening 'Nooo' or 'Actually', a contradiction with a pile of qualifiers or methods, often ending in '!'
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners' blunt line is something most people already feel or do (game music is music, drink water, use the default browser), so the middle's fuss is the target (taste-vindicated and simple-practice-wins: 32/47 horseshoes). Flops: using the curve to declare the poster's contested opinion as the genius take (7/31: race, communism, God, crime, parenting), which reads as an argument rather than a joke; confused layouts where the ends don't match or the order is wrong (11/31); niche game references (5/31); correct shape but a middle that is a reasonable point and an end that is just wrong (8/31, e.g. routers, flat earth, market timing).
- **Tech memes:** 11 tech-ish uses. In the top 70: 'just write simple and clear code' (13), 'use chrome' (13), AI art is dumb (13), 'Socks make great gloves' mocking a chatbot's over-explanation (6), Apple ecosystem (6), AI slop (5). Tech-tagged posts score 0–2: 'just drop the whole thing in ChatGPT' over a parsing pipeline (2), Linux Mint vs Arch (1), regex (0). The working tech shape is simple-practice-wins against an overbuilt stack.
- **Misreads:**
  - A middle box that is a jargon list with no objection ('Hosted gateways, fine-tuning, inference, data pipelines'): the midwit must argue against the blunt line, not list parts.
  - A middle box shorter or simpler than the ends ('He lost the takedown, so he lost'): the middle is the effortful, sophisticated-sounding one.
  - A blunt line that isn't vindicated by the facts, or that is a niche technical step ('Just reward it when it succeeds'): the ends must be something a follower instantly nods at.
  - A calm, reasonable middle. In winners the midwit protests ('Nooo…!'); a measured alternative reads as two valid options.
  - Using the curve to assert a contested opinion as the smart one: 7/31 flops, and a risk for news about policy fights.
- **Test:** Do the left and right boxes say the same short, blunt line, and does the middle box argue against it or bury it in complications, so the blunt line reads as the winner?
- **Slot problems:** The three slots and the derived repeat match 47/70 real uses. Two things are wrong. First, the overthought MUST asks for 'a tangled, anxious take', which yields jargon lists and calm alternatives; real middles argue against the blunt line ('Nooo, you need…', 'Actually…'). Second, the 34-character cap applies to both slots, but real middles run p50 33 and p90 about 100, so the overcomplication gets cut. The simple slot could also take a viewer line, since the ends are the poster's own view in about 40/47. Proposal: Same boxes. The MUST text turns the middle into a protest against the blunt line, which separates the 47 horseshoe winners from our jargon-list misses; the middle's length cap rises to the real p90, and the blunt line can be the viewer's voice.
- **Our examples:**
  - `simple-practice-wins` · Another study finds that scaling up compute beats hand-built tricks for AI models. → simple: Make it bigger / overthought: Nooo, you need custom modules, clever rules and hand-tuned features for every task! / expert: Make it bigger
  - `taste-vindicated` · A survey finds most people still use the browser that came with their phone. → simple: Whatever came with the phone / overthought: Nooo, use a privacy browser with 14 extensions and your own DNS! / expert: Whatever came with the phone
  - `simple-practice-wins` · Security guidance drops forced password changes and odd complexity rules in favor of long passphrases. → simple: Just use a long password / overthought: Nooo, 12 characters, a symbol, no repeats, and change it every 90 days! / expert: Just use a long password
  - `taste-vindicated` · A lab ships yet another 'best model ever' and the internet argues over benchmark charts for days. → simple: [viewer AS-05] I'll use the free one / overthought: Actually, you have to compare 9 benchmarks, context windows and price per token first / expert: [viewer AS-05] I'll use the free one
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: Horrible) simple=Cloud robots need good internet ; overthought=Hosted gateways, fine-tuning, inference, data pipelines: The middle is a jargon list, not an objection to the blunt line, and 'cloud robots need good internet' isn't a naive-sounding take that turns out right; it's just a requirement.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) simple=Just reward it when it succeeds ; overthought=Add reaching and alignment rewards to guide it: The shape is close (simple vs over-engineered), but the topic is niche reward shaping a follower can't judge, and the middle is calm and short instead of a protest.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) overthought=He lost the takedown, so he lost ; simple=A voice can be protected: The middle is shorter and simpler than the ends: it's the shallow headline reading, not overthinking. The blunt line is a legal nuance, not a naive take.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) simple=Just log in again ; overthought=Pose as a Pixel 7 to fix login: Overbuilt hack vs plain fix is the right engine, but only works if the facts show the plain fix is what works; the middle also needs the protest register ('Nooo, spoof a Pixel 7…').

### They don't know (`they-dont-know`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 31 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries one news turn well: foreknowledge, where a price change, cap or shutdown is coming for happy users who haven't read the notice, plus the stale-news inversion for a late launch. It cannot carry a backfire or a double standard, and a headline is public by definition, so the writer has to find who in the story is still dancing without it.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** One deadpan person sits on a private fact while the room parties without it. The laugh is the gap between the crowd's carefree fun and what he is holding: either a petty, absurd or sad secret about himself treated with grave importance, or something he knows is about to hit them. The flatter the secret's stakes versus his solemn face (or the darker what's coming for the dancers), the harder it lands.
- **Picture:** A crude MS-Paint drawing: a lone wojak in a party hat stands against the back wall, cup in hand, with a flat, quietly knowing face, while two couples dance and laugh in the foreground (one bottom left, one right). Real posts put the thought in the white space top right; label posts write on the lone guy and on the dancers.
- **Figure:** The lone guy is the poster/'me' in 59/70; a named third party in 6/70 (Internet Explorer x2, Steve, a teacher, TF2's Soldier, 'Winter'); an object in 4/70 (first bread slice, remainder, nucleus, an original meme); a group in 1/70 (introverts). The dancers are 'they': the rest of the room, a class, parents, normal people, NPCs, other kids.
- **Voice:** The fixed frame 'They don't know (that) X' appears in 44/70; 7 of those split it across two boxes. X is a first-person clause about the speaker ('I...', 'I'm...', 'my...') in 29/44, a plain fact or news clause in 13/44, and 'about' + noun phrase in 2/44. Label posts (17/70) use 'ME' or 'ME WHO + clause' for the guy and a plural noun phrase for the crowd ('THE REST OF THE CLASS', 'EVERYONE CELEBRATING...'). All caps in about 40/70. Present tense throughout; no question marks; trailing '...' or '!' in 5/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → secret (white space top right, his thought: in 1-box posts (31/70) the whole line 'They don't know (that) X'; we print the frame and write X); B2 → secret (top right, second line: in frame-split posts (7/70) B1 is 'THEY DON'T KNOW' and B2 is X); B1 → knower (on or beside the lone guy: in label posts (about 17/70) a label for the lone guy: 'ME', 'ME WHO...', 'the first slice'; for us a logo or viewer line); B2 → null (on the dancing couples (right / bottom left): in label posts the crowd: 'THE REST OF THE CLASS', 'MY PARENTS', 'EXTROVERTS', 'THE NPCS HAVING FUN'; no slot of ours (see proposal 'crowd')); B3 → null (over the dancers: in 3-5 box posts (8/70): quotes from the crowd or extra crowd labels)
- **Patterns:**
  - `hidden-self` (41%, petty vs proper / understatement (trivial secret, solemn face)): [knower]: me or a player / [secret]: a fact about myself, in first person ('I...', 'my...'), that the room doesn't see: a petty secret, a niche brag, a hidden struggle, a secret identity. Real shape: Grimly keeping to himself that he can rhyme two fruit names, or that he ate all his snacks before the film started.
  - `coming-for-the-crowd` (10%, dramatic irony / ominous foreknowledge): [knower]: the one who read the fine print / [secret]: what is about to hit the dancers / [crowd]: the people having fun now. Real shape: The only student who noticed the test is printed on both sides, watching the class relax.
  - `insider-fact` (10%, smug insider): [secret]: a fact or bit of news the knower has that the room hasn't caught up on. Real shape: Knowing the big game update already dropped while everyone keeps dancing.
  - `outsider-label-pair` (17%, contrast / outsider): [knower]: me, the one whose situation differs / [crowd]: everyone else doing the normal thing. Real shape: The kid whose break starts in three weeks, beside everyone celebrating theirs now.
  - `literal-party` (14%, literalism / relatable): [knower]: me at a party, in one caption describing being dragged there or wanting to leave. Real shape: An introvert at a party their parent forced them to attend.
  - `stale-news` (3%, inversion (the knower is the one behind)): [knower]: someone famously late / [secret]: old news he thinks is a secret. Real shape: A slow old browser smugly sitting on the news that a war ended decades ago.
- **Slot targets:** secret p50 24 / p90 44, first-person clause ('I still...', 'my...') or a short fact clause; the X after the printed 'They don't know'; knower p50 22 / p90 45, label 'ME' / 'ME WHO + clause'; for us a logo or a viewer line; crowd p50 20 / p90 38, plural noun phrase for the people having fun: 'everyone + -ing phrase', 'the rest of the class'
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners keep the secret something the room plainly lacks and the knower plainly has: a first-person secret (29/70) or what's coming for the dancers (7/70), in under about 45 characters. Flops: 10/31 drop the secret entirely and caption a vague situation or confused contrast ('me at parties / or what I want to be'); 9/31 hang on names only an in-group knows (game clans, an NFT, a streamer, a niche cartoon); 5/31 have a secret nobody would care to have (all the periodic table elements, a one-day school week, the plural of octopus); 1/31 defeats itself (the room is literally an end-of-summer party, so they do know). 6/31 have the winning shape and are simply unseen (silica gel to feel included, time traveller, school starts tomorrow).
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 2 tech posts, both 0 upvotes, both hidden-self brags ('I'm a programmer', 'I'm building a better Shopify'). The news fits are coming-for-the-crowd (a price change, cap or deprecation the happy users haven't read yet) and stale-news (a company smug about shipping what rivals had long ago).
- **Misreads:**
  - Restating the story's headline as the secret ('the culprit was an employee'). If it's in the news, the room knows it. The secret must be something the dancers plainly lack.
  - Writing the secret in the third person about someone else, when real X is a first-person clause about the knower in 29/44 framed posts. The guy is the one sitting on it.
  - Leaving 'they' undefined. With a logo on the guy and no crowd, the viewer can't tell who doesn't know (users? rivals? the public?), and the gap that makes the joke disappears.
  - Using a secret with no stakes for anyone (5/31 flops): a fact nobody at the party would want.
  - Jargon or insider names in the secret (9/31 flops), e.g. a model codename or a benchmark name.
  - Turning it into an accusation of a person (a culprit, an employee). The secret is the knower's own, or a harmless thing coming for the crowd, never a jab at a private individual.
- **Test:** Is the top line something the lone guy privately holds (his own secret, or what's about to hit the dancers) that the partying crowd plainly does not know, rather than the story's headline restated?
- **Slot problems:** Two problems. (1) Our 'must' asks for 'a real fact from the story' in neutral voice, which pushes the writer to restate the headline in third person; real X is the knower's own first-person secret in 29/44 framed posts or what's coming for the crowd in 7. (2) There is no slot for 'they'. About 17/70 real posts label the dancers, and for news the crowd (free users, rivals, the rest of the market) is what makes the gap legible. Proposal: Real uses are first-person secrets or ominous foreknowledge, and about a quarter name the crowd. Moving 'secret' to the knower's own voice and adding an optional crowd label lets a news story say who is left out of the knowledge. The crowd box sits on the right dancer's shirt, clear of both right-side faces (which end at y 0.42 and x 0.83) and of the knower box.
- **Our examples:**
  - `coming-for-the-crowd` · A chatbot app announces its free plan gets a daily message cap next month. → knower: [viewer AS-04] Me, who read the pricing page / secret: the free plan gets capped next month / crowd: everyone chatting for free
  - `hidden-self` · A survey finds most office workers use chatbots mainly to write emails. → knower: [viewer AS-07] Me, the office 'AI person' / secret: I only use it for emails / crowd: coworkers asking me about agents
  - `stale-news` · A big voice assistant finally adds a feature rival chatbots shipped a year ago. → knower: [logo: amazon] / secret: I just invented talking back / crowd: every other chatbot
  - `outsider-label-pair` · A new AI feature launches for US users first, other countries later. → knower: [viewer AS-11] Me, refreshing the app abroad / secret: I still don't have it / crowd: everyone posting their demos
  - `coming-for-the-crowd` · An AI lab sets a retirement date for an older model many apps still run on. → knower: [viewer AS-18] Me, reading the shutdown email / secret: the old model retires in June / crowd: apps still built on it
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) secret=the culprit was an employee ; knower=[logo OpenAI]: The 'secret' is the story's own headline, so the room does know it; it's third person, not the knower's own fact; 'they' is undefined; and it points at an unnamed employee instead of a petty secret or something coming for the crowd. A right reading would have OpenAI privately smug about its own role (hidden-self) or the office not knowing what's coming (coming-for-the-crowd).

### Two guys on a bus (`two-guys-bus`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 24 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries a split outcome well: one change (a price move, a regional rollout, a mandate) that delights one group and strands another, or the same thing done by choice vs by force. It has no room for a backfire or an escalation, and it needs both sides to be on the same ride, so stories without a clear winner and loser don't fit.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 2.
- **Mechanism:** Same bus, same ride, opposite experience: one man gets the view, the other gets a wall. The joke is that one small difference (choice vs obligation, first vs last, does vs doesn't) turns the identical situation into joy for one and misery for the other, and the closer the two labels are in wording, the sharper that one difference lands.
