- **Picture:** A colour cartoon of a bus interior. On the left a sad, slumped man sits by a window filled with a grey rock wall; on the right a beaming man clasps his hands at a window showing a sunrise over green mountains and a river. Real posts put one label over each man (left on the rock wall, right on the mountains); 3-box posts add a header on the ceiling.
- **Figure:** Both men are labelled in about 62/70 posts. They stand for two versions of one thing, activity or condition (taking a shower vs having to take one, last day vs last ever day) in 35/70; two groups of people (iPad kids, Swifties, people who use Twitter) in 24/70; 'me' vs 'me' or 'me' vs others in 7/70; other 4/70. Only 2/70 label the windows (beach vs desert, a 'sunny side / rock and a hard place' pun).
- **Voice:** Short labels, not sentences, in about 58/70. Plain noun phrases ('FRIDAY', 'IPAD KIDS', 'DC MOVIES') in about 20; gerund phrases ('TAKING A SHOWER', 'READING FOR FUN') in about 16; 'people who / those who / a man who + verb' in about 10; 'X when Y' ('GUYS WHEN...', 'ME WHEN...') in about 8; quotes or full sentences in about 9; walls of motivational text in 3. The two labels share most of their words in about 25/70 (one word or clause flipped). All caps in about 45/70. No first person except 'ME' labels (7/70).
- **Boxes:** B1 → view (right: over the happy man / the mountain window: in 2-box posts, the lucky version or group (taking a shower, reading for fun, people who don't use it)); B2 → stuck (left: over the sad man / the rock wall: in 2-box posts, the grim version or group (having to take a shower, reading because you're forced to)); B1 → null (ceiling, centre (3-box posts, 10/70): the shared ride: the topic or event both men face (TWITTER, 'Getting older', 'Clash Royale'); then B2 is the lucky side and B3 the grim side in 4 of the 6 readable cases)
- **Patterns:**
  - `same-thing-flipped` (30%, minimal pair / choice vs obligation): [view]: the thing done the good way (by choice, the first time, for fun) / [stuck]: the same thing with one word flipped (forced, the last time, for school). Real shape: Chemistry as a hobby beside chemistry as a school subject.
  - `one-event-two-reactions` (9%, split outcome): [topic]: one event / [view]: the group it delights / [stuck]: the group it crushes. Real shape: A pop star retiring: everyone else relaxed, the fans devastated.
  - `does-vs-doesnt` (9%, inversion of envy (abstaining is the good seat)): [view]: people who don't do X / [stuck]: people who do X. Real shape: People who stay off a social app beside people who use it.
  - `group-contrast` (13%, contrast): [view]: a group whose life is easy here / [stuck]: a group stuck with the hard version. Real shape: Kids who play outside beside tablet kids.
  - `same-words-two-readings` (14%, wordplay / perspective): [view] and [stuck]: identical or near-identical text, read as joy on one side and dread on the other. Real shape: The same 'a relative is visiting tomorrow' line on both sides, one thrilled and one dreading it.
  - `a-vs-b-preference` (10%, taste contrast): [view]: the thing the poster prefers / [stuck]: the thing they look down on. Real shape: Hand-drawn animation beside computer animation.
  - `inverted-sides` (4%, inversion): [view]: the 'bad' choice / [stuck]: the 'good' choice. Real shape: Being a hater on the sunny side, forced positivity on the wall side.
- **Slot targets:** view p50 22 / p90 45, noun or gerund phrase naming the lucky version or group, parallel to stuck; stuck p50 24 / p90 45, the same phrase with one condition flipped, or the unlucky group; 'Me, ...' when the viewer; topic p50 12 / p90 30, noun phrase or logo naming the shared ride (optional; 10/70 use a header)
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make it obvious which side is lucky and keep both labels on one shared ride: about 25/70 winners use near-parallel wording versus 3/24 flops. Flops: 10/24 pair two things with no shared ride or no clear good side (lifting vs cardio, two unrelated phone habits, two cartoon characters); 6/24 hinge on sports scores, a niche game or school software; 6/24 are obvious or literal (happy / sad, Friday / Monday again, identical text with no second reading); 2/24 are single-box motivational lines.
- **Tech memes:** No posts in the tech tags, but platform contrasts do well in the top set: 'a man who is not / a man creating AI slop' (34 upvotes) and four 'doesn't use / uses Twitter' posts. AI news fits one-event-two-reactions (one change, two kinds of user) and same-thing-flipped (using AI by choice vs because work says so).
- **Misreads:**
  - Labelling the window instead of the man. Our 'view' slot asks for 'the better thing others get to look at', but real posts label the lucky person or version; only 2/70 label the scenery.
  - No second party. Our card has no slot for the happy man, so the meme can't say who is lucky; the view becomes a prize instead of half of a matched pair.
  - Two labels that aren't one ride: Sam Altman's logo against 'Karp and Amodei at 51 in all' shares no event, has no lucky side and names people a casual follower may not know.
  - Making it about taste or envy rather than circumstance. Real winners flip a condition (forced, last, can't); 7/70 do pure taste and they sit near the bottom.
  - Long, unparallel labels. When the two boxes don't echo each other, the single difference that makes the joke is lost (10/24 flops).
- **Test:** Do the two labels name the same ride (one event, product or activity) in two versions, with the view side clearly the lucky one and the rock-wall side clearly the grim one?
- **Slot problems:** Our card treats the format as 'left-out person vs the better thing others have'. Real uses label both MEN as two versions of one situation (62/70), and only 2/70 label the scenery. The card has no slot for the happy man, no optional header for the shared event (10/70), and its 'must' (real access, not taste) misses the main engine: one condition flipped on the same ride. Proposal: Real posts label both men as a matched pair. Moving 'view' down onto the happy man's side (y 0.27-0.44, clear of his face at y 0.47) makes it his label, and an optional ceiling header carries the news event in 3-box style. 'stuck' stays where it is, clear of the sad man's face (y 0.54).
- **Our examples:**
  - `one-event-two-reactions` · A chatbot moves its newest model from the free plan to paid plans only. → topic: The new model / view: Paid subscribers / stuck: [viewer AS-04] Me, on the free plan
  - `same-thing-flipped` · A large company tells every employee to use its AI assistant daily. → view: Me, using AI for fun / stuck: [viewer AS-08] Me, using AI per the memo
  - `group-contrast` · A new AI voice feature launches in the US, with Europe 'later this year'. → topic: Voice mode / view: US users / stuck: [viewer AS-17] Me, in Europe
  - `does-vs-doesnt` · A rival chatbot launches and many users switch to it. → view: People who just signed up / stuck: [viewer AS-13] Me, with a year of old chats
  - `same-thing-flipped` · An AI app ends its launch discount after one week. → view: Signing up launch week / stuck: Signing up the week after
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) stuck=Me, checking if bank sync works in my country ; view=Bank sync for US banks: It keeps one ride (the same app) with a clear lucky side (US banks) and a clear stuck side (me, elsewhere): group-contrast. It labels the window rather than a second man, which works here because 'for US banks' implies the lucky group. Shorter, parallel labels ('US users' / 'Me, outside the US') would land faster.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) stuck=[logo Sam Altman] ; view=Karp and Amodei at 51 in all: No shared ride and no lucky side: a count of tracked statements is not a view anyone enjoys, nothing is flipped between the sides, and the right box is a fragment. It also leans on a name casual followers won't know.

### Imagination Spongebob (`imagination-spongebob`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 27 flops. News fit: **weak** (The engine is 'this only exists in fantasy'. Aimed at a news story, that means calling a company's claim make-believe, which we bench. Read as delight, it endorses the pitch. What survives is the viewer's own hopes (a rollout reaching them, one subscription, knowing the model names), which use the story as a trigger but carry no news turn: no reversal, backfire or double standard.). Our memes misused it: 4 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** His rainbow-hands glee is the face you make about something that only exists in fantasy. The joke is naming a thing people want (a date, teachers who respect students, a world without ads) and answering 'IMAGINATION': the gap between the childlike wonder and the flat fact that it isn't real. A second engine runs the same gap the other way: a harsh or dark line delivered with rainbow cheer.
- **Picture:** SpongeBob, wide-eyed and grinning, sweeps both hands up and apart as a sparkling rainbow arcs between them. Real posts put a setup across the top over the rainbow and a short punch (often the word 'IMAGINATION') across the bottom under his chin.
- **Figure:** SpongeBob is rarely a labelled party. He is the voice saying 'imagination' about something that isn't real (21/70), the sarcastic or cheerfully dark narrator (14/70), or 'me' delighted at something small (7/70). No top post labels him as a company or brand.
- **Voice:** The word 'IMAGINATION' (or 'in your imagination', 'is called imagination') appears in 16/70 and is the punch in 13 of them, usually alone in the bottom box. 'A world with(out) X' in 6/70. 'Me...' or 'When...' setups in about 14/70. 'NOBODY CARES / NOBODY ASKED' in 6/70. All caps in about 55/70. Setups are noun phrases or 'when' clauses; the punch is one word or a short flat clause. 12/70 are off-format (letter gimmicks, text walls).
- **Boxes:** B1 → dull (top, over the rainbow: the wished-for thing or setup ('Me trying to get a girlfriend', 'A world with no X'); in 1-box posts (27/70) the whole joke); B2 → imagined (bottom, under his chin: the punch: the word 'IMAGINATION' in about 13 of the 43 two-box posts, or a sarcastic payoff ('NOBODY CARES', 'NOTHING WILL HAPPEN')); Bnull → pitcher (beside his left hand: no real counterpart: 0/70 posts label him as a company)
- **Patterns:**
  - `only-in-imagination` (19%, deflation / understatement): [dull]: a thing someone wants or claims to have / [imagined]: IMAGINATION. Real shape: A diner asked how they'll pay the bill, answering with the magic word.
  - `wishful-world` (9%, wish fulfilment): [dull]: A world with no X (a universal annoyance), alone or with a short 'it would be heaven'. Real shape: Picturing a world where online ads simply don't exist.
  - `claim-as-fantasy` (3%, mocking a claim (benched for us)): [dull]: a belief or claim / [imagined]: IMAGINATION. Real shape: A policy slogan captioned as pure make-believe.
  - `sarcastic-rainbow` (10%, sarcasm (cheer vs dismissal)): [dull]: a setup someone cares about / [imagined]: a gleeful 'nobody cares / nobody asked / now go away'. Real shape: Someone shares a school fact, and the reply is a beaming 'nobody asked'.
  - `cheerful-dark` (10%, tone inversion): [dull] and/or [imagined]: a grim or self-pitying fact said as if it were wonderful. Real shape: A sparkly announcement that everyone will eventually die.
  - `small-delight` (10%, overreaction to something small): [dull]: a tiny win / [imagined]: me, overjoyed. Real shape: Celebrating a first follower like a lottery win.
  - `fake-wonder-obvious` (4%, fake wonder): [dull]: 'Did you know...' / [imagined]: the obvious answer, said as a revelation. Real shape: Marvelling that five apples minus two leaves three.
- **Slot targets:** dull p50 25 / p90 55, noun phrase or 'Me, -ing...' naming the wished-for thing; or 'A world with no X'; imagined p50 11 / p90 20, the word 'Imagination' (13/43 two-box posts) or a two-word flat payoff
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners name a specific thing people want and can't have before the 'imagination' punch (13/13 such winners name it: a date, a girlfriend, teachers' respect, a gift). Flops: 7/27 are sincere statements with no gap (imagination is a gift, you are better, check out my stream); 6/27 rely on names or languages outsiders can't read; 5/27 have a setup that isn't a wish (an apple, an assassin, home sellers); 4/27 are dark with no cheerful twist (a ghosting, rent, trauma); 3/27 say 'nobody cares' about nothing in particular; 2/27 are letter gimmicks. 5/27 flops use the word 'imagination' with no concrete wish in front of it.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: one tech post (2 upvotes) uses the rainbow as 'magic' for how AI works, and one top post is an 'Artificial Imagination' pun. Pointed at a company story, the main engine ('this doesn't exist') becomes mocking a claim, which we bench. Only the viewer's own hopes or a wishful world survive.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting a company's pitch in the bottom box ('Frontier Models for Every Robot', 'everyone gets an AI appliance at home'). Under rainbow hands it reads as either an endorsement or a sneer at the claim, and both are wrong for us. All 4 of our memes did this and Lucas rated all 4 No.
  - Reading the format as 'genuine delight at something small'. Only 7/70 winners do that; the main engine is that the thing exists only in fantasy (21/70).
  - A dull-vs-grand pair with no gap ('One remote researcher job posting' / 'Agentic systems automating models of biology'). Real posts pair a wish with 'imagination', not a fact with its marketing.
  - Labelling SpongeBob with a company logo. 0/70 real posts do; it makes the company the giddy one.
  - Names a casual follower won't know ('Sharma's Xbox Labs', 'Feldman'), which is also what 6/27 flops do.
  - Writing 'imagination' with no concrete wish in front of it (5/27 flops).
- **Test:** Does the top name something the viewer wants that plainly isn't real (so 'Imagination' lands as the deflation), with no company claim, tagline or pitch anywhere on the meme?
- **Slot problems:** The card's logic ('genuine delight at something small', a dull thing vs its glowing reframe, plus a company 'pitcher') fits only 7/70 real posts and invites the pitch-shown-straight failure all 4 of our memes made. The real structure is a wish on top and a fixed 'Imagination' punch at the bottom (or a one-box 'A world with no X'). No real post labels SpongeBob with a company, so 'pitcher' has no counterpart. Proposal: Real winners are a concrete wish plus the fixed word. Fixing the punch removes the slot where our writer put company pitches, and dropping 'pitcher' stops the logo-on-SpongeBob endorsement reading. Boxes are unchanged and clear of his face (y 0.28-0.81). The template stays benched for claims; this shape only voices the viewer's own wishes.
- **Our examples:**
  - `only-in-imagination` · A chatbot's new feature rolls out gradually, region by region. → wish: [viewer AS-11] Me, getting it on day one / punch: Imagination
  - `wishful-world` · Three AI labs each release a new flagship model in the same week. → wish: A week with no new 'best model' / punch: Imagination
  - `only-in-imagination` · Another AI app launches a paid tier for its best features. → wish: [viewer AS-02] Me, paying for just one AI app / punch: Imagination
  - `only-in-imagination` · An AI lab renames its model lineup for the second time this year. → wish: [viewer AS-16] Me, knowing which model is the good one / punch: Imagination
  - `only-in-imagination` · A free AI plan lowers its daily message cap. → wish: [viewer AS-04] Me, finishing a project on the free plan / punch: Imagination
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) dull=Fans decompile games and port them ; imagined=Sharma's Xbox Labs: game makers, engineers, scientists ; pitcher=[logo Microsoft]: The bottom is a company's org-chart pitch shown straight under rainbow hands, with an unknown name; no wish and no fantasy gap.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) dull=One remote researcher job posting ; imagined=Agentic systems automating models of biology: Fact vs marketing, with jargon in the punch. Nothing is wished for and nothing deflates.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) dull=Upload data, Pi runs the training on its computers ; imagined=Frontier Models for Every Robot: The product's own tagline as the punch reads as an endorsement (or as a claim being mocked). The top box is a product description.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) dull=An AI chipmaker likes the idea of AI in every home ; imagined=Feldman: everyone gets an AI appliance at home: Restates a CEO's prediction twice, once with a name casual followers won't know. As a format this is claim-as-fantasy, which is benched.

### Monkey Puppet (`monkey-puppet`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 21 flops. News fit: **strong** (Its engine is the double standard and the hidden culprit, two of the most common news turns: a company scolding rivals for what it does itself, a rule its author breaks, an outage caused by the party everyone relies on. The only condition is that the implication is true to the story's facts.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** Someone says or does something that, without knowing it, points straight at the puppet: a sweeping claim it is the exception to, an accusation it is guilty of too, a mystery it caused. The puppet's stiff glance away is the guilty party hoping nobody notices; the joke is the reader catching the link a beat before the label confirms it.
- **Picture:** Two side-by-side stills of a red-haired monkey puppet: in the left it glances sideways out of the corner of its eyes; in the right it looks away, stiff and guilty. A tall white band across the top holds all the text; real posts end the band with the label of whoever the puppet is.
- **Figure:** The puppet is the party implicated by the line above in 32/70: a third party that is secretly guilty, the exception or the culprit in 25/70 (tomatoes, moms on Candy Crush, Google and Bing, the Kool-Aid Man) and 'me' guilty of the same thing in 7/70. It is 'me' in an awkward or caught moment in 19/70 ('when your mic wasn't on', 'me who got Uzbekistan'), an awkward observer in 14/70 (the kid who knows Morse code, the rest of the class) and 'me' with an odd thought in 4/70; 1 other.
- **Voice:** Dialogue labels with colons in about 30/70 ('People: ...', 'Teacher: ...', 'Moms: ...'), ending on a bare label plus colon for the puppet in about 25/70. 'When you...' in about 17/70; 'Me...' in about 15/70. Third-person labels for the implicated party are short (1-4 words) in 25/25 implicated posts. Sentence case and all caps both common; asterisk actions ('*has drought*') in 3/70. The 1-box form packs statement and label into one block (35/70).
- **Boxes:** B1 → fact (white band, first line(s): the statement, accusation, event or 'when you...' moment; in 1-box posts (35/70) it also ends with the implicated party's label ('...Tomato:')); B2 → looker (white band, last line above the puppet: the implicated party as a short label, usually ending in a colon ('Google, Yahoo, Bing, etc:', 'Hockey:', 'Lifeguards at the beach:', 'Me:')); B3 → null (white band: in 3-4 box posts (3/70), extra dialogue turns before the final label)
- **Patterns:**
  - `claim-exception` (20%, hypocrisy by exception): [fact]: someone's sweeping rule or complaint / [looker]: the one thing that breaks it, as a label with a colon. Real shape: People insisting fruit never belongs on pizza, then the tomato glancing away.
  - `accuser-guilty` (7%, double standard): [fact]: X accuses or scolds someone for doing Y / [looker]: X (or its peers) doing Y too. Real shape: One app banned for harvesting data, while the big search engines glance away.
  - `secret-culprit` (9%, dramatic irony): [fact]: an event or mystery / [looker]: the party that secretly caused it. Real shape: A state in drought, and the first-grader who kept chanting for the rain to go away.
  - `guilty-me` (10%, self-incrimination): [fact]: someone gets in trouble or a rule is stated / [looker]: me, who did the same thing. Real shape: Watching a classmate get scolded for something you also did.
  - `caught-moment` (21%, relatable embarrassment): [fact]: When you [realise you did something awkward and others may have seen]. Real shape: Realising your microphone was on the whole time.
  - `awkward-observer` (20%, unintended meaning / dread): [fact]: someone innocently does or says X / [looker]: the one person who understands what X really means. Real shape: A nervous pen-clicker, and the kid who knows Morse code reading something terrible in it.
- **Slot targets:** fact p50 34 / p90 70, a labelled statement or accusation ('X: claim') or 'When you...' clause; looker p50 18 / p90 45, short label of the implicated party, ending in ':' (or a logo, or 'Me, who...' for guilty-me)
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the statement point at the puppet without saying so, and the label is short (25/25 implicated labels are 1-4 words). Flops: 10/21 have no implication at all (a complaint, a story, a wall of text, a pun) so the puppet has nothing to be guilty of; 5/21 have the implicated-party shape but the link is obvious, needs niche knowledge or runs long (McDonald's and 'good food', a Norwegian history jab, a federalism paragraph); 3/21 make the puppet an oblivious reactor or contradict the setup (WWIII and 'me checking if DoorDash still delivers'); 3/21 have the winning shape and were simply unseen.
- **Tech memes:** No posts in the tech tags, but tech double standards are among the top winners: an app banned for harvesting data with the big search engines as the puppet (613 upvotes), YouTube's moderation complaint with Roblox as the puppet (170). The accuser-guilty and secret-culprit shapes map straight onto AI news: a company scolding rivals for what it does too, or the party whose change caused the outage everyone is complaining about.
- **Misreads:**
  - Making the looker someone who reacts to the fact instead of someone the fact implicates ('Me, checking the answer it was very sure about'): the AI is the guilty one there, not the viewer.
  - Writing a 'fact' that implicates nobody: 'No word on which chat apps add it', 'Permission and review details: none'. A missing detail in a press release has no guilty party, so the side-eye has no reason.
  - Explaining the link instead of letting the label land: real posts stop at the bare label ('Hockey:') and let the reader connect it.
  - Long or foreign-language facts ('Madame X, épouse Y'); real facts are p50 34 characters.
  - Accusing a party of something the story doesn't support. The implication must be true to the facts (the company really does the thing, or really caused it).
- **Test:** Does the top line, without naming it, point at the party labelled above the puppet (it's the exception, does the same thing, or caused it), so the puppet is guilty rather than just reacting?
- **Slot problems:** The two lines and their boxes match the real format. What we ask for is off. The card asks for 'an awkward fact someone would rather not look at', and our writer reads that as any unflattering detail (a missing permission note, an unannounced partner list). Real winners need a statement, claim or event that secretly implicates the puppet: it's the exception, it does the same thing, or it caused it. The looker should accept a plain text label (tomatoes, hockey, lifeguards), not only a logo or viewer, and real labels end with a colon. Proposal: The boxes are right, but the musts invite 'awkward detail' facts that implicate nobody, and the looker can't hold the short third-party labels (tomatoes, hockey, the search engines) that carry 25/70 winners. Same ids and geometry; new musts, a text option for the looker, and the colon the real format uses.
- **Our examples:**
  - `secret-culprit` · An Amazon Web Services outage takes down dozens of popular AI apps for several hours. → fact: Every AI app down at the same time / looker: [logo: amazon]
  - `guilty-me` · A study finds people share far more personal detail with chatbots than they realise. → fact: Study: people tell chatbots way too much / looker: [viewer AS-09] Me, remembering what I told it last month
  - `accuser-guilty` · A tech company warns investors about an AI bubble in the same report that raises its own AI spending. → fact: Company: careful, AI might be a bubble / looker: Same company's AI budget:
  - `claim-exception` · A survey finds most workers say they never use AI at work, while usage logs show most do. → fact: Workers: I never use AI at work / looker: Their browser history:
  - `guilty-me` · A company's memo bans pasting work documents into personal chatbots. → fact: Memo: no work files in chatbots / looker: [viewer AS-08] Me, three reports deep
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) fact=Judge lets Apple read private LINE messages ; looker=[logo OpenAI]: The ruling exposes OpenAI's side of the case, so OpenAI is the party the fact points at and the glance away reads as 'please don't look at those'. It's the caught-moment shape aimed at a company. It could be sharper with the label doing more work, and 'LINE' is a name casual followers may not know.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) fact=AI turned 'Madame X, épouse Y' into 'Ms X marries Y' ; looker=Me, checking the answer it was very sure about: The guilty party is the AI, but the puppet is labelled as the viewer reacting to it, so the side-eye means nothing. The French example is long and opaque to the audience. The card itself bans 'Me, checking'.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) fact=No word on which chat apps add it ; looker=[logo Google DeepMind]: A gap in the announcement isn't something anyone is guilty of; nothing points at DeepMind.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) fact=Permission and review details: none ; looker=[logo Google]: A fact-sheet note in place of a statement or event; no claim it's the exception to, no accusation it's also guilty of.

### Clown Applying Makeup (`clown-makeup`)
Coded 70 top uses, 4 tech memes, 35 flops. News fit: **strong** (It is built for the backfire: a company's real sequence of decisions ending in the backlash they caused, which is a common news shape (6 of the top 70 are platform or policy stories). It also carries escalation and hypocrisy. The one constraint from our rules is the finale: a company can't be given an invented 'why do they hate us', so its finale is the real result or its own real claim, and the 'why' line belongs to the viewer.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** Each panel is the clown's own next move, and each move makes the outcome more obvious, until the full clown asks why the obvious outcome happened (or denies causing it). The joke is self-inflicted cause and oblivious effect: the reader sees the link the clown can't, and the makeup goes on one bad decision at a time.
- **Picture:** Four stacked photo panels down the right side of a man becoming a clown: white base paint with eyes closed, then painted eyes and red lips, then a rainbow wig pulled on, then a red nose and a wide painted grin. The left 55-60% of every row is blank white, where real posts write one line per panel.
- **Figure:** The man is the party doing the foolish things: 'me' or 'you' in a self-own in 18/70; schools or teachers in 14/70; parents in 14/70; a company, platform or mods in 9/70 (Netflix x2, YouTube, Roblox x2, Disney, site mods, Discord mods, a YouTuber); another third party in 10/70 (a Karen, Covid, a country, a senator); other 5/70. About 11/70 name the clown in a label in B1 ('NETFLIX', 'TEACHERS:', 'PARENTS').
- **Voice:** Steps 1-3 are actions, not thoughts or takes, in about 60/70: imperatives or present-tense verbs ('Make them take useless tests', 'Remove dislike counter'), 'we/I + verb' plans ('we give them lots of homework', 'let's block half the websites') or 'you + verb'. Step 4 is a question in 41/70, nearly always 'Why do/does [they/everyone] [hate/X] [us/me]?'; quoted with marks in 8. Sentence case mostly, all caps in about 8/70. Each line is one clause; lines rarely run on.
- **Boxes:** B1 → step-1 (left of panel 1 (white base): the first move; in about 11/70 a label naming the clown instead ('NETFLIX', 'TEACHERS:'), which is our 'clown' logo); B2 → step-2 (left of panel 2 (face paint): the next, worse move); B3 → step-3 (left of panel 3 (the wig): the worst move yet); B4 → step-4 (left of panel 4 (full clown): the punch: 'Why do they hate us?' in 40/70, or the absurd result, denial or last rung); B5 → null (repeated labels between steps: in 5-8 box posts (10/70), the clown's name repeated per panel ('NETFLIX', 'SCHOOL', 'PARENTS'))
- **Patterns:**
  - `self-inflicted-why` (57%, oblivious cause and effect): [step-1..3]: the clown's own moves, each worse / [step-4]: 'Why do they hate us?' / 'Why is X happening?' about the obvious result. Real shape: A school gets kids up early, piles on homework and ignores bullying, then wonders aloud why kids dislike school.
  - `escalation-ladder` (14%, escalation): [step-1..4]: the same move, one rung further each time, ending at the absurd extreme. Real shape: Promising to wake at 6:50, then 6:55, then 7:00, and the last panel is mid-morning.
  - `chain-to-outcome` (11%, backfire / anticlimax): [step-1..3]: a sequence of moves / [step-4]: the absurd or anticlimactic result they lead to. Real shape: A lawmaker's long fight to ban an app ends with the ban lasting half a day.
  - `denial-finale` (7%, hypocrisy): [step-1..3]: the clown's bad moves / [step-4]: the clown's claim of innocence, virtue or blame on something else. Real shape: A creator who reused others' work and went after a critic, closing with 'no ill intentions'.
  - `doubling-down` (4%, self-defeating loop): [step-1..3]: a fix that makes it worse / [step-4]: more of the same fix. Real shape: A parent takes away a struggling kid's things, grades drop, so they take them away for longer.
- **Slot targets:** clown p50 8 / p90 14, a name label ('NETFLIX', 'TEACHERS:'); for us a logo, or the viewer; step-1 p50 23 / p90 48, verb phrase: the clown's first move; step-2 p50 25 / p90 50, verb phrase: a worse move; step-3 p50 30 / p90 50, verb phrase: the worst move; step-4 p50 27 / p90 50, 'Why do/does ... ?' question, or the plain result, or the clown's own claim
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners keep one agent, three moves that escalate and a final panel the moves plainly caused (40/70 end on the oblivious 'why'). Flops: 11/35 have no arc at all (a game playthrough, steak orders, a cartoon cast list, numbers, a burger story); 8/35 depend on niche lore (a game's storyline, a fictional racing league, a strategy-game faction, a football federation); 13/35 have the winning shape but are copies of saturated topics (procrastination, homework, parents and phones) that the top set already holds; 2/35 are edgy accusations with no oblivious turn; 1/35 is the literal makeup steps again. Note: this packet's Know Your Meme section is a copy of another meme's page and was not used.
- **Tech memes:** 4 tech posts, all weak (9, 3, 0, 0 upvotes): they use the panels as a ranking ladder (editors from vi to VS Code, programmer salaries) rather than self-inflicted cause, which is why they flop. Company stories in the top set do well when the steps are a platform's real decisions and the finale is the backlash (Netflix and password sharing, YouTube removing dislikes, Roblox).
- **Misreads:**
  - Writing the steps as a 'line of thinking' or opinions. Real steps are the clown's actions (about 60/70), and each one causes part of the outcome.
  - Making step 1 'reasonable' by rule. Winners often start already bad; what matters is that each step is worse and all point at one result.
  - Ending on a neutral claim with no link to the steps. The finale must be caused by steps 1-3: the obvious result, the oblivious 'why', or a denial that the steps contradict.
  - Putting an invented 'Why do they hate us?' in a real company's mouth. That's an invented quote; for companies use the real result or the company's real stated claim, and keep the 'why' question for the viewer as clown.
  - Using the panels as a ranking or ladder of unrelated things (the tech flops: editors, salaries) instead of one party's sequence.
  - Steps that need lore or niche names (8/35 flops).
- **Test:** Are steps 1-3 one party's own moves getting worse, and does the last panel land on what those moves caused (the result, the party's oblivious question, or a claim the steps contradict)?
- **Slot problems:** The boxes match (four panel lines plus an optional label), but what we ask for is off. The card asks for a 'line of thinking' starting with a 'reasonable' step and ending on 'the story's real claim or spin'. Real winners are a sequence of the clown's own actions, each worse, ending on the result they caused: the oblivious 'why' in 40/70, a result or anticlimax in 8, a denial in 5. The 'clown' slot accepts only an entity, but 18/70 winners make 'me/you' the clown. Proposal: Same boxes, which sit clear of both faces. The musts change from 'a line of thinking ending in spin' to 'the clown's own escalating actions ending in what they caused', and the clown can be the viewer, which covers the 18/70 self-own winners.
- **Our examples:**
  - `self-inflicted-why` · A chatbot company launches a $200-a-month tier on top of its existing plans. → clown: [viewer AS-02] / step-1: Pay for one AI app / step-2: Add another one for images / step-3: Try the new $200 tier / step-4: Why am I broke?
  - `chain-to-outcome` · An AI app drops its free plan, raises prices and cuts message limits, and reports losing users to rivals. → clown: [logo: the app's company] / step-1: Drop the free plan / step-2: Raise the price / step-3: Cut the message limit / step-4: Users leave for rivals
  - `escalation-ladder` · A lab releases a new flagship model weeks after its last one. → clown: [viewer AS-19] / step-1: Pick a model for the project / step-2: Rewrite every prompt for it / step-3: Finish the setup on Friday / step-4: New model drops Monday
  - `denial-finale` · A company that trains on user chats by default and keeps them for years publishes a post saying it puts privacy first. → clown: [logo: the company] / step-1: Train on chats by default / step-2: Bury the opt-out / step-3: Keep chats for years / step-4: [CLAIM] We put privacy first
  - `self-inflicted-why` · A study finds chatbots often cite sources that don't exist. → clown: [viewer AS-14] / step-1: Ask the chatbot for sources / step-2: Skip checking them / step-3: Paste them into my report / step-4: Why is my boss emailing me?

### Roll Safe Think About It (`roll-safe`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 23 flops. News fit: **strong** (A common news turn is a player 'solving' a problem by removing the measurement, renaming the thing, closing the door or exploiting a rule's letter; avoid-by-removal and loophole carry it directly, and the viewer's own workaround to a price or limit carries the rest. It fails when the story has no move with a visible hole, and the writer must not invent a motive the facts don't show.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** The figure is smugly proud of reasoning that is technically valid but obviously dumb or self-defeating: it solves a problem by removing the thing the problem is about, obeys a rule's letter while defeating its point, or proves something with a fake syllogism. The laugh is the gap between the 'think about it' confidence and the hole everyone can see in the logic.
- **Picture:** Landscape photo: a young man in a black leather jacket on the left half, grinning slyly at the camera and tapping his temple with one finger; a plain grey wall fills the right-centre and a shop window with a neon 'Opening' hours sign sits bottom right. Imgflip puts B1 as top text and B2 as bottom text across the full width; our card puts one box on the wall right of his head and an optional logo on his jacket.
- **Figure:** The implied speaker of the clever line, the poster/'me' or a voice giving 'you' advice, in 62/70 (explicit 'I/me/ME:' in 14 of those); a named third party in 2/70 (Newton, 'Faking your age on Discord be like'); no figure role (anti-memes, plain reactions) in 6/70. 0/70 label him as a company.
- **Voice:** Statements voiced by the thinker, never narration: 'Can't/You can't … if …' frames 10/70; 'If … then …' conditionals 9/70; first-person 'I/me' 14/70; dialogue set-ups ('Doctor: … / Me: …', 'GF: … / Me: …') 3/70; questions 4/70. All caps in 63/70 (Imgflip default). In the 42 two-box uses B1 is the premise and B2 the twist, split at 'if', 'so', 'then' or the colon in 36/42. No end punctuation in 55/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → dodge (top text, full width (our box: the wall right of his head): the premise: the problem, rule or fact the logic starts from ('You can't get X', 'Genie says no more wishes', 'Doctor: 50% success rate'); in 1-box uses the whole line); B2 → dodge (bottom text, full width (our card folds it into the same wall box): the twist: the 'clever' conclusion or workaround ('if you don't Y', 'so I bought two', 'then do it twice')); B3 → null (extra caption boxes (4/70 posts): extra steps of an argument or a credit line; no slot of ours); Bnull → dodger (his jacket: no real counterpart: 0/70 real uses label him; 1/70 names a third party (Newton) in the caption text instead)
- **Patterns:**
  - `avoid-by-removal` (17%, absurd avoidance, literalism): [premise]: Can't [bad outcome] / [twist]: if you [remove the thing the outcome needs]. Real shape: You can't lose your job if you never got one in the first place.
  - `letter-of-the-rule-loophole` (21%, loophole, fake math): [premise]: the rule, offer or fact as stated / [twist]: the move that obeys its letter and defeats its point. Real shape: A book promises to fix half your problems, so the poster buys a second copy.
  - `fake-smart-syllogism` (33%, false inference, shower thought): [premise]: If [true-ish fact] / [twist]: then [a ridiculous conclusion stated as obvious]. Real shape: If school is not for sleeping, then home is not for studying.
  - `pun-dressed-as-insight` (16%, wordplay): [premise]: a set-up stated like a fact / [twist]: a pun or double meaning presented as the clever discovery. Real shape: Coders like dark mode because light draws bugs.
- **Slot targets:** dodge p50 60 / p90 95, a whole premise + twist sentence ('Can't X if you Y'); real 2-box total ~60, 1-box p50 63; our 48-char cap is tight; premise p50 33 / p90 49, the problem, rule or fact, stated flatly ('Can't get X', 'If X', 'X promises Y'); twist p50 27 / p90 50, the smug conclusion or workaround, starting with if / so / then / a verb; dodger p50 0 / p90 0, logo only; no real counterpart, use only when the line is plainly that company's logic
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners carry an inference with a visible hole: 50/70 are avoid-by-removal, loophole or fake syllogism, and 11/70 are puns. Flops: 10/23 state a fact, an action or a plain workaround with no inference at all (a launch date 'Tesla Roadster unveils October 1st / Remember that', 'Posting in fun so I get points', 'I know you're lying / continue it'); 6/23 use the 'can't X if Y' frame but with a non-sequitur or a copy of an old winner ('can't get locked if you talk to the lock', 'can't have heart problems without a heart'); 3/23 are overlong rambles; 2/23 are weak puns; 2/23 other. The frame words alone don't save it: the twist must be technically true AND obviously beside the point.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 2 tagged tech posts (5 and 0 upvotes). The two tech-flavoured winners in the top list are both the same bug pun (spiders as web developers, programmers and dark mode). Our news use (a company skipping the check that would find its errors) is avoid-by-removal and is the natural news shape.
- **Misreads:**
  - Stating the news plainly with no inference: 'Too many applications? Close the scheme early' is a real action described straight, the same shape as the 'Tesla Roadster unveils October 1st' and 'Posting in fun so I get points' flops (10/23 flops).
  - Using the 'Can't X if you Y' words with a Y that does not actually remove X's precondition (a non-sequitur), as in 6/23 flops; the twist must be technically true.
  - Making the dodge a sensible workaround. The format praises a stupid-smart move; a genuinely good fix has no hole for the viewer to see.
  - Making it vague: 'skip the check' loses the concrete thing that is being removed ('the human check'), and the hole is only funny when you can name it.
  - Treating it as only one frame: the card forces 'Can't [problem] if you [dodge]', which is 12/70; loopholes (15/70) and fake syllogisms (23/70) are the bigger families.
  - Labeling the figure with a company logo by default; real uses leave him as the self-satisfied thinker (62/70), so a logo should appear only when the line is clearly that company's own logic.
- **Test:** Does the line state a problem or rule and then a smug move or conclusion that is technically true but obviously dumb or self-defeating (not a sensible fix and not just a description of the news)?
- **Slot problems:** The one wall box asks for the whole joke in 48 characters, while 42/70 real uses split it into a premise box and a twist box (2-box p50 33 + 27 = 60 chars), and the split is where the punch lands. The MUST also forces one frame ('Can't [problem] if you [dodge]', 12/70) and asks for 'a real excuse or workaround', which steers the writer to plain workarounds (the main flop shape) instead of technically-true-but-self-defeating logic. The jacket logo has no real counterpart (0/70) and is fine only as optional. Proposal: Splits the one box into the premise/twist pair that 42/70 real uses have, gives the line the real length (~60 chars total instead of 48), keeps the twist clear of the face (face box ends at x 0.43) and of the 'Opening' sign (x > 0.86, y > 0.65), and rewrites the MUST from 'a real workaround' to 'technically true but self-defeating', which is what separates winners from the plain-statement flops.
- **Our examples:**
  - `avoid-by-removal` · An AI lab stops publishing the safety test results for its new models. → premise: Can't fail the safety report / twist: if you stop publishing it
  - `letter-of-the-rule-loophole` · A chatbot's free plan now caps users at a few messages a day. → premise: Free plan: 10 messages a day / twist: [viewer AS-04] So I made a second email
  - `fake-smart-syllogism` · A study finds chatbots agree with users far too often. → premise: If the chatbot agrees with everything / twist: [viewer AS-14] then I've never been wrong
  - `avoid-by-removal` · A social app removes public like counts after complaints about its engagement numbers. → premise: Can't have falling likes / twist: if nobody can see the likes
  - `letter-of-the-rule-loophole` · Streaming services keep raising prices every few months. → premise: Price goes up next month / twist: [viewer AS-02] so I cancel the day before, every month
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) dodge=Can't find errors if you skip the human check: Avoid-by-removal: the problem (errors) disappears from view by removing the thing that finds it. True to a story about cutting human translators, concrete, and the hole is obvious.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) dodge=Can't catch errors if you skip the check: Same correct shape, but 'the check' is vaguer than 'the human check' and drops the story's specific; the hole is less visible.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) dodge=Too many applications? Close the scheme early: A question plus an imperative that just describes what an employer did. No technically-true-but-dumb inference; 'scheme' is jargon. The shape would be 'Can't get too many applications / if you close the job early'.

### Friendship ended (`friendship-ended`)
Coded 70 top uses, 6 tech memes, 12 flops. News fit: **strong** (Companies publicly replacing a partner, supplier, default model or in-house tool, and users or developers moving en masse, are frequent news turns, and the melodrama of a public breakup carries the reversal well. It fails on 'added a second partner' stories with no drop, and the joke is thin unless the switch is widely recognized or the new friend is ironic.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** It stages a switch of preference or allegiance as a solemn, public breakup and rebound, with the old friend literally X'd out; the laugh is the melodrama applied to a trivial or fickle swap, and recognizing a shift the whole crowd made. The strongest uses add irony: the new 'best friend' is worse, darker or the enemy, or the switch is triggered by something tiny.
- **Picture:** A garish Pakistani-Facebook-style photo: two men shake hands in a tight clasp at the centre, with rainbow-gradient text 'Friendship ended with MUDASIR' across the top and 'Now SALMAN is my best friend' stacked between their heads; two small inset photos of a third man in a red polo (the dumped MUDASIR) sit bottom left and bottom right, each crossed out with a big green X. The real format pastes new names over MUDASIR (B1) and SALMAN (B2).
- **Figure:** The speaker (the man shaking the new friend's hand) is the implied poster/'me' in 49/70; a named group or fandom in 13/70 (in a box: 'YouTube Kids', 'Me when…', 'Italy in WW2 be like'; or in the title: 'Kids', 'every 9yr old', 'Pokemon fans', 'gamers'); a country in 6/70 (China in 1979, Italy in WW2); unclear or broken in 2/70. Named speaker in a box 9/70, and in the box or title in 8 of the top 20 vs 0 of 12 flops.
- **Voice:** Bare names or noun phrases in both boxes in 61/70 (no verbs, no articles); all caps 60/70 (Imgflip default). B1 and B2 p50 8 characters each, p90 15 and 19. Speaker labels are 'ME', a group name, or '[X] be like' (9/70). No sentences or punctuation in the name boxes in 66/70; the template's own printed words supply the grammar.
- **Boxes:** B1 → ended (over 'MUDASIR' in the top line: the old ally, product or thing being dropped; a bare name or noun phrase); B2 → new-friend (over 'SALMAN' between the two heads: the replacement in the same role; a bare name or noun phrase, usually the newer or trending one); B3 → speaker (user-added caption, often low on the image: who is switching ('ME', 'Italy in WW2 be like', 'YouTube Kids every time a new game gets popular') in 9/20 posts with 3+ boxes; otherwise a grammar patch ('ARE', 'OUR') or a hashtag)
- **Patterns:**
  - `crowd-moves-on` (64%, melodrama for a trivial switch, recognition): [ended]: the thing everyone used or liked / [new-friend]: the newer thing in the same slot that the crowd moved to; [speaker] optional: the group. Real shape: Gamers dumping the old console for the new one the week it launched.
  - `ironic-new-friend` (14%, inversion, self-own): [ended]: the healthy, normal or loyal option / [new-friend]: a worse, darker or self-defeating replacement the speaker proudly picks. Real shape: A gamer dropping real life for an online game; toddlers trading food for small toys.
  - `allegiance-flip` (9%, historical or political betrayal as friendship drama): [ended]: one side or partner / [new-friend]: its rival; [speaker]: the country or player that switched sides, often with 'in [year] be like'. Real shape: A country in the late 1970s ditching one superpower for the other.
- **Slot targets:** ended p50 8 / p90 15, bare name or short noun phrase (logo for a company); new-friend p50 8 / p90 19, bare name or short noun phrase in the same category as ended; speaker p50 9 / p90 30, logo of the switching company, or a short group label ('Every coder', 'Me, mid-project')
- **Winners vs flops:** Low-engagement template (top post 35 upvotes), so the gap is small. Flops have the same A-to-B shape as winners (10/12 are a same-category swap), but 6/12 name a niche or unknown pair, or a pair with no known shift (eito/ghost boy, Vyond/FlashThemes, AMD/Broadcom), and 0/12 name who is switching, vs 8 of the top 20. 2/12 misuse it (two opposite statements about pineapple pizza; a reworded pun). Winners name a switch a recognizable crowd was visibly making (Among Us to Roblox, PS4 to PS5, iFunny to Imgflip) or give it an ironic new friend (the top post).
- **Tech memes:** No tagged tech memes. 6/70 top uses are software or hardware swaps (RJ-45 to SFP+, Java to Python, Moon Animator to Blender, Steam chat to Discord, Forge to Fabric, TLauncher to SKLauncher), all crowd-moves-on, scoring 2–4; the one tech flop (AMD to Broadcom, 0) names a pair with no known switch. For news it fits a company publicly replacing a partner, supplier or default, and users moving apps.
- **Misreads:**
  - Two names with no real switch between them (the AMD/Broadcom flop): the old and new must fill the same role and the facts must show one replaced the other.
  - Using it for 'X added Y alongside X': adding a second partner is not a friendship ended; the old one must be dropped.
  - Leaving the switcher unclear when it isn't the viewer: winners name the group or player (8/20 top) and flops never do (0/12).
  - Writing sentences in the name boxes ('Now we use X for search'): the printed words are the grammar; the boxes take bare names (61/70).
  - Niche names a casual follower doesn't know (6/12 flops): both sides must be instantly recognizable.
- **Test:** Do the two names fill the same role, with the first plainly dropped and the second taking its place, and is it clear who made the switch?
- **Slot problems:** Mostly right: two name slots over the printed names match 68/70 real uses, and the 24-character cap is above the real p90. One gap: the speaker slot accepts only an entity, but in real uses the switcher is a group or 'me' as often as a single player (13/70 groups, 49/70 implied me), so a crowd or viewer switch (devs moving tools, users moving apps, AS-13, AS-19) cannot name its speaker. Let it take a short text or viewer label too. Proposal: Boxes unchanged. The speaker slot gains text and viewer kinds so crowd and viewer switches can name who is switching (named speakers appear in 8 of the top 20 and 0 of 12 flops), and the MUST text rules out 'added alongside' stories that are not a real drop.
- **Our examples:**
  - `crowd-moves-on` · Questions posted to a big coding Q&A site keep falling as programmers ask chatbots instead. → ended: Stack Overflow / new-friend: The chatbot / speaker: Every coder
  - `crowd-moves-on` · Apple replaced Intel processors in its Macs with its own chips. → ended: [logo: intel] / new-friend: Own chips / speaker: [logo: apple]
  - `ironic-new-friend` · A retailer cuts its phone support team and sends all customer calls to an AI bot. → ended: Support team / new-friend: FAQ bot
  - `crowd-moves-on` · A lab ships a new model two weeks after the last one. → ended: Last week's model / new-friend: This week's / speaker: [viewer AS-19] Me, mid-project
  - `ironic-new-friend` · A popular app's redesign buries features users relied on. → ended: The new update / new-friend: Old version / speaker: [viewer AS-13] Me, rolling back

### Hide the Pain Harold (`hide-the-pain`)
Coded 70 top uses, 4 tech memes, 26 flops. News fit: **strong** (Backfires, flops, delays and spent-a-fortune-got-little results are constant in tech news, and hope-then-deflate carries them directly with a real number as the reveal; the viewer version carries AS-12, AS-14 and AS-15. It is weak only when the story has no disappointing result, and the deadpan-groaner half of the corpus does not transfer to news.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** The strained smile is someone insisting everything is fine while it plainly isn't. The top line sets up something normal or hopeful, the bottom line reveals the specific thing that hurts (a deflating result, a self-own), and the grin that covers it is the laugh. In this corpus he is just as often the deadpan teller of a dad or dark joke (37/70), where the groan is the pain.
- **Picture:** Two stacked stock photos of the same white-haired, bearded older man at a desk with a laptop, holding a pink mug: in the top panel he smiles mildly at the screen; in the bottom panel he looks at the camera with a strained, fixed grin that reads as hidden pain. Pale wall with two small plants and a ring ornament fills the left of each panel. Imgflip's B1 sits in the top panel and B2 in the bottom panel; our card puts the pain on the top wall and a logo on his shirt in the bottom panel.
- **Figure:** Harold is the poster/'me' telling the story in 63/70 (56/70 use I/my/me; 6 start 'When you…'); an outside group or institution grinning through its own bad act in 3/70 (a government reopening schools, the British leaving India); no figure role in 4/70. 0/70 label him as a company.
- **Voice:** First-person past-tense anecdote or statement in 56/70 ('I made…', 'My wife…', 13/70 are 'my wife' jokes); 'When you…' 6/70; quoted speech in 15/70, usually in B2. Two-box uses split setup/reveal in 55/70; the B2 reveal is shorter than B1 (p50 37 vs 49 chars). Ellipses link the two boxes in 4/70. Mostly all caps (Imgflip default) with full sentences; punctuation kept.
- **Boxes:** B1 → pain (top panel (our box: the wall left of his head): the setup: the situation, effort or hope ('I made a meme about Sprite…', 'Resolution to lose 15 pounds'); in 1-box uses the whole joke); B2 → null (bottom panel, beside the strained grin: the reveal: what actually happened, the deflating number or the punchline ('…it only got 7 ups', 'Only 30 more to go'); we have no text slot here); Bnull → smiler (his shirt, bottom panel: no real counterpart (0/70 label him); only useful when the pain belongs to a company)
- **Patterns:**
  - `hope-then-deflate` (33%, expectation vs reality, understatement): [setup]: the effort, plan or hope, stated cheerfully / [pain]: the specific result that crushes it, often a small or wrong number. Real shape: Posted a meme about a soda brand; it got single-digit upvotes.
  - `deadpan-groaner` (53%, pun, dark twist): [setup]: a first-person anecdote / [pain]: a pun or dark punchline delivered flatly. Real shape: Told the doctor no one in the family suffers from mental illness, because they all enjoy it.
  - `grinning-through-their-own-act` (9%, denial, irony): [setup]: an institution or crowd doing something obviously bad / [pain]: its cheerful 'no problem here' line or the obvious cost. Real shape: A government sending kids back to school mid-pandemic, captioned 'I don't see a problem here.'
- **Slot targets:** pain p50 37 / p90 59, today: the whole joke in one top box (real 1-box p50 74); proposed: the bottom reveal, a short clause with a concrete number or result; setup p50 49 / p90 75, first-person or plain statement of the effort, plan or hope; hope-then-deflate winners run shorter, ~25–45; smiler p50 0 / p90 0, logo only, optional; only when the pain is a company's
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners have a B2 that turns B1: 60/70 deliver a reveal or punchline the setup didn't give away. Flops: 10/26 state the pain flatly with no turn ('Nintendo raised Switch 2 prices / I can't buy one because I'm broke', 'The power just went out / pray for 6am'); 6/26 are private or garbled; 8/26 are weak puns or groaners (the deadpan-groaner form only works when the pun is tight); 2/26 are decent realizations. The top post works because the deflating detail is tiny and exact ('7 ups').
- **Tech memes:** 4 tagged tech posts, top score 5: too few to say. The one AI post (an AI coding assistant 'fixing' an issue by rewriting thousands of files, 'that's fine, right?') is hope-then-deflate with an exact number, the right shape for news.
- **Misreads:**
  - Putting the whole pain in one top box: the bad news then sits over the calm top face and the strained grin below gets only a logo; real uses reveal the pain in the bottom panel (55/70 are two-box setup/reveal).
  - Stating the pain as a plain complaint with no setup to deflate (10/26 flops, including a price-hike news line).
  - A vague pain ('results were disappointing') where winners name the exact small number or result ('7 ups', '30 more to go').
  - Labeling Harold as a company by default: he is 'me' in 63/70; a company label works only when the setup is that company's own cheerful plan.
  - Pain that isn't in the facts ('Still second' when the story only says 'catching up'): the deflating number must be a reported number.
- **Test:** Does the top line set up something that looks fine or hopeful, and the bottom line reveal the specific result that hurts, so the grin reads as covering it?
- **Slot problems:** The card has one text slot, in the top panel, for 'the pain', and a logo in the bottom panel. Real uses (55/70) put a setup in the top panel, where Harold looks content, and the painful reveal in the bottom panel, where the grin strains; the bottom text is the punch. Our layout delivers the pain over the wrong face and leaves no room for the setup that makes the reveal land. The logo slot has no real counterpart and should be optional. Proposal: Matches the setup/reveal split of 55/70 real uses and puts the pain beside the strained grin, where the picture delivers it. The new bottom box (x 0-0.57, y 0.5-0.7) ends left of the bottom face (x 0.58) and sits over the pale wall and plants; the top box is unchanged.
- **Our examples:**
  - `hope-then-deflate` · A big AI firm spends heavily on a new flagship model, but users keep choosing the previous version. → setup: Spent billions on the new model / pain: Users switched back to the old one
  - `hope-then-deflate` · A viral screenshot shows a chatbot confidently confirming a wrong calculation. → setup: Asked the AI to check my math / pain: [viewer AS-14] It agreed with my wrong answer
  - `hope-then-deflate` · A consortium announces a $500 billion AI infrastructure plan. → setup: $500 billion for AI data centers / pain: [viewer AS-12] Me, debating a $4 coffee
  - `hope-then-deflate` · An AI travel agent books a user's trip to a town with the same name in the wrong country. → setup: Let the AI agent book my trip / pain: [viewer AS-15] Landing in Paris, Texas
  - `grinning-through-their-own-act` · A company pushes its assistant upgrade back by another year. → setup: Smart assistant coming next year / pain: Same 'next year' as last year
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) pain=Spent $14.3 billion. Still second. ; smiler=[logo Meta]: Right engine (big effort, deflating result, exact number), but setup and reveal are crammed into the top box over the calm face, and 'Still second' must be a fact: a story about catching OpenAI and Anthropic suggests behind both, so the rank needs checking. Split it: top 'Spent $14.3 billion on AI', bottom the reported result.

### Spider Man Triple (`spiderman-triple`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 35 flops. News fit: **strong** (Copycat launches, matching price moves, renamed products and the same claim from every lab are constant tech news, and same-thing-three-names carries that turn directly; outages and leaks give the blame circle. It cannot carry a contradiction or a contrast, which is a different format.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** Three things with different names point at each other and turn out to be the same thing: the joke is the recognition that the 'different' options are copies (2x2, 2+2, 2²; Shorts, TikTok, Reels). The second use is the blame circle: three parties each point at another for something they all share, so nobody takes it.
- **Picture:** Cartoon of three near-identical Spider-Men in a warehouse alley: one on the left in the classic suit pointing right, one in the middle (standing back) pointing with both hands at the other two, and one on the right seen from behind in the newer suit pointing back at the middle one. Each label sits on or near its Spider-Man; real 4-box uses add a caption box on top. Note: the packet's Know Your Meme section is about an unrelated TikTok audio, not this format.
- **Figure:** Each Spider-Man is a thing or name that is secretly the same as the other two in 43/70 (numbers, words, flags, products, characters); a person in a blame situation in 12/70 (family members, coworkers, nations); a part of 'me' in 4/70 (me, the voices, my life); literal lookalike people (three Spider-Man actors, kids in the same costume) in 6/70; unclear in 5/70.
- **Voice:** Bare names or noun phrases in 52/70 (no verbs); blame uses add short quoted accusations ('IT'S YOUR JOB', 'NO U DID') in 5/70; captions start 'POV:', 'When…' or name a group in 13/70. 3-box p50 6/7/10 characters, the right box longest. No end punctuation in 61/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → left (the left Spider-Man (in 4-box uses B1 is a top caption and B2–B4 are left, middle, right): the first lookalike's name or label); B2 → middle (the middle Spider-Man, pointing at both: the second lookalike); B3 → right (the right Spider-Man, seen from behind: the third lookalike, often the least obvious member ('Square root of 4', 'EAU', Poland's flag 'the other way around'); longest box, p50 10 vs 6–7); Bnull → null (top caption (B1 in the 12 four-box uses; the whole text in 7 one-box uses): the situation or the shared thing ('POV: your mother asks who stole her credit card', 'Companies that have terrible attrition'); we have no slot for it)
- **Patterns:**
  - `same-thing-three-names` (61%, false difference, recognition): [left] / [middle] / [right]: three names for one thing (equivalent values, homophones, renamed products, copycat features), with the least obvious one last. Real shape: Three short-video feeds from three apps, labeled as the same Spider-Man.
  - `blame-circle` (17%, buck-passing, hypocrisy): [caption]: who did it? / [left] [middle] [right]: three parties who each point at another for a thing they all share. Real shape: Three departments each telling the others that a repair is their job.
  - `literal-lookalikes` (9%, literal recognition): [caption]: a situation where identical-looking people meet (same costume, same skin, same actor role). Real shape: Kids at a school party all arriving in the same costume.
- **Slot targets:** left p50 6 / p90 18, bare name, logo, or product/feature name; middle p50 7 / p90 18, bare name, logo, or product/feature name; right p50 10 / p90 30, bare name; the least obvious member of the three; caption p50 25 / p90 47, the shared thing or the 'who did it?' situation, as a noun phrase or 'POV:'/'When…' clause
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the sameness instantly checkable: 31 of the 43 same-thing uses are things a viewer can verify at a glance (identical flags, equal sums, homophones, obvious copycat apps). Flops: 21/35 list three things that are merely in the same category rather than the same (PowerPoint/Canva/Google Slides; Spanish/Portuguese/Italian; three private names); 7/35 are blame circles with no specific accuser or stakes (the blame-circle format is saturated, and repeats score 0–2); 7/35 are one-box narratives or overlong captions. Winners also repeat proven jokes (the 2x2 math joke is 10/70 top uses), so recognizability beats novelty here.
- **Tech memes:** 2 tagged tech posts (2 and 0 upvotes): pointer assignments and a chemical-symbol trio. In the top list, 'Shorts / TikTok / Reels' (66) is the copycat-product case that fits news best, and 'AI / AI / AI' (titled AI flagging AI, 7) shows the accuse-each-other-of-being-the-same twist. A flop 'AI Detector / AI / People that use AI detectors' fails because the three are not the same thing.
- **Misreads:**
  - Labeling three things that are different or opposite: our Anthropic meme put two opposite court rulings on two Spider-Men; the format says the three are the same.
  - Using a Spider-Man as a caption ('Opposite rulings' on the right Spider-Man) because there is no caption slot; every Spider-Man must be one of the lookalikes.
  - Three company logos with nothing that shows what they share: logos alone don't tell the viewer which move is identical; the shared move must be named (caption or feature names).
  - Three things that are merely in the same category (three slide apps, three languages; 21/35 flops) instead of copies of each other.
  - Treating the right Spider-Man as a punch line: the three are equals; the right one is only the least obvious member.
- **Test:** Are the three labels plainly the same thing or the same move under different names (or three parties each blaming another for one shared thing), with what they share visible from the meme?
- **Slot problems:** The three label slots match the 49 three-box uses, and the MUST already demands the same kind of move. Missing: the caption box that 12 four-box uses (and 7 one-box uses) carry, which names the situation or the shared thing. For news this is essential: three company logos don't show what move is identical, and without a caption the writer jams the situation into a Spider-Man (our meme). Also, 'PUNCH slot: right' is wrong: the three are equals, the sameness is the punch. Proposal: Adds the caption real 4-box uses carry (12/70), so logo trios can show what is identical and the writer never spends a Spider-Man on a caption. The new box sits on open floor (x 0.30-0.66, y 0.88-0.99) clear of all three faces; the three label boxes are unchanged.
- **Our examples:**
  - `same-thing-three-names` · Three AI companies shipped near-identical 'deep research' modes within a few months of each other. → left: [logo: openai] / middle: [logo: google] / right: [logo: perplexity] / caption: Deep research mode
  - `same-thing-three-names` · Google's chatbot was renamed and re-tiered, so the same product has carried several names. → left: Bard / middle: Gemini / right: Gemini Advanced / caption: [viewer AS-16] Me, finding the app
  - `blame-circle` · After a large outage, the cloud host, a CDN and a DNS vendor each point to another as the cause. → left: Cloud host / middle: CDN / right: DNS provider / caption: Who broke the internet?
  - `same-thing-three-names` · Three labs each launch a model billed as their best ever within the same week. → left: [logo: openai] / middle: [logo: google] / right: [logo: anthropic] / caption: [viewer AS-05] Best model ever, this week
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) left=SF judge: for Anthropic ; middle=D.C. panel: upheld 2-1 ; right=Opposite rulings: The two rulings are opposites, so the three Spider-Men are not the same thing; the right Spider-Man holds a caption, not a lookalike; and 'upheld 2-1' doesn't say what was upheld. It is a contradiction story, which this format can't carry (a two-panel contrast format fits it).

### where monkey (`where-monkey`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 27 flops. News fit: **strong** (Delayed rollouts, keynote promises, missing features and hype for the next thing a day after launch are constant tech news, and the too-soon-or-never-came setup carries that turn directly, in the viewer's voice for AS-06, AS-11 and AS-15. It needs the setup slot; a bare 'Where X?' alone is the flop shape.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** Dim, blank-faced monkeys demand something in caveman grammar ('where phone'), and the laugh is the gap the setup reveals: the thing is obviously right there, or they want the reward absurdly soon (six-pack after ten sit-ups, the next game a day after release), or it was promised and never came. The setup carries who and when; 'where X' is the dumb, entitled punch.
- **Picture:** Three orangutans sit on the red sofa and desk of a TV breakfast-news set, staring blankly in different directions with a mug and papers in front of them; city skyline windows behind. Imgflip's B1 is a top caption (the setup) and B2 a bottom caption ('where X'); our card has only the bottom box, with 'Where {x}?' printed by code. Note: the packet's Know Your Meme section is about an unrelated TikTok audio, not this format.
- **Figure:** The monkeys are the poster/'me' (often 'me and the boys') in 45/70; a named group or institution in 14/70 (toddlers, GTA fans, the IRS, EA, the British after WW2, Minecraft devs); no figure role (random monkey reaction) in 11/70.
- **Voice:** 'where X' appears in 53/70; X is a bare noun with no article or verb in 51/53 (p50 6 chars, p90 13), with a question mark in only 11/53. The setup is a 'Me when…' / '[Group] when…' / '[Who] after…' clause ending with a colon in 16/61 multi-box uses; first person in 45/70. Absurd specific timing ('5 seconds later', '10 situps', '1 day old', 'every 2 hours') in 17/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → null (top caption: the setup: who is asking and when ('Me when my phone is literally in my hand:', '10 year old me after doing 10 situps'); we have no slot for it); B2 → missing (bottom caption across the desk: 'where X' in caveman grammar: a bare noun, no article, no verb (code prints 'Where {x}?')); B3 → null (extra caption (13/70): a middle step ('Me after five minutes') or a speaker label)
- **Patterns:**
  - `too-soon-or-never-came` (56%, impatience, expectation vs reality): [asker]: who, right after a trivial effort or a promise ('Me 5 seconds after ordering') / [missing]: the reward or promised thing, as a bare noun. Real shape: Fans demanding the next sequel one day after the last one shipped.
  - `searching-for-the-obvious` (11%, absurd obliviousness): [asker]: who, while the thing is plainly in reach or just happened / [missing]: that thing. Real shape: Looking for your phone while holding it.
  - `where-quality` (10%, deadpan critique): [asker]: a product, show or content stream / [missing]: the quality it conspicuously lacks ('funny', 'fun', 'graphics'). Real shape: Watching a short-video feed and asking where the fun went.
- **Slot targets:** missing p50 6 / p90 13, bare noun, 1–2 words, no article ('feature', 'charger', 'next model'); asker p50 37 / p90 68, 'Me/[Group] when…' or '[Who], [time] after [trigger]' clause, often with an exact time or count
- **Winners vs flops:** 61/70 winners have a setup box that says who and when; the setup is where the joke's gap lives. Flops: 15/27 have no 'where' at all and use the monkeys as a random reaction; 5/27 are a bare 'where X' with no setup (a fan demanding an unannounced sequel, 'Where Titanfall 3', scores 0, while 'GTA fans when GTA 6 becomes 1 day old / where GTA 7' scores 8); 3/27 have a setup but the absence is literal and unremarkable (the internet goes out, 'where internet'). The difference is a setup that makes the demand absurd: too soon, obviously present, or long-promised.
- **Tech memes:** 2 tagged tech posts (2 and 0 upvotes: a debugger asking for a semicolon, a bare 'Where JIRA issue?'). In the top list, product and company jabs score 6–11: a game studio's yearly release, a version-numbering change, maintenance downtime, a studio's graphics. News uses fit too-soon-or-never-came: the feature promised in a keynote, the rollout 'in the coming weeks'.
- **Misreads:**
  - A bare 'Where [X]?' with no setup, which is all our card allows: it reads as a plain demand (the 'Where Titanfall 3' flop shape); 61/70 winners say who is asking and when.
  - An absence that is simply true and unremarkable ('where internet' when the internet is down): the setup must make the demand absurd (too soon, obviously present, long-promised).
  - Writing X as a phrase or sentence ('the promised AI upgrade for older phones'): real X is a bare noun, p90 13 characters.
  - Using the monkeys as a generic blank reaction with no 'where' (15/27 flops).
  - An absence not in the facts: a feature 'missing' that was never promised or is already out.
- **Test:** Does a setup say who is asking and when, and does 'where X' ask for a bare noun whose absence is absurd given that setup (obviously there, demanded too soon, or promised and never delivered)?
- **Slot problems:** The card has one slot, the bottom 'Where {x}?', and no setup. Real uses put the setup in a top caption in 61/70: who is asking and when, which is where the joke's gap lives (too soon, obviously there, promised long ago). Without it the meme is the bare-demand flop shape. The 26-character cap on X is also double the real p90 (13); X should be a bare noun. Proposal: Adds the setup caption 61/70 real uses carry and that separates winners from the bare-demand flops, and tightens X to the real bare-noun length. The new top box (y 0.01–0.15) ends above all three faces (the highest starts at y 0.16).
- **Our examples:**
  - `too-soon-or-never-came` · A phone maker announces an AI feature that will roll out 'over the coming weeks'. → asker: [viewer AS-11] Me, one minute after the keynote / missing: feature
  - `too-soon-or-never-came` · A launch demo shows an assistant doing everything; the shipped app can't do most of it. → asker: [viewer AS-06] Me opening the app after the demo / missing: magic
  - `too-soon-or-never-came` · A carmaker's long-promised driverless taxi service slips again. → asker: Riders, year six of 'next year' / missing: robotaxi
  - `too-soon-or-never-came` · A phone maker stops including a charger in the box. → asker: Me unboxing my new phone / missing: charger
  - `searching-for-the-obvious` · A browser adds an AI sidebar that opens on every page by default. → asker: [viewer AS-15] Me, sidebar open on every tab / missing: off switch

### Boardroom Meeting Suggestion (`boardroom`)
Coded 70 top uses, 3 tech memes, 21 flops. News fit: **strong** (It carries the most common tech-news turn: a company answering backlash by doubling down (more ads, higher prices, more AI no one asked for) while ignoring the obvious fix. The rejected idea comes straight from the users' complaint in the story. It is weak for neutral launches with no problem.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 2.
- **Mechanism:** The organization asks how to fix a problem it caused; the two welcomed ideas double down on the cause, and the one obvious fix everyone outside the room wants is the one that gets its speaker thrown out. The joke is that the sensible answer is unthinkable inside the room, which indicts the institution.
- **Picture:** Three-tier comic. Top: an angry boss in a brown suit leans on the table under a wide speech bubble, staff watching. Middle: three staffers each under a bubble; the first two are eager (one with a raised finger), the third leans back relaxed with the widest bubble. Bottom: the boss's furious close-up, the relaxed guy's calm face, and a tall building with him flying out of a smashed window.
- **Figure:** The window guy is the voice of the sensible, obvious or customer-wanted answer in 32/70 (29 a company or institution's obvious fix, 3 the dutiful option in a 'me' meeting); the boss stands for a named company or institution (Netflix, YouTube, Disney x8, Apple, Lays, Roblox, schools) in 27/70, for 'me' or the poster in 5/70, and is generic in the rest. In 10/70 the window guy's answer is about the window itself (meta).
- **Voice:** Question is a spoken line, problem + 'what should we do?' or 'any ideas?' in 24/70, often opening with 'Alright/Ok/We need'. Ideas are short imperatives or noun phrases; 'More X' / 'Add X' form in 12/70 posts. The rejected idea is the longest middle box (4-box p50 18 vs 12–13) and starts with 'Stop' in 8/70 or carries 'actually'/'just' in 9/70 ('make an actually good movie', 'just freeze water'). Sentence case and caps both common; exclamation marks on the welcomed ideas in 7/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → question (top speech bubble (the boss): the problem plus 'what do we do?'; in 3-box posts B1 also holds idea-1 (20/70), and 11/70 put the whole dialogue in B1); B2 → idea-1 (middle row, left bubble: the first welcomed idea: more of the thing causing the problem); B3 → idea-2 (middle row, middle bubble: the second welcomed idea, parallel to the first (in 3-box posts this box is the rejected idea)); B4 → rejected (middle row, right bubble (the man thrown out the window): the obvious, sensible fix the audience wants); B5 → null (extended or reversed versions (extra panels): follow-up dialogue in 8/70 posts with 5+ boxes; no slot of ours)
- **Patterns:**
  - `sensible-fix-ejected` (41%, hypocrisy, institutional self-sabotage): [boss]: the company / [question]: the problem it caused + 'what do we do?' / [idea-1], [idea-2]: two moves that do more of the cause / [rejected]: the plain fix customers ask for, often 'Stop X' or 'Actually Y'. Real shape: A streamer losing subscribers is pitched more weak originals and more price hikes; the guy who says stop raising prices goes out the window.
  - `duty-ejected` (4%, self-knowledge): [question]: me facing a choice ('bedtime, what now?') / [idea-1], [idea-2]: two indulgences / [rejected]: the responsible option. Real shape: At bedtime the board votes for videos and scrolling; 'go to bed' is thrown out.
  - `window-meta` (14%, self-reference, literalism): [question]: a problem about windows, injuries or staff / [idea-1], [idea-2]: indirect fixes / [rejected]: 'stop throwing me out the window'. Real shape: The board asks how to cut window repair costs; the guy suggests not throwing him through them.
  - `punchline-answer` (16%, wordplay, absurd escalation): [question]: a quiz or prompt / [idea-1], [idea-2]: straight answers / [rejected]: a pun, brainrot or reference that is the actual joke. Real shape: Asked for chemical symbols, the third answer spells a slang word out of them.
  - `blunt-truth-ejected` (9%, honesty punished): [question]: asking for opinions or advice / [idea-1], [idea-2]: agreeable answers / [rejected]: the blunt or unpopular truth. Real shape: Told the boss plans to destroy the world, two say 'cool'; the third asks where he'll live.
- **Slot targets:** question p50 35 / p90 54, spoken problem + short question ('X is falling. Ideas?'); idea-1 p50 12 / p90 23, imperative or 'More X' noun phrase; idea-2 p50 13 / p90 21, imperative or noun phrase, parallel to idea-1; rejected p50 18 / p90 34, plain imperative fix, often 'Stop X' / 'Just X' / 'Actually X'; boss p50 8 / p90 12, company name or logo (named in a box or title in 27/70)
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the two welcomed ideas visibly worse than the ejected one, so the ejection indicts the room (sensible-fix-ejected 29/70, meta 10/70). Flops: 8/21 rely on niche references (Team Cherry, Castle Make and Play, Hive Inc, Monster Jam, Valve TF2); 5/21 offer three neutral options with no sensible-vs-self-serving contrast (Halloween costumes, sleep/eat/think); 5/21 cram long sentences into one box; 5/21 extend to 5–8 boxes of dialogue.
- **Tech memes:** The 3 tech-tagged posts all got 0 upvotes (programming languages, Azure savings, brace style): fair technical options with no institution doubling down. AI themes in the top list work as sensible-fix-ejected against a platform: YouTube's AI verification (24↑), Roblox's AI face scan (28↑), pro-AI companies ignoring an opt-out (23↑).
- **Misreads:**
  - Reading the card's 'Logic' line ('a dumb idea gets thrown out'): in 32/70 the ejected idea is the sensible one; only 2/70 eject a genuinely dumb idea.
  - Filling idea-1 and idea-2 with neutral features or real plans from the story (our Shopify 'Personal shopper / Budget rules'): the welcomed ideas must be the self-serving or problem-feeding moves, or the ejection means nothing.
  - A slogan or the headline as an idea ('Minutes, not weeks'): the ideas are pitches in a meeting, not taglines.
  - An ejected idea the reader cannot see is sensible ('Just an evaluation'): the rejected bubble must be the obvious fix in plain words.
  - Three neutral options, the flop shape (5/21 flops, 3/3 tech posts).
  - A boss nobody knows (Industrial Next): the institution must be one readers already resent or recognize.
- **Test:** Is the idea thrown out the window the obvious, sensible fix (what users or common sense want), while the two welcomed ideas feed the problem or dodge it?
- **Slot problems:** Ids and geometry fit the real 4-box layout (23/70, the most common).
