The card's Logic line ('a dumb idea gets thrown out the window') contradicts real use (the sensible idea is thrown out in 32/70). idea-1 and idea-2 ask for 'an answer that goes over well: a real option in the story', which produces neutral features; real welcomed ideas feed the problem. The question MUST does not ask for a problem the company caused. The rejected slot cannot take a viewer line, though it often voices exactly what users ask for. Proposal: Same ids and boxes; the Logic line becomes 'the sensible idea gets thrown out the window', the two welcomed ideas must feed the problem, and the rejected idea must be the obvious fix (sensible-fix-ejected, 29/70 winners). Our two memes failed on exactly these musts.
- **Our examples:**
  - `sensible-fix-ejected` · A search engine answers complaints about its AI summaries by adding more AI summaries. → boss: [logo: google] / question: People hate the AI answers. Ideas? / idea-1: More AI answers / idea-2: Bigger AI answers / rejected: An off switch
  - `sensible-fix-ejected` · A chatbot app cuts its free tier while launching a pricier plan. → question: Free users are leaving. Ideas? / idea-1: A pricier plan / idea-2: Fewer free chats / rejected: [viewer AS-04] Keep the free tier
  - `sensible-fix-ejected` · A phone maker's AI notification summaries keep garbling news headlines. → question: The summaries are wrong. Fix? / idea-1: Add a disclaimer / idea-2: Italic font / rejected: Turn them off
  - `duty-ejected` · A new model ships two days before a user's project deadline. → question: Deadline is Friday. Plan? / idea-1: Try the new model / idea-2: Rewrite the prompts / rejected: [viewer AS-19] Finish the project
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) question=How do we sell it? ; idea-1=Personal shopper ; idea-2=Budget rules ; rejected=You approve every buy: The ejected line is a recognizable user-safety wish (confirm each purchase), which is the right kind of rejected idea. But the two welcomed ideas are neutral product features, not moves that feed a problem, and the question is a sales question rather than a problem the company caused, so the ejection does not indict anyone.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) question=How do we show it works? ; idea-1=Minutes, not weeks ; idea-2=Stage demo ; rejected=Just an evaluation: No problem, no doubling down, and 'Just an evaluation' is not an obvious fix anyone outside the room wants. 'Minutes, not weeks' is the headline used as an idea. Obscure company as the room.

### Types of Headaches (`types-of-headaches`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 34 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries the user-side cost of a news item (a paywall, ads, a rename, a breaking update) well, but only that: it has no slot for a reversal, a double standard or who is to blame, and it fails whenever the story's pain belongs to a company rather than a user.). Our memes misused it: 6 of 7.
- **Mechanism:** A petty, everyday annoyance is ranked as a worse pain than real medical conditions, so the whole head goes red over something tiny. The joke is the false equivalence plus instant recognition: the reader has felt exactly that, which makes the exaggeration feel true.
- **Picture:** A medical-style chart titled 'Types of Headaches' on white: three bald profile heads with red pain zones labeled Migraine, Hypertension and Stress, and a fourth head (bottom right) that is entirely red with no label. The one text box sits above the all-red head, level with the 'Stress' label.
- **Figure:** The red head is the sufferer: 'you/your' in 22/70, 'me/I' in 4/70, implied first or second person in 44/70. It is never a company or a third party in 70/70; the label is what the sufferer endures.
- **Voice:** Gerund phrase in 26/70 ('Getting jumpscared at 5 a.m.'), 'When…' clause in 18/70, bare noun label in 20/70 (8 of those are school words), noun + participle or a quoted message in 6/70. Second person 'you/your' in 22/70. No end punctuation in 66/70. Sentence or title case in 51/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → worst (above the all-red head (bottom right): the petty everyday annoyance the sufferer endures (69/70 use only this box))
- **Patterns:**
  - `others-ruin-it` (26%, petty vs serious, relatability): [worst]: someone (sibling, parent, a stranger) interrupting, spying on or messing with your thing, as a gerund or 'When…' clause. Real shape: A younger sibling playing on your computer.
  - `recurring-nuisance` (24%, petty vs serious): [worst]: a small, repeated nuisance of media or tech (ads, lag, wifi, autocorrect, an error message, a song on loop), as a noun or gerund phrase. Real shape: Two unskippable ads before a short video.
  - `so-close-fail` (20%, absurd specificity): [worst]: a tiny failure right at the end, or a small self-inflicted loss, with absurd specificity ('at 95%', 'three times in a row'). Real shape: Failing a long game level just before the finish.
  - `whole-institution` (10%, understatement, sweeping label): [worst]: one bare word for a whole hated thing (School, Homework, Math). Real shape: Just the word for the subject everyone dreads.
  - `literal-pain` (11%, literalism): [worst]: a physical pain gag that is literally all-head (paint, brain freeze, a kick). Real shape: Biting ice cream with your front teeth.
- **Slot targets:** worst p50 38 / p90 70, gerund phrase or 'When…' clause naming a specific annoyance the sufferer endures; one bare noun only for a universally hated thing
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners name an annoyance nearly every reader has felt (63/70 are school, family, games, ads or small mishaps). Flops: 9/34 lean on niche references (whirlpool, School F.A.S.T.s, Stipend, Subnautica, rival-game slang, a named person); 5/34 are too dark or personal to be petty (not waking up, a breakup, a cracked skull); 4/34 add a second box ('POV: Everyone', 'Me:'); 4/34 explain the situation at length; 1/34 is geopolitics. Winners stay petty: the comedy needs the pain to be small.
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts. Tech annoyances inside the top list do well as recurring-nuisance (unskippable ads 455↑ and 256↑, slow wifi 269↑, lag, autocorrect, 'username taken', a USB plugged in wrong): 10/70, all from the user's side of the screen.
- **Misreads:**
  - Writing a company's business constraint as the worst headache ('No robot training data', 'Terms still to be agreed'): in 70/70 real uses the head is a person suffering, not a firm.
  - Pasting a fact or deadline from the article ('Fees answer by October 26'): it must be a felt annoyance, not a data point.
  - Making the pain serious or medical ('Looking through a straw' for a vision-loss patient): the joke needs a petty pain; real suffering makes it cruel (5/34 flops are too dark).
  - Clipping to a 1–5 word noun label because the card says 'named the way the others are': real p50 is 38 characters and 44/70 are gerund or 'When' situations.
  - Niche references the reader has not lived (9/34 flops).
- **Test:** Is the worst headache a petty, instantly recognizable annoyance that a person suffers (the reader thinks 'ugh, yes'), rather than a company's constraint, an article fact, or a serious harm?
- **Slot problems:** The single slot and box are right (69/70 real uses fill only this box). What we ask for is wrong: the MUST asks for 'a real cost or constraint in the story' named like a medical label in 1 to 5 words, which yields company constraints and article facts (5 of our 7 memes). Real uses name a petty annoyance a person endures, usually as a gerund or 'When…' phrase (44/70) at p50 38 characters, and the slot cannot take a viewer line even though the card lists viewer situations. Proposal: The joke is a petty pain ranked above real conditions, felt by a person (70/70). Accepting viewer lines ties it to the card's AS situations, and the longer gerund form matches real p50 38. The box drops 0.02 in height so two lines stay off the top of the red head.
- **Our examples:**
  - `recurring-nuisance` · A chatbot starts showing ads in free-tier answers. → worst: [viewer AS-04] An ad before the chatbot answers
  - `recurring-nuisance` · A lab renames its model lineup for the second time this year. → worst: [viewer AS-16] Finding my model renamed again
  - `so-close-fail` · An API update quietly changes its output format and breaks scripts. → worst: [viewer AS-18] The API changing the night before a demo
  - `so-close-fail` · A stronger model ships a day after many users finished projects on the old one. → worst: [viewer AS-19] A better model the day after I ship
  - `recurring-nuisance` · A chatbot is caught citing sources that do not exist. → worst: [viewer AS-14] Checking a source the chatbot made up
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) worst=Cancelled trip, unanswered fees: A person's pain from the story (an attendee stuck with a cancelled trip and unanswered fees), short and concrete. It would be stronger as one annoyance ('Fees nobody answers about'), but it has the right sufferer.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) worst=$100-and-up tiers only: A real user pain (paywall, AS-02/AS-04) but written as a pricing fact, not as the experience ('Finding out it's $100 a month').
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) worst=Terms still to be agreed: A deal status between a company and a government; nobody's head hurts.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) worst=Fees answer by October 26: A deadline fact, not a felt annoyance; reads as a notice.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) worst=Latest Grok needs Premium: Paywall pain a user feels (AS-02), but phrased as a product fact; 'Needing Premium for the new Grok' puts the reader in the red head.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) worst=Looking through a straw: Turns a patient's real vision loss into the joke. The format needs a petty pain, and the story is good news for the patient.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) worst=No robot training data: A company's technical constraint; no person suffers it.

### Evil Kermit (`evil-kermit`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 24 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries hypocrisy (stated value vs what the company did) and the demo-vs-reality double well, and the viewer's own temptation (AS-02, AS-19) very well. It is risky for company stories because the writer must not invent a vice or a quoted intention; only facts that show a company doing the dark move qualify.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** The hooded figure is the corrupt double of the one in the light: the same self, thing or group, but the version that gives in, rots or preys. The joke is the recognizable gap between the respectable version and its shadow (I should sleep / one more episode; kid-friendly YouTuber / his old videos).
- **Picture:** Landscape still on a dark grey background: Kermit seen from behind on the left, facing a black-hooded, shadowed Kermit on the right who looks down at him. Real uses put text at the top (Kermit's side / setup) and bottom (the hooded one's line); our 'kermit' logo slot sits on Kermit's back.
- **Figure:** Plain Kermit is 'me' in 27/70 (B1 starts 'ME' or 'I'). The hooded Kermit is my own inner voice pushing a vice in 20/70, an external menace looming over the innocent one in 18/70, and the dark or degraded version of the same thing in 18/70; 13/70 are one-box dialogue or pun gags.
- **Voice:** B1 opens with 'ME' or 'ME:' in 27/70 and uses 'I should' in 6/70; B2 opens 'ALSO ME:' / 'INNER ME:' / 'MY INTRUSIVE THOUGHTS' in 7/70. In the temptation pattern B2 is an imperative or a permission ('you are allowed to…', 'do it at 8') in 12/20. Dark-twin pairs are parallel noun labels in 18/18 (Deep web / Dark web). ALL CAPS in 58/70 (Imgflip default). No end punctuation in 55/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → good (top (plain Kermit's side): who is tempted plus the good intention ('ME: I should…'), or the innocent version of a thing); B2 → inner (bottom (hooded Kermit): the inner voice's imperative, the menace, or the dark version); B3 → null (extra caption (5/70): a follow-up line; no slot of ours)
- **Patterns:**
  - `inner-temptation` (29%, self-knowledge, hypocrisy): [good]: 'Me: I should [virtuous plan]' / [inner]: 'Also me:' + an imperative pushing the lazy, indulgent or self-serving opposite. Real shape: Me planning to be productive; my other self telling me to stay on the computer all day.
  - `dark-twin` (26%, parallel contrast, wordplay): [good]: an innocent thing or group / [inner]: its sinister, degraded or later version, worded in parallel. Real shape: A family-friendly creator on top, the same creator's videos from five years ago below.
  - `looming-menace` (26%, dread, expectation vs reality): [good]: me (or a kid) enjoying something / [inner]: the thing about to ruin it. Real shape: Me enjoying free time; tomorrow's test in the hood.
  - `lazy-shortcut` (3%, petty vs proper): [good]: the real problem with the work / [inner]: the cheap shortcut that hides it, as an imperative. Real shape: The vocals are terrible; the hooded producer says to add echo.
- **Slot targets:** good p50 25 / p90 52, 'Me: I should…' first-person intention, or a short noun label for the innocent version; inner p50 24 / p90 60, imperative from the inner voice ('Also me: …'), or the parallel dark-version label; kermit p50 2 / p90 10, who is tempted: 'ME' in 27/70; a named company only in 1/70 (Google results)
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the shadow a clear corruption of the top line: a vice the reader shares (20/70) or a parallel dark version (18/70). Flops: 6/24 are one-box dialogue with no pair ('Can you please pass the Jelly?'); 6/24 rely on niche references (Technoblade, Charlie's demon form, Pokémon Champions); 3/24 have a menace too weak to dread (beef stew, the group chat). 5/24 flops use the classic temptation pattern but with a stale vice or an over-explained inner line (the houseplant spiral runs 90+ characters), so the pattern alone is not enough: the vice needs a specific twist (closing Chrome tabs vs using the overheating laptop as a hand warmer).
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts. Tech appears inside the top list as dark-twin (Google results / Bing results, 49↑) and inner-temptation (close Chrome tabs / laptop as hand warmer, 16↑; Duolingo's streak threat, 18↑). Too few to say more.
- **Misreads:**
  - A good line that is a corporate goal and an inner line that is a dry business detail ('Fund AI safety research' / 'Pay it in Nvidia compute, via partners'): the hooded line must read as a temptation or a corruption at a glance, in the inner voice.
  - Dropping the voice: real temptation memes mark who speaks ('Me: I should…' / 'Also me: …'); without it, two facts in a row read as a list.
  - Inventing a vice: the card is right that the dark move must be in the facts; a company's stated value paired with what it actually did is the safe news form.
  - One-box dialogue with no pair (6/24 flops).
  - An over-explained inner line (flops run 60–150 characters; winners p50 24).
- **Test:** Does the hooded bottom line read as the corrupt double of the top line (the inner voice pushing the vice, or the dark version of the same thing), rather than a second neutral fact?
- **Slot problems:** The good/inner pair matches the 2-box layout (53/70). Problems: (1) the card covers only the temptation reading (20/70) and misses dark-twin (18/70), which suits news (demo vs shipped, free vs paid, old vs new); (2) neither text slot accepts a viewer line, though the card lists AS-02, AS-13, AS-19 and the format's home voice is 'me' (27/70); (3) the MUSTs do not ask for the inner line in the inner voice (imperative), so we write two neutral facts. Proposal: Adds the viewer voice the format is built on and the dark-twin pattern (together 38/70 of winners beyond the company temptation reading), and makes the inner line an imperative or a parallel shadow instead of a second fact.
- **Our examples:**
  - `inner-temptation` · Two labs ship new flagship models in the same week, each with its own subscription. → good: [viewer AS-02] Me: cancel one subscription / inner: [viewer AS-02] Also me: add the new one
  - `inner-temptation` · A stronger model ships while many users are halfway through projects. → good: [viewer AS-19] Me: finish on the old model / inner: [viewer AS-19] Also me: redo it all tonight
  - `dark-twin` · A phone maker's AI assistant shines in the keynote demo but ships slower and narrower. → good: The assistant in the keynote / inner: [viewer AS-06] The assistant on my phone
  - `inner-temptation` · A chat app that promised privacy turns on training with user chats by default. → good: Protect user privacy / inner: Train on chats by default / kermit: [logo: the app's maker]
  - `lazy-shortcut` · A company answers its chatbot's wrong answers by adding a small warning label. → good: The answers are wrong / inner: Add a tiny warning
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) good=Fund AI safety research ; inner=Pay it in Nvidia compute, via partners: The real shape is there (a virtuous plan whose dark twin pays the money back to itself, a self-serving shortcut grounded in the story), but neither line has a voice, 'via partners' is dead weight, and the inner line is not short or sharp enough to read as temptation. 'Fund safety research' / 'In our own chips' would land.

### Three-headed Dragon (`three-headed-dragon`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 26 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries an unlikely entrant joining a field of giants, a weak entry next to strong ones, and the viewer next to the pros. It cannot carry a reversal or a sequence, and it tempts the writer to brand a real company as the dud without facts to back it.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 2.
- **Mechanism:** Three members of one set are shown as equals, but the goofy head is the one that obviously does not belong at the other two's level: the dud, the fake, the joke entry, or me. The laugh is the pretended parity (Germany, Italy, Japan; astrophysics, astrology, astronomy).
- **Picture:** A drawing of King Ghidorah on a dark sky: three golden dragon heads on long necks. The left head snarls with teeth bared, the middle head glares menacingly, and the right head is a cartoon derp with googly eyes and tongue out. Real uses put a set name across the top and one label on each neck.
- **Figure:** The heads are members of one set: things, groups, products or eras in 57/70; 'me' is the goofy head in 7/70 (the sit-ups head, the GIF-pronouncer, 'my research paper'); real companies or products label the heads in 9/70 (ChatGPT/Meta AI/Grok, Samsung/Apple/Android, KFC/McDonald's/Chick-fil-A, X/Facebook/Reddit users).
- **Voice:** Labels, never sentences: heads are noun phrases in 63/70 (p50 9–14 characters), with a qualifier on the goofy head in 12/70 ('ME WHO…', 'THE ONE WHO BURNS WATER', quote marks around 'research paper'). The top box is a set name in 35/39 4-box posts, often 'X in a nutshell' / 'X be like:' / 'Types of X' (14/39). ALL CAPS in 55/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → mess (top, above the heads (4-box posts only, 39/70): the set's name: 'Types of X', 'X in a nutshell', 'The strongest mobs'); B2 → head-left (left neck (fierce): a legitimate member); B3 → head-right (right neck (the goofy head): the dud member; Imgflip's default order puts the goofy head before the middle one); B4 → head-middle (middle neck (fierce): the other legitimate member)
- **Patterns:**
  - `two-real-one-dud` (57%, false equivalence): [set]: a category / [head-left], [head-middle]: two serious, proper members / [head-right]: the member that is a joke next to them. Real shape: Under 'Axis powers': Germany, Japan, and Italy as the derp.
  - `me-the-dud` (10%, self-deprecation): [set]: something serious people do / [head-left], [head-middle]: the real ones / [head-right]: me, doing a lame version. Real shape: Sit-ups, pull-ups, and me hearing 'push-up' and thinking of ice cream.
  - `same-thing-three-contexts` (14%, contrast, expectation vs reality): [set]: one thing / heads: that thing in three settings or eras, the goofy head being the setting where it looks silly. Real shape: My vocabulary with a teacher, with a crush, and with my friends.
  - `whole-dragon-label` (7%, visual metaphor): [set] only: the whole beast as one chaotic thing ('X be like:'). Real shape: A shopping cart's wheels be like: three heads going three ways.
- **Slot targets:** mess p50 18 / p90 33, noun phrase naming the set ('AI assistants', 'Types of X'); head-left p50 11 / p90 32, noun label or entity; head-middle p50 9 / p90 26, noun label or entity, parallel to head-left; head-right p50 10 / p90 28, noun label of the dud, often with a deflating qualifier ('Me, who…')
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the dud obvious to anyone (Italy, astrology, the mobile kid, the baby zombie): 47/70. Flops: 10/26 use names or in-jokes outsiders cannot rank (Audrey/Nikki/Tiffany, Braxton/Keller/Gavin, Kokushibo/Akaza/Douma, Bangladesh cricket squads); 6/26 give three heads with no clear dud (ups/fedex/usps, three Pikachu toys); 4/26 write sentences on the heads (houseplants, packing, 2 AM brain); 2/26 are local ads (Prime Traders).
- **Tech memes:** 1 tech-tagged post (coding students by month, 0↑, with a URL). In the top list, ChatGPT / Meta AI / 'Grock' (9↑), X / Facebook / Reddit users (9↑), and programmers / '10 year old gamers' / gaming PC users (8↑) all use two-real-one-dud on tech brands; the dud must be one readers already rate lower.
- **Misreads:**
  - Reading it as 'three parties in one mess' (our card's Logic): real uses are a set with one dud (47/70), not a conflict. Our AWS meme put OpenAI, Anthropic and Amazon on 'One Bedrock shelf, two rivals' with Amazon as the goofy head for no reason the facts show.
  - Making the goofy head a company the story does not show as lesser (our Shopify meme: Amazon as the derp next to Perplexity and Meta): the dud must be obvious to a reader and supported by the facts.
  - Writing a situation in the top box instead of the set's name ('Agents shopping for you').
  - Three peers with no clear dud (6/26 flops).
  - Names outsiders cannot rank (10/26 flops).
- **Test:** Do the three heads belong to one named set, with the goofy right head plainly the member that does not measure up to the other two (the dud, the joke entry, or 'me')?
- **Slot problems:** The card frames the format as 'three parties, one mess' and the top slot as a shared situation, but real uses name a set and make the goofy head the member that does not belong (47/70). All three heads accept only entities, yet 61/70 real heads are text labels and 7/70 make 'me' the dud; the logo-only heads push the writer to three blue-chip peers with no dud. Note Imgflip's default order: in 4-box posts B1 is the set, B2 left, B3 the goofy right head, B4 middle. Proposal: Renames mess to set and lets heads take text and the viewer, so the writer builds two-real-one-dud (40/70) and me-the-dud (7/70) instead of three logo peers with no dud, which is what both our memes did.
- **Our examples:**
  - `two-real-one-dud` · A soda brand announces it has trained its own AI model. → set: Companies with AI models / head-left: [logo: google] / head-middle: [logo: meta] / head-right: A soda brand
  - `me-the-dud` · Labs publish reasoning benchmark scores for their new models. → set: Who reads benchmarks / head-left: Researchers / head-middle: Investors / head-right: [viewer AS-12] Me, nodding along
  - `same-thing-three-contexts` · Users report a model answering differently than in its launch demo. → set: The same model / head-left: In the keynote / head-middle: In the benchmarks / head-right: [viewer AS-03] In my chat today
  - `two-real-one-dud` · Three agent products launch in one week; one can only book restaurant tables. → set: Agents this week / head-left: Writes the code / head-middle: Runs the browser / head-right: Books a table, sometimes
  - `me-the-dud` · A chip maker and a cloud giant announce huge AI data center builds. → set: AI compute in 2026 / head-left: [logo: nvidia] / head-middle: [logo: microsoft] / head-right: [viewer AS-12] My laptop fan
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) mess=Agents shopping for you ; head-left=[logo Perplexity] ; head-middle=[logo Meta] ; head-right=[logo Amazon]: Three peers with no dud: nothing in the story makes Amazon the goofy one, and the top line is a situation, not a set.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) mess=One Bedrock shelf, two rivals ; head-left=[logo OpenAI] ; head-middle=[logo Anthropic] ; head-right=[logo Amazon]: The host is drawn as the derp beside the two labs it sells; the facts make Amazon the shelf, not a lesser model. A correct use would put Amazon's own model as the dud only if the story shows it trailing.

### Flex Tape (`flex-tape`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 19 flops. News fit: **strong** (Tech news is full of big problems met with small fixes (a disclaimer for hallucinations, an age prompt for child safety, a label for deepfakes, a blog post for an outage), and those fixes are facts, so the mismatch needs no invention. It fails only when the story has no problem, which is when our writer misused it.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** A huge, gushing problem gets a tiny, cheap or dismissive fix applied with total confidence, as if it solves everything. The joke is the size mismatch between leak and patch (stage 4 cancer / band-aid), often sharpened by escalating the leak.
- **Picture:** Two panels from the infomercial. Top: Phil Swift, grinning and mid-gesture in a dark polo, next to a Flex Tape banner and a clear tank gushing water from a hole (top right). Bottom: a close-up of his hand pressing a small black patch of tape over the hole, water stopped. Real uses label the gushing tank, Phil, and the tape.
- **Figure:** Phil (the patcher) is labeled in 63/70: an authority or institution responsible for the problem in 40/63 (school nurse 15, schools 6, parents or mom 9, teachers 3, 'society' or 'people' 4, management, Disney, YouTube), 'me' in 15/63, a peer group in 8/63. The leak is a person's suffering or a big problem; the tape is the response.
- **Voice:** Leak is a noun phrase (p50 23 characters), escalated into a list of 3–5 problems in 9/70 ('broken leg, fractured skull, heart attack…'). Patcher is a short noun label, p50 8 characters ('SCHOOL NURSE', 'PARENTS', 'ME'). Patch is a bare noun in 31/70 (ICE, BAND AID, PIZZA PARTY), a quoted stock phrase in 18/70 ('BECAUSE I SAID SO', 'MAN UP'), a gerund or clause in 21/70. ALL CAPS in 53/70.
- **Boxes:** B1 → leak (the leaking tank (top right): the big problem, often an escalated list); B2 → patcher (on Phil (top left): who applies the patch: the responsible authority or 'me'); B3 → patch (the tape under the hand (bottom panel): the tiny, cheap or dismissive fix: an object, a stock phrase, or an action)
- **Patterns:**
  - `authority-tiny-fix` (41%, understatement, size mismatch): [leak]: a serious problem, escalated / [patcher]: the authority responsible / [patch]: a laughably small, cheap or symbolic fix. Real shape: Low wages and bad conditions, patched by management with a pizza party.
  - `shut-it-down` (23%, dismissal, hypocrisy): [leak]: a valid point, complaint or feeling / [patcher]: the one who does not want to hear it / [patch]: the stock line that ends discussion, in quotes. Real shape: A kid winning an argument, sealed by a parent saying it's because they said so.
  - `me-coping` (20%, self-deprecation): [leak]: my big problem (work, feelings, broken code) / [patcher]: me / [patch]: the small, avoidant thing I use instead of a fix. Real shape: Homework due, patched by me with 'tomorrow'.
  - `literal-or-meta` (13%, literalism, absurdity): [leak]: anything that leaks or needs covering / [patch]: Flex Tape itself, or a literal cover. Real shape: A big hole, and the tape fixes everything.
- **Slot targets:** leak p50 23 / p90 55, noun phrase for a big problem, can stack symptoms; patcher p50 8 / p90 21, short noun label for the responsible party, or 'Me'; patch p50 13 / p90 37, bare noun for a tiny fix, or a quoted stock dismissal
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make the patch visibly tiny against the leak (authority-tiny-fix and shut-it-down, 45/70). Flops: 5/19 use niche or local references (LIHTC/PHA/voucher, Greek TV idents, Andrew Johnson, D&D characters); 4/19 have an adequate fix and no mismatch (Taco Bell at 2 am, a dishwasher); 4/19 company posts get the roles wrong or leave no tiny patch (British Airways data leak patched by 'passengers', Roblox's long age-check line, Epic/Fortnite sprites); 2/19 repeat the nurse/ice joke verbatim; 1/19 is an ad.
- **Tech memes:** 2 tech-tagged posts: broken code patched by me with random Stack Overflow code (5↑, me-coping, works) and a Rust generics rant (0↑, too long and niche). Company stories in the flops fail when the patch is not obviously small or the patcher is not the one responsible.
- **Misreads:**
  - No leak at all: our memes put a claim or a perk where the leak goes ('Frontier models for every robot', 'Drug experience not required') and a limitation as the patch. The leak must be a real problem.
  - A sensible, proportionate fix as the patch ('One vote per person per account'): the joke needs a patch laughably small next to the leak (4/19 flops have no mismatch).
  - Using the format for a contradiction or catch (promise vs fine print): that is a different template.
  - Dropping the patcher: 63/70 real uses label Phil, and the joke indicts whoever applies the tape.
  - Wrong patcher: the one who caused or owns the problem must hold the tape, not its victims (British Airways flop).
  - Explaining the patch at length (Roblox flop): the tape is one small thing.
- **Test:** Is the patch laughably small, cheap or dismissive next to a big, clearly bad leak, and is the one holding the tape whoever owns the problem (or 'me')?
- **Slot problems:** The three regions match Imgflip's 3 boxes (63/70). Problems: (1) the patcher is optional and entity-only, but real uses label Phil in 63/70, mostly with a responsible authority or 'Me' as text; (2) the patch MUST asks for 'a real workaround', which lets a proportionate fix through; the format needs a fix laughably small or dismissive next to the leak; (3) the leak MUST does not insist the leak is a real, big problem, so the writer put launch claims and job-ad lines there in 3/3 memes. Proposal: Makes the patcher required and textable (63/70 label it, 40 with the responsible authority), and builds the size mismatch into the leak and patch MUSTs, which our 3 memes all missed.
- **Our examples:**
  - `authority-tiny-fix` · A chatbot keeps inventing legal cases; its maker adds a small reminder under each answer. → leak: Made-up court cases in filings / patcher: The chatbot's maker / patch: A gray footnote
  - `authority-tiny-fix` · An AI companion app with adult content is found full of minors; it adds a birthday prompt. → leak: Kids all over an adult AI app / patcher: The app / patch: "Enter your birthday"
  - `authority-tiny-fix` · AI-generated fake photos flood a social feed during a disaster; the platform adds a small label. → leak: Fake disaster photos everywhere / patcher: The platform / patch: A tiny 'AI info' tag
  - `me-coping` · A model update breaks prompts that users had tuned for months. → leak: [viewer AS-18] Every prompt I tuned, broken / patcher: [viewer AS-18] Me / patch: Adding 'please'
  - `shut-it-down` · Users complain a new model got worse at coding; the lab answers with a fresh benchmark chart. → leak: Users: it got worse at code / patcher: The lab / patch: Another benchmark chart
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) leak=Open vote on who gets tracked next ; patch=One vote per person per account: The leak is a feature, not a problem, and the patch is a sensible rule, so there is no mismatch. Niche story.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) leak=Frontier models for every robot ; patch=A few companies get early access: The leak is the launch promise, not a problem; the patch is a promise-vs-reality catch, which this format does not do.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) leak=Drug experience not required ; patch=PhD required, no visa sponsorship: Two job-ad requirements read as a contradiction, not a leak and a tiny fix; a hiring post has no gushing problem.

### All My Homies Hate (`all-my-homies-hate`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 12 flops. News fit: **ok** (The plain form carries only a standing grievance (ads arrive, limits shrink, prices rise), which fits AS-02/AS-04 stories well but has no turn. With the proposed 'Screw A / homies use B' line it can carry a switch: a rival launch, a free alternative, an exodus from a changed product. It can't stage a reversal or escalation.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** A tough-looking gang pledges loyal, collective hatred (or allegiance) to something small and universally annoying, as if it were a rival crew. The laugh is the mismatch between gang-solidarity posture and a petty target everyone already resents (unskippable ads, low-battery pop-ups, Mondays), and the reader joins the gang by agreeing.
- **Picture:** A sepia street photo of six young men in puffer jackets and hoodies posing outside a liquor store, throwing hand signs, most with jeans sagging below the waist. Faces fill the top third; the only clear text space is the legs and pavement across the bottom quarter (our 'hated' box), plus the torsos in the middle band.
- **Figure:** The crew stands for the poster and his friends ('me and the homies') in about 58/70. A named outside group is the figure in 2/70 (the Italian community, 'other people captioning this'). In 7/70 the photo is used for its sagging pants, not the crew, and 3/70 are off-format.
- **Voice:** All caps in 61/70. The frame line is fixed and recognised: 'ALL MY HOMIES HATE' verbatim in 26/70 plus 'Me and the homies (all) hate' in 4/70. X is a bare noun phrase in 24/30 of the hate posts (UNSKIPPABLE ADS, FAKE PNGS, GPU PRICES, TIKTOK); clauses or 'when…' scenarios in 6/30. The rejection line opens with Screw (10), F*ck/F**k/Fu*k (8) or Frick (3) followed by a bare noun (21/22 under 4 words). No punctuation or a single period in 64/70; no first-person 'I' except 'me and the homies'.
- **Boxes:** B1 → rejected (proposed) (top of the image, over the shelves and heads on Imgflip; we can't touch faces, so mid-band over the torsos: either the fixed frame 'ALL MY HOMIES HATE' (26/70) or 'Screw/F*ck X' naming the rejected thing (22/70)); B2 → hated (bottom, across their legs: X, the thing the crew hates (when B1 is the frame), or 'All my homies [hate X / play Y]' (when B1 is 'Screw X'))
- **Patterns:**
  - `crew-hates-annoyance` (37%, petty vs proper (gang solidarity aimed at a petty target)): All my homies hate [hated: a specific, widely shared annoyance or disliked thing, as a noun phrase]. Real shape: The crew united against video ads you can't skip.
  - `screw-x-homies-do-y` (21%, allegiance switch (reject A, back B)): [rejected]: Screw [the thing being dropped] / [hated]: All my homies [use/play/watch] [the alternative they back]. Real shape: Down with one programming language, the whole crew uses its rival.
  - `screw-then-hate-tautology` (10%, redundancy / mock-emphatic repetition): [rejected]: Screw [X] / [hated]: All my homies hate [the same X]. Real shape: Screw Mondays; all my homies hate Mondays.
  - `crew-photo-caption` (17%, inversion (tough image, soft activity)): [the crew described doing something wholesome, nerdy or childish]. Real shape: The gang hunting for a bookshop at the mall.
  - `sagging-pants-gag` (10%, visual wordplay): [a sale or remark that puns on their pants being half down]. Real shape: A store notice that all pants are half off.
- **Slot targets:** hated p50 14 / p90 30, bare noun phrase, the X only (code prints 'All my homies hate {x}'); real B2 including the frame is p50 26, p90 50; rejected p50 10 / p90 22, bare noun phrase after a fixed 'Screw'; 1–3 words
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners name a thing nearly everyone already dislikes, in 1–4 words: the top 3 hate-posts (KPop Demon Hunters 36, unskippable ads 25, double YouTube ads 18) and 12/26 plain hate posts are consumer annoyances (ads, low battery alerts, autocorrect, GPU prices, the broken ice-cream machine). Flops (12): 5/12 don't use the hate/allegiance frame at all (birthday wishes ×2, 'pov' ×2, hallway); 3/12 hate something only a niche knows (Flock camera, a book character, a K-pop fan in-joke); 2/12 replace X with a long invented scenario (the ChatGPT 'buffering IRL' line, the toxic-people report story); 1/12 leaves X off the image ('all my homies hate' with the target only in the title); 1/12 is an ambiguous one-word X ('ice'). No winner explains X; no winner is longer than about 50 characters in the X box except the off-format ones.
- **Tech memes:** Only 1 tagged tech post (Java vs Kotlin, the screw-x-homies-do-y swap, 3↑). But tech targets do well in the top list: generative AI (14↑), NFTs (14↑, the tautology form), fake PNGs (15↑), double ads on YouTube (18↑), TikTok (11↑), GPU prices (6↑). The one AI flop wrote an invented scenario instead of naming a thing. Tech works when X is a widely felt annoyance (ads, paywalls, limits, prices) or a format swap (drop A, use B).
- **Misreads:**
  - Writing a scenario or sentence as X ('when ChatGPT starts buffering and replies…'). Real X is a bare noun phrase the crew can chant; the AI flop did this and got 0.
  - Picking an X only insiders know (a niche app, a model codename, a policy name). Flops on Flock and fandom names got 0; winners hate things everyone has met.
  - Aiming the hate at a person or a hurt group. Our card forbids it, and real top posts that did it (furries, Karens, a country) are exactly the ones we can't publish.
  - Using a neutral or newsworthy thing nobody resents ('the new model'). The crew's hatred has to be the reader's own; a thing the audience likes or doesn't know reads as a random opinion.
  - Explaining why the homies hate it ('due to them having hypocrisy…'). Reasons appear in 3/70 and add nothing; the target alone is the joke.
  - Labeling the men in the photo as a company or putting logos on them. The crew is always 'us'; our watch forbids it and no top post does it.
- **Test:** Does the bottom line name, in a short noun phrase, a specific thing most viewers already resent (or the alternative they'd switch to), so the crew's 'hate' reads as the viewer's own gang-sized grudge rather than a sentence, a person, or a niche opinion?
- **Slot problems:** One box covers only the plain 'All my homies hate X' form (37% of top uses). The second-biggest form, 'Screw A / All my homies use B' (21%), is the one that carries a news turn (users dropping one product for its rival, AS-10/AS-13), and our card can't make it. The single slot is otherwise right: X is the only variable and code printing the frame matches real use. Proposal: Real uses split between the plain hate chant (26/70) and the reject-A, back-B swap (15/70, plus 7/70 tautologies). Adding an optional 'Screw' line over the torsos lets the writer make the swap, which is the form that fits switching and rival-launch stories, without touching faces (faces end at y 0.32). 'Screw' is the mildest top word real posts use (10/22); if Lucas wants no edge at all, 'Forget' is the substitute.
- **Our examples:**
  - `crew-hates-annoyance` · A popular chatbot starts showing ads to free users. → hated: ads in my chatbot
  - `crew-hates-annoyance` · An AI app's free plan now caps users at a handful of messages a day. → hated: the daily message cap
  - `crew-hates-annoyance` · A lab's model picker grows to a dozen near-identical model names. → hated: the model picker
  - `screw-x-homies-do-y` · A free open-source model matches a paid chatbot on common tasks. → rejected: the $20 plan / hated: the free model
  - `screw-then-hate-tautology` · A photo app moves its AI editing tools behind a subscription. → rejected: subscriptions / hated: subscriptions

### Sleeping Shaq (`sleeping-shaq`)
Coded 70 top uses, 2 tech memes, 28 flops. News fit: **strong** (Inverted priorities are a double standard, and double standards are a common news turn: a company slow on safety or user complaints but fast on a rival, a platform strict on trivia and lax on abuse. The catch is that both halves must be in the facts; the writer may not invent the contrast.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 0.
- **Mechanism:** The same party sleeps through something huge and jolts awake for something tiny. The laugh is the inverted priorities: alertness runs backwards to importance, which exposes a double standard, a self-serving focus, or a skewed sense of what matters.
- **Picture:** A 2×2 grid. Right column: Shaq asleep on a pillow captioned 'i sleep' (top), then a deep-fried, glowing-eyed Shaq wide awake captioned 'real shit?' (bottom). The left column is two blank white panels where the text goes, top beside the sleep, bottom beside the jolt.
- **Figure:** Shaq is an authority or institution judging others in 31/70 (teachers ×17, schools ×8, a game company, PETA, Roblox, Google, Alexa); a parent or relative who plays favourites in 15/70; 'me' or 'people' with odd priorities in 20/70; 4/70 off-format.
- **Voice:** Two parallel 'when' clauses with the subject repeated: '[Subject] when [clause]' in about 45/57 two-box posts, present tense, third person for an authority or first person ('me when') for personal ones (12/70). Lower or sentence case in about 45/70. The big thing is often exaggerated into a list or extreme (robs a bank, kills a million people…) in 14/70 and the small thing shrunk with a mock-precise measure ('0.0000001 seconds', 'one degree', '0.0001% louder') in 9/70. No one rewrites 'i sleep' or 'real shit?' in winners; they're printed.
- **Boxes:** B1 → sleeper + missed (top-left white panel, beside 'i sleep': '[Subject] when [big thing happens]' — the subject is usually written into the line ('Teachers when…')); B2 → wakes (bottom-left white panel, beside 'real shit?': '[Same subject] when [tiny thing happens]'); B3 → sleeper (in 4-box posts (10/70) B1/B3 or B2/B3 are the subject label repeated per panel: a bare label ('TEACHERS', 'DADS', 'SOCIAL MEDIA'); our logo does this job)
- **Patterns:**
  - `ignores-harm-punishes-trivia` (44%, inverted priorities / hypocrisy): [sleeper]: an authority / [missed]: when something serious happens on its watch / [wakes]: when something trivial or harmless breaks its rule. Real shape: Teachers sleeping through a hallway fight, then wide awake for a student's hood.
  - `double-standard` (21%, double standard (favouritism)): [sleeper]: the judge / [missed]: when the favoured party does something big / [wakes]: when the other party does something small. Real shape: Parents shrug at the youngest's rampage but snap at the oldest's tone.
  - `odd-personal-priorities` (29%, petty vs proper (self-serving focus)): [sleeper]: me or people / [missed]: a thing that should matter a lot / [wakes]: a small thing that matters only to me. Real shape: Unmoved by a movie's mass death, devastated when the dog is hurt.
- **Slot targets:** sleeper p50 9 / p90 16, a bare name label; for us a logo or 'Me'; missed p50 25 / p90 55, 'when' + present-tense clause naming the big thing (subject removed); real B1 with subject is p50 39, p90 72; wakes p50 22 / p90 48, 'when' + present-tense clause naming the small thing, parallel to missed
- **Winners vs flops:** In 66/70 winners the bottom thing is plainly smaller than the top thing yet gets the bigger reaction. Flops (28): 10/28 have no inversion — the wake-up is a sensible reaction ('someone shuts the door loudly', 'something crawling on my leg', 'realize you have a math test') or the two lines don't compare at all ('21 / 67', August vs October temperatures); 6/28 need niche knowledge (anime characters, a YouTuber's review, a forum user); 3/28 retype 'i sleep' / 'real s--t' inside the boxes; 2/28 are long run-ons. The remaining 7/28 have the right inversion (museum guards, KFC, teachers and hats) and flopped on staleness: the teachers/hood trope appears 12 times in the top 70, so repeating it gains little.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 2 tech posts, both winners on the inversion. Google sleeping through your account being hacked but alert to a new-device login (304↑), and Alexa ignoring 'Alexa' but waking for any other word (105↑). Both show a product's vigilance pointed at the wrong thing — the template's best tech shape.
- **Misreads:**
  - A sensible wake-up: if the bottom thing really is more important (a rival launches, a regulator acts), there's no inversion and no joke. Our card's 'what finally gets that player's attention' invites exactly this; 10/28 flops did it.
  - Two unrelated things: the top and bottom must be the same party's reaction in the same domain, so the reader can compare them.
  - Writing 'i sleep' / 'real shit?' into the slots; the photo already prints them and 3 flops repeated them.
  - Making the sleeper's inattention up. Both halves must be true of the same party; a plausible-sounding double standard the story doesn't show is invented news.
  - Putting a person who was hurt on the 'missed' side as a joke. Winners keep the serious thing as the authority's failure, never mock the victim; for us harm stories stay off this card.
  - Leaving out the size gap: winners exaggerate the big thing or shrink the small one; a flat pair ('Me at school / Me at video games') got 0.
- **Test:** Do both lines describe the same party, with the thing it sleeps through (top) plainly bigger or more important than the thing that jolts it awake (bottom)?
- **Slot problems:** Two problems. (1) The MUSTs never say the 'missed' thing must be bigger and the 'wakes' thing smaller or more self-serving; 'what finally gets that player's attention' reads as a normal priority, and the inversion is the whole engine (66/70 winners). (2) The card allows AS-10 and AS-11 (viewer situations), and 20/70 real uses are 'me', but sleeper accepts only an entity, so the viewer can't be Shaq. The boxes themselves are right. Proposal: The real engine is inverted priorities (66/70); the MUSTs have to demand the size gap and the parallel 'when' clauses, and the viewer must be able to sleep (20/70 real uses; AS-10/AS-11 are already allowed). Boxes unchanged.
- **Our examples:**
  - `ignores-harm-punishes-trivia` · A phone maker pauses its AI news summaries only after a news outlet complains, weeks after users first reported garbled alerts. → sleeper: [logo: apple] / missed: when users report garbled alerts / wakes: when a newsroom complains
  - `double-standard` · An AI lab facing copyright suits over its training data accuses a rival of training on its model's answers. → sleeper: [logo: openai] / missed: when authors sue over its training data / wakes: when a rival trains on its answers
  - `odd-personal-priorities` · A chatbot's new voice feature rolls out gradually by account. → sleeper: [viewer AS-11] Me / missed: when my phone needs a security update / wakes: when the app says 'new voice mode'
  - `odd-personal-priorities` · A rival chatbot ships the memory feature users wanted from theirs. → sleeper: [viewer AS-10] Me / missed: when my app ships 30 fixes / wakes: when the other app gets memory

### Laughing Leo (`laughing-leo`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 26 flops. News fit: **weak** (The engine is personal smugness and schadenfreude. On news it mostly becomes laughing at a company's failure, which is mockery (the reason it's benched). The one safe turn is the viewer spared by a backfire (didn't upgrade, didn't switch), which is narrow and depends on the facts naming a clear loser-free gap.). Our memes misused it: 1 of 1.
- **Mechanism:** Leo is smug, delighted superiority: the one laughing is in on something the others aren't (he won, he was right, his prank worked, the person who had it coming tripped). The joke lands when the setup makes the reader see why this person, specifically, gets to laugh.
- **Picture:** Leonardo DiCaprio as Calvin Candie, seated at a dinner table in a waistcoat and cravat, head tilted back in a wheezing, smug laugh, raising a small glass of liquor. Text goes in a band across the top (Imgflip B1) and across his suit at the bottom (B2); our card uses the wall right of his head and a logo on his suit.
- **Figure:** The poster or 'me' having the upper hand in 30/70 (a small win or being proven right 18, pretending to be classy with the glass 5, pulling a prank 7). An onlooker enjoying someone's comeuppance in 7/70. Leo as the laughing audience for a one-liner joke in the text in 19/70. A third party laughing at the poster in 6/70. Other 8/70.
- **Voice:** All caps in about 50/70. One box in 31/70 (mostly the one-liners, written as complete sentences, up to 120+ characters). Two-box posts split as setup / laugher label in about 14/39, using 'ME:' or 'ME WHO…' or 'X:' dialogue labels; the rest split a sentence across boxes. Past or present tense first person; quoted speech in about 12/70 ('MY FRIEND: "…"'). No two-box winner puts both the claim and its refutation in one box.
- **Boxes:** B1 → claim (ours) / setup (proposed) (top band, above his head: the setup: a situation, what someone said, or a whole one-liner joke (31/70 are one box)); B2 → laugher (bottom, across his suit: who is laughing ('ME:', 'ME WHO…', 'The teacher:') or the joke's second half)
- **Patterns:**
  - `smug-upper-hand` (26%, superiority / loophole): [setup]: what others say or worry about / [laugher]: me (or a party) who is spared, right, or ahead because of a fact. Real shape: A tech reviewer warns an old phone loses support; the poster holding an even older phone laughs.
  - `one-liner-audience` (27%, wordplay / dark joke): [setup]: a complete pun, dad joke or dark one-liner; Leo is the audience laughing at it. Real shape: A spa where fish eat dead skin was cheaper than a funeral.
  - `comeuppance` (10%, poetic justice): [setup]: someone who had it coming fails in a fitting way / [laugher]: me, watching. Real shape: The bully trips over the kid he pushed down.
  - `prank-payoff` (10%, dramatic irony): [setup]: the trick I set up and the moment it works. Real shape: Telling a girlfriend and a mom that the other one is hard of hearing, then waiting.
  - `pretend-classy` (7%, mock-sophistication): [setup]: a kid's version of a fancy grown-up thing, matching the glass. Real shape: A six-year-old sipping juice from a wine glass.
- **Slot targets:** setup p50 45 / p90 85, a situation or what someone says (dialogue label + quote); real B1 p50 57, p90 120 including one-box jokes; laugher p50 10 / p90 31, a short label: 'Me:', 'Me, who…', or a logo
- **Winners vs flops:** In winners the reason for the laugh is on the image: the laugher has won, been proven right, set the trap, or watched a deserved fall (37/70), or the text is a complete joke Leo is laughing at (19/70). Flops (26): 8/26 give no reason to laugh at all ('rich man', 'my honest reaction', 'he has a point', not wanting to get up); 5/26 are bitter or sad under a gleeful face (losing leads after a Reddit ban, crying over Grok for two hours, a self-checkout taking 'my will to live'); 4/26 need niche knowledge (a sports ownership vote, an anime fight, wartime truces); 3/26 are one-liners without a real punch (smart fridge warranty, gun names); 2/26 are offensive. Of the 5 top uses that set a claim against a fact (money can't buy happiness / kid buying ice cream), every one splits claim and fact into separate boxes or speakers.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: 1 tagged tech post (a code pun, 0↑). Tech-adjacent winners are smug-upper-hand: answering 'lag' to a thunder question (936↑), the older-iPhone owner laughing at a support warning (19↑), unplugging the teacher's ethernet (93↑). All three AI/tech flops (Grok made me cry, smart fridge, Reddit ban) had no upper hand.
- **Misreads:**
  - Laughing at spin with nobody laughing: our only meme put a quoted corporate line on the wall and no laugher, so the reader can't tell who has the upper hand or why. Real uses name the laugher in 39/70 two-box posts.
  - Squeezing a claim and its refuting fact into one line. Winners that do claim-vs-fact give the claim to one box or speaker and the fact to the laugher.
  - Laughing at a failure or a loss (a startup's bankruptcy, layoffs). The face is gleeful superiority; aimed at a loss it turns into mockery of the injured, which our watch forbids and which is why the card is benched.
  - A sad or bitter situation under the laugh (5/26 flops). Leo only works when the laugher is genuinely winning.
  - No reason for the laugh on the image ('my honest reaction'). The setup must show what makes this laugher superior.
  - Using 'Them:' as the speaker. A vague pronoun hides who said the spin; winners name the speaker ('TECH YOUTUBER:', 'MY PARENTS:').
- **Test:** Can you tell from the image who is laughing and why they have the upper hand (a win, a loophole, being proven right, a deserved fall), without the laugh landing on someone's loss or harm?
- **Slot problems:** The card builds on a 7% sub-pattern (claim vs fact, 5/70) and then breaks it by forcing claim and fact into one wall box, with the laugher optional. Real use is a setup plus a named laugher who has the upper hand; the laugher is the point of the face. The narrow wall box beside his head also fights the real setup length (p50 57). The template is benched for mocking claims, so this matters only if it returns. Proposal: In 37/70 winners the image shows who is laughing and why they are ahead; ours shows a quote and no laugher. A top setup band (faces start at y 0.19) and a required laugher across the suit match Imgflip's B1/B2 and make the upper hand visible.
- **Our examples:**
  - `smug-upper-hand` · A chatbot's Pro plan price doubles overnight. → setup: Pro users: "price doubles today" / laugher: [viewer AS-04] Me, still on the free plan
  - `smug-upper-hand` · A new AI feature only runs on this year's phones. → setup: Everyone: "is it on my phone yet?" / laugher: [viewer AS-11] Me, who bought it last week
  - `smug-upper-hand` · A company that bet early on its own chips gets them while rivals wait months for GPUs. → setup: Rivals: "GPUs sold out until spring" / laugher: [logo: google]
  - `smug-upper-hand` · A popular chatbot goes down for several hours; a free model that runs on your own laptop keeps working. → setup: Everyone: "is the chatbot down?" / laugher: Me, running it on my laptop
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) claim=Them: "best opportunity to maximize value": No laugher and no visible fact undercutting the quote, so there's no upper hand on the image; 'Them' hides the speaker; and the story is a bankruptcy, so the laugh lands on a failure, not on spin.

### Bad Luck Brian (`bad-luck-brian`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 18 flops. News fit: **strong** (The engine is a backfire, the most common news turn: upgrade then the limit drops, finish a setup then a new model ships, a launch ad that's wrong. The facts supply both halves, and AS-04/AS-11/AS-19 map onto it directly. It fails only on harm stories and on misfortune the story doesn't report.). Our memes misused it: 5 of 6.
- **Mechanism:** A normal or hopeful step, then the one outcome that undoes or punishes exactly that step. The laugh is the cruel precision: the universe answers his specific effort with the specific worst result, often by taking the setup's own words literally.
- **Picture:** A school portrait of a freckled blond teen in braces, a plaid sweater vest and a light-blue polo, grinning awkwardly against a purple backdrop. Classic advice-animal layout: setup across the top above his hair, punch across the bottom over his sweater. Our card uses only the bottom plus an optional logo on his shoulder.
- **Figure:** Brian is an unnamed unlucky protagonist written in the third person in about 52/70 (verb-first, no subject: 'WAITS IN LINE…'). First-person 'I/me' in 7/70, 'when you' in 3/70; 8/70 are one-liners where Brian is only the face of the joke.
- **Voice:** Third person, verb-first, present tense with no subject in about 50/70 (GETS, BUYS, CALLS, FINALLY…). Setup and punch split over two boxes in 58/70; the punch is 1–4 words in about 22/58 (MEDUSA, FORGOT WALLET, MOM ANSWERS, AS HIMSELF, TIDE PODS). 'Finally' or 'works up the courage' opens 6/70 setups to raise hope before the fall. All caps in 63/70. No hedging, no explanation of why it's unlucky.
- **Boxes:** B1 → setup (proposed; today crammed into 'outcome') (top band, above his head: the step he takes, a verb-first clause ('BUYS A PACK OF BATTERIES')); B2 → outcome (bottom, across his sweater vest: the outcome that undoes it, often 1–4 words ('BATTERIES NOT INCLUDED')); Bnull → unlucky (our logo on his shoulder; no real box: who the bad luck happens to, when it's a company)
- **Patterns:**
  - `step-undone` (39%, backfire / wasted effort): [setup]: a normal or hopeful step, verb-first / [outcome]: the true result that wastes, cancels or punishes exactly that step. Real shape: Stays up all night to finish homework; school is cancelled for snow.
  - `literal-twist` (29%, literalism / wordplay): [setup]: a step described with a phrase that has a second meaning / [outcome]: the result that takes the phrase literally. Real shape: Takes a trip down memory lane; gets a flat tire.
  - `absurd-escalation` (14%, absurd specificity / escalation): [setup]: a hopeful step / [outcome]: an absurdly specific, outsized catastrophe. Real shape: Finally makes eye contact with a girl; she's Medusa.
  - `self-roast-one-liner` (14%, self-deprecating wordplay): one line: a dad-joke or insult about the speaker's own misfortune. Real shape: Entering an ugly contest and being barred as a professional.
- **Slot targets:** setup p50 33 / p90 67, verb-first third-person clause, no subject ('Finally upgrades to Pro'); outcome p50 26 / p90 46, short clause or noun phrase, the undoing result; 1–4 words in about 38% of two-box posts; unlucky p50 8 / p90 14, a logo; real posts never label him
- **Winners vs flops:** In winners the outcome answers the setup's specific step (all of the 27 step-undone and 20 literal-twist posts). Flops (18): 6/18 have no step-and-undoing pair at all ('I'm / Autistic!', 'sold my thoughts / for a penny', 'goes to sleep Friday / wakes up Monday'); 4/18 are long, niche run-ons (fandom anniversaries, rap battles, a starfish restaurant); 3/18 are mild or merely sad without a twist (library closed, a book title); 2/18 drop Brian for an unrelated subject ('that one bro'); 2/18 have the right shape but the outcome is generic or predictable (beat a boss / it was the tutorial, smart speaker only speaks Latin). Winners' punches are concrete; no winner ends on a generic noun like 'issues' or 'problems'.
- **Tech memes:** No tagged tech posts. Tech-flavoured winners use the step-undone shape: 20-character password typed perfectly into an unselected box (160↑), copyright strike without uploading anything (22↑), a Note 7 from every family member (328↑), switching to Geico and paying more (208↑). This is the ideal shape for AS-04/AS-19 news: upgrade, then the limit drops; finish setup, then a new model ships.
- **Misreads:**
  - Cramming setup and outcome into one line ('Robots run on the cloud. Network issues.'). Real posts split them top and bottom in 58/70, and the setup is the protagonist's own step, not a product description.
  - An outcome the facts don't show, or a generic risk ('network issues', 'more incidents'). Bad luck must be a real event in the story, and winners' punches are concrete.
  - Putting the failure first and the fix second ('Test AI hits real forms. All tests lose web.'). The order is hope, then the fall; reversed, there's no Brian.
  - Earned consequences instead of luck: provoking someone and getting a response is comeuppance, not bad luck.
  - Using it for stories with victims (a false homicide report, an injury). Brian is harmless misfortune; real harm turns it cruel.
  - Leaving the protagonist unclear. With no logo, Brian is the viewer; a stranger from the story (an unnamed inventor) gives the reader nobody to sympathise with.
- **Test:** Does the top line show a normal or hopeful step, and the bottom a true outcome that undoes or punishes exactly that step, in that order?
- **Slot problems:** Real Brian is two boxes (setup top, outcome bottom) in 58/70; our card crams both into one bottom line ('A normal step and the cursed thing that followed, in one line'). That removes the pause between hope and fall and invites telegraphic two-sentence summaries, which is what all six of our memes became. The optional logo is fine, but nothing says that without it Brian is the viewer. Proposal: Match the real top/bottom split (58/70) so the hope and the fall get their own lines. The top band ends at y 0.18, above the face (y 0.30); the bottom box starts at 0.78, below it.
- **Our examples:**
  - `step-undone` · A chatbot cuts the price of its Pro plan a week after a big sign-up push. → setup: Finally pays for Pro / outcome: Price drops next week
  - `step-undone` · A lab ships a new model a month after the last one. → setup: Finishes setup for last month's model / outcome: New model ships
  - `step-undone` · A new AI feature launches in the US first, other countries later. → setup: Updates the app on launch day / outcome: Not in your country
  - `step-undone` · A search company's chatbot ad shows a wrong answer about a telescope. → setup: Shows off chatbot in launch ad / outcome: Ad answer is wrong / unlucky: [logo: google]
  - `literal-twist` · An AI app's 'unlimited' plan turns out to have a usage cap. → setup: Buys the unlimited plan / outcome: Hits the limit
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) outcome=Encrypts every chat. Scanner moves onto phone.: The step-undone shape is there (a privacy step defeated), but it's crammed in one line, Brian's identity is unclear (the viewer? a company?), and the story is an anti-scam tool launch, so the 'curse' is a reading of the facts rather than an event.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) outcome=Builds own framework. Company moves off it.: A good wasted-effort pair that fits AS-19, but 'Company' is vague and the source is an opinion piece, so the outcome isn't a reported event.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) outcome=Test AI hits real forms. All tests lose web. ; unlucky=[logo Anthropic]: The failure comes first and the remedy second, so there's no hopeful step to undo; it reads as a summary. The story involves a false homicide report, which is harm, not luck.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) outcome=Robots run on the cloud. Network issues.: A product description, not a step the unlucky party takes; 'network issues' is a generic risk the story doesn't report, so the misfortune is invented.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: No) outcome=Flags only police cars. Two officers visit.: The step-and-response shape is right, but it's provocation and consequence (comeuppance), not luck; the protagonist is a stranger the reader can't place, and the setup doesn't say who built what.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) outcome=Turns off web for some tests. More incidents. ; unlucky=[logo Anthropic]: Correct order and shape: a protective step, then the outcome it was meant to prevent, owned by the logo. Weak point: 'More incidents' is a vague punch; a concrete one would land harder.

### You know, I'm something of a scientist myself (`something-of-a-scientist`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 26 flops. News fit: **ok** (It carries one news shape well: a company making a token move into someone else's field (a platform ships a startup's feature, a retailer dabbles in AI) and the viewer's AS-07 'AI person at the table' moment. It can't carry a reversal, backfire or escalation, and it needs the act to be small, which many launches aren't.). Our memes misused it: 0 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** Someone does a tiny, amateur or accidental thing and smugly awards themself the full professional title for it. The laugh is the gap between the small act in the top line and the grand title in the bottom one; the bigger the gap, the funnier.
- **Picture:** A wide still of Willem Dafoe (Norman Osborn, Spider-Man) on a college campus in a suit and tie, head tilted with a tight, pleased half-smile. Imgflip puts the setup across the top and the catchphrase 'You know, I'm something of a X myself' across the bottom; our card has a logo top-left and the catchphrase at the bottom.
- **Figure:** The poster or 'me' in 57/70 (often 'N year old me', 13/70). A third party claiming a title it hasn't earned in 8/70 (celebrities, parents who play puzzle games, Disney in June, staff asking for raises). Other 5/70.
- **Voice:** Setup is first person, 'ME WHEN…' / 'ME AFTER…' / 'N YEAR OLD ME…' in 41/70, present or past participle clause, all caps in about 45/70. The bottom keeps the catchphrase verbatim or near-verbatim in 51/70; 'scientist' is kept as X in 19/70 (all child-mixing or science-adjacent acts), and swapped for a fitting role in the rest (chef, historian, genius, engineer, artist, criminal). X is a bare role noun with 'a/an', 1–2 words in 60/66. The setup carries the specifics; the title is generic.
- **Boxes:** B1 → act (proposed; today missing) (top band: 'ME AFTER / WHEN [small act]' — the deed that 'earns' the title (66/70 have it)); B2 → field (bottom band, across his suit: 'You know, I'm something of a [X] myself' with X swapped (51/70 keep the frame word for word or nearly)); Bnull → claimer (our logo top-left; real posts name the doer inside B1 ('ME', 'PARENTS THAT…', 'DISNEY'): who did the act)
- **Patterns:**
  - `small-act-big-title` (56%, inflation / mock self-credit): [act]: me (or the claimer) after doing a tiny, amateur or accidental thing / [field]: the full professional title that thing would never earn. Real shape: A kid mixing two drink powders crowns himself a scientist.
  - `overreach-third-party` (11%, hypocrisy / posing): [claimer]: a party / [act]: a token or adjacent move outside its lane / [field]: the identity or expertise it is now posing as. Real shape: A big company changing its logo for one month, now 'something of a' member of the group it markets to.
  - `pun-on-frame` (14%, wordplay / literalism): [act]: a situation / [field]: a role that is a pun or literal reading of the act. Real shape: Getting an 'A+' on a blood test, therefore a scientist.
  - `misdeed-as-profession` (6%, inflation into villainy): [act]: a small misdeed / [field]: the criminal job title for it. Real shape: A kid feeding a chocolate coin into an arcade machine, now a counterfeiter.
  - `loophole-membership` (6%, opportunism): [act]: a situation with a perk for a group / [field]: the group the speaker suddenly claims to belong to. Real shape: Asked to donate to the poor at checkout; turns out he's somewhat poor himself.
- **Slot targets:** act p50 45 / p90 70, '[Doer] after/when [small act]' clause; real B1 p50 54, p90 87; claimer p50 8 / p90 14, a logo, or 'Me' for the viewer; field p50 9 / p90 20, bare role noun, 1–2 words, without article (code prints the frame); real B2 with frame p50 44
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners show the act and make it small (child-mixing, a 4-ingredient meal, a periodic-table joke): 39/39 small-act posts state the act in the top box. Flops (26): 5/26 print only the catchphrase with no act ('surveyor', 'seaboy', 'meme creator'), so there's no gap to see; 5/26 are off-format (a property ad, a tragedy line, Homer); 6/26 are tech or AI self-credit (ChatGPT wrote my code → programmer, AI photoshop → digital artist, two Cursor subscriptions → angel investor, 'print hello world' → scripter) with the right shape but a gap the audience doesn't find surprising; 6/26 recycle the exact stock setup (potions, Mentos, mixing in the shower) with nothing new; 1/26 jokes about real fires (arsonist). Shape alone doesn't win here; a fresh, concrete small act does.
- **Tech memes:** No tagged tech winners; the top tech-adjacent post is Ctrl+F → data scientist (5↑). Six flops are AI-assisted self-credit (AI wrote it, so I'm a programmer/artist). For news, the stronger tech use is the third-party overreach: a company making a token move outside its field and posing as the expert.
- **Misreads:**
  - Dropping the act. Our card has no slot for it, so the meme shows a logo and a title and the gap lives only in the caption; 5 one-box flops did the same and scored 0.
  - Using the claimer's real or literal claim as X (a chip company that does make chips → 'chip maker'). With no gap between act and title there's nothing to laugh at; the card's 'from the player's own claim' pushes toward this.
  - A big, real act. If the act really earns the title (a lab wins a maths olympiad → mathematician), it's just true. The act must be small, token, adjacent or accidental.
  - Stock setups: Mentos, potions and mixing shampoo are the top posts' setups and 6 recent flops repeated them. Use the story's own specific act.
  - Misdeed-to-profession on a real person's alleged crime. It worked for us once (Lucas rated it Awesome), but it pins an arrested employee's act on the company and leans on 'allegedly'; use with care.
  - Self-credit for using AI ('AI made it, so I'm an artist'): 6 recent flops; the audience reads it as a brag, not a gap.
- **Test:** Does the meme show (or make unmistakable) a small, token or accidental act, and does the bottom title claim far more expertise than that act earns, rather than a role the claimer really has?
- **Slot problems:** Two problems. (1) There's no act slot: 66/70 real uses show the small deed in the top box, and the joke is the gap between that deed and the title. Ours shows only a logo and the title, so it works only when the headline does the setup (our one success did). (2) The 'field' MUST says to take the role from the player's own claim in the story, marked [CLAIM]. Real X is a title the meme awards for a small act, not something the claimer said. Copying a real claim gives no gap. Proposal: The gap between deed and title is the engine (in 66/70 the deed is on the image). An act line across the top (over his hair; the face starts at y 0.29) and the logo moved just below it keep every box clear of the face.
- **Our examples:**
  - `overreach-third-party` · A search engine adds a step-by-step homework solver to its results. → act: Adds a homework solver to search / claimer: [logo: google] / field: math teacher
  - `overreach-third-party` · A phone maker builds call transcripts into its notes app. → act: Puts call transcripts in Notes / claimer: [logo: apple] / field: court reporter
  - `overreach-third-party` · An online store adds an AI line that sums up thousands of product reviews. → act: Sums up 2,000 reviews in one line / claimer: [logo: amazon] / field: critic
  - `small-act-big-title` · A major chip export rule makes headlines for a week. → act: Me explaining the chip rule at dinner / claimer: [viewer AS-07] Me / field: trade lawyer
  - `small-act-big-title` · A chatbot launches a research mode that writes a sourced report. → act: Me after one AI research report / claimer: [viewer AS-07] Me / field: analyst
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) claimer=[logo OpenAI] ; field=detective: Misdeed-as-profession: an employee planting a recording device, crowned with a grand title. The gap (a hidden bug → 'detective') is there and the title is short and generic. It leans on the headline for the act, since the card has no act slot, and pins one employee's alleged act on the company logo.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) claimer=[logo OpenAI] ; field=detective (v3 regeneration, recording-device headline): Same meme from the second wording of the story; same strengths and same reliance on the caption for the act.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Awesome) claimer=[logo OpenAI] ; field=detective (v3 slate): Identical fill; consistent with the Awesome rating.

### Inhaling Seagull (`inhaling-seagull`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 29 flops. News fit: **ok** (Escalation stories (more tiers, bigger rounds, slower rollouts) and deflations (a big promise that turns out mild) fit the run and the screamed-answer forms well. The core trigger-and-scream form needs a person overreacting, which for news means the viewer; a company screaming its own position does not work.). Our memes misused it: 2 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** Three panels of wind-up promise something huge, and the joke is the ratio: either a tiny trigger gets an outsized scream, or a run of beats climbs to a final beat that is the loudest, most extreme (or a deflating twist) and gets belted. The scream must be disproportionate to its cause or be the peak of a build; a measured line screamed is not funny.
- **Picture:** Four stacked panels of one seagull on a green ledge: calm and upright (1), head tilting back with beak opening (2), bent fully backwards mid-inhale (3), then a red-tinted, glowing, blurred full-throated scream (4). Each panel is about a quarter of the tall image; real uses put one text line at the top of each panel.
- **Figure:** The seagull is the poster or a second-person 'you' in 39/70; a family member (mom, brother, dad) in 7/70; a third party or group (teachers, a dog, the USA, gamers, Dumbledore) in 9/70; no character at all, just the voice of a song, verse or count, in 15/70.
- **Voice:** ALL CAPS in 62/70. Panels 1-3 are short narrated clauses or labels; the scream is an exclamation, often with stretched vowels or '!!!' (21/70). '*INHALES*' or another stage direction in asterisks appears in 24/70. Speaker tags ('ME:', 'MOM:') in 14/70. Escalation runs use present-tense second person ('YOU FIND…', 'SHE ASKS…') in 14/70 and repeat a stem with one changing word in 6/70. No hedging, no full stops.
- **Boxes:** B1 → inhale (top of panel 1 (calm seagull): the trigger or the first, smallest beat); B2 → null (top of panel 2 (head tilting back): the second beat: the reaction starting, a bigger step, or the next word of a sentence); B3 → yeller (top of panel 3 (full inhale): '*INHALES*' or a stage direction in 18/70; otherwise the third, bigger beat. Our card puts a logo here instead.); B4 → yell (panel 4 (red scream): the scream: the peak, the payoff or the twist, often in elongated caps)
- **Patterns:**
  - `tiny-trigger-huge-scream` (29%, disproportion): [inhale]: a small, everyday provocation / [build]: the reaction starting or a speaker tag / [wind-up]: *INHALES* / [yell]: an outsized, emotional scream about something trivial. Real shape: A sibling is barely touched, takes a breath, and screams for their mother.
  - `question-screamed-answer` (6%, understatement screamed (bathos)): [inhale]: a mild question / [build]: a beat of pause or a 'why?' / [wind-up]: *INHALES* / [yell]: the short, blunt or deflating answer, screamed. Real shape: A kid asks why they must clean their room; the parent inhales and roars the non-reason.
  - `escalating-to-peak` (27%, escalation, absurd specificity): [inhale]: step 1 / [build]: bigger step 2 / [wind-up]: bigger step 3 / [yell]: the biggest, most absurd or most specific step, in parallel wording. Real shape: Making memes on a phone, then a handheld console, then a PC, then the school laptop right now.
  - `run-then-twist` (7%, expectation vs reality): [inhale]-[wind-up]: a run of beats that builds an expectation / [yell]: the last beat swerves into the letdown or the joke reading. Real shape: A date goes better and better until she pulls out a game console instead.
  - `word-crescendo` (16%, form as volume): one short sentence split across the four panels, a word or two each, the last words the scream. Real shape: A student's sentence about not wanting the camera on, split word by word, ending in the scream.
  - `belted-song` (16%, shared reference): a song or verse wound up across panels 1-3, the famous high note in panel 4 (not usable for us: lyrics). Real shape: The opening chant of an animated film, building to the big held note.
- **Slot targets:** inhale p50 20 / p90 40, short narrated clause or question, present tense, caps; build p50 20 / p90 45, next beat, parallel to inhale, or a speaker tag; wind-up p50 15 / p90 33, '*INHALES*' or the third, bigger beat; yell p50 21 / p90 50, exclamation, 2-6 words, caps, may stretch a vowel or end '!!!'
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners make panel 4 the loudest beat: a scream disproportionate to a tiny trigger (24/70) or the peak or twist of a rising run (24/70). Flops break that: niche references (Godzilla eras, fandom OCs, a Roblox tower, Lego villain) in 8/29; a word crescendo of nothing, with no situation behind it, in 6/29; a final panel that is not a peak (four equal annoyances, a sincere pep talk, ending on '*INHALES*' with no scream) in 6/29; a long, wordy or corporate scream in 3/29.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: one tech use (a databases course taught in a spreadsheet, 1 upvote), an escalation ending in an anticlimax.
- **Misreads:**
  - A calm explainer Q&A: a generic 'So what is going on with X?' and the headline fact as the yell ('OpenAI must hand over its hiring files'). There is no gap between trigger and scream, so nothing is funny.
  - Screaming a measured policy position with a logo ('The rules should be binding'): real screams are petty, emotional, absurd or deflating, never a stance.
  - Using only panels 1 and 4. 51/70 real uses fill all four panels; the build across panels 2-3 is the engine.
  - A logo on panel 3 where real uses write '*INHALES*' or the third beat; the yeller is better placed in the scream panel.
  - A final panel that isn't the peak: equal items, a sincere line, or stopping on the inhale (6/29 flops).
  - Long yells: real screams are a few words; flops with 10+ word screams (meeting jargon, inbox quote) sit at 0.
- **Test:** Is the final scream clearly bigger than what came before it: either an outsized reaction to a small trigger, or the peak (or twist) of a rising run of beats, rather than a calm fact or position?
- **Slot problems:** The card has two text boxes (panel 1 and panel 4) plus a logo on panel 3, but real uses are a four-beat build with one line per panel (51/70 use 4 boxes, 12/70 use 3). Leaving panels 2 and 3 empty kills the escalation pattern (27% of top uses) and the word crescendo. The yeller logo sits where real uses write '*INHALES*'. The card's MUST also frames the yell as 'a real reaction, complaint or fact from the story', which invites a headline restated, and it never says the yell must be disproportionate to its trigger or the peak of a run. Proposal: Matches the four-panel build that 51/70 real uses fill, keeps the inhale and yell ids and boxes, adds panel 2 and panel 3 lines (panel 3 defaulting to '*INHALES*' as in 24/70 real uses), and moves the optional logo off panel 3 into the scream panel. All boxes clear the four face boxes in geometry.ts.
- **Our examples:**
  - `tiny-trigger-huge-scream` · A chatbot starts showing ads inside answers on its free tier. → inhale: [viewer AS-04] ME: ASKS FOR A RECIPE / build: THE ANSWER HAS AN AD IN IT / wind-up:  / yell: AN AD?! IN MY CHAT?!
  - `escalating-to-peak` · A lab ships three new model tiers in the same week. → inhale: THE NEW MODEL / build: THE NEW MODEL PRO / wind-up: THE NEW MODEL PRO MAX / yell: [viewer AS-16] WHICH ONE DO I USE?!
  - `escalating-to-peak` · A big feature is 'rolling out gradually' over several weeks. → inhale: IT'S ANNOUNCED / build: IT'S ROLLING OUT / wind-up: IT'S ON MY FRIEND'S PHONE / yell: [viewer AS-11] NOT ON MINEEEE
  - `word-crescendo` · A popular AI app's redesign removes the old chat layout. → inhale: I / build: JUST WANT / wind-up: THE OLD / yell: [viewer AS-01] LAYOUT BACK!!!
  - `question-screamed-answer` · A country's long-awaited AI rulebook turns out to be voluntary guidelines. → inhale: SO WHO ENFORCES THE NEW AI RULES? / build:  / wind-up:  / yell: THEY'RE SUGGESTIONS!!!
- **Our memes, coded:**
