  - WRONG (Lucas: No) inhale=So are voluntary pledges enough? ; yell=The rules should be binding ; yeller=[logo Anthropic]: An interviewer's question and a calm policy position. Nothing tiny set it off and nothing outsized comes out; screaming a stance reads as us editorializing.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) inhale=So what is going on with Apple and OpenAI? ; yell=OpenAI must hand over its hiring files: Explainer prompt plus the headline restated in news register. No ratio, no build, no emotion.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) inhale=So what are NZ's rules for AI? ; yell=Light-touch and voluntary: Question-screamed-answer: the question promises substance and the screamed answer is a two-word deflation, the bathos of yelling something mild. It skips panels 2-3, but the gap carries it.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) inhale=How can recruiters tell it is AI? ; yell=Reading off an Autocue: The screamed answer is absurdly low-tech and specific; the gap between a serious question and that answer is the joke.

### Scooby doo mask reveal (`scooby-mask`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 29 flops. News fit: **ok** (Rebrands, wrappers, 'new' features copied from rivals and renamed models are regular AI news turns, and the rebadge reveal carries them well. But each reveal must be in the facts, and the 'ugly truth' form can slide into accusing a company of a hidden motive the story does not report, so usable stories are narrower than for a reversal template.). Our memes misused it: 3 of 3.
- **Mechanism:** The reveal says the shiny, scary or new front was always something the reader already knows, and that thing is plainer, older, cheaper or closer to home than the front. The deflation is the joke: the hot startup is a wrapper, the mysterious culprit is you, the 'new' thing is last year's.
- **Picture:** Two-panel cartoon. Top: Fred, smug, reaches over to a tied-up figure in a white ghost hood. Bottom: Fred holds the empty hood and the figure is revealed as a scowling, mustached man. Text goes on the ghost's body (top left), the man's body (bottom left) and, in 4-box uses, on Fred in each panel.
- **Figure:** The ghost is the front, the man is what it really is. The unmasked man is a plainer, worse or more familiar thing in 25/70, the same thing under another name in 11/70, the poster themself ('ME') in 8/70, and the obvious suspect in 4/70. Fred, when labeled (about 21 four-box uses), is 'ME', 'EVERYONE' or a 'LET'S SEE WHO YOU REALLY ARE' line.
- **Voice:** Noun-phrase labels, not sentences, in the two-label uses (48/70), ALL CAPS in 64/70. The unmasked box is the shortest (B2 p50 12 chars in 2-box uses). Fred's lines are stock: 'LET'S SEE WHO('S) REALLY…' appears in about 24/70, 'ME' on Fred in about 12/21 four-box uses, 'KNEW IT' / 'AS EXPECTED' in 3/70. Specific details make the reveal: an age and a first name, 'the customer', 'a ChatGPT wrapper'.
- **Boxes:** B1 → disguise (ghost's body, top left (or a caption across the top in 1-box uses): the front: the name, product or claim as presented); B2 → unmasked (in 2-box uses, the unmasked man, bottom left: what it really is); B2 → revealer (in 4-box uses, Fred, top panel: 'ME' or 'LET'S SEE WHO YOU REALLY ARE'); B3 → revealer (in 4-box uses, Fred, bottom panel: 'ME' again, or a reaction ('KNEW IT', 'AS EXPECTED')); B4 → unmasked (in 4-box uses, the unmasked man, bottom left: what it really is)
- **Patterns:**
  - `glossy-front-plain-truth` (36%, deflation, expectation vs reality): [disguise]: the appealing or scary front / [unmasked]: the plainer, cheaper, older or worse thing it really is. Real shape: An online girlfriend unmasked as a middle-aged man with a specific, unglamorous name.
  - `same-thing-renamed` (16%, false novelty): [disguise]: the 'new' name or product / [unmasked]: the existing thing it copies or rebrands. Real shape: A short-video feature unmasked as the rival app it copied.
  - `culprit-is-me` (11%, self-own): [disguise]: the mystery problem ('who's been wrecking my sleep') / [revealer]: Me / [unmasked]: me, or my own habit. Real shape: Whoever keeps stopping the poster from working turns out to be the poster.
  - `obvious-suspect` (6%, anticlimax): [disguise]: 'Let's see who's behind X' / [unmasked]: exactly who everyone expected / [revealer]: 'Knew it'. Real shape: The ones responsible for a broken society are unmasked as the same group in both panels.
  - `caption-only-reveal` (21%, reference): one caption narrating Fred's line ('Let's see who's really behind…') with the answer in the title or a named character. Real shape: A children's show villain named over the whole image.
- **Slot targets:** disguise p50 18 / p90 50, noun phrase: the front as presented; unmasked p50 12 / p90 34, noun phrase: the plain truth, or a logo, or 'Me'; revealer p50 4 / p90 27, 'Me' / 'Everyone' label, or a stock Fred line
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners unmask something the reader recognizes at once and that is lower than the front (48/70 two-label posts; the top post is a fake persona revealed as a specific man). Flops: the reveal is an argument or a list instead of a thing (4/29: 'uneducated, insecure, low-empathy…', 'spaghetti pipelines, broken dashboards…'); niche names (6/29); box roles jumbled, with the front on Fred or the truth on the ghost (6/29); AI-slop opinion posts where the reveal is a claim, not an identity (4/29). Tech reveals win when the truth is a deflating, recognizable thing ('hot AI startup' → 'ChatGPT wrapper', 15 upvotes; an assistant revealed as the old paperclip helper, 19) and flop when it is an abstraction ('AI' → 'linear algebra', 0) or a widely known fact with no deflation ('Copilot' → 'ChatGPT', 0).
- **Tech memes:** No tech-tagged posts, but 4 tech/AI uses are in the top 70 (AI startup → wrapper, essay → ChatGPT, assistant → the old paperclip, smart speaker → voice search engine) and 10/29 flops are tech/AI. The winning tech move is rebadge-deflation: a hyped product is really an older, plainer product.
- **Misreads:**
  - Unmasking the company that is already named in the front ('An open model strategy' → Amazon logo): it is just the speaker, not a reveal, and nothing deflates.
  - Unmasking a company as the thing someone accused it of ('Pentagon's supply chain risk' → Anthropic): that reads as us endorsing the accusation, and the 'truth' is not something the reader already recognizes.
  - Putting an argument, a list or an opinion under the mask instead of a short, recognizable identity (4/29 flops).
  - Forcing the unmasked slot to be a logo: real reveals are mostly a thing or concept (36/48 two-label posts), and only about 6/70 unmask a company.
  - Jumbling roles: the front must sit on the ghost and the truth on the man (6/29 flops swap or scatter them).
- **Test:** Is the face under the mask something the reader already recognizes, different from the disguise and plainer, older or closer to home than it, so that the reveal deflates the front?
- **Slot problems:** The unmasked slot accepts only an entity, so every meme becomes 'X is really [company]', usually the company already in the story, which gives no reveal. Real uses unmask a recognizable thing or concept (36/48), a rename (11/70) or 'me' (8/70). The MUST ('the facts must show it is the same company, model or person') steers the writer to restate who made the claim instead of what the front really is. The card never says the truth must be plainer, older or more familiar than the front. Proposal: Boxes and ids unchanged. Unmasked now takes text and viewer as well as a logo, and the MUST asks for the deflating, recognizable truth that 48/70 real uses run on, instead of naming the company that already appears in the front.
- **Our examples:**
  - `same-thing-renamed` · An assistant relaunches under a new brand name with the same features. → disguise: The all-new assistant / unmasked: Last year's assistant, renamed / revealer: [viewer AS-16] Me
  - `glossy-front-plain-truth` · A startup's 'proprietary AI' is reported to run on another lab's public model. → disguise: Our proprietary AI / unmasked: [logo: openai] / revealer: 
  - `glossy-front-plain-truth` · A phone maker labels its long-standing photo cleanup tool as an AI feature. → disguise: AI photo magic / unmasked: The old cleanup tool / revealer: 
  - `culprit-is-me` · A report says chatbot use is driving up data-center power demand. → disguise: Who's using all that power? / unmasked: [viewer AS-07] Me, regenerating one picture all night / revealer: Me
  - `obvious-suspect` · A lab's model tops a leaderboard that the same lab runs. → disguise: The independent leaderboard / unmasked: [logo: the lab in the story] / revealer: Knew it
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) disguise=Pentagon's supply chain risk ; unmasked=[logo Anthropic]: The 'truth' is just the company the label was pinned on, so the meme says the Pentagon was right. No familiar, plainer thing is revealed and nothing deflates.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) disguise=An open model strategy ; unmasked=[logo Amazon]: The front is Amazon's own stated strategy and the reveal is Amazon, so there is no gap between front and truth. A real use would unmask the strategy as something plainer, such as the one partner it funds.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: Yes) disguise=Slow down for guardrails ; unmasked=[logo Anthropic]: It lands as irony because readers know Anthropic as the safety voice and the story is its model's false tip. But it is a hypocrisy reveal on a company's own slogan, not a deflation; putting the incident on the ghost and the safety lab under the mask would read more cleanly.

### No - Yes (`no-yes`)
Coded 70 top uses, 0 tech memes, 27 flops. News fit: **strong** (Double standards, reversed pledges, rebrands and loopholes are steady AI-news turns, and the format carries a company's contradiction in two short lines with its logo on Drake, as our rated Amazon meme shows.). Our memes misused it: 4 of 5.
- **Mechanism:** Both panels are the same act or goal, and the approved one is the self-serving, sneakier or contradictory version: an institution refuses the fair thing and happily does the unfair one, or the same act gets a no under one name and a yes under another. The laugh is recognizing the double standard or the loophole, not seeing a preference.
- **Picture:** The Drake 'Hotline Bling' two-panel in a small, flat layout: left column, Drake in an orange puffer turning away with a raised hand (top) and smiling and pointing (bottom); right column, two large empty white panels. Text goes in the white panels; 3- and 4-box uses add a short label on Drake himself.
- **Figure:** Drake is the poster or an implied 'me' in 53/70. A named actor (a studio, a government, the UN, dentists, 'my brain', a toddler, a group of people) is put on Drake in 17/70, 14 of them as a label on the Drake panel and 3 as a prefix inside the text.
- **Voice:** Two short labels or gerund phrases in 50/70 (B1 p50 23, B2 p50 19 chars), mostly ALL CAPS. The actor label on Drake is 1-4 words ('GOVERNMENT', 'DENTISTS', 'MY BRAIN'). The pair often shares its opening words so only the swapped part changes (17/70). 8/70 cram a whole joke into one box and ignore the panels; they score low.
- **Boxes:** B1 → no (top-right white panel: the rejected act or name); B2 → yes (bottom-right white panel (2-box uses); in 3/4-box uses, a label on top Drake: the approved act, or the actor's name); B3 → yes (bottom-right white panel (3/4-box uses): the approved act); B4 → chooser (label on bottom Drake (4-box uses): the actor's name again)
- **Patterns:**
  - `actor-picks-unfair` (13%, hypocrisy): [chooser]: an institution or company / [no]: the fair, decent or promised act / [yes]: the self-serving or unfair version it actually does. Real shape: A school refuses to punish the bully and gladly punishes the whole class.
  - `same-act-double-standard` (7%, double standard): [no]: act X when someone else does it, or under one framing / [yes]: the same X when the actor does it, or under a kinder framing. Real shape: The same hours of forced work rejected as one thing and approved when called school.
  - `same-thing-renamed` (11%, euphemism, false novelty): [no]: the plain name / [yes]: the same thing under a nicer or trendier name. Real shape: Rejecting a spread by its old name, approving it under a plant-based label.
  - `proper-vs-sneaky` (13%, petty vs proper): [no]: doing it the proper way / [yes]: the lazy, sneaky or roundabout workaround. Real shape: Skipping the recipe and letting tradition guide the cooking.
  - `pun-or-loophole` (6%, wordplay, technicality): [no]: the act / [yes]: a word-swap that technically obeys while doing the same thing. Real shape: A ban on talking about someone, obeyed by singing about him instead.
  - `sincere-taste` (26%, values contrast (weak)): [no]: one thing / [yes]: the thing the poster prefers. Real shape: One cartoon series over its reboot.
- **Slot targets:** no p50 23 / p90 52, gerund phrase or noun label; yes p50 19 / p90 60, gerund phrase or noun label, parallel to the no, carrying the specific detail; chooser p50 9 / p90 15, short actor label (company, institution, group)
- **Winners vs flops:** This variant is low-traffic (top post 29 upvotes), so the signal comes from the vote spread. Hypocrisy and double-standard pairs (actor-picks-unfair plus same-act-double-standard, 14/70) average about 9 upvotes and hold 4 of the top 6 posts. Sincere taste pairs are the largest group (18/70) but average about 5. Flops are mostly neutral A-vs-B pairs with no shared goal (10/27: two children's shows, two toy brands, two jobs) and stock lazy-vs-proper pairs (6/27: dishes vs TV, studying vs scrolling); 3/27 cram dialogue into one box.
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: no tech-tagged posts and none in the top 70.
- **Misreads:**
  - Two unrelated or neutral options from a niche story ('Tutorials and outside rules' / 'Five styles and a weekly plan'): no shared act, no double standard, the largest flop group (10/27).
  - A 'no' that is a claim or observation rather than an act someone refuses ('Code quality matters less now').
  - Abstract policy wording ('Voluntary pledges alone') where real boxes name a concrete act a reader can picture.
  - Leaving the actor implicit when the joke is a company's double standard: real hypocrisy uses name the actor on Drake (14/70).
  - Picking the objectively better option: the approved box should be the self-serving or sneakier version, not the winner.
- **Test:** Do both boxes show the same act, goal or thing, with the approved box being the self-serving, contradictory, renamed or sneakier version of it, rather than two unrelated options?
- **Slot problems:** The slot layout (two panels plus an optional actor on Drake) matches real use (50/70 use two boxes, 14/70 add the actor label). The MUST text is too loose: it asks only for two 'real positions' from the story, which lets the writer fill a neutral pair from stories with no actor and no contradiction. It should require the same act or goal in both boxes, with the yes being the self-serving, contradictory or sneakier version, and it should require the chooser whenever the meme is about a company's double standard. Proposal: Boxes and ids unchanged; the MUST text now asks for the same-act contrast that the best-voted real uses run on, and stops the neutral A-vs-B pairs that make up the largest flop group.
- **Our examples:**
  - `actor-picks-unfair` · A company that pledged to keep its chatbot ad-free starts testing ads in it. → no: Ads in our chatbot / yes: 'Sponsored suggestions' in our chatbot / chooser: [logo: the company in the story]
  - `same-act-double-standard` · An AI firm that trained on scraped web data objects when a rival trains on its model's outputs. → no: Others copying our model's outputs / yes: Us copying the whole web / chooser: [logo: the company in the story]
  - `same-thing-renamed` · Apps relabel their existing chat features as 'agents'. → no: Calling it a chatbot / yes: Calling the same chatbot an agent
  - `proper-vs-sneaky` · A chatbot launches a study mode that won't hand students the answers. → no: Using the new study mode / yes: Opening a second chat that just gives the answer
  - `pun-or-loophole` · A contest bans AI-generated entries, and entrants start labeling theirs 'AI-assisted'. → no: AI-generated art / yes: AI-assisted art
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) no=Voluntary pledges alone ; yes=Willing to sign the voluntary deal ; chooser=[logo Microsoft]: The shape is right, a stated position against an act that seems to contradict it, but both boxes are abstract and nearly the same word, so a reader can't see the double standard at a glance.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) no=Code quality matters less now ; yes=Pick the one models know best: The 'no' is an opinion, not something refused, and the two lines share no act. Niche developer blog, no actor.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) no=Tutorials and outside rules ; yes=Five styles and a weekly plan: A neutral pair from a niche essay: no shared goal, no hypocrisy, no workaround.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) no=Needing Xcode for iOS apps ; yes=Posing as a Pixel 7 to log in: Proper-vs-sneaky with a concrete, funny workaround detail, but the story is niche developer tooling, and the 'no' is a requirement rather than a refused act.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) no=No favorites among model makers ; yes=Puts $50 billion into OpenAI ; chooser=[logo Amazon]: Actor-picks-unfair: a stated neutrality against a concrete act that contradicts it, with the actor on Drake. The number makes the punch box specific.

### Panik Kalm Panik (`panik-kalm-panik`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 24 flops. News fit: **strong** (Backfires, reassurances that come with a catch, and fixes that create the next problem are everyday AI-news turns, and the viewer as 'you' gives the head a clear point of view. The burned-in labels do the emotional work, so the text only has to state three facts.). Our memes misused it: 4 of 4.
- **Mechanism:** The third beat turns the second: the thing that calmed you is exactly what should scare you (often the same words repeated, now read the other way), or a new fact cancels the relief and shows the first fear was real and worse. The comedy is the relief becoming the trap, not three bad events in a row.
- **Picture:** A 3x2 grid. The right column shows the bald 3D 'Meme Man' head: clutching his head with the printed word 'Panik', serene with 'Kalm', then clutching again, blurred, with 'Panik'. The left column is three empty white panels, one per row, where the text goes.
- **Figure:** The head is the reader as 'you' in 48/70 (second-person narration), 'I/me' in 14/70, a group or institution (gamers, Hollywood, a generation) in 4/70, and an impersonal scene in 4/70.
- **Voice:** Plain narrated clauses in present tense, sentence case, from one point of view: second person 'you' in 48/70, first person in 14/70. Not labels. The third box is set in caps for emphasis in 11/70. The repeat pattern copies the kalm line word for word or nearly (15/70). Lengths: B1 p50 28, B2 p50 21, B3 p50 24 characters.
- **Boxes:** B1 → panik (left panel, row 1: the first worry, narrated as a scene); B2 → kalm (left panel, row 2: the thing that seems to fix it); B3 → panik-again (left panel, row 3: the turn: the relief restated or cancelled)
- **Patterns:**
  - `relief-repeated-reread` (21%, reframe by repetition): [panik]: a small worry / [kalm]: the comforting fact / [panik-again]: the SAME comforting fact, repeated (often in caps), which on a second read is the danger. Real shape: On a plane the engines go quiet so you can sleep; the third panel just repeats that the engines went quiet.
  - `new-fact-cancels-relief` (56%, reveal, expectation vs reality): [panik]: a worry / [kalm]: an explanation that settles it / [panik-again]: one concrete new fact that cancels the explanation and makes it worse. Real shape: A noise at night turns out to be the dog, and you don't own a dog.
  - `fix-backfires` (11%, backfire): [panik]: a problem / [kalm]: the action you take to fix it / [panik-again]: that very action causes a worse problem. Real shape: Choking on spit, drinking water to fix it, choking on the water.
  - `other-shapes` (11%, none (weak)): two-box or one-box jokes, a taste ranking or a list, not using the turn. Real shape: Three media formats ranked as panic, calm, panic.
- **Slot targets:** panik p50 28 / p90 67, present-tense clause, 'you'/'I' + a worrying situation; kalm p50 21 / p90 46, present-tense clause: the relief, from the same point of view; panik-again p50 24 / p90 46, the kalm repeated (often caps), or one short new fact that cancels it
- **Winners vs flops:** In 62/70 top uses the third beat turns the second (repeat 15, new fact 39, backfire 8). Flops mostly lack that turn: in 8/24 the third beat is just the original problem coming back or a predictable next step (the kid still sets the stove on fire, the friend finds the stash, it's actually Monday); in 3/24 the kalm isn't reassuring at all; 5/24 lean on niche game or fandom references; 2/24 are garbled; 2/24 are abstract opinions ('memes spread disinformation').
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: one tech post (an out-of-bounds array access where 'the program still works' is repeated, 0 upvotes, crammed into one box) and one AI taste ranking (17 upvotes). The repeat pattern clearly suits software ('it still works').
- **Misreads:**
  - Three headline beats in a timeline ('A hidden recorder at OpenAI' / 'OpenAI's models trace the device' / 'DOJ charges an OpenAI engineer') with no one whose panic it is: the head needs one point of view.
  - A third beat that isn't worse for the person panicking ('Two Tesla vets start a robot firm'): real third panels make the relief the trap.
  - No panic at all: three neutral facts from a calm essay ('Fun needs no outside pressure…').
  - Headline voice and jargon ('reaching hints', 'pass or fail') where real uses narrate a scene a reader can picture.
  - The predictable next step as the turn (8/24 flops): 'it got worse' is not a turn unless it flips the kalm.
- **Test:** Does the third box turn the second, so that the relief itself becomes the reason to panic (repeated and re-read, cancelled by a new fact, or causing the new problem), all from one point of view?
- **Slot problems:** The three slots match the 3-row layout (64/70 use 3 boxes). The MUST text asks only for 'the first worrying development', 'what seems to resolve it' and 'the twist that makes it worse, from the plot', which produces a news timeline. It never fixes a single point of view (whose panic it is), and it never requires the third beat to turn the second, by repeating the relief so it now reads as danger, by a fact that cancels it, or by the fix causing the new problem. Proposal: Boxes and ids unchanged. The MUST text now asks for one point of view and a third beat that turns the second, which 62/70 real uses do and our four slates and most flops do not.
- **Our examples:**
  - `relief-repeated-reread` · A chatbot adds a memory feature that can recall earlier conversations. → panik: [viewer AS-09] You told the chatbot way too much last year / kalm: It has a new memory feature / panik-again: IT HAS A NEW MEMORY FEATURE
  - `new-fact-cancels-relief` · A new model launches 'today', but only on the most expensive plan. → panik: [viewer AS-11] Everyone is posting the new model / kalm: It's out today / panik-again: On the top plan only
  - `new-fact-cancels-relief` · A study finds chatbots often cite sources that do not exist. → panik: [viewer AS-14] Your report needs sources / kalm: The chatbot gives you five / panik-again: None of them exist
  - `fix-backfires` · A provider will retire an older model that many apps still use. → panik: [viewer AS-18] The new model breaks your app / kalm: You stay on the old one / panik-again: The old one retires next month
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) panik=A hidden recorder at OpenAI ; kalm=OpenAI's models trace the device ; panik-again=DOJ charges an OpenAI engineer: There is a twist (it was an insider), but it reads as a news timeline: whose panic is it? The third beat doesn't cancel the kalm for one point of view.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) panik=Fun needs no outside pressure ; kalm=A weekly plan and a challenge ; panik-again=Only rule: stop if it's not fun: No worry, no relief, no turn: three calm points from an essay.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) panik=Robot only gets pass or fail ; kalm=Researchers add reaching hints ; panik-again=The hints lowered its success: The fix-backfires shape is right (the fix makes it worse), but the story is niche research and the wording is jargon a casual reader won't parse.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) panik=Robot stations too hard to program ; kalm=Tesla just bypasses the stations ; panik-again=Two Tesla vets start a robot firm: Founding a startup is not a renewed panic for anyone, and the second beat is not a relief from the first person's view.

### Surprised Pikachu (`surprised-pikachu`)
Coded 70 top uses, 1 tech memes, 32 flops. News fit: **strong** (Backfires and predictable consequences are a daily news turn (a cost-cutting move causes the complaints, a policy invites the lawsuit), and our rated memes confirm it: all four Yes ratings use the own-move form with a concrete result.). Our memes misused it: 6 of 11.
- **Mechanism:** Shock at a result anyone could see coming: the setup makes the outcome obvious, and Pikachu's blank shock is the punchline because the one who is shocked caused it, or invited it with their own rule. On Imgflip it also runs as a bystander reaction to a normal act revealed as shocking in context, and as a pun on a literal name.
- **Picture:** A white text band fills the top 40% of a square; below it, a close, slightly blurred Pikachu stares out with round eyes and a small open mouth. The setup goes in the white band; real uses end with a short label naming Pikachu ('Me:', 'Teacher:', 'Everyone else at…:') placed last, right above or beside the face.
- **Figure:** Pikachu is named by a final label in 47/70. It stands for the poster ('Me:') when they cause their own result or realize something; for the other party who gets their own logic turned on them ('Teacher:', 'Dad:', 'Mom:'); for bystanders in a context that makes a normal act shocking ('Everyone else on the airplane:', 14/70); or for a person whose name is a pun ('Kid named Hoops:', 9/70).
- **Voice:** Scene with speaker tags: 'Me:', 'Teacher:', 'Everyone:' and actions in asterisks (*does X*), in about 40/70. The final label ends in a colon in most of the 47/70 that name Pikachu. Mixed case more often than caps. The setup is the longest box (B1 p50 35 chars); the label is tiny (B3 p50 8). Nothing after the label: the face is the punch.
- **Boxes:** B1 → obvious (white band, top: the move, often with a speaker tag ('Me: *does X*')); B2 → obvious (white band, second line (3-box uses): the result, or the other party's line); B2 → surprised (last line before Pikachu (2-box uses): who is shocked, as a label ending in a colon); B3 → surprised (last line before Pikachu (3-box uses): 'Me:' or the other party's label)
- **Patterns:**
  - `own-move-predictable-result` (13%, dramatic irony): [obvious]: Actor does X. X's obvious result happens. / [surprised]: Actor:. Real shape: Bending something past its limit, it breaks, and the bender is shocked.
  - `own-rule-turned-back` (16%, turnabout, literalism): [obvious]: Actor states a rule or demand. The other party follows it to the letter, or turns the same logic back. / [surprised]: Actor:. Real shape: A teacher says to just go to the toilet without asking, a student leaves mid-test, the teacher is shocked.
  - `normal-act-shocking-context` (20%, context reveal): [obvious]: Me: does a normal thing / [surprised]: everyone else at [a place where it is a disaster]:. Real shape: Opening the sunroof, with the label naming everyone else on the submarine.
  - `kid-named-x` (13%, literal pun): [obvious]: someone says a word that is also a name / [surprised]: kid named [that word]:. Real shape: A coach says to shoot hoops; a kid named Hoops reacts.
  - `late-realization` (17%, obvious-in-hindsight): [obvious]: me when I realize [an obvious fact] / no separate label. Real shape: Realizing as a kid that teachers don't live at the school.
  - `pleasant-shock` (13%, inversion): [obvious]: something unexpectedly good happens to me / [surprised]: Me:. Real shape: The quiet kid speaks and the whole class is shocked.
- **Slot targets:** obvious p50 45 / p90 80, two beats: the move, then its obvious result (present tense, actor named or tagged); surprised p50 8 / p90 30, label ending in a colon: 'Me:', a company, or 'Everyone at…:'
- **Winners vs flops:** Winners give a concrete cause and label the shocked party (47/70). Flops: a bare 'when you realize…' / 'me when…' caption with no cause and no twist (9/32); garbled or nonsense text (5/32); long or niche setups (5/32); a predictable result that is stock and unspecific (self-watering plant forgotten, friend-zoned, 6/32); a 'surprise' that isn't one or doesn't follow from the setup (4/32).
- **Tech memes:** Too few to say: one tech post (a programming-language joke, 1 upvote). Among our own memes, the predictable-consequence form carries tech news well when a specific actor's own move causes the result (4/4 Lucas Yes are this shape).
- **Misreads:**
  - A second beat that isn't a consequence of the first ('Web off for some tests. Now off for all.'): the same decision widened is not a predictable result anyone is shocked by.
  - No actor and an abstract result ('Skips the human check. Fluent errors slip through.'): real setups name who did it and show a concrete outcome.
  - Leaving Pikachu unlabeled: 47/70 real uses name who is shocked; without it the reader can't tell whose shock it is.
  - Shocking a party who didn't cause the result: the shocked one must be the actor, or the one whose own rule was turned back.
  - Stating an allegation as fact to make the result predictable ('Plants a recorder. Faces DOJ charges.') when the story says it is alleged.
  - A bare 'me when I realize…' with no cause (9/32 flops).
- **Test:** Does the top text show a move and the result it obviously leads to, with the party shocked at Pikachu being the one who made that move (or whose own rule was turned back)?
- **Slot problems:** The two slots fit the image (the white band, plus a label by Pikachu). But 'surprised' is optional and entity-only, while real uses name the shocked party in 47/70, often as 'Me:'. Our unlabeled memes leave the shock unassigned. The obvious MUST asks for 'the move and its predictable result' but never says the shocked party must be the one who made the move (or whose rule was turned back), which let through a widened policy and an actorless result. Proposal: Boxes and ids unchanged. Surprised becomes required and accepts the viewer, as 47/70 real uses label Pikachu (often 'Me:'); the obvious MUST now ties the result to the labeled party's own move, the canonical form and the one all four of Lucas's Yes ratings use.
- **Our examples:**
  - `own-move-predictable-result` · A company replaces its support staff with a chatbot, and customer complaints pile up. → obvious: Swaps the support team for a bot. Complaints pile up. / surprised: [logo: the company in the story]
  - `own-rule-turned-back` · An AI firm that trained on scraped web content objects to a rival training on its outputs. → obvious: Trains on everyone's work. A rival trains on its work. / surprised: [logo: the company in the story]
  - `own-move-predictable-result` · A chatbot's memory feature shows users it saved a year of their chats. → obvious: Tells the chatbot everything for a year. It remembers everything. / surprised: [viewer AS-09] Me:
  - `own-move-predictable-result` · A chatbot's free tier adds new daily usage limits. → obvious: Runs every task through the free tier. Hits the new daily limit. / surprised: [viewer AS-04] Me:
  - `own-move-predictable-result` · Students are caught submitting essays with sources a chatbot made up. → obvious: Pastes the chatbot's sources in unchecked. The teacher checks them. / surprised: [viewer AS-14] Me:
- **Our memes, coded:**
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) obvious=Builds a police-only tracker. Police knock on his door.: Own move, obvious result, concrete and specific; the actor is clear from 'his'.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) obvious=Everyone mass-applies with AI. 12,000 applications show up.: Collective own move with a concrete, specific result; the number makes it land. A label would make the shocked party explicit.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) obvious=Skips the human check. Fluent errors slip through.: No actor, and 'fluent errors' is abstract; nobody is shocked.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) obvious=Bugs OpenAI's office. Its models trace it.: An ironic own-move result (bug an AI lab, its AI catches you), but the missing subject reads as OpenAI bugging itself, and Pikachu is unlabeled.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) obvious=Plants a recorder. Faces DOJ charges.: Right shape, but it states as fact an act the story calls alleged, and the result is ordinary rather than ironic.
  - WRONG (Lucas: ungraded) obvious=Builds its own framework. Few understand it.: Vague, no actor, no news turn.
  - PARTLY (Lucas: ungraded) obvious=AI lifts prices. Fed hikes. AI loans cost more.: A backfire on the AI boom is the right idea, but 'AI' isn't an actor, three beats are one too many, and the middle step must be in the facts.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: ungraded) obvious=Gives test AI live web. Has to cut it off. ; surprised=[logo Anthropic]: Own move, predictable result, the actor labeled at Pikachu.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) obvious=Hires ex-Apple staff. Apple gets the files. ; surprised=[logo OpenAI]: Own move, a result that follows, the actor labeled.
  - RIGHT (Lucas: Yes) obvious=Tracks only police cars. Police show up.: Same strong own-move shape as the v2 version, tighter.
  - WRONG (Lucas: No) obvious=Web off for some tests. Now off for all. ; surprised=[logo Anthropic]: The second beat is the same decision widened, not a consequence; the company is not surprised by its own policy.
